# Commentary on Husserl, *Zur Phänomenologie der Intersubjektivität. Texte aus dem Nachlass. Dritter Teil: 1929–1935* (Hua XV)

Citations give the Husserliana XV pagination (ed. Iso Kern, 1973). Kern's editor's introduction (XV–LXX) is blank in the scan used, so the commentary begins with Husserl's texts. Titles in the volume enclosed in angle brackets are Kern's; they are reproduced here in translation like Husserl's own. Retained terms: ego, alter ego, epoché, monad, eidos, cogito. Fixed renderings: Eigenheit/Eigenes as ownness/what is my own; Fremderfahrung as experience of the other; Einfühlung as empathy; Vergegenwärtigung as presentification; Appräsentation as appresentation; Leib and Körper as lived body and physical body; Paarung as pairing; Bekundung as manifestation; primordinal (Husserl's 1929 spelling) and primordial are kept as he writes them.

Six misreadings beset these manuscripts, and the commentary guards against them throughout. The reduction to the sphere of ownness is an abstraction performed inside the transcendental attitude; it does not return to the solitary human being of the natural attitude, who still carries the sense "experienceable by everyone." Appresentation of the other rests on a passive, associative transfer of sense; it is not an inference by analogy, and pairing is not a reasoning. The other is "in" me intentionally, not really (reell): the monads share no lived experiences, yet each implies the others. Empathy in Husserl's sense names the whole constitutive achievement of experiencing another subject; it is not Lipps's projective feeling-into, nor a mood of sympathy. Normality and anomaly are constitutive and teleological titles for concordance of experience, not statistical or medical ones, and the relativity of homeworld and alien world is not a cultural relativism, since every relative world is constituted as a variant of one world. Finally, the late texts on the primal ego (Ur-Ich) do not posit a single supra-individual subject alongside or above the plurality of egos; the uniqueness at stake precedes the very contrast of one and many.

## I. Texts from the Context of the Composition and First Revision of the *Cartesian Meditations*, March 1929 to March 1930

### No. 1. First Version of the Fifth Cartesian Meditation (End of March/Beginning of April 1929)

Kern's note places this text against the published Meditations: it is the draft of the passage announced in the Second Meditation (Hua I, 34) and carried out at length from Hua I, 121 onward. It is the compressed nucleus, written immediately after the Paris lectures, of what the Fifth Meditation would expand.

#### § 1. Exposition of the Problem of the Experience of Others, Set Against the Objection of Solipsism

(3–4) Husserl opens by granting the objection its full force. Once I have reduced myself to my transcendental ego, everything I can know is a synthesis of my own experiences; the others I experience are, so far, unities constituted in me, "represented" others rather than others themselves. If phenomenology is consistent self-explication of the ego, it seems condemned to be transcendental solipsism, and the question of how I could reach egos that are genuinely other seems unaskable inside it. Husserl presents the objection as a chain of apparent self-evidences: my field of knowledge cannot exceed my field of experience, and that field is exhausted by my ego. The whole Meditation will turn on showing that the last link conflates two senses of "my": what belongs to me as a stretch of my own lived experience, and what is constituted in my experience as transcending it.

(4) Husserl refuses to answer the objection on its own terrain. The supposed self-evidences may turn out to be nonsense, and to oppose them with dialectical counterarguments or "metaphysical" premises would repeat the error. The only legitimate procedure is concrete work: to uncover the explicit and implicit intentionality in which the alter ego announces and confirms itself in me, the syntheses and motivations in which the sense "other ego" takes shape and proves itself as being, even as being there "in person." These experiences are transcendental facts in my sphere. The closing question carries the methodological thesis: there is no other source for the sense "existing others" than the interrogation of these experiences. The solipsism problem is thereby converted from a question about the reach of knowledge into a task of constitutive analysis, the same conversion the Fifth Meditation performs at Hua I, 121–122.

#### § 2. The Noematic-Ontic Mode of Givenness of the Other as Transcendental Guiding Clue for the Constitutive Theory of the Experience of Others

(4–5) The guiding clue is the other exactly as he is given to me, taken purely as correlate of my cogito before its noetic structure is disclosed. Even this noematic content is many-layered. The other is experienced as a world object, but not as a mere natural thing: he is a lived body animated by a psyche, a "psychophysical" object in the world. At the same time he is experienced as a subject for this same world, experiencing it and experiencing me as I experience him. The world, therefore, is given in my reduced life not as my private synthetic product but as accessible to everyone, with the others themselves there for everyone. Husserl applies here the procedure of Ideas I § 150: the noema serves as the transcendental guide (Leitfaden) for the analysis of the constituting noeses. The double givenness of the other, as object in the world and as subject for the world, is the duality the late texts will thematize as the paradox of human subjectivity (cf. Krisis § 53).

(5) The constitutive principle must hold without exception. Every sense any being has for me, both its "what" and its "it is in actuality," is sense in and from my intentional life, clarified in systems of concordant confirmation. Every meaningful question about others must therefore be posed and answered through a systematic unfolding of the intentionalities in which the being of others "makes itself" for me and is explicated according to its legitimate fulfilment content. Husserl does not soften the egological principle to accommodate the other; he radicalizes it. The scare quotes on "makes itself" (sich macht) mark constitution as sense-formation, not as production of the other's existence.

(5) The problem first looks special: a transcendental theory of empathy, of the other's being-there-for-me. It soon proves to found a transcendental theory of the objective world as a whole, nature included. "There for everyone" belongs to the very sense of objectivity whenever we speak of objective actuality, and cultural objects (books, tools, works) refer by their origin to subjects, mostly other subjects, while also carrying the sense "for everyone." The theory of the other is thus not one regional chapter of constitutive phenomenology; it conditions the constitution of objectivity as such. This reverses the order of Ideas II, where intersubjectivity enters only after the constitution of material nature.

#### § 3. Reduction of Transcendental Experience to the Sphere of Ownness

(6) Since the constitution of other subjects is in question, and with it a layer of sense that radiates from them over the whole objective world, the first methodological requirement is a "thematic epoché" inside the transcendental sphere already reached. We exclude from the thematic field every constitutive achievement of intentionality directly or indirectly related to other subjectivity, and we delimit the system of actual and potential intentionality in which the ego constitutes itself in its ownness, together with the synthetic unities that are inseparable from it and so belong to its ownness. The thematic epoché is a second, abstractive operation within the transcendental attitude; it is not a repetition of the universal epoché, which already suspended all positing.

(6–7) Husserl insists that this abstraction has an unusual sense. In the natural attitude I find myself facing others; if I abstract from them in the ordinary sense, I remain "alone." But that solitude changes nothing in the natural sense of the world as experienceable by everyone, a sense that clings even to the naturally understood ego and would survive a universal plague that left me the last survivor. Transcendental ownness is not the ordinary human ego reduced to a correlate-phenomenon within the world-phenomenon. It is an essential structure of universal constitution in which the transcendental ego lives as constituting an objective world. The ego's ownness, its being as a monad purely in and for itself, includes even the intentionality directed at what is other; only its synthetic achievement, the actuality of the other for me, is excluded for method's sake. In that intentionality a new sense of being arises that exceeds the monad: an ego not as myself but as "mirrored" in my monad. The "alter" refers back to me; the other is a mirroring of me, and yet not a mirroring proper; an analogue of me, and yet not an analogue in the usual sense. The double denial ("and yet not") fixes the problem the remaining sections must solve: a modification of my own sense that nevertheless posits something absolutely not mine. The plague example reappears in Hua I § 44 and is the textual basis for distinguishing the sphere of ownness from any empirical solitude.

(7) To make the problem intelligible we must characterize the sphere of ownness, at least roughly. The exclusion of the achievements of other-experience is not the phenomenological epoché regarding the validity of these modes of consciousness; that epoché is already contained in the universality of the transcendental reduction. We stand on transcendental ground already. What changes is that we no longer take the phenomena of other being, contained in the world-phenomenon, as indices for constitutive systems. We look away from the others and from everything whose sense presupposes their existence, and ask what remains. The distinction between epoché (bracketing validity) and abstraction (disregarding a stratum of sense) is the decisive guard against reading the primordinal reduction as a further reduction of being; it is a reduction of the theme.

(7–8) Turning to myself under this abstraction, I can still say with apodictic certainty that I am, in this streaming life with all its experiences, including the experiences of the other. Among the constitutive systems we must now set apart those in which consciousness of others plays no constitutive role. In the world-phenomenon there separates out a coherent universal under-stratum: mere nature, stripped of the sense "there for everyone" as well as of all spiritual and cultural predicates. Within this nature my lived body is uniquely distinguished: the only object that is not merely a physical body, the only one "in" which I immediately rule and hold sway, organ by organ. I perceive by touching and seeing kinaesthetically and can always do so; I push and strike by setting my kinaestheses in play. My body perceives itself (one hand the other, a hand an eye), so that organ becomes object and object organ, and it can act on itself practically. Husserl thus derives the lived body as the first primordinal transcendence through the reflexivity of perception and action. The organ-object reversal anticipates the double-sensation analyses of Ideas II § 36 and Hua I § 44, and it will later bear the problem of how my lived body can be apprehended as a physical body at all (Nos. 16, 37; Beilagen LIV–LV).

#### § 4. The Basic Character of the Ownness of Lived Experiences and Potentialities of Experience: Originality in Apodictic Self-Perception

(8–10) The abstracted under-stratum of mere nature and own body constitutes itself in my own experiences and their potentialities, and the basic character of their ownness must first be described. The apodictic evidence in which the reduced ego finds itself bears on my being in the unity of an endlessly open stream of experience. Within this self-perception I distinguish the present, perceived in the stricter sense and streaming, from the endless past and future of my life, which are not perceived properly but held in retention and protention. These are accompanied by evidences of the "I can": I can awaken the sinking past in recollection, repeat recollections of the same past, identify the present as it lapses into the past, and do likewise for the future. Only so is there for me an existing, freely accessible, re-identifiable stream of experience in an immanent temporality. However hard the clarification of inner time-consciousness, the unbreakable evidence of the "I am" contains the equally unbreakable being-for-me of my stream, an evidence with the character of a perception of an endlessly open universe. It does not exclude deception in particulars (whether an experience really occurred, whether an expected one will come), and that possibility belongs to my being. But error only overlays being: to every illusion there corresponds a priori a being that can be sought by a prescribed method, though only approximated, since its full determination is an "idea." Within this structural form and limitation, apodictic self-perception is the only possible way an endlessly open life can be originally given as a unified object, the best conceivable originality. Everything enclosed in this givenness is called "original" in a transferred sense: original to the ego as its own. Husserl here relocates apodicticity. It no longer attaches to a punctual cogito, as in the First Meditation's Cartesian frame, but to the open stream with its horizons of possible error; the apodictic core coexists with presumptive content (cf. Hua I § 9 and the critique of Cartesian apodicticity in Erste Philosophie II, Hua VIII). The concept of originality defined here is the one against which the other's "secondary originality" will be measured.

#### § 5. Extension of the Domain of What Is One's Own: The Originality of What Is Constituted in the Apodictic Ego Inseparably from It

(10) We next include everything that proves inseparable from the apodictic ego, even though it is neither a really inherent part of the stream nor the sphere of potentialities inseparable from it, but is constituted in it through actualities and potentialities. On the side of the ego-pole this includes habitualities, the abiding convictions in which the ego is specifically an ego, though still in the abstract sense that knows no "you." On the object side it includes the objects the ego constitutes in originality: synthetic unities that are inseparable poles of the acts that come to coincidence in the synthesis of identification. Ownness is thus defined by inseparability, not by real inherence. This criterion lets constituted transcendencies belong to my ownness, and it prepares the decisive contrast: the other will be the first constituted object that is not inseparable from my syntheses.

(10–11) Hence "transcendent" objects belong here too, provided they are given as indices of explicable infinities of potential syntheses, and provided their endless disclosure yields only synthetic series in which their unity is inseparably and originally given, as an immanent pole or point of intersection. My lived body and the whole of "mere" nature fall into this sphere. Once the intentional achievements of empathy are disregarded, nature and corporeality are constituted as spatial unities transcendent to the stream, but merely as manifolds of objects of possible experience, where that experience is my own life and its object nothing but a synthetic unity inseparable from it. Husserl calls these "primordinal transcendencies." The concept of an immanent transcendence, worked out first in Ideas I §§ 41–44 for the perceived thing, is here made to define a whole "world" of ownness.

(11) It follows that the concretely taken ego has a universe of what is inseparable from its apodictic being, an original sphere in which it is for itself and has a "world" that is exclusively from it and intentionally in it, in an intentionality of original self-giving belonging to it alone. Corresponding illusions, possibilities of phantasy, and eidetic objectivities also belong to this domain, so far as they are constituted under the guiding abstraction. The inclusion of eidetic objects matters: ideal objects constituted by me alone are as much my own as the primordinal thing, and the later constitution of ideal objectivity will require intersubjectivity just as nature does (cf. § 10 below, and Origin of Geometry, Hua VI, 365–386).

#### § 6. The Experiences of Perceiving Others and the Transcendencies Constituted in Them, as Against What Is Primordinal

(11–12) Of course the experiences of perceiving others belong to the ego taken in its own being, its concrete monad, together with all experiences related to the world in the full sense. Once we take them thematically into account and ask what they constitute, we see that in them a fundamentally new kind of transcendence arises, a whole manifold of new transcendencies, the first being the other egos. For clarity Husserl now introduces his terminology: the primordinal ego, its primordinal experiences and primordinal transcendencies, as against what belongs to the transcendental sphere in a secondary sense, as constituted in it, namely the sense in which other egos and their ownness become conscious and experienceable for me. The term "primordinal," first fixed here, distinguishes two senses of "in me": the experiences of empathy belong to my ownness, their objects do not.

(12) Experience is original consciousness, and the other stands before me "in person"; yet this experience is not primordinal. I experience him as a primordinally experiencing subject, but I do not experience his ego itself, his experiences, his intentional objects, his perspectives on things, nor things as his perceptual things. The case differs from my co-experience of the back of a thing or of something hidden behind it. There appresentation includes the potentiality of turning the appresented into the presented: I can walk around and see it. What is the other's own is appresented in a way that a priori excludes direct experience; were it directly given, it would become a moment of me, and the other would no longer be other. Husserl's argument turns on the impossibility of fulfilment as constitutive of the sense "other." The other is not an epistemic shortfall that better access would remove; inaccessibility in original form is the very mode of his originality (Hua I § 52, the verifiable accessibility of what is originally inaccessible, taken up in Beilage XLVIII below).

#### § 7. The Genuine Sense of the Task of a "Theory of Empathy"

(12–13) The pure and genuine sense of a theory of empathy now emerges. Within the primordinal ego, the absolutely closed sphere of my ownness in which my experiences of empathy occur, how is this higher-level intentionality to be explicated? By systematically disclosing the inner and outer synthetic horizons implied in this secondary experience of the alter ego, we must show how it confirms and constitutes the sense and actual being of another, and so answer every rational question about ego and alter ego from the source that creates sense. The founding of other-experience on primordinal givens means an intentional interweaving indicated in its own horizons, which co-determines its sense and must be disclosed. Husserl replaces the traditional question of how empathy reaches its object with the question of what founding structure the sense "other" implies. The theory of empathy is not an explanatory psychology of a faculty but a horizon-analysis of a founded intentionality.

(13) The guide at the start is the simple sense "other," taken just as it appears in other-experience. "Other" means another ego, alter ego, and I draw the sense "ego" originally from myself. Yet at the start, still caught in the natural apperception of the world, I find myself as a human being, and the ordinary human ego already carries moments of sense that presuppose an open circle of co-existing others. The abstraction to the primordinal is therefore needed even for the sense "ego." The sense alter ego refers back to my own ego in this purity, and "alter" indicates a modification lying in the mode of givenness that intentionally contains my own self as modified. We are directed to a new apperception founded in my constantly living self-apperception, whose sense presents itself as a variation of the first sense "I," something like an analogue. At the most primitive level of other-experience it is the pure primordinal I-myself that is "empathized" in the form "other." Husserl reads "alter" as an intentional modification in the strict sense of Ideas I, a sense that carries its unmodified original within it as reference. This is the root of his later thesis that the other is a "modification of myself" (No. 33; Beilage L), which commentators from Levinas onward have read as the reduction of alterity to sameness. Husserl's own text distinguishes the modification of sense from any appropriation of being.

(13–14) Next come the particular contents of the primordinal sphere that found this apperception and pass, modified, into the sense of the alter ego. Basic here are the primordinal transcendencies: my lived body, the nature related to it, and the psyche belonging to my body. Within this articulated "surrounding world" of my pure ego, the other's lived body as physical thing appears as the motivational basis of other-experience: the body over there apprehended as another's lived body. Constitutive clarification must show how the sensuous similarity between that body and mine lets the apprehension of that body acquire the sense "co-existing lived body, zero-member of a primordinally constituted world of its own with its own ego and conscious life." My body carries the sense-stratum of animation (ruling and being able to rule) and is the zero-object around which the appearing world is oriented. All of this is appresented, in an empty presentification that cannot take on the originality of actual presentation, and can be made intuitive only in intuitive presentifications, somewhat as my past can be intuitively had only in recollection. Referred back to my living self-presence through the primordinally present body as middle term, this presentification is an apperception based on assimilation to that presence, not an "inference by analogy." The other's body is given to me primordinally as a physical body only; its sense "lived body" arrives by transfer. Husserl's crucial addition is the content transferred: not merely animation, but zero-point status, the other as centre of his own orientation. The "here/there" analysis of Hua I § 53 is already contained in this formula.

(14–15) Apperception is no inference, no act of thinking. Every apperception of something new on the basis of an earlier, originally instituting apperception is in a sense an analogizing transfer, but not an analogical inference. Once the child has grasped the purpose of scissors, it simply "sees" scissors without thinking back to the first pair. The primal ego (Ur-ego) for the experience of every ego is the one I myself originally am, conscious of itself as original in constant self-experience, noticed or not. The basic peculiarity of the apperception "other" is that its instituting original is always simultaneously alive and present, so that ego and alter are always and necessarily in original pairing. Pairing is the most original form of intentional synthesis, which in contrast to the synthesis of identification we call "association." In it what appears as distinct is brought to appear as a unity of similarity, a "pair," and I and the other are always given together, even if I attend to neither, so long as another is in my field of experience. Two features separate this case from every other apperceptive transfer. Ordinarily the instituting original lies in the past (the first scissors); here the original, my self-presence, is always co-present with what it founds. And the transfer is not one-directional but a pair, a mutual overlapping. Husserl's scissors example recurs in Hua I § 50, and pairing as passive association links this text to the Analyses Concerning Passive Synthesis (Hua XI, 1920–26), where association had been defined as the synthesis of similarity and contrast among the merely distinct.

(15) The detailed explication of the lines of motivation and, before that, of the sense of such pairing association, and with it the clarification of how the other is constituted in a kind of secondary originality, as "experience," involves difficulties that cannot be pursued here, still less the problem of phenomenological genesis. What is clear is that there can be no other way of clarification than to pursue methodically the intentional problem of constitution contained in the title "experience of the other." Husserl explicitly brackets the genetic question; No. 1 stays within static founding analysis. The deferred genetic problem, how the apperception "other" is originally instituted in the life of a monad, is the one the texts of Parts III and IV take up (Beilage XLV, "The Child. The First Empathy").

#### § 8. What Is Transcendentally Constituted, as Primordinally One's Own and as Not One's Own. The Givenness of What Is Not One's Own Through Presentification

(15–16) Something further, crucial for the course of the argument, is now intelligible. The first step in clarifying other-experience already shows that everything that can ever be constituted as being for me as reduced ego falls under two titles: primordinally own and not own. What is not own in the primary sense can only be an open manifold of alter egos, concrete for themselves with their own ownness, but originally given to me only in presentifying experiences. The peculiarity of these presentifications is that their sense can never appear primordinally; what is presentified can be confirmed only through concordant presentifications, as foreign, as other. It appears noematically in an intentional modification, just as my past is given only in the modification "past" (past present), and yet as rationally confirmed, as existing for me. The past transcends the present; foreign being transcends my own. But it is a variation of my own: the alter ego is ego in itself, yet given as modification of mine, co-existing. In this inner intentional mediation, by the concordance of changing modes of givenness, its being is certain, so that while living in this synthesis I cannot doubt or cancel its actual being. Only thus has the other's existence possible sense and validity for me. The analogy with recollection becomes a structural thesis here: as the past is genuinely transcendent to the living present while constituted in it, so the other is transcendent to my primordinal being while constituted in my life. The parallel of memory and empathy recurs through the whole volume (Nos. 20, 28; Beilagen XXXII, XLI, L), and in its late form Husserl distinguishes the secondary originality of recollection from the tertiary originality of empathy (Beilage L).

#### § 9. The Understanding of Transcendental Subjectivity as Intersubjectivity

(16–17) It is equally evident that in this explication, which I perform as transcendental ego, I necessarily reach the others as transcendental others; the reduction is not breached, and the others are not introduced naively as pre-given beings in the world. The self-disclosure of my ego and of the intentionalities in it that constitute beings leads essentially to the distinction of own and not own, and so to the open manifold of alter egos as being together with my primordinal ego, not in it with primordinal originality, but experienced in it in the most original way possible for them, through presentifying "manifestation." Husserl rebuts the charge that intersubjectivity can only be smuggled in. The others are reached as a result of the reduction's own explication, which is why they are transcendental others, co-constituting subjects, not mundane persons.

(17) Considered from the standpoint of modes of givenness, I myself, my primordinal ego taken concretely, am the primal monad (Urmonade) for all other monads that are manifested in me. The same necessarily holds for each of them with respect to how the others are co-included in it, as synthetic unities of manifestation, though always manifested as primordinally being for themselves. The privilege of the primal monad is a privilege of mode of givenness, not of being, and it is reciprocal: each monad is primal monad for itself. The "for themselves" guards against any reading of the other as merely my product, and it anticipates the formula of No. 33 that the uniqueness of the primal ego is compatible with, indeed constitutive of, a plurality of monads.

(17) Transcendental subjectivity thus extends into intersubjectivity, or rather it does not extend but understands itself better. It understands itself as a primordial monad that intentionally carries other monads within it and must posit them as transcendental others, for concordance of experience, while it continues concordant, makes certainty of being necessary. The transcendental ego first posited after the reduction is still undetermined; it lacks distinctions that lie essentially within it, and it understands nothing yet of the transcendental intersubjectivity that must be intentionally included in it as an ego that experiences an objective world. The correction "not extends but understands itself better" is the philosophical centre of No. 1. Intersubjectivity is not added to the ego; it is the ego's own concrete structure, disclosed by self-explication. The "naive" ego of the first reduction (compare Hua I § 13) is abstractly egological only because it has not yet been explicated, which is why the later texts (Nos. 4, 5) argue for performing the reduction to intersubjectivity directly.

#### § 10. Constitution of Intersubjective Nature. The Connection of My Monad with All Others

(17) If many transcendental subjects exist for me experientially and as being for one another, they are separated in their being by their primordinal ownness. It is easy to see how the transcendencies each constitutes as primordinal nevertheless become identifiable through the intentional community of monads, and how an intersubjective, now objective, world is constituted for "everyone," a world in which each ego is objectified as a bodily-psychic reality, an animal, a human being. The same holds for the ideal worlds each constitutes within its ownness. Husserl states the thesis that the second half of the Fifth Meditation (§§ 55–56) will work out: separation in ownness and identity of the constituted world are compatible because identification spans the separated spheres.

(18) The first transcendence to become intersubjective, the "primal object," is the other's body, constituted first as a synthetic unity of my own sphere and at the same time, through other-experience, as the other's lived body. Following this identity back to its original coincidence, we reach the presentification of his bodily experience, whose co-presentified horizon of ownness leads to the other monad. A synthesis of identification thus spans from my ownness into another that is presentified and can only be presentified, much as within my ownness a synthesis unites my present and my past when a number-formation now produced is identified with the one produced earlier in memory. There a perceived and a presentified sense come to evident identification; temporally separated acts are synthesized through presentification, and thereby they not only constitute the same object but also connect experiences that are temporally outside one another. On this rests the unity of my immanent time. Likewise the ownness of the other ego is separate from mine, including the constitution of his body for me and for him; yet through the synthesis of primordinal and presentified experience the body is experienced as the same, and a kind of connection between my ego and the other is established. The argument has a precise structure: identity of the object across separated acts produces a connection of the acts themselves. Husserl draws the model from the constitution of immanent time, where recollection does not only re-present a past object but binds past and present experiences into one stream. The connection of monads is thus formally parallel to the unity of a single stream, a parallel Nos. 19–20 develop into the constitution of intermonadic time.

(18–19) This carries further. The nature oriented around that body, co-presentified as the horizon of that body, is in consequence synthetically the same as the nature to which that body belongs as a body in my ownness, the nature oriented around my body. The same nature is constituted, as my experiential unity and as his, in like systems of aspects and perspectives, but realized in different correlated courses, as our ordinary saying indicates: were I to take his place and he mine, his courses would be like mine now and vice versa. So the first layer of an objective world, in the order of founding rather than of genetic time, is common universal nature with common space and time, "objective" nature. Transcendentally it signifies a connection of my monad with all monads that can be there for me, a connection of synthesis made possible by mutual presentifying experience, while any really inherent connection, any real passage from my ownness into theirs, is excluded. Objective nature is not a third thing beside my nature and the other's; it is the identity of both established through the "as if I were there" of empathy. The exclusion of real passage is the phenomenological reformulation of Leibniz's windowless monads: the monads have windows of sense, not of reell content.

(19) With common nature, to which the bodies of the monads belong, "animality" is constituted as well. No merely objective nature is conceivable without psychophysical beings in it. The first animal in the constitutive order, or rather the first human being, is the other, and only from him do I receive the sense "human being." All monads then objectify themselves in their ownness, and through the synthesis of these spheres, as psychophysical beings in an objective nature. Two theses are striking. Objective nature essentially implies animate beings in it, so a nature without subjects is a constitutive abstraction; and my own humanity is constituted second, through the other, a reversal of the natural order that Husserl develops in No. 37 as the "spatialization of the lived body through empathy."

#### § 11. Constitution of Social Communities and Cultural Surrounding Worlds

(19–20) Ascending further, we understand the possibility of I-thou acts or "social acts," and thus of objectively existing social communities of various orders, among them the distinguished types of personalities of higher order. Further, the constitution of a human and cultural surrounding world for every human being and community, objective only in a limited way. It is limited because the experience a person gains of a personal community and its surrounding world has horizons that are sufficiently determinate and disclosable for me only if certain "historical" presuppositions are fulfilled, though I can appropriate such presuppositions indirectly and more or less adequately. This holds for all cultural formations arising from personal communalization and its history. Husserl distinguishes two levels of objectivity. Nature is objective for everyone who has a body; culture is objective only for those who share, or can acquire, a historical horizon. This limited objectivity is the seed of the homeworld/alien-world problematic of Part III (No. 27; Beilagen XI, XLVIII). "Personalities of higher order" takes up the doctrine of collective persons from the Kaizo essays (Hua XXVII) and Hua XIV.

(20) So, on the pure ground of transcendental subjectivity as intersubjectivity, a world for everyone is constituted, and necessarily so, solely from constitutive syntheses, rich in problems, that draw all sense from monads that are themselves synthetically interwoven through "mirroring," which is no empty illusion. The parenthesis corrects the Leibnizian metaphor: mirroring is a constitutive achievement with its own evidence and right, not a representation that might fail to correspond to anything.

#### § 12. The Sense of Phenomenological-Transcendental "Idealism"

(20–21) This general reflection reveals the full and proper sense of transcendental "idealism." The appearance of solipsism vanishes, though the principle keeps its fundamental validity: everything that is for me can draw its sense of being only from me, from my sphere of consciousness. This idealism proves to be a monadology which, for all its deliberate echoes of Leibniz's metaphysics, draws its content purely from the phenomenological explication of transcendental experience, that is, from the most original evidence in which all evidence must be grounded. Phenomenological explication is no "metaphysical construction," no theorizing with borrowed premises from the metaphysical tradition. It stands in the sharpest contrast to all that through its procedure of pure "intuition," or rather pure explication of sense through fulfilling self-giving. With regard to the objective world of realities (and the ideal worlds that are the fields of a priori sciences) it does nothing but explicate the sense this world has for us all before any philosophizing, a sense it has only from our experience, which philosophy can distort but never alter, and which, by essential necessity and not by our weakness, carries horizons in need of clarification. The section condenses Hua I § 41 and § 62. Two points mark the idealism as Husserl's own. The egological principle is retained without concession, so that the refutation of solipsism is not a retreat from idealism but its completion. And idealism claims no more than explication of the world's pre-philosophical sense; it changes nothing in what the world is taken to be, only clarifies what that taking implies. The closing remark that horizons belong to experience by essence, not by weakness, anticipates the thesis of No. 2 that the existence of the world is always presumptive, and the later argument that no apodictic proof of the world's existence is possible or required.

### No. 2. The Dependence in Being of Everything That Is, First of All Transcendental Subjects, on Me, and Then of Myself on Them (Second Half of the 1920s)

Husserl filed both texts "for the Paris lectures," that is, with the Cartesian Meditations. Kern judges them heterogeneous in origin: the first part of section a, up to the heading on normality (33), probably predates the Meditations (perhaps 1926), while the remainder of a and all of b were likely written in autumn 1929. The break matters for interpretation. The theory of empathy in a is still framed as a descriptive progression from thing-perception to the other, while the normality problem and the thesis of mutual dependence belong to the period of No. 1.

#### a) Theory of Empathy. Intersubjectivity. The Objection of Madness; the Idea of Normality as a Presupposition Contained in the Ego

(22) Naive life is mundane experiencing life, in which the world is constantly there, always "on hand." Every question is posed about this world, which for anyone experiencing or occupied with anything always already exists: what it is like, how it can be known and by which scientific methods, which needs it rouses and satisfies, what it is worth, how it can be shaped toward ends or used as means. All plans and actions refer to it. Husserl characterizes the natural attitude by its form of questioning. Every question, theoretical, axiological, or practical, presupposes the world as their common ground; the world is not one theme among others but the horizon of all themes, the structure the Krisis calls the "universal ground" (Hua VI § 37).

(22–23) The phenomenologist's attitude is directed at pure subjectivity, at its experiencing and its whole life, including what makes up for it the actual and virtual existence of the world. Its theme is not the world but what in subjectivity's own life constitutes the consciousness that there is a world, and this world as its own: what gives the current world-belief its content, what characterizes perceiving life as one in which existing things are "given" as directly seen, what motivates the certainty in normal perception and how it is further confirmed, what motives may demand confirmation even after perception, and how subjectivity comes to know that things exist outside perception, surrounded by an infinite spatial field of unperceived things, in a world that is only ever partly experienced. To this attach all the problems of world-life concerning a world that is "in itself." The change of theme is exact: the phenomenologist does not stop believing in the world but asks what the belief consists in. The final clause sets the decisive problem, the sense "in itself," which No. 2 will refer to intersubjectivity.

(23) The epoché states the rule: no judgment is made straightforwardly about the world, only about "pure" subjectivity, and about the world only as what is conscious in subjective life, either actually, in some subjective mode, or as a habituality of subjectivity, the purely subjective potentiality of having an open field of possible experience beyond actual experience, with determinate routes of access. The abiding existence and being-in-itself of a world as "the" surrounding world of the subject is taken purely as what it is for and in the subject, as sense formed in it and as possibilities, grounded in it, of carrying out confirmations and of bestowing characters such as "actual," "true," "consistently confirmed and to be confirmed." Husserl defines the epoché by what it permits, not by what it forbids. The in-itself is not denied; it is re-read as a habitual system of possibilities, so that "being in itself" means "confirmable without end."

(23) The first transcendental description is of my own perceptions, then recollections and other forms of experience, then continuous extensions and discrete syntheses, and finally of being outside experience and of the idea of abiding being-in-itself in its true in-itself. All of this proceeds first in my "introspection," with the in-itself first understood as abiding confirmable being for me in the ideally infinite system of my own possible original experience. Husserl marks the order of description and its provisional limitation. Being-in-itself appears first in an egological form, the in-itself of a solitary experiencer; the subsequent analysis must show that this sense is incomplete, since the full in-itself includes "for everyone." "Introspection" is in quotation marks because it is not psychological inner observation but transcendental reflection.

(24) Beginning necessarily with the perception of things, I must as reflecting phenomenologist first be a spectator of my naive perceiving and of what the naively ontically directed ego exclusively has in view. I describe what lies in view according to what the continuing perception not only has in view at a moment but brings into view. I distinguish the thesis of belief, or rather (since the ego-subject is not yet my interest) the characters of being, the thematic, the object-pole and its emerging determinations, from the open ontic horizon and the thematic process as process of fulfilment, of continuing perception of the same identical pole. That pole is determined for me, becomes known, and keeps its acquired determinations ("retained"); new content keeps arriving, yet the object is meant as existing from the outset, since the indeterminate horizon is from the start a horizon of being, in a belief of indeterminate sense that merely becomes determinate. The backward horizon of the start of perception must also be brought out, though that belongs to the wider life-contexts in which every perception is woven. I must point to the subjective modes of the "objective," the thematic itself understood as a subjective occurrence, and move toward the full concretion of perceiving life. A perceptual statement expresses only the thematic, which is concrete only within the milieu of lived experience. The thesis that the indeterminate horizon is from the start a horizon of being, so that further perception only determines what was already posited, is the core of Husserl's horizon-intentionality (cf. Experience and Judgment § 8). The reflecting spectator here is an early working form of the disinterested spectator of Erste Philosophie II (Hua VIII).

(24) Kinaesthetic motivation is constantly present, unthematic yet within the inner field and essential to what presents itself as existing and so-existing in continuing identification. Motivated by the kinaestheses, the object shows itself ever "more completely," adumbrating itself through adumbrating data, so that appearances of shape and colour change continuously while the substrate appears as being thus and thus. The same substrate with the same determinations appears in manifold modes of appearance, in degrees of perfection, nearness and distance. Kinaesthesis is the unthematic condition of thematic identity: without the "if I move, then this appears" no adumbrations would form a series. The analysis prepares the later point that the other's body is appresented as the centre of his own kinaesthetic system (No. 15; Beilage XIX).

(25) In these analyses I no longer describe what the straightforward perceiver is thematically directed at, what he aims at in a forward-reaching striving and attains, but a deeper subjective layer disclosed only in reflection. Admittedly, as I find it by changing attitude it was not so lived in straightforward perception: what was then thematic is no longer so, and what was unthematic is now theme, with the formerly thematic contained in modified form. Yet I recognize that only through such a change of attitude is "the same" before me, whose concretion I can know only thus, and which in itself can only be known thus. Husserl confronts the classic objection, revived later by Natorp and then by Heidegger's critique of reflection, that reflection alters what it grasps. He concedes the alteration and denies that it falsifies: thematic modification is the only access to the concrete life, which is "in itself" knowable only by reflection. The defence is repeated in No. 26 ("everything subjective, even the other's, accessible only through reflection").

(25) The unity of each perception is the unity of a nexus of grounding. Each thematic positing is motivated by subjective circumstances, by the adumbrations kinaesthetically connected with them in an "if-then," and so by what appears in them, and it is evidently grounded in the course of fulfilment, but with an essential proviso: the open horizon is not yet grounded, and possibilities of disappointment remain open. Perception is a grounding, and therefore a form of reason, already at the level of passive motivation. The proviso of possible disappointment is not a defect in this grounding but its structure, the presumptive character that No. 2 will extend to the existence of others and of the world.

(26) So far everything subjective in thing-perception has been considered purely as directly experienced and experienceable by me. To make the experience of others mediately my own, and to clarify the world in its being-in-itself as against any experiencing subjects and as there for everyone, I must first make empathy, my experience of the existence of other ego-subjects, intelligible. The sentence fixes the double function of the theory of empathy as in No. 1: it explains how others' experience becomes mediately mine, and it clarifies the in-itself of the world, which is precisely its independence from any particular subject.

(26–27) Is it not self-evident that I first have external experience of the other's body as a thing within my egological world, with well-grounded knowledge of it as long as experience is concordant? Second, this body in its subjective mode of givenness for me is not only a motivated unity of appearances. Experienced things take on many changing characters in the context of my life, all "associative" characters. If a tree appears beside another tree in a similar mode of appearance, I cannot help being conscious of "both" together, as a pair: from one there issues an awakening and a kind of coincidence at a distance onto the other and back. Objects of experience as such can carry characters of "reminding" of others, pointing to them, indicating, of appresentation. The associative belongs essentially to the constitution of the world of things: what is experienced as existing points to what co-exists as "there too," and this appresentation has its own way of confirming its anticipatory belief; indeed the horizon of every single thing involves appresentation, associative foreshadowing and possible confirmation by self-giving perception. Husserl's marginal note corrects the order of exposition: the associative in general in my egological sphere must come first, and only then the other's body. The correction states the method of later texts (No. 15; Beilage LIV): pairing of bodies is a case of a general associative law, and the theory of empathy presupposes a phenomenology of passive synthesis. The two trees give the elementary form of pairing ("coincidence at a distance"), the term that No. 1 used for ego and alter.

(27–28) A special mode of appresentation arises when a body I otherwise experience like any other thing enters into associative relation with my constantly appearing lived body. My body is distinguished because all my experience of other things, and all my personal, ego-centred relating to my surrounding world, is associatively interwoven with it. The associative relation of similarity-pairing that the body over there enters with mine brings about an analogical, or apperceptive, transfer to it of the original peculiarity of my body as lived body. In an apperception belonging to my life, a subjective life is indicated and appresented in belief by that body: an experiencing, having things before it, having an abiding world, being related as an ego in affection and action, a life not mine, not originally experienced or experienceable by me. Everything belonging to my life and constituted in it as synthetic unity is exclusively mine; what is appresented is an analogue of all this, presentified instead of originally present, its manifolds of appearance belonging not to my actual and possible experience but to a presentified actual and possible experience. Yet not arbitrarily: in determinate motivation, with determinate sense and with its belief, which is belief in being, referred to an indicated life with indicated perceptions. To this appresentation belong paths of consistent confirmation, running within my own life but keeping wholly to modes of presentification and in principle unable to pass into direct experience of my own. As long as this indirect experience runs concordantly, I cannot help believing in what is appresented and experiencing that body as lived body, though not as a body whose lived corporeality I experience originally, but as another's. Husserl's statement that "what is directly experienced is always only presentification" makes the paradox exact: the presentification is itself originally experienced as my lived experience, while its content can never be. Confirmation of the other therefore takes place entirely inside my life but never as fulfilment in my own original intuition. This structure is the basis for Husserl's later naming of empathy a "perception" in a modified sense (Beilage IV).

(28) First, then, I have my original world, my nature and myself ruling in it through my original body. This world is my system of "objectivity," but objectivity here means a constitutive formation belonging to my own life: my non-ego, which is not my immanent temporal life itself, since its objectivity consists in being constituted as identical and re-identifiable in the synthetic unification of actual and possible experiences, hence ideal and supra-temporal relative to immanent time and the individuality of the pulses of life, constituted with its own objective spatio-temporality. Husserl separates two senses of objectivity. Even the primordinal world is objective in the minimal sense of a re-identifiable unity standing over against the stream; it is "ideal" in relation to immanent time because its identity is not that of any lived experience. Intersubjective objectivity will be a second, founded sense.

(28) To my original world and to myself as personal and constituting subject I now add the other subjects with their constituting life and constituted world, centred on their bodies. I gain them as a secondary objectivity constituted mediately in modes of presentification, indicated, or appresented by presentification, by bodies in my original world, which now receive the apprehension "other lived bodies with ego-subjects ruling in them." The other is classified as a "secondary objectivity," a constituted unity of a higher level, which keeps the account within the theory of constitution and away from any appeal to a non-constituted direct contact.

(28–29) More precisely, the motivation is such that I can apprehend the other body as another's lived body only by apprehending it as his original lived body, and so by identifying the body "other lived body" belonging to my original world with the body "own lived body" given to him in his appresented original world; consequently also by identifying the nature he experiences as his original nature with the nature I experience as mine. Apprehending the other as other already includes an identification across two original worlds: his body is the same as my body-over-there. The identification of the two natures follows from it as a consequence, not as a further inference, which is the argument No. 1 § 10 compressed.

(29) In this purely transcendental reflection, performed in consistent epoché, I do not merely find within my original life and world a special kind of "representations" of others and perhaps a genetic understanding of how I come to have them. It has been made clear how in my original sphere a distinctive kind of experience is possible and actually functions, in which one of my original things becomes the substratum of an appresentation, a presentification motivated by the subjective way that thing is experienced, which posits another subjectivity as one with that body, yet so that what is co-posited is not one with the original body in my world. For on closer view the body indicates, by analogy with my own body, a second original corporeality, with its restricted modes of appearance and its being a zero-point of orientation, and so a second original world and a subjectivity originally existing for itself, posited together with mine through presentifying experience that can be and is consistently confirmed. The details of the motivation belong to a "theory of empathy." Husserl contrasts two results the transcendental analysis might have. The weaker would be an account of how I come to have "representations" of others, which is what the solipsist objection expects. The stronger, which he claims, is that a specific form of experience functions, positing what is not one with anything in my world. The distinction answers in advance the objection of the next paragraph.

(29–30) Do we not then have two natures and worlds, one constituted originally in me, the other presentified as constituted originally by the other? Do I have the other body in my original world and a double in the appresented one? And could I not be solus ipse, consistently finding in myself motives for appresentations of other subjects and their worlds while the other did not exist? Two objections are raised together. The first, the "double" (Doppelgänger), is a misreading of the structure; the second, the possibility of total deception, is the sceptical form of solipsism. Husserl answers the second first.

(30) The answer, in the last resort: at any moment external experience, both original and secondary, could deceive me, fail to be confirmed, cancel itself. The certainty of my first world is presumptive and yet indubitable while concordance continues; so with the appresented world. My belief could become invalid, but only if motives for this arose in my own life and the counter-experiences confirmed themselves consistently. In the course of concordant experience I am not free to believe or not, to doubt or remain in doubt; certainty is necessary and so indubitable. Husserl places the existence of the other on exactly the same footing as the existence of the world: presumptive in principle, indubitable in fact. Doubt is not a free act of the will, as in Descartes, but requires motivation by discordant experience. The answer to scepticism about others is therefore the same as the answer to scepticism about the world (cf. the non-apodicticity of world-existence argued in Hua VIII).

(30–31) But is it not possible that my life runs as it does, all my secondary experiences of others are consistently confirmed, and yet the others do not exist in themselves? Their own being is being in an original life, which I posit only as motivated by my life, and the confirmations are modes of my life. Husserl presses the objection to its limit. I should then believe rationally that others exist, with a certainty justified in infinitum in the only conceivable way. But is it not then unthinkable that the other does not exist? To say it is possible that he is not implies that this is thinkable; how am I to make the possibility evident? Only by bringing his non-being, or his being otherwise, to intuitive fulfilment. In general I see that I cannot carry out the possibility that I do not exist while experiencing myself; the possibility that another exists or not I can carry out only as the possibility of an empathy and its confirmation or disconfirmation in the course of my life. If I assume confirmation can never be broken, neither by chance nor by my intervention in social intercourse, I have shown the non-being of others to be impossible and unthinkable, in the only way such showing is itself thinkable. It belongs to the content of my experience of others that they have a being-for-themselves presentified with their surrounding world; so it is unthinkable for me that they lack their own life, and unthinkable that for any other ego matters stand otherwise. However I vary myself and my world, this type of other-experience remains, and to each variant of me corresponds a possible other. To vary him on the basis of empathy is to vary myself and my motivations; he is always other, a modification of me, my analogue, intuitable only as I find myself in him or him in me, which requires imagining my body turned into his or his into mine. The argument is a verificationist reduction of the sceptical hypothesis: a possibility is genuine only if it can be intuitively fulfilled, and the only fulfilment the non-being of the other admits is discordance within empathy. A non-being of others compatible with infinitely concordant empathy is an empty word. The final sentences anticipate the eidetic thesis of No. 22: the eidos "transcendental ego" implies the eidos "transcendental intersubjectivity," since every variation of me varies my others with me.

##### The Objection of Madness

(31) What if I go "totally mad" and my world of experience dissolves into a "swarm" without unified sense? Then there are no animals or human beings for me. Does it follow that there are none? Could they not exist, although for me every reason to posit them has ceased? The objection inverts the previous one. Before, others might be absent while my experience posited them; now they might be present while my experience no longer could. If being-for-me depends on concordant experience, does the collapse of concordance abolish the others?

(32) Others are not subjective formations in my life, although they can be for me only through consistently concordant empathy. They are not like physical things, actualities constituted and realized in my egological sphere; they are presentifications of what has actuality originally in another life. If others are already there for me, I can readily imagine them becoming abnormal, going mad, down to a limit where no original nature remains constituted for them. I can imagine them recovering, their interrupted constitution joining the later one, the gaps filled through others' understanding experiences. Anyone can go mad, and I can imagine my own madness, provided I already have others in concordant experience and imagine my unities of experience dissolving and re-forming and my reaching through those others again a universal concordant world. But could not all go mad, and could there not be many pure subjects, each for itself in a worldless life, without community, which presupposes a commonly constituted world? There would then be a plurality of solipsistic egos, unknowable and never knowable as such. Yet does the being of a plurality not presuppose its knowability as plurality, and thus the possibility of a life communalized with all egos, a common world again? Husserl adds, then strikes, the remark that no answer seems possible. The distinction between physical things and others is decisive: the thing's being is exhausted by its constitution in me, the other's is not, since it is the presentification of a life actual elsewhere. Hence my madness does not annihilate the other, whose being does not depend on my concordance in the way a thing's does. But the question of universal madness remains, and Husserl answers it with the principle that being implies possible knowability, applied to plurality itself: a plurality that could never be known as such is not a plurality. The struck sentence shows how open he held the question in 1926. The scenario of a "worldless" plurality of monads returns in No. 33 and Beilage XXXIX as the problem of monads without world.

(32–33) If others exist indubitably for me, then necessarily the original world I experience as my constitutive ideal formation is the same one every other experiences. At first this seems contradictory: my original world is a formation constituted in me, the other's in him, each subjectivity having its own original realizations. How can there be identity, experienced evidently through empathic presentification? The answer: I experience in different experiences, each with its intentional object, that both experience the same, as in every synthesis, above all between present and remembered objects. So I must identify the things I experience and those experienced by the other; my experiences and the other's presentified experiences necessarily come to synthetic coincidence. "Thing" and nature "before" empathy were the ideal unity of my actual and possible experiences, experience of the first, original level. With empathy, thing and world are synthetic unities of my experiences and those of all others, the syntheses between subjects, established through empathy, functioning as the new element. Since the common world is constituted with open horizons for ever new subjects, for everyone, the world is now the unity of actual and motivated possible concordant experiences belonging to an open endless manifold of subjects in actual and possible community. The world is redefined as an ideal pole of concordant experience across an open plurality of subjects. The "before" in quotation marks marks a founding level, not a time before others existed. And the openness of the world to ever new subjects introduces the problem the next section takes up: the subjects constituting the common world must be normal.

##### Mental Normality. Normality as Presupposition of Truth; Normality as the Faculty of Reason

(33–35) I who weigh these possibilities am "mentally normal": I have concordantly progressing experience and in it nature and the spiritual world in confirmed validity, pre-given and continually given as simply actual. I am free in normal, generally successful empirical and logical thinking and rational action. I can think myself mad, but while thinking it I am not mad, as this very thinking shows, which grounds the madman "rationally" as a possibility. The thinkability of madness presupposes the non-mad, and all kinds of "anomaly" prove to be essential modifications of normalities that are therefore prior in themselves. If I am to think a community of madmen as a real possibility, or even as a mere possibility of concordant phantasy, that possibility is referred to me and presupposes my normal being. I also see that I could go mad and in that state have no world and no other, and I see its real possibility in my factual constitution, as disease of my body together with the psychophysically associated disease of my psyche, perhaps to complete madness that leaves me not even a concordant world. But only as a normal ego do I have this possibility, by which I can say I could have been abnormal from the start. Likewise any other not yet mad could have been, and all could be, with me always presupposed in the background as the thinking ego that constructs the possible anomalies from its normality and finds them actual in its normally constituted world, as part of a normal world related to normal subjectivity. Every normal other stands equal to me in this and could appropriate the same possibilities; the normal other refers essentially to me as normal ego, and once his being for me is grounded, mine refers equally to him. He can see that his normal being is the essential presupposition for his entertaining a mad world. Husserl transforms the objection of madness into an argument from the priority of normality. Anomaly is a modification of normality in the strict sense of intentional modification: it presupposes and refers back to its unmodified original, as the possible non-being of a thing presupposes a world of concordant experience. The thinker of the possibility of universal madness is thereby always exempt from it. Two guards are needed. The priority is not empirical, as if most people were sane; it is the priority of the original over the modified. And it is not egocentric, since the same priority holds reciprocally for every normal other. The theme is developed fully in No. 10 and Beilagen VII and XIII.

(35) If my normal being is the bearer, the "Archimedean foundation," not only of the world actual for me but of all worlds thinkable for me, and of the actual and possible other egos related to them, then how far does the essential form that makes me a normal ego extend? Does it not necessarily include a surrounding world that presents an objective world, and so the co-existence of others? Varying myself, I move within this form; each concrete possibility of my ego within it yields me differently, but as a normal ego. Husserl asks whether normality, now recognized as an eidetic form, already includes intersubjectivity. If it does, the solitary ego is not even an eidetic possibility, and the question of No. 22 is opened.

(35–36) How then do "concrete ego" and "normal ego" relate? As totally mad, am I not concrete, or concrete only because I was formerly sane or can become so? Is that necessary? And the same for each other, if my horizon necessarily contains normal and abnormal egos. Or can I and every subject be concrete only within a generative, connected, endless nexus of subjects, which a priori can be concrete only as a normal one, with abnormal subjects as modifications of normal ones that always precede them in themselves? This sounds daring, even adventurous, but the possibilities and necessities must be thought through. What is the sense of the normality we have encountered? It relates to true being, to the universe of truth and being that is philosophy's theme, and so to subjectivity as the cognitive source of truth. A subjectivity must exist in order to ground its own being; its being would hang in the air if it were not for itself, self-confirming, grounding itself in possible thought. Without a thinker there is no thinkability, and the empty thinkability of a fictitious subject presupposes that I, or someone shown by me to exist, demonstrate it as possibility. If I, the philosophizing thinker, become subject of knowledge of the possibilities of true being, as I must if I seek clarity, I already presuppose in myself a far-reaching structure. This structure of normality, as the essential structure of the "reason" correlative to true being, enters necessarily into every other actual or thinkable for me who is to be a bearer of truth, a subject of transcendental reason. The paragraph introduces generativity for the first time in the volume: concreteness may require a generative chain of subjects. Husserl then identifies normality with the structure of reason as correlate of true being. Normality is no longer a psychological predicate but a transcendental one: it names the conditions under which subjectivity can be the source of truth. The principle "without a thinker no thinkability" is the transcendental form of the Leibnizian thesis that possibilities require an actual mind, restated in Beilage XXXIII as "the primacy of actuality over possibility."

(36) If there is to be a world, if subjects are to be for themselves and with one another, if a world of numbers, essential truths, and essences themselves are to be possible, there must be normal subjectivity. I precede everything that is for me not merely as an indeterminate ego but as a normal subject of reason, and describing this is the great theme. The claim extends normality from the real to the ideal: even essences presuppose normal subjectivity. It corrects the indeterminacy of the ego in No. 1 § 9 by naming the determination it lacked, rationality, which will become in the 1930s the teleology of reason in transcendental intersubjectivity (Nos. 22–23).

(36–37) As phenomenologist I set out the ego as the necessary starting point of a philosophical reflection that is to make the universe of beings constitutively accessible. At first it comes into view concretely but undisclosed, a fact I disclose step by step. Soon I see that I cannot reach a universal disclosure of the fact, and must turn to the task of the essential form of this ego and its structures, to be explicated in infinitum. Is this ego not from the start, as essence of my transcendental subjectivity, the normal rational ego? Or rather, does not my goal of making intelligible the universal constitution of everything that is for me, in the unity of a totality of beings, oblige me to begin with particular guiding clues and constitutive investigations and proceed to possible universes of being, possible worlds, so that the constitutive structures are of themselves united as the unity of the rational ego? Of course the potentialities of unreason belong to it too, associative passivities, everything that must be there for an ego to be concrete as a rational ego. I can and must bring all modifications of discordance, all anomalies, into the investigation and master all possibilities and impossibilities. The passage moves from fact to eidos as Hua I § 34 does, but with a specific result: the eidetic form of the ego is the form of a rational ego. Reason does not exclude unreason but includes it as its own potentiality; passivity and association belong to the rational ego's concreteness. The thesis anticipates the later formula that the transcendental ego is a teleological structure in which reason is the immanent goal of all functioning.

(37–38) In fact the world contains irrational subjects, temporarily or permanently "mentally ill," and as such they are real, constituted concordantly as much as rational subjects, who in any case, like me, are not always rational. Yet my rationality, with the idea of being constituted in it, my being in possible consistent reason, always precedes. From here the questions are: which forms of irrational subjects are possible as modifications of a presupposed rationality, and how does the presupposition of a world of other subjects and of rational subjects stand, from the standpoint of the possibility of a totality of being, of my being for myself and then of an open plurality of others? An objective world presupposes an open plurality of subjects; does it presuppose one of rational subjects? This does not exclude madmen, who perhaps for deeper essential reasons must exist as real possibilities and even actualities in a real world. But must an objective world exist while I necessarily exist, namely while I philosophize? Are there essential grounds in my egological essence that make it necessary that an objective world exist, with rational subjects for whom it proves intersubjectively true and concrete? Here the great problem of generation arises. Does the essence of my being, and mediately of others' being, include a constitutive structure by which a world with generatively connected human generations necessarily exists, encompassing me as constituted human being? Do human beings in generative connection belong necessarily to the totality of being and so to every world, so that despite the "death" of individuals human beings are necessary and death in the world always preserves subjectivity as rational subjectivity? Husserl drives the inquiry to its most speculative point. The question whether an objective world is necessary given my necessary existence asks for a transcendental deduction of the world from the ego, which Husserl elsewhere declares impossible (the world's existence is presumptive); here he asks whether the eidos of the ego demands at least the form of a generative world. "Death" in quotation marks marks the transcendental problem developed in Beilage VIII and Beilage I: what birth and death mean for a constituting subjectivity that cannot itself begin or end in its own experience.

#### b) Transcendental Dependence of Others, of the Generation, and of the World on My Ego

(38) What motivates, in any sense "grounds," my transcendental beginning? My generative connection with my parents and ancestors? But do we not run into great difficulties? The question is posed at the transcendental level. Every human being has a generative origin; the transcendental ego, as source of the sense "generation," cannot without circularity be grounded in what it constitutes. The difficulties are those of every naturalistic account of the constituting subject.

(38–39) I must say again and again that I, the phenomenologist in the transcendental attitude, am not a human being philosophizing but the transcendental ego, experiencing and thinking phenomenologically. Whatever is for me, the universe included, is constituted in me through my transcendental life, including the whole generative nexus to which I as constituted human being belong as an object of possible worldly experience. The others are validity-formations arising in me in determinate motivation within my closed transcendental life, habitual acquisitions, re-identifiable and synthetically confirmable by renewed experience. So I see and say that in truth and according to the sense constituted in me they are "my likes," with their transcendental life, which I experience analogically through the self-presentifying mode of appresentation, a mode that lets me come ever closer to the other's self and gain ever more perfect knowledge of him, always in modes of self-presentification. My own transcendental being is also for me from a constitution I achieve and have achieved, and a validity that arises in me and justifies itself in first apodicticity. All this is itself transcendental-subjective occurrence and acquisition in me, as I see by reflection, iteratively, the possibility of free iteration itself being a transcendental acquisition. So I hold others in validity, confirmed and to be further confirmed and improved in content, just as I hold myself and our common world. But I precede everything in my being and being-valid-for-myself; all validity is from my validity. One might say this means "only" the triviality that I come first in the order of knowledge. But does this "only" not betray an inadequate grasp of the transcendental situation? Does it not mean that the being of all others depends on my own being, in the transcendental sense? Husserl refuses the epistemological reading of the egological priority. The priority is not merely that I must exist to know others, but a dependence in being, though "in the transcendental sense," that is, a dependence of sense and validity. The phrase "self-presentification" (Selbstvergegenwärtigung) is notable: empathy presentifies the other's self, and in doing so is my own self-presentification as other, the formula Beilage XLI makes central. My own being too is a constituted validity: the ego is not exempt from constitution but is self-constituting, with "first apodicticity."

(39–40) This becomes clear when I consider that every freely possible re-thinking of my transcendental being includes a re-thinking of the whole world and of the transcendental others. If I vary myself as world-constituting, first of a primordinal world, so as to constitute any possible world, I vary all others with me. If I vary my transcendental life so that its constitutive connections change and this world, as the world of my appearances, opinions and confirmations, would contain some other I experience in a changed form or not at all, then there would be a different other or none. For "there is an other" means for me only that he is from my constitution. So the being of others depends transcendentally on mine. Yet it belongs to the sense of the other existing for me that the same holds for him. Thus the world, and the others and through them the intersubjective world, are so constituted for me that everything that is depends on my being, but also that everything that is depends on the being of the others, who are for me. Husserl argues by eidetic variation: the others vary with me, I do not vary with them. The second half of the title, the dependence of myself on them, follows not from this asymmetry but from the sense of the other: what holds for me holds for him. The concluding formula is the mature doctrine in nuce. Dependence is reciprocal and total in content, yet asymmetrical in mode, since the reciprocity is itself constituted "for me." This is the structure later named the "absolute" and "unique" ego, which includes its own relativization among others (No. 33; Beilage XLII).

### No. 3. Ontic Priority of Constitutive Subjectivity. The Impossibility of Thinking It Away from the Constituted World in Its Worldly Self-Objectivation. Apodicticity of the Ego and Hypothetical Apodicticity of the Alter Ego (End of October to 4 November 1929)

Kern prints only the last six sheets of an eighteen-sheet manuscript; the first twelve treat the necessity of the ego. Husserl's cover note lists the whole as concerning the ontic priority of constitutive subjectivity, its self-constitution as animal and human in the world, and the constitution of the infinite spatio-temporal world through natural and spiritual history, and judges it "important thoughts, but not well presented."
