The fragment begins in mid-argument, after the necessity of my ego for any conceivable world has been established.

(40) From me, from my here-and-now present with its horizons of possible experience and their temporalizing modifications, present, past and future have their original sense of being; and from my eidetic variations every eidetically possible world has its sense as being in these modes. I coincide with all my possibilities, phantasy-possibilities of what I can do, and the present of my world coincides with the present coexistence of every variant of me and my possible world. The temporal modes are anchored in the living present of the ego; every possible world is temporally structured relative to a possible variant of that present. The "coincidence" of the actual ego with its variants is the condition of eidetic variation: the variants are mine, not others' possibilities.

(40–41) A past without my world-life I can understand and know from the present in which I live and from which I also know the past stretch of my life. A past before my birth is still constituted from my present, by the same route through which I know my own past. The remark prepares the problem Beilage I and Beilage VIII will address, the sense of a world-history in which I did not yet live.

(41) Others I know through present empathy "immediately," yet in another sense mediately. Empathy, motivated in my present life, is in itself mediately presentifying. The corporeality in my primordinally experienced world appresents the other; I then experience others as co-experiencing subjects, and in this co-experience I gain the objective world as ours, with myself and my primordinal present carrying this whole experience and knowledge of the world and the being of the others. This holds in every way of conceiving a world, primordinal or objective, in eidetic variation. The immediate/mediate formula fixes empathy's double character precisely: immediate as an experience, not an inference; mediate in its intentional structure, which reaches the other through his body. That this "carrying" by my present holds in every eidetic variant makes the egological priority an essential, not a factual, one.

(41–42) Can I re-think the world so that it remains as it is, an existing world, while I do not exist or occur in it? I can re-think it in infinitely many ways, but every re-thinking of a thing, the Feldberg for example, necessarily varies me as subject for that world; my experiencing life could no longer run as it does. Conversely, every variation of my experiencing life that changes the systems of concordant experience changes the whole world existing for me. Varying things outside me I lose myself as this factual present ego of this factual life, but not my ego as present ego of the changed world; I am the same ego with necessarily another experiencing life. Varying the world quite freely, I am necessarily present in every variant as co-varied ego, as "present" ego of each fictive "present" world, and past and future of that world belong to that present. Their constitution has the same style, always referred to my varied primordinal ego. The argument for the inconceivability of my absence turns on a correlation: the world cannot be varied without varying me, so in any world I imagine I am implied as its experiencer. The identity of the ego across variations is formal identity of the pole, not identity of life-content, which is why Husserl can say I remain "the same ego" with "another life." The Feldberg, near Freiburg, is Husserl's habitual local example.

(42) In a sense this holds for every other human ego in the world and his variations, but only thus: every other is other for me, actual in the actual world, quasi-existing in each possible world and then existing for me, the modified me, as "other." The other I experience from my primordinal being, through manifesting presentification, is another ego, a subject for this same world, experienced by me as constituting, as constituting from his present the world-present and, in further forms, world-temporality with its past and future. The other is also a centre of temporalization, but his centrality is given to me as such within my variants; the symmetry is real but stands inside an asymmetry of givenness, the formula of No. 2 b now applied to time.

(42) If my being cannot be thought away from the world I experience, must I not also say that the other's being, every other's, cannot be thought away, that every conceivable world must contain every other? The question generalizes the result of the preceding paragraph and so threatens it with paradox: if every other is necessary, the eidetic variation of worlds would include no world without these particular others.

(42) If the other constitutes the world as I do, and I experience him so, he must say to himself with insight when he meditates as I do: "I cannot think myself away from the world; however I re-fashion it, it contains me again as re-fashioned, as the ego who from my primordinal sphere experiences things and persons as my others and through them everything worldly as intersubjective, as 'objective.'" He is right, for he sees it apodictically. And must I not recognize that he is right; can I not co-experience his insightful thinking, though not primordinally? The other's Cartesian meditation is itself something I can empathize. Husserl makes the transcendental reflection, not only perception, an object of empathy, a move that grounds the later idea of a community of phenomenologists (Nos. 13, 21).

(42–43) Does this not embarrass us? I say apodictically: I am, though I am often deceived about myself. My being-so contains much that is not apodictic, yet I am, and I am as experiencing this world, whose certainty of being is not apodictic but belongs as bracketed, as meant, to the apodictic. It is also apodictic that however I imagine the world of experience, I as a human being would belong to it: to the variation of my factual life necessarily belongs a variation of my empirical ego, the psychophysical human being in the world. The other's being, not only his being-so, is not apodictic for me, but it is for him. That means: if I am certain that he exists, I am certain that he can become aware of himself, by self-reflection, as existing with apodictic certainty. But this second certainty depends on the first. The other is not simply necessary for me, either as worldly or as world-constituting; I can be deceived about his being, and so also about his constituting necessarily the same world. Husserl resolves the paradox with the concept named in the title, "hypothetical apodicticity." The other's apodicticity is genuine but given to me under the hypothesis of his existence, which is empirical. Two further points matter. The world is "bracketed" yet belongs to the apodictic as meant: the world-phenomenon, not world-existence, is part of the apodictic ego. And my humanity belongs necessarily to every world I imagine, the thesis of worldly self-objectivation announced in the title.

(43) Consider now the stepwise constitution of the objective world. "As long as" no other is constituted in my primordinal sphere, supposing this were really conceivable, my primordinal world would be a mere "formation" of my primordinal ego. But as soon as I experience an alter ego as another human being and hold him in legitimate though only presumptive validity, I have posited a primordinal world of the other as formation of his transcendental ego. At the same time a certain circle of my and his primordinal world is evidently identical, and this identity propagates through mutual co-validity to what is surplus on either side, so that a common world, and then a common spiritual cultural world, is constituted. The scare quotes on "as long as" and the parenthetical doubt mark the primordinal world without others as a founding abstraction, perhaps not even a genuine possibility. The "surplus" (das Überschüssige) is what one subject experiences and the other does not; mutual co-validity transfers it into the other's horizon, so the common world exceeds each primordinal world.

(43–44) I now have two egos, or more: mine apodictically certain, in which the other is empirically certain, as certain of himself apodictically and as experiencing me empirically. Correspondingly I have not two worlds but one world constitutively related to both egos, with the privilege of the self-reflecting one, myself. I experience the world as the world of us both by having the other in empirical certainty; I no longer have my primordinal sphere as world but have it constitutively related, through him, to his, with each having his primordinal "aspect" and his way of constituting it into world through the other. The world is now the common world of two transcendental egos and two mutually experienced human subjects, not a formation belonging to one ego's concretion alone. If the world is constituted, from me but by both of us, and so has intersubjective sense, it cannot be without these two subjects; each knows apodictically that it belongs to it, and must take over the other's apodictic certainty of belonging to it under the hypothesis that the other really exists. The "Doppelgänger" objection of No. 2 is now answered directly: there are not two worlds but one, since the primordinal worlds become aspects of a common world. The world's necessary inclusion of the other is apodictic only hypothetically, conditional on the presumptive certainty of his existence.

(44–45) Suppose instead of one other an open, indeterminate plurality of others is constituted. Then the world in my validity has its sense determined not only by my actual community with determinate persons, directly or through their reports, but also by the indeterminate others anticipated as actual or as open possibility. The world is determinate and openly indeterminate, and every human being entering my experience is there not only as a worldly being but as transcendental ego, as other whose experiences count for me too, experiencing in unity with me and knowing himself at one with me. Everything worldly is intersubjectively constituted. The constitution of intersubjectivity and of the intersubjective world is constantly "on the march," with a horizon that anticipates new sense from new subjects. So "everyone," including every subject anticipated indeterminately, cannot be struck from this world in advance. The horizon of others is itself constitutive: the world's sense includes the anonymous others not yet met. The phrase "on the march" gives the world's intersubjective sense a dynamic, open character that the Krisis will call the "community of horizon."

(45–46) The sense of the world for me is never finished but endlessly open. It is formed further not only by my primordinal experience but, quite differently, by empathy; each concordant empathy gains me a co-subject for the world and a new prefiguration of sense related to him and his community with me and all other co-subjects. Yet as a "mature human being" reflecting, I already have the world as communal: an open endless world with an open manifold of possibly co-existing unknown humans and animals, with the expectation that new others will appear as new co-bearers of the world's sense. So this is an extension of the sense-formation "objective world" within a general sense-formation already accomplished. Its open horizon and general style are prefigured: unknown others are filled in by newly known ones, while an open horizon of unknown ones always remains. Husserl distinguishes the material extension of the world's sense by new others from the formal sense "world for everyone," already accomplished in the "mature" adult. The adult's world is the starting point of reflection; the genetic question of how the child acquires it is reserved (Beilage XLV).

(46) Now it is clear in what sense I must say that I stand equal to every other as constituting co-bearer of the world. Like me, every other is necessary for the existence of the world that is actual for me. I can think none away without giving up this world, neither any determinate other nor, implicitly, any subject anticipated in the open horizon. The paradox of (42) is resolved: every other is necessary not for any world whatever, but for this world, whose sense includes him. To think him away is not to think this world without him but to think another world. The equality of constituting co-bearers is the transcendental counterpart of the egological priority; both hold, at different levels.

(46–47) The relativity of the sense-formation "being" in world-constitution must not be overlooked. The first sense-formation is primordinal, always mobile, with its inner and open outer horizons. This openness is only the substratum for the higher sense-formation through intersubjectivity, which must not be understood as a genetic succession. Intersubjectivity does not merely enrich the primordinal ego's sense-formations with closer determinations, some of which it could have reached itself, or fill its outer horizons with things it could not otherwise have reached. The primordinally relative being is preserved, with every primordinally confirmed property, when others co-function in constituting, but only in a certain way. For the communalization of experiences involves identification, yet not as if each world object were given in a mere repetition of constitution, all realities completely alike in the synthesis of my experience and the other's. On the one hand, "repetition," like constitution of like in different egos, is not yet constitution of the same and so of the same world in numerical identity; on the other, intersubjectivity constitutes as the same more than what is primordinally alike. Two distinct theses are stated. Numerical identity across subjects is not mere qualitative sameness of constitution: two egos each constituting a similar world would not thereby share a world, which requires the identifying synthesis of empathy. And the intersubjective world contains as identical more than the overlap of primordinal worlds, namely cultural objects, which have no primordinal correlate at all. Husserl's footnote against the genetic reading reiterates No. 1's "founding, not genetic-temporal."

(47–48) First, the intersubjective synthesis makes of what is first primordinally existing an intersubjective being, for which every experience of each co-functions. In this synthesis there is again concordance and discordance. Keeping to concordance, to the realm of true being, the synthesis between what is alike constituted in each primordinal sphere (coinciding individually via the identity-coincidence of the other's body in both spheres, which belongs originally to empathy) yields only a core of the objective world, common nature. What one constitutes in surplus enters necessarily into the other's open horizon as closer determination, and conversely. Appearances are not identical and interchangeable except in everyday life of a "normal" community; psychophysical anomalies exist (illness, congenital differences of sensibility). But the relativity of "appearing things" to the body, which in experience lets the thing itself be constituted as the identical in all such appearances, belongs already to each primordinal sphere and is the same for every subject. Ideally each has the same things in appearances corresponding to his psychophysical circumstances, and mutual understanding identifies the same thing from the normal ground empathy itself provides, referring differences back to psychophysical circumstances; otherwise they would count as discordances forcing one side to be taken as illusion. The colour-blind man sees quite correctly; his deviating colour experience belongs to his "psychophysical constitution." I would take it as false only if I took the "normal" seeing of the "normal" community as the norm of truth. Were my constitution changed by "illness," I would see the same things as he does. The physical thing "itself" stands in this relativity, which does not first arise with intersubjectivity. The colour-blind example dismantles the identification of normality with truth: anomalous perception is not false but relative to a different bodily constitution, and the thing itself is the identity across all such relativities. Since relativity to the body already exists within one primordinal sphere (the same thing looks different with an injured eye), intersubjectivity extends an existing structure rather than creating a new one. This is why nature is the core of objectivity, as against culture in the next paragraph. Beilage II, to which Husserl refers, distinguishes modalization from mere discordance between normal and abnormal subjects.

(48) Cultural determinations are different. They stem from intersubjectivity. Technical works belong to the world of everyone, not because each has already constituted them primordinally. A bridge in its general purpose for everyone exists only through intersubjectivity and has a quite different relativity from mere nature. Likewise an association or a people is not in the world merely because a heap of psychophysical beings is there; these beings are subjects functioning constitutively in community for the formation of the association and for each one's achievements as member, and in the state through law, which again rests on the constitutive achievement of communalization. The contrast completes the previous paragraph: nature is intersubjective by identification of primordinally alike things, culture is intersubjective in its very constitution. Social entities are not aggregates of individuals but correlates of communal constituting acts, the thesis Husserl had developed in the Kaizo essays and would deepen in No. 29 and Beilage XXXI.

(48) But we must ask whether we have not made a secret idealizing presupposition on which the whole striking account depends. The doubt targets the necessity of every other for this world, which rested on the assumption that this world exists.

(48) I presupposed the existence of the world in continuing experience. It is experienced as a "historical" world in the extended sense, which I can question about its "history" and experience as infinite in past and future, the history of the past prefiguring that of the future. The idealization is that this world is assumed as a determinate infinite totality, though experience gives it only as presumptive. The "extended sense" of history includes natural history, as the cover note says.

(48–49) Transcendentally I find myself as primordially constituting ego, my experiencing life related to others and theirs to others, according to the world naturally experienced for us. From concordance of experience I have the infinite world-horizon in pre-validity. But it is determined only through experience, within its prefigured horizon-structure, which holds presumptively, and with an unknown content presupposed as determined in itself. The world-horizon is valid in advance (Vorgeltung), its form prefigured, its content an unknown presupposed as determinate. The structure is that of the Krisis's "world-horizon," and it is the source of the idealizing presupposition.

(49) Consider that the world for me is always the world valid with its current sense, relatively determined, and so it is "the" world that affects me and into which I live, co-determining its content by my free acts. The same holds for others, who are valid for me from experience with a certain content, including a domain of their co-constituting life. We have the world in its being-so in continued relativity. Through experience we posit it in an infinity of horizon of which we in fact know nothing, which is presumptively prefigured but not given in its infinities. What possibilities arise here? "The" world is always the world-as-currently-valid, and the others too are given in a determinate content. The relativity of the world's being-so is total; only its form is fixed. The question that follows asks what variation within this relativity allows.

(49) Things, animals, human beings can be illusions, even intersubjective ones. They determine us, yet they are not, and we strike them out. The world valid for us so far changes in its sense of being without exceeding the general sense, the total region "world in general." It seems we could strike things and even subjects out of the world, so that while they count as belonging they do not, without the world changing; only our "idea of the world" changes. But here we move under the presupposition of the idea of an existing world, in the relativism of meant worlds relative to the existing one. Illusion means that concordant experience runs otherwise than prefigured. In considering possible worlds, however, we vary a possibly concordantly existing world, to which we ourselves and all our changes of the "world-idea" belong. Holding to the idea of an existing, fully determined and concordant infinite world, we are all necessary for it, actual in the actual, varied in the varied world. Husserl distinguishes two operations easily confused: correcting the world (striking out illusions within a presupposed existing world) and varying it (imagining another possible world). Correction leaves the world-idea intact and can delete subjects mistakenly posited; variation produces a different world, and in it all its subjects are again necessary. The "secret idealization" is thus not a flaw in the argument but its ground: the necessity of subjects holds relative to the idea of a fully determined world, which is itself an idea in the Kantian sense, never given.

(50) What of life and death? The single question closes the fragment and opens the problem on which the necessity of subjects for the world founders or must be reformulated: individuals enter and leave the world, so a world can exist without any given person. Beilage I and Beilage VIII pursue it, and the formula of No. 2 a, that death in the world might always preserve subjectivity as rational subjectivity, is the direction Husserl takes.

### Appendix I. Primordinal and Solipsistic Reduction (October/November 1929)

(50) What is "entering life," being born? Is it a mode of waking? Are there modes of waking: waking from sleep, which synthesizes present wakefulness with an earlier one as past, and another waking, a wakefulness with no constituted past behind it? What can that mean transcendentally? "Transmigration of souls"? Could a lowest ego, a plant-like one, be understood as not yet awake in the human sense? The appendix takes up the question with which No. 3 broke off. Husserl treats birth by analogy with waking because a constituting subject cannot experience its own beginning; every waking it knows connects to a prior waking. A waking without past would be an absolute beginning, which transcendental life cannot give itself. The speculative options (transmigration, a dormant "plant-like" ego) mark the problem as open; Beilage VIII and the texts on instinct and the child (Beilagen XLIII, XLV) return to it, and they converge on the idea of a pre-egoic, instinctive life that is "sleeping" rather than absent.

(50) How do I re-think the actual world in imagination so that one of its things is struck out of the varied world? The question starts the analysis of "thinking away" from the simplest case, a thing, to prepare the case of a person.

(50) The actual world here means the experienced world, such that every reality ascribed to it is an object of possible experience for me and for every co-subject, and such that, however far we went and whatever paths of experience we took, we could never meet experiences that would force us to cross out our certainties about it and devalue it as illusion. Actuality is defined as the ideal non-occurrence of cancelling experience across all possible courses of intersubjective experience. The definition is already intersubjective: actuality is actuality "for everyone."

(50) Conversely, re-thinking the world so that an object of possible experience confirmed in concordant intersubjectivity is struck out means imagining the style of experience related to it changed into one that devalues it as illusion. This variation is a variation of the psychic inner life of us all. The one who re-thinks holds himself identical in the variation and continues to exist, varied, in the varied world, and he keeps his fellow human beings as belonging to it, since as "objective world" it is a world for everyone, which refers to co-subjects. To remove a thing from the world is not to delete an item while all else stays the same; it is to alter everyone's experience, including mine. The re-thinker and his fellows persist, since the world as objective implies co-subjects.

(50–51) What if I "think away" another person? It is like the previous case, a special case of it. Others are in the world for me through the mode of experience called empathy, which also has concordant confirmation and possible reversal of being into illusion, so I could think any one other away, and one after another. But all of them together? Myself as experiencing human being I can never think away while I am to have a world of experience; my present as experiencing, seeing and hearing bodily, is unalterably necessary. But if all my fellows turned into illusion, the rest of the world could not remain unchanged, and above all I could not, particularly in my psychic being, in which all manifolds of experience concordantly referred to others would change radically or disappear. I would be thoroughly changed, though myself; social life, the whole intersubjective cultural world, would have vanished. What remains as world keeps the identity with the actual world that makes talk of a variation of the actual world meaningful, just as I remain the same, the same and yet fundamentally transformed, not changed in the sense of a real alteration. The argument shows what "solus ipse" would actually be: not the present world minus others, but a radically different world in which I too am radically different. The distinction between variation and real alteration is precise: in variation I do not undergo a change; I am imagined as another possible self, identical as ego-pole.

(51) Am I still a human being, the same, only solus ipse in an imagined world? Is the world still a world in the natural sense, which is always meant as a world for everyone and so already spiritual, cultivated? If we call every pure essential variation of the world "world," we must keep the name. But the solipsistically reduced world must not be confused with the primordinal world, nor the solipsistic reduction with the primordinal reduction. The primordinal reduction reduces the world I hold valid to what of it I experience and can experience originally; it reduces me to my primordinal ego as a stratum of my concrete ego. All my empathic experiences belong to the primordinal, though not the others experienced in them, however legitimately; likewise with the determinations of intersubjective culture. A solipsistic world with its space-time and realities could, however, in further experience acquire new sense in a special way: motivations for empathy could arise in me, others could then legitimately be there through concordant confirmation, the solipsistic world would acquire the sense of an intersubjective world, and I would become a human being in the ordinary sense. Husserl's distinction is the key guard for reading the whole volume. The primordinal reduction is an abstraction within the actual, fully intersubjective world: it isolates a stratum, keeping all empathic experiences as mine. The solipsistic reduction is an eidetic variation: it imagines a different world in which no others are experienced at all. The primordinal world is a layer of the actual world; the solipsistic world is a possible world. Conflating them produces the standard objection that the Fifth Meditation starts from a solipsist ego and cannot get out.

(51) In following the possibilities contained in a solipsistic ego or world, a genesis becomes thinkable: of the solipsistic ego into a human being in a human world, and of the solipsistic world into an intersubjective human world. It belongs to the essential possibilities of a solipsistic world that it may reveal itself to the ego, in the course of experience, as not merely solipsistic but as a restricted presentation of an intersubjective world; it belongs to the essence of the ego that finds itself solus that it could find itself, as it lives on, a socius among companions. The solipsistic world is not closed; it contains the possibility of its own transcendence. Husserl's formula, solus as possible socius, makes solitude an incomplete mode of community, the same structure as anomaly relative to normality in No. 2.

(51–52) This could indicate that I and every human being have a subjective, and ultimately transcendental-subjective, development, perhaps by essential necessity, in which I first build up intentionally in my transcendental immanence a solipsistic world, however poor, and then step by step, through empathic experience, the being of others and through them a world in the full sense. Conversely, once this development has occurred and I have the full world as "mature human being," the worlds of lower levels, which were the only valid ones for me as an ego of lower level, necessarily become restricted aspects, "presentations" of the one full world, whose last-won "presentation" is the one valid for me from my fully formed experiencing life. The hypothesis is genetic and carefully hedged ("could indicate," "perhaps"). It treats the solipsistic world as a stage of development, as in infancy, that is retroactively reinterpreted as an aspect of the full world. The retroactive reinterpretation is the important structural point: earlier worlds are not falsified but subordinated as partial presentations. Later texts complicate the hypothesis by introducing instinctive intersubjectivity from the start (No. 34; Beilage XLV), so that the infant's world is never simply solipsistic.

### Appendix II. Distinction Between Modalization and Discordance Between Normal and Abnormal Human Beings in Connection (After 1930)

(52) First, bodies in perceptual givenness for me, the single ego, are intuitive unities of self-givenness in perspectival manifolds of "appearances," unities of appearance; likewise for all past time, as earlier perceptual givenness or as having been perceivable. The presupposition is concordance, consistent confirmation. The first level is egological: the thing as appearance-unity under the condition of concordance within one subject's experience.

(52) Second, bodies are thus experienceable for everyone; for each they are such unities of appearance, concordant for him. The second level asserts that each subject separately has such concordant unities, not yet that they coincide between subjects. Intersubjective agreement is the next, third step.

(52–53) For normal communities of experiencers, sensory normality, people agree with one another. Each from his place, in his orientation, has his appearance-series and appearance-unity, the same that the other would have on changing his orientation, and by understanding this and understanding the other accordingly, things always agree, for a normal human community. For abnormal people they do not. Yet I come to an understanding with the abnormal person, who remains for me one who experiences the same things in his manifolds of appearance. A core must be common, including what lets spatio-temporal form be identified not only generally but individually. How does nature, and then the world, constitute itself for me as identical experiential nature for all under the idea of the same nature presenting itself in intersubjective concordance, though each has his own concordant unity of appearances, the normal his normal, the abnormal his abnormal ones? Normality and anomaly are concepts that relate only to connection between subjects. In connection "the" nature presents itself as identical for all in one stratum, the stratum in which "exchange" of appearances is possible, taking the other's appearances as those I would have in his orientation and circumstances, and this concerns spatio-temporality and especially spatio-temporal position. What goes beyond this, belonging to the concreteness of the other's natural experience, is experienced in discordance as deviation from the normal, from familiar sensory tradition (essential generalities are intersubjective without further ado). But the deviation can soon be anticipated in kind and degree. If his vision is abnormal he still has colours, and I can determine the "colour equations"; if he is born deaf, I can communicate with him through the other senses. Understanding about a common nature presupposes that the anomalies have a style that permits a reduction to normality. This concordance and discordance must be distinguished from the modalization and elimination of illusion that occur in the constitution of nature for the single ego and again within normal community. Three theses are compressed here. Normality is a relational concept: it exists only in the "connection" (Konnex) of subjects, not in an isolated subject. The common core of nature is spatio-temporal form, individually identifiable, which survives every sensory anomaly, while secondary qualities may diverge. And discordance between normal and abnormal is not illusion: the abnormal subject's experience is not cancelled as false, as an illusion is in modalization, but understood as a styled deviation that can be reduced to normality by rule. "Familiar sensory tradition" anticipates the generative, historical concept of normality in Part III, where the normal is what is handed down in a homeworld.

### No. 4. The Personal Surrounding World in Its Articulation. Reduction to Pure Intersubjectivity and Reduction to the Ego. On the Beginning of the Second Cartesian Meditation (7–9 March 1930)

Husserl's own table of contents for the manuscript reads: the personal surrounding world in its articulation; the person as belonging to itself; for us, for everyone, the world is given only as surrounding world; from there the transcendental turn. The text belongs to the first revision of the Meditations and tests an alternative entry into the reduction: through personal psychology and its surrounding world, instead of through the Cartesian doubt.

(54) For every waking person the surrounding world (Umwelt) is articulated according to the difference between the cogitations of specific wakefulness, which have the form "I am affected, I experience, I think, I do," and the non-waking background experiences. Accordingly it divides into what currently "concerns" the person, comes into consideration, occupies, disturbs or stimulates her, and what is dead background. Husserl begins with the person, not the pure ego, and with the world as it matters, not as it is perceived. The division follows the structure of attention from Ideas I § 35, now read practically as the distinction between what concerns me and what does not.

(54–55) All waking cogitations are unified by centring in the personal ego, and not only formally. From the unity of its habitualities the ego has at any time the unity of a current interest, and since it has many abiding interests the waking sphere is further articulated: what has unity through a current interest, such as the activities of the vocational interest now alive; what from the background awakens another habitual interest, as father or citizen; and finally sheer disturbance unrelated to any interest, an explosion before apperception makes it "significant." Husserl's note defines the significant (das Bedeutsame) as what interests in fixed habituality and is apperceived as interesting. The ego is centred not only as a formal pole but as a person with a plurality of standing interests, each organizing its own sector of the world. Vocation (Beruf) is the paradigm; the Krisis will define the natural attitude itself as a "vocational" attitude toward the world (Hua VI § 35).

(55) Characters of significance are the intentional characters that correspond to these differences. The articulation concerns the mere things of the surrounding world as well as animals and everything already bearing habitual significance. Significance is a noematic character, an intentional property of the object as apprehended, not a projection added to a neutral thing. The claim extends to "mere things": even nature as encountered by persons is always already significant.

(55) The surrounding world always already has its abiding typical shape, apperceptively grasped, though its articulation is not always of the same genetic originality. Situations recur in similarity, and to the habituality of interests corresponds the surrounding world, passively apperceived as articulated in significances. To the habitual person, the person as she "is" and not only as centring pole, corresponds the actually experienced surrounding world as correlate. "Apperception in general" is a habituality first actualized in an experiencing understanding at a glance, an experience with an empty yet specific horizon, concerning not only the "familiar" that makes the thing a thing of the type horse, but also what it comes into consideration for, for me and for others co-intended emptily. Husserl's notes specify the "properties of significance" as habitual apperceptive characters indicating how an object figures in familiar interest-situations, and stress that the person is an interested ego, so that her correlate is the interesting, significant world. The correlation person/surrounding world is the personalist version of the noetic-noematic correlation of Ideas I and of the personalistic attitude of Ideas II § 49. The decisive addition is that significance carries a co-intended reference to others: the use-character of a thing is apperceived as useful also for others.

(55) For developed human beings everything perhaps already has its significances. Even "completely uninteresting" backgrounds are only relatively so, outside the dominant interest and belonging to no positive "life-interest" instituted in the universality of life. Yet anything can occasionally become interesting, usable, valuable, and be considered with regard to whether it might come into play; it then enters the contrast of useful and useless, the latter a negative character of significance that can also become apperceptive. For the "developed" human being everything in the surrounding world plays some role, even a negative one. Insignificance is itself a mode of significance. The world of the developed person admits no neutral residue, which rules out a model in which value is laid on top of a value-free nature (the "objectivist" model the Krisis attacks).

(56) The concrete surrounding world is thus thoroughly "significant," "interesting," articulated and typified according to personal interests, the correlate of the person, who exists as the identical bearer of her habitualities. Besides articulation there is stratification: what already has significance for some interests comes into consideration for others and gains significances of a new dimension. Significances do not lie side by side, united by one interest, but belong to different dimensions. A tool can be an object of use, of trade, of aesthetic regard and of inheritance at once, each a dimension founded on the previous ones. Stratification corresponds to the founding of evaluative and practical acts on doxic ones in Ideas II, Part I, Chapter 1.

(56–57) So far we spoke as if a person's surrounding world had its significance structure from her alone. But persons have a peculiar double position: they are objects of the pre-given surrounding world, bearing significances, and they are persons who communalize and institute significances in community. Predicates of significance of objects in the surrounding world common to a communicating humanity refer in their very sense to the known or unknown subjects from whose acts they stem. The single subject has private acts as opposed to communicative acts in which she functions communicatively. An association of persons also has acts, by analogy with a single person: the acts of its members interwoven in unified communicative function, with the unified result of their common achievement, which exists in the surrounding world and bears a significance referring to those persons and their acts. The double position of persons, object in the world and subject for it, reappears here at the level of significance. Cultural predicates contain a reference to their producers, often anonymous; this is the noematic trace of intersubjectivity that the Origin of Geometry will call the "sedimented" sense of a tradition. The analogy between a single person and an association of persons is the doctrine of "personalities of higher order" from No. 1 § 11.

(57) Communalization of persons into an abiding community may arise at a first level from natural living-together and originally instinctive sources, as a habituality of disposition and typical achievement (family, people), or at a higher level from voluntary institution, itself the communalized will of the founders (club, foundation of a state). A natural or instituted community may be restricted to determinate individuals or be open by its sense, a community with change of members, though not related to arbitrary persons but to those prefigured by the horizon of openness instituted with it. The two distinctions, natural versus instituted, closed versus open, give a typology of social formations. The mention of "instinctive sources" anticipates the late theory of instinctive intersubjectivity (No. 34; Beilage XLIII), and the "prefigured openness" of membership is the social analogue of the open horizon of others in No. 3.

(57–58) Constitution of the common world through "altruistic" experience (Husserl's term here for experience of the other) means that every person entering my field bodily, whom I immediately apprehend as other, can without further ado be understood as having all the private apperceptions and object-types I have already formed. First I understand, in continuous concordant confirmation, that he has that body as lived body in the same significance-structure in which I experience mine, and that the common spatial world can contain private "cultural objects" related to him, like those I know originally from my own achievement. The new formations arising from my and others' communalization then allow similar objects to be apprehended as significant spiritual formations of persons outside this circle, not formed by me and my companions. One can also watch a communalization coming into being, through agreement, conversation, common work, since these have their exteriority, understood apperceptively first externally and then in genuine intuitive understanding of their inner sense. Understanding the other's world proceeds by transfer of my own types, then by extension to strangers' cultural formations, a stepwise movement from homeworld to alien world that Part III systematizes (No. 27). The distinction between external apperceptive understanding and genuine intuitive understanding foreshadows the limits of understanding in Beilage XXVII and Beilage IL.

(58) An association of persons also resembles a single person in that it can join other associations or persons, and then has, relative to the higher association of which it is a member, relatively private acts and also communicative acts reaching beyond. The private/communicative distinction is iterable: what is communal at one level is private at the next. The resulting nesting of communities culminates, as the next paragraph says, in humanities.

(58) All this concerns the persons who function as ultimate elements of personal communities and finally of humanities. Where they are abiding unities, they have abiding personal characters, and so far as they are experienceable to themselves and others, they have, as objects in the surrounding world (to which the subjects always belong reflexively), their personal significance-characters, whose types language names: official, judge, postal official, citizen, father. Social roles are significance-characters of persons as objects, typified and named. The reflexive point is essential: the subjects belong to the surrounding world they constitute, which is the condition for persons being given to each other in roles.

(58–59) The articulation of the surrounding world concerned the world in its typical apperceptive structure for persons who live into it and give it ever new individually typical characters. "The" world is essentially given to them only as surrounding world, yet in experience they themselves distinguish world and surrounding world. "Surrounding world" was the universal title for objects and their determinations, known by experience or experienced within a horizon of unknownness. Purely ontically oriented, we did not speak of the changing subjective modes of givenness of these objects, their orientations, for single persons or for normal subjects of a normal community of understanding having the same objects in correlated appearances. But this ontic universe is itself a universe in subjective form, already in having a perceptual field as central sphere, a subjective present field and central sphere of awakeable pasts and a future horizon, and in each phase a central sphere for an endless horizon of unknownness. The continuity of present surrounding worlds is complex. In the living present streaming into the future, our surrounding world, while keeping its general structure, changes in content, though we tacitly presuppose concordance: new objects become known from the empty horizon, and we ourselves create new shapes in what is already there. The paragraph introduces the double sense of "subjective": the surrounding world is subjective as relative to persons, but the ontic analysis bracketed a deeper subjectivity, the modes of appearance. And even the ontic surrounding world has subjective form, since it is centred on a perceptual present. The world changes not only by discovery but by our own shaping, the practical dimension the Krisis gives the life-world.

(59–60) More than that: as new subjects come into community with us, in loose or close forms, ever new content arises, ever new communal culture enriching the surrounding world as "our" world. In this constant change we always have an intersubjective world as relative surrounding world, ever renewed though in a preserved essential structure, and in this change "the" world constitutes itself continuously as the identical one, while the practically valid surrounding worlds are subjective modes of appearance of the world. Ontically oriented, we experience "the" world, of which only this or that was known, with its unknown horizon, differently for each. As theorists we contrast the world itself as truly existing with modes of appearance, now including the changing individual and communal surrounding worlds. Unlike orientations and perspectives, these surrounding worlds, with their objects and "spiritual" predicates, count as objectively actual, as belonging to the world itself. The subjective here lies in their open horizons and in the possibility, abstracted from so far, that being may prove illusion, that true being and merely valid being must be distinguished. For the theorist, experience then needs critique and the experienced world confirmation. That is far from enough, since new problems of subjective givenness arise, and of overcoming this manifold relativism of being-for-us toward a final determination of true being as the "irrelative," among them the problems of normality and anomaly. Husserl distinguishes two kinds of appearance. Perspectives are known to be subjective; surrounding worlds are taken as the objective world itself, and their subjectivity shows only in their open horizons and their corrigibility. The problem of the "irrelative" (das Irrelative), true being extracted from all relativities, is the problem the Krisis will find unsolvable for objective science and solvable only transcendentally.

(60–61) The world is experienced only as surrounding world and only so is it for us, in all the structures of sense in which it means what it means to us, the persons who themselves belong to the world and are, like everything worldly, beings for us, given in turn to us. And again "us": we who speak of the world, more precisely I now and all others facing me or known to me, and in the horizon of unknownness the open endless plurality of persons I must presuppose or might experience. The "us" to whom the world is given is itself given to us; the reflexivity is unavoidable. Husserl unpacks the "us" from me, now, outward to the open plurality, the structure that motivates the transcendental turn in the next paragraph.

(61–62) Thorough descriptions are needed of the modes of givenness of my surrounding world and therein of my others, who are presupposed if the world is to be experienceable for me as the fully objective world it is valid as, having sense from others. I must already have something experienceable in order to experience others as others; and if the objective world exists only by means of actual and possible others, what is first experienceable, what constitutes the experiential being of others for me, can be subjective only in a way that does not yet have the sense of objective being. If I say that for everyone the world is given from him, or for us in common from us, in surrounding-world modes, these others already presuppose "us," and this "us" presupposes me, now reflecting. The "we" extends from me to present, past and future others, to my ancestors and theirs in the endless chain of generations with their horizons. All these are human beings in the world, along with animals, which are not machines but personal beings with differently varied surrounding worlds. All these persons and communities are in the world, the always pre-given world, in which their subjective life and surrounding worlds have their place. But again: pre-given to whom, self-evident for whom? To us. Are there no riddles here? Are not human persons, like all objects, given environmentally in subjective modes, and as truly existing in the world only through transcendence, as "appearing," referring to possibilities of confirmation and overcoming of relative modes of givenness? Here the transition to transcendental subjectivity is motivated. Husserl reaches the reduction by a regress the published Meditations did not use: from the person in her surrounding world, through the "we" that presupposes me, to the recognition that persons are themselves appearances in the world. The motivating paradox is that the world containing the subjects is pre-given to those same subjects. This is the path the Krisis follows from §§ 53–54, and the inclusion of animals as "personal beings" is unusual and notable (cf. Beilage X; No. 35).

(62–63) We took the path: how is the world subjectively given, to persons? Persons were our theme, which led to the world for persons and its many subjective modes. Persons were in this world, which in positivity we already had as self-evidently existing, concrete with the content experience gives. Secretly we knew that experience alone does not suffice and that science on its basis determines objectively true being; we presupposed, without explication, that subjective modes of givenness under the title experience give us the sense "world," a first sense to be reshaped by science. We knew much more: the experiential world could be questioned, leading to spatio-temporal causal nature, animals and humans, culture, and the subjective modes in which everything is environmentally given to these persons. The specifically personal was the psychic, and psychology the universal science of persons, ontologically their eidetic science. Under the title of a universal science of persons we had personal life, habitual being, surrounding world and its formation, and the genesis of personal habitualities. Pure intentional psychology led beyond practical personality to far deeper noetic structures and so to the concrete psyche as it exists for itself. We thus had the complete essential prefiguration of possible personality and personal intersubjectivity, and therein the structure of the concrete "world-presentation," which in positivity is set over against the world itself. That world was presupposed as nothing other than the unity, constituted in pure subjects and their community, of synthetically connected levels of "world-presentations," with the horizon of presumed true being always to be identified through new surrounding worlds, describable in form as the immanently constituted presentation of the world's true being. Finally the awareness awakens that this presupposed world-personality, taken in purity, can be posited absolutely, and that in it is the site of apperception and of the formation of the apperception "world" and "human being in the world." The retrospect turns the psychological path into a path to the reduction. Pure intersubjective psychology already contains the whole structure of constitution, but positivity still sets the world "itself" over against the world-presentation. The transcendental turn consists in seeing that the world itself is nothing but the ideal unity of world-presentations, and that the subjectivity in which they form can be posited absolutely. This is the "parallelism" of phenomenological psychology and transcendental phenomenology (Hua IX, the Encyclopaedia Britannica article and the Amsterdam lectures), with the transcendental step reduced to a change in how the same content is taken.

(63) All beginning is naivety; in the naivety of intersubjective world-experience we attempt an ontology of the world as world of experience. We can begin with nature and pass to the psyche, starting concretely with the experienced world and dismantling it, then abstracting from nature to proceed purely personally. But then one must not, as in the Amsterdam lectures, pass straight to the purely psychic as ego cogito understood as the ultimate noetic, suggesting that this is already sufficiently concrete. The next cogito means: I live into a "surrounding world," with acts environmentally related, but under phenomenological-psychological reduction. Why must the psychologist bracket the world? Husserl criticizes his own Amsterdam lectures (Hua IX, 302–349) for leaping from the psyche to the bare noetic cogito. The correction is that the concrete cogito is already world-related as environmental: the first datum of reduced psychology is the person-in-her-surrounding-world, not the stream of experience. The life-world approach of the Krisis grows from this self-criticism.

(64) Perhaps because it belongs to the sense of the apperception "human being" that this real being exists in itself even if what it is conscious of does not exist or is quite different. The world in which this human being is a single spatial real is presupposed, yet in the sense of the apperception one finds that he "represents" the world. "Representations" may deceive, but they are what they are; so with the world-representation. It is a subjective mode of consciousness of "world," and world in it means what the ego experiencing intends. It is posited as a psychic fact, and the prejudgment of the world's existence is bracketed. Husserl's note prefers instead to start from the regress from the experienced real to its modes of appearance, to which the possibility of deception can then be attached. The psychological epoché is justified by the sense of the psyche itself: a psyche's representations are what they are whether or not the represented exists. The note's preference is significant. It replaces the argument from possible deception, the Cartesian motive, with the regress from object to appearance, the "way through ontology."

(64) But how is a pure psychology to begin and proceed systematically? It is the same question that arises in the transcendental attitude, where the ego must first be taken concretely as "representing the world," and the explication of this world-representation then leads to surrounding worlds and subjective modes of everything worldly meant. How do I actually proceed? Modes of intuition, of temporalization. Why not begin with the environmental modes of givenness, and then: what is environmental is given in subjective temporal modes? The method question is identical for psychology and transcendental philosophy. The suggestion to begin with environmental givenness and descend to temporal modes inverts the order of Ideas I, which began with the perceived thing and time-consciousness and only later reached the personal world.

(64) How does this relate to the presentation in the Ideas and in the drafted Meditations? The single question measures the new path against the existing ones, and the heading that follows names the specific point of contrast.

#### On the Beginning in the Second Meditation, but Especially on the Concept of Intersubjective Unity of Life, Intersubjective "Immanent" Time, and Intersubjective Spatial Orientation

(64) It is important to emphasize the following. The single sentence announces the two procedures the next paragraphs distinguish; its own content is the demand that the double possibility of beginning be made explicit, which the Meditations had suppressed.

(64–65) If I explicate the world aesthetic-ontologically as world of experience, that means: from my and our experience, as world for everyone, who must experience it while somehow understanding everyone: "What I experience and what everyone else experiences as world is the same, with the same structure." The ontology of the life-world ("aesthetic" in Kant's sense of the Transcendental Aesthetic, the world as intuited) is intersubjective from the start, since its object is the world for everyone. The phrase "world-aesthetic" anticipates Formal and Transcendental Logic's "transcendental aesthetic" (Hua XVII, Conclusion) and the Krisis's life-world ontology.

(65) If the further question is how a world, with all its ontological structures, is essentially given in subjective modes, then purely psychologically it is: I experience the world in manifold experiences together with others whom I experience as in it. What is the world experienced purely in this immanent life of intersubjectivity, and in which subjective modes and syntheses is it given as consistently confirming itself for me? Here I immediately come, in my own case, to my subjective temporality as form of my phenomena. Husserl formulates the transition from ontology to the theory of givenness and notes that it leads at once to time. The phrase "immanent life of intersubjectivity" sets the agenda of the following paragraphs: intersubjectivity also has an immanent life and an immanent time.

(65) But I also come to the "we" and our experience, our experiential world, our phenomena of this same world, our syntheses in community, our common surrounding worlds as commonly experienced and identical, their change and their synthetic unities in community, each a phenomenon of our identical ontological world. On the other side there is the distinction of each one's egological perceptual world and its synthesis from "appearances," every common surrounding world, in its relativity as mere intersubjective world-appearance, being a synthetic unity of egological phenomena. Two levels of appearance are ordered: egological perceptual worlds, and communal surrounding worlds synthesized from them, the latter themselves appearances of the one ontological world.

(65) First, my reduced world, the world purely as world of my experience, can mean the world proving itself to me ontically from experience alone and its phenomena, with the others' experience co-valid, so that I say: my world of pure experience is our world of pure experience. This first sense of reduction keeps empathy in force. The "reduced" world is not primordinal; it is the life-world taken as experienced, with others co-functioning.

(65) Second, I can at once ask: the world in its ontological structure is first of all world from our common experience. We take it, reducing, purely as the unity meant in our experiences in their manifold subjective modes of appearance, self-given and to be given synthetically through concordant confirmation. The second sense starts not from "my" world with others co-valid but from "our" world directly. The difference is one of starting point: ego or we.

(65) Here note: we reduce to the "we" in its community of life as world-life and the world-constituting phenomena in it, and then have an immanent common time of this "we": we-present, we-past, we-future. The reduction to intersubjectivity yields a common immanent time. Husserl transposes the structure of inner time-consciousness to the "we," a bold step that the texts on intermonadic time (Nos. 19–20) and on monadic temporalization (No. 38) develop.

(66) From the start intersubjectivity as purely spiritually connected unity, embracing all pure subjects, is the experiential ground for all descriptions, though only naively, without being thematized. It is taken as the universe of everything subjective, including all relative surrounding worlds and the ontological world experienced in the universal synthesis of subjective world-phenomena, presumed in experience as idea of identical truth in itself and yet confirmed, which may figure as "world-representation." The naivety of the reduction to the "we" is that intersubjectivity is used as ground without its own constitution being clarified. That is the price of beginning with the "we," and the reason the egological path keeps its priority.

(66–67) It is important that intersubjectivity, taken purely psychically, is essentially constituted with an intersubjective immanent time-form, the universal form for everything intersubjectively subjective. We have, first, for each ego its egologically reduced immanent time, its "private" time, in which the immanent time and content of every other ego presents itself. Second, the intersubjective synthesis presents itself in each ego, and each can find and describe intersubjectivity in its purity; each finds its immanent present in coincidence with every other's present and contained in the present as intersubjective. These remarkable relations concern immanent temporal contents too. Each ego has its primordial world-phenomenon, its primordially reduced existing world, constituted as unity of primordial presentations in the form of a fixed orientational spatiality, an egological-objective spatiality. In the synthesis of community each such primordial world becomes a phenomenon, a mode of givenness, of the same intersubjective world. Each has its primordial present and egological time; yet all these presents belong to one intersubjectively constituted present, they are intersubjectively simultaneous, now. A common homeworld, a common surrounding world, is also constituted. As to space, each egological place becomes intersubjectively identical. Each is oriented differently for every other ego, since each lived body is another (there is no common here, unlike the common now). Yet intersubjectivity has the existing world oriented intersubjectively. Every community in each communal present has its communal place, a locality but not a point: the dwelling place of the family or the city, the territory of a people or state, the earth for humanity. Thus the intersubjective world appears spatio-temporally oriented in intersubjective surrounding worlds. Objective time-form has its correlate in the intersubjectively constituted modes of temporalization, objective space in intersubjective modes of spatial orientation. Objective space-time and the objective world itself are an idea. What "actually" constitutes itself is always a relative objectivity, as already with primordial time or the immanent time of sensation-data: ever new levels of relative constitution, ever new horizons, finally the highest idea. The paragraph contains three discoveries. Time and space are asymmetrical in intersubjectivity: there is a common now but no common here, since presents coincide while bodies cannot. Communities nevertheless have a common "here," not a point but a dwelling place, territory, the earth, which is the origin of the doctrine that the earth is the ground of intersubjective orientation ("Grundlegende Untersuchungen zum phänomenologischen Ursprung der Räumlichkeit der Natur," 1934, the Earth-ark text). And objectivity is graded: every level, down to immanent time, is a relative objectivity, and the objective world is the ideal limit, an idea in the Kantian sense. The word "homeworld" (Heimwelt) appears here for the first time in the volume.

(67) After these reflections we shall say the following. The sentence draws the methodological consequence from the analysis of intersubjective time and space, which the next paragraphs state as two procedures.

(67) The universal description of the world purely as world of experience, together with the description of world-experience itself in its subjective modes, can proceed in two ways. The double procedure is the pivot of the text: one way begins with the "we," the other with the ego.

(67) First: how does the world we experience together, always the same experienced world in our world-life, appear to us? How is it described in the ontological essential structures in which it is necessarily the commonly existing world, and how does it appear modally? It appears "environmentally": each has his surrounding world, in community we have common surrounding worlds, each special community its own; yet in this change consciousness relates to "the" world as unity. What typology, what essential structures, what systematic construction obtain here? We who belong to the world are included in changing subjective modes. In which modes of appearance do I appear to myself, and how do I distinguish what appears to me "primordially" from what presupposes the others' appearing for me? What are the experiences to which I owe the being-for-me of others and so of the "we," which in its concreteness is the whole life of experience and world-life in which the existing world is constantly pre-given as our practical field? The first way starts with the life-world and the "we," and arrives at the primordial and at empathy only at the end, as a question internal to the description of world-appearance. It is the Krisis order.

(68) Second: we again presuppose the ontological explication of our experiential world, but instead of entering into the ways it is commonly given to us in common modes of relativity, we ask back at once: the world of our experience is first of all the world of my experience, the co-experiencing others are already given to me as worldly, in subjective modes. I reduce to the ego and the cogitations from which the world is for me, and ask how it is so. I reduce at once to the primordial ego, then ask how it becomes social ego, and how the community confirmed for it, in which each person is my like and for which everything valid for me is valid, comes to experience a common world, with what typology of intersubjective modes and higher syntheses. The second way is the Cartesian Meditations order: primordial reduction first, empathy second, community third. Husserl presents both as legitimate, not as alternatives one of which is false; the difference is whether the primordial is reached at the beginning or at the end.

#### Double Sense of Immanence and Immanent Time

(68) For the ego, first, we speak of experiences, acts, sensation-data, apprehensions, affections, constituting themselves immanently in time in "the most original time-consciousness." How did I come to this? Guided by sensualist psychology: I sought to reduce to what is the ego's own, to the life that is transcendentally mine and unaffected by whether the meant transcendent exists or is illusion. Husserl names the historical source of the first concept of immanence: the sensualist model of a stream of data, inherited from Brentano and the British tradition, which Ideas I adapted. The admission is self-critical.

(68–69) Epoché concerning the objective world; but the objective world is given in subjectively relative transcendencies, which in this starting point are not seen and not "transcendentally reduced" to the subjectivity that constitutes them. The defect of the sensualist starting point is that it leaps over the relative transcendencies, surrounding worlds and primordial nature, between the stream and the objective world. They are neither immanent in the sensualist sense nor objective; the first concept of immanence leaves them unreduced.

(69) This must be taken into account. Then I do arrive ultimately at a "stream of consciousness" and its immanent time-form, apart from the ego-pole and habituality. The stream remains the ultimate level, but it is reached through the intermediate transcendencies, not by direct leap.

(69) This concerns intersubjectivity too: we have intersubjective pure life as an immanent sphere. The first concept of immanence applied to the "we" gives an intersubjective stream.

(69) Second, if we take everything subjective together, first all relative transcendencies, we gain an immanent sphere in a new sense and an immanent time-form for it, for example the time-form of subjective surrounding worlds in intersubjectivity, as already in the egological sphere we find subjective transcendencies. The second concept of immanence includes constituted but subject-relative unities, the "immanent transcendencies" of No. 1 § 5 generalized. Immanence now means relativity to subjectivity, not reell inherence.

(69) This distinction is important for the theory of the reduction. Starting from intersubjectivity one can establish an intersubjective reduction by bracketing the world in itself and reducing to the universe of the intersubjective, which includes all single subjectivity. Then a second step, reduction to the ultimately constituting intersubjective life, the intersubjective "stream of experience," the communalization of all egological streams. The intersubjective reduction is a two-step procedure that follows the two concepts of immanence: first to the relative transcendencies (the intersubjective life-world as phenomenon), then to the constituting life. Husserl here formulates the "reduction to intersubjectivity" that Hua VIII and the Krisis propose as a non-Cartesian way.

(69) If one sets out directly toward egological phenomenology: first, reduction to the subjective, but then note, second, that subjective objectivities are still subjective and require a new reduction. The same two steps apply on the egological path. The result of No. 4 is that every reduction, egological or intersubjective, must pass through the relative transcendencies before reaching constituting life; the Cartesian way of the Meditations had compressed the two steps into one.

### No. 5. On the Problem of Intersubjectivity in the *Cartesian Meditations* (Probably 1930)

#### a) The Path from the Phenomenological Reduction. On the First Meditation, Possibly as a Final Retrospect

(70–71) The transcendental epoché, it is said (Hua I § 8), makes possible the "transcendental reduction." To what is one reduced? Here there is an ambiguity. The epoché makes accessible the universe of transcendental, absolute being, a new world of temporal individuals, a realm of transcendental temporality, first in the form of a transcendental experience that provides the ground of validity for theoretical and practical mediations. Since the ego of the epoché is the one for whom this experience exists, who performs it theoretically and also passively lives through it, and who on that basis practises description, we must distinguish under the title "transcendental": the subject-pole of transcendentally experiencing and other acts, with the whole concrete life of which it is pole; and the realm of the transcendental, including that of the alter egos, which comes to experience and knowledge within this concreteness, within the concrete ego. Husserl's footnote fixes the status of the epoché: it is not a single act but a habituality, instituted by an act in the mode of "in general," a standing decision to remain in epoché and perform only transcendental acts, so that the unity of "transcendental life in the epoché" is instituted as a universal horizon of possibility beyond the actual. The ambiguity of "reduction to" is the theme of the whole text: the reduction leads both to the ego as pole and to a field that contains more than the ego. The footnote is important against the reading of the epoché as a momentary suspension that one must constantly renew; it is a habitual stance with the structure of a resolve.

(71) The transcendental ego includes possible reflective experience, by which it experiences and knows itself, its transcendental experiences and acts, and its primordially reduced world. Taking this reflective experience in its iterability, through which the ego is experienced as identically the same while continually reflecting and taking new content from itself, the realm of transcendental objects divides: first, the ego itself as the transcendental in which all experience and thought lie and through which everything transcendentally existing for it is intentionally contained; its own actual and possible life is that in which it and everything transcendental becomes conscious and known. Second, the transcendental that may come to experience and validity in the ego but is not the ego itself, what is "transcendent" to it. Whether such a transcendence can really be experienced transcendentally in the ego, and then necessarily as another transcendental ego and an open totality of such egos as "monads," is not decided in advance. The division is formal and neutral: the second class might be empty. Husserl thus restates the solipsism question as an open alternative within transcendental experience, not as a threat to it.

(71) We are dealing only with a difference formally prefigured as thinkable. What is evident in advance is only that if, after the epoché, the ego of the epoché remains as ego of an experiencing transcendental life, we necessarily arrive at this formal distinction. The distinction between self and possible transcendent within the transcendental field is an analytic consequence of the ego's being an experiencing ego, since experience as such has an object that may or may not be the ego itself.

(71) At the start I know nothing of a "concrete ego" or of my monad, as against others, as a connected endlessly open field of experience, a field of access to a "world" of absolute being. It seems at first that I can be sure of extending abstention from all pre-givens, prejudices and pre-validities over the world of experience valid for me, which includes me, this human being among others. The concreteness of the ego is a result, not a datum, of the reduction. The second sentence states the first, naive confidence in the universal epoché, which the next paragraph troubles.

(71–72) But the epoché puts me in difficulty. How can I say "I abstain from every validity, from belief in the being of the world and of myself, this human being," when "I abstain" contains the ego as abstaining? "I" means "I, this human being." I run into the apodicticity of "I am," and must ask whether it conflicts with the universality of an epoché that includes me, or how, performing it universally, I can see that I can hold myself as human being out of validity, as mere "phenomenon," while positing myself, the same ego, apodictically as performing it. If this is consistent, then after the epoché I would not be the ego of natural "self-experience," constantly pre-given in it, nor a component of its content, and yet I would already be present in it and in everything worldly as the ego for whom pre-givenness exists. Husserl articulates the paradox of the "I" of the epoché: the same word names the bracketed human being and the unbracketable performer. The solution is announced in the counterfactual: the transcendental ego is not a part of the world's content but the one for whom the world is pre-given, present in all worldly experience without being an item in it. This is the paradox of human subjectivity of Krisis §§ 53–54 in its first form.

(72–73) These are unclear words at the start. But I can say this much: the world-epoché as a general abstention from the "thesis" of the world's being is at the start an evident possibility in the following sense. I reflect that in waking life I continuously experience the spatial world, have it intuitively in perception, and in this experience have constantly, habitually, a universal spatio-temporal world-horizon, a universe "spatio-temporal world" pre-given as existing for me. This gives the universal thesis "world, always existing for me." This general thesis, a universal continuous certainty of being that I have had and will have throughout my life, I subject to the epoché. I can do so readily in the form that arises in a very indeterminate overview of the world's pre-given being. But have I thereby shown the possibility of an unconditionally universal epoché and ego-reduction, its real imaginability as constantly carried through my whole life? I claim moreover to be able to experience and judge and begin with "I abstain," "I am." I pass from life in natural pre-givenness to life in universal epoché. Is that possible? The epoché is certainly possible as regards the general, unexplicated thesis; but is it possible if I carry it out explicitly as total, extended to every worldly particular and consistently maintained? That is too quick and hardly convincing. Husserl questions the Cartesian way of the Meditations from within. The epoché of the general thesis (Ideas I § 32) is easy because the thesis is indeterminate; the question is whether a total epoché, covering every particular validity, can actually be lived, since all particular validities are interwoven and I continue to experience and judge. The self-criticism parallels Hua VIII's argument that the Cartesian way leaps to an empty ego, and it motivates the non-Cartesian paths of No. 4.

(73) First, the general thesis, presupposing the explicit, logically universal constitution of the world as "universe"; second, if this possibility is secured in an act of totality-construction, so is the epoché as universal; third, then the question what remains experienceable and thinkable. The schematic answer locates the condition of the universal epoché: the world must first be constituted explicitly as a totality, a "universe," by an act of construction; the epoché then applies to that constructed totality. The universality of the epoché depends on a prior logical idealization of the world, which is why Husserl elsewhere calls it a "construction" (Konstruktion) that must be shown to be fulfillable.

#### b) Especially on the Fifth Meditation. The Path from the Phenomenological Reduction

(73) After the epoché the gaze turns first to the "transcendental phenomenon world" or the transcendentally concrete experience of world-consciousness, whose ego is now the transcendental ego standing in the reduction and practising phenomenology.
