First comes phenomenological experience with theoretical intent, and on its basis description with its concept- and judgment-forming acts. The order matters: the first theme is the world-phenomenon, not the ego; the ego is reached as the subject of world-consciousness. And the ego is specified as the phenomenologizing ego, a theme Beilage III develops.

(73) "Reduction to transcendental subjectivity" will prove ambiguous. The subjectivity posited in the epoché is to be understood as "my monadically own" subjectivity, that of the phenomenologizing ego, and as the transcendental intersubjectivity disclosed in it. The ambiguity announced in a) is now resolved: the reduction leads to two subjectivities, one contained in the other.

(73–74) If subjectivity means the primordially concrete ego, the ego-pole as pole of its actual and possible acts and one with them, then the parallel concept is concrete intersubjectivity as the totality of primordially concrete egos, whose life is distributed among single concrete egos and also "connected," since each ego's intentionality carries intentional mediations that not only give it unity of monadically immanent presence and monadic time but also constitute a transcending unity of intersubjective presence and intermonadic, all-monadic temporality. The fundamental difference between primordiality and intersubjectivity lies, within what I can posit in the epoché, between the first universal temporalization, which constitutes "my monad" as concrete whole, and the founded temporalization by which compresence is constituted, the con-presence of other monads and the con-temporality of their monadic times with my whole time; and further the mutual con-temporality of the monads existing for me, for which mine in turn is con-temporal. We thus have, "transcendentally-objectively," a monadic world in the unitary form of one time "in" which all monads are, each with its own time fitted into the all-time. Each monad has its streaming presence, in which the whole intentionality of its past and future is implicitly contained, continuously unified as unity of a primal association and its mediations, with discrete unifications (association at a distance) constituted on its basis. Within its filled primordial time each monad also has experiences of empathy, through which the constitution of con-temporalities and of other monads passes. The paragraph translates the primordial/intersubjective distinction into temporal terms: primordiality is the first temporalization, intersubjectivity a founded temporalization producing con-temporality (Kon-temporalität). Two theses stand out. The monadic world has one time "in" which all monads are, a transcendental all-time; and the means of constituting it, empathy, are themselves events in each monad's primordial time. "Primal association" (Urassoziation) names the passive unity of the living present, the term of the C-manuscripts (Hua Mat VIII). The temporal formulation of intersubjectivity is the line that leads to No. 38, "Temporalization: Monad."

#### c) On the Fifth Meditation

(74–75) I must distinguish the subjectivity now phenomenologizing transcendentally, as actual ego or monad, from transcendental subjectivity simply, which proves to be transcendental intersubjectivity, containing the phenomenologizing one. Transcendental subjectivity "simply" is intersubjectivity; the phenomenologizing ego is one monad within it. The phrasing reverses the order of givenness: the ego that discovers intersubjectivity knows itself as included in what it discovers.

(75) Moreover, the subjectivity now phenomenologizing knows itself in its past, when it was not phenomenologizing but still transcendental subjectivity, and in its transcendental experience of others it knows other egos as not phenomenologizing (though occasionally as such), but as transcendental egos. Being transcendental does not depend on knowing it. Every ego, before or without the reduction, is transcendental subjectivity in anonymity, a point that the next paragraphs develop into the "double sense" of the world.

(75) The transcendental ego has universal transcendental experience as its experiencing life. This is on the one hand transcendental self-experience, in which I as ego living in the reduction experience myself, my intentional life and its intentional objects as such, including, as ideal unity over against immanent time, the world purely as world of my primordial experience. On the other hand it is transcendental experience of others and through it transcendental experience of the world as objective. Transcendental experience has two branches, self-experience and other-experience, and the objective world is reached only through the second. The primordial world belongs to self-experience as an ideal unity, as No. 2 a had defined it.

(75) What does it mean to speak of transcendental experience of the world? It means making thematic, in the epoché, the subjectivity performing universal world-experience, world-knowledge and world-life, in which everything worldly has its sense of being, and co-thematizing the world meant and confirmed in this life. But that is nothing other than the world of life, meant and valid there now correctly, now falsely, now mythologically, now scientifically, only taken as such, seen by the transcendental spectator as constitutive unity of transcendental monadic subjectivity. We have no other, nor can anyone; another would make no sense. The epoché opens transcendental experience in its open infinity, the transcendental universe of the absolute, the universe of monads with everything constituted in it. The transcendental world is not a second world behind the life-world. It is the same world, including its errors and myths, seen as constituted. "The world of life" (die Welt des Lebens) is almost the Krisis term; the claim that "another would make no sense" is the anti-Kantian thesis that there is no thing in itself beyond the constituted world.

(75–76) All this is transcendentally experienced or experienceable with the appropriate direction of experience, and so the world as transcendentally constituted. It is the world of the natural attitude for the naturally oriented ego and "we"; but the natural attitude and naturally oriented subjectivity with its world are contained in universal transcendental experience and its horizon, within which the transcendental gaze can turn to the natural world. So it is the same world we always spoke of that becomes transcendental theme and intelligible in its full, absolute sense of being as achievement of transcendental intersubjectivity, inseparable from it as an achievement performed and remaining in it: an idea forming itself in it and confirmed continuously in levels of relativity, correlate of a presumptive yet valid, in its way evident, infinity of prefigurations in ever new relative confirmations. The natural attitude is not abolished but contained in the transcendental one as a theme. The world's "absolute sense of being" is its being an idea, a correlate of infinite presumptive confirmation; absolute here means fully clarified, not non-relative, since what is clarified is precisely its relativity.

(76) This yields a double sense. First, the world in the natural attitude as the world in which I and everyone live, perhaps in the anonymity of the transcendental; perhaps no one in all humanity has ever performed the epoché. Second, humanity has awakened transcendentally, has won transcendental self-knowledge and so made thematic its own being in naturalness and the world in this naturalness. The double sense is historical. Humanity can exist transcendentally without knowing it; the epoché is the event of its awakening. This historicizes the reduction and anticipates the Krisis thesis that phenomenology is humanity's self-awakening to its transcendental being, and No. 23's "veiled manifestation" of transcendental intersubjectivity in human history.

#### d) Reflection on the Fifth Meditation

(76–77) The "concrete" ego, the monad constituted through primordiality, and concrete intersubjectivity, concrete in the second sense, as a "connected" manifold of concrete egos: connected because in my ego-consciousness other egos with their life are conscious, co-valid and confirmed as related in their life to me and mine, thus "connected" with me in turn. In this intentional interpenetration what others are conscious of becomes accessible to me; my consciousness is intentionally related to the other's and through it to what it is conscious of, and conversely, this reversal, the other's being conscious of me, being itself conscious to me, so that my consciousness returns to itself in a circle through the other consciousness disclosed in it, and so perhaps everyone's, as I and anyone can recognize. "Concrete" has two senses: the monad, concrete through its primordiality, and intersubjectivity, concrete in a higher sense as the connected whole. The connection is a reflexive circle: I am conscious of the other's consciousness of me, so that my self-consciousness passes through him. Husserl's spaced emphasis (my consciousness returns to itself through the other) states the structure later developed in Nos. 24 and 28: self-consciousness in its full form is mediated by the other. The thesis invites comparison with Hegel's recognition, which Husserl does not cite, and differs from it in being a structure of intentional implication, not of desire or struggle.

(77) Transcendental intersubjectivity is the one existing for me; my transcendental life is stratified, its basic stratum the first "concretion," the "connected" life in "immediate" intentionality, the stratum of primordiality. But what do immediacy and mediacy mean here? First, mediacy of intentional implication: consciousness of something as going through a consciousness of something, which can be iterated. But that holds already of recollection, of every "presentification." What is the basic character of the higher-order mediacy that exceeds primordiality? What distinguishes empathy? How can a monadically concrete present posit a concrete co-present of other monads; how can one primordiality posit a second? Everything depends on the concept of primordiality and of the "other" disclosed in it, on the mediacy of consciousness of the other as one that is neither mere symbolic indication nor mere presentification, which can be of what is one's own. Husserl closes by locating the unsolved core. Intentional mediacy is common to empathy and memory, so it cannot be what distinguishes empathy. Empathy is neither a sign of the other (symbolic indication, as in Logical Investigations I, where the other's experiences were "indicated" by expression) nor a presentification of something of mine. The positive determination of this third kind of mediacy, one primordiality positing a second co-present, remains the task; the volume's later formula of "tertiary originality" (Beilage L) is one answer.

### Appendix III. The Twofold Thematic After the Transcendental Epoché (Probably 1934)

(77) What of this can play its role right at the beginning? The question introduces the note as a reflection on the order of exposition in a planned introduction to phenomenology, written several years after the Meditations project.

(77) Is there not a twofold thematic, beginning "naively" as grasping and explicating the transcendental ego as it offers itself after the epoché, and then becoming twofold, in founding? Research demands, first, constitution of the monadic world by co-positing the alter egos and their primordialities; second, reflection on the phenomenologizing ego and its activity. The two directions are the outward constitution of intersubjectivity and the reflexive thematization of phenomenology itself. Both are founded on the naive first explication. The second is the theme of Fink's Sixth Cartesian Meditation (1932), on which Husserl worked with Fink, and the note shows Husserl accepting it as a co-equal task.

(78) First, holding the world and myself as human ego in validity, I already have co-subjects in validity in open infinity. Reduction shows that I, transformed by the reduction, have in constant performance a life of validity in which other transcendental subjects are valid for me. The reduction of the world to world-phenomenon yields, as to my human ego, the ego that as primal-modal ego (urmodales ego) holds and posits its alter egos in open infinity. That others as transcendental must be posited only after I have posited ego is self-evident. The positing "ego" continues as I perform manifold acts and claim them as transcendentally mine, beginning with the positing of the world-phenomenon and its explication; these positings are already later than the first empty positing: ego. Now I circumscribe primordiality: all intentionality that is mine, actually or potentially performed by me, their identical pole; it is my life and what is exclusively acquired in it. The term "primal-modal" ego is the 1934 vocabulary: others are modifications of the ego, which is their unmodified original mode (cf. No. 33, Beilage L). The order of positing is strict, an empty "ego" first, then acts, then world-phenomenon, then others, and it is an order of validity-founding, not of genesis, as the next paragraphs make clear by contrast.

(78) Note: in natural worldliness I have world-consciousness and self-consciousness with the sense of being in the world. I am "directed" at myself only occasionally, in reflective-active self-perception. Does it not belong to the essence of every reflective act to presuppose the straightforward act as prior, and a second-order reflection a first-order one? Reflection presupposes the unreflected. The remark prepares a genetic regress from the reflective ego of the epoché to a pre-reflective one.

(78) Further: every act presupposes affection; what it is directed at is already in the field of consciousness, ungrasped, the background intuitive or non-intuitive. The intuitive need not be perceived, but as remembered it leads back to earlier perception, as intuitive but unremembered it leads mediately to perception; the non-intuitive also leads in another way to perception or memory. Every act rests on prior passive affection, and every intuitive or empty content points back to perception. This is the genetic principle of Analyses Concerning Passive Synthesis and Experience and Judgment: perception is the primal mode to which all other modes refer.

(78) Further, all background acts presuppose corresponding earlier straightforward acts transformed into background. All reflective acts become acquisitions; reflective consciousness must already have been acquired. Even reflectivity is a habitual acquisition with a history, so the ego that performs the epoché is a historically formed ego.

(78) All this is rough talk: paths of regress as unfolding of a genesis. I come back to an ego that has not yet performed reflection, has no self-consciousness as act but has self-consciousness as background, to a primal affection and a primal hyle as affecting. The genetic regress ends at a pre-reflective ego with only background self-awareness, affected by "primal hyle" (Urhyle). The terms belong to the late time-manuscripts and to Beilage XLV's "first empathy." The paragraph distinguishes this genetic regress from the static order of positing in the first point.

(78) If I abstract from others and from everything that gains sense through them, I gain the primordial field; all capacities reaching beyond it are switched off. But can I have self-consciousness from self-reflection as "ego without others"? The closing question links the two directions of the note. If reflective self-consciousness is an acquisition, and if the full form of self-consciousness returns to itself through the other (No. 5 d), then the primordial abstraction may remove the very conditions of the reflection that performs it. The question puts the primordial reduction in doubt at its root and points to the texts of Part III on community with oneself as parallel to community with others (Nos. 24, 28; Beilage XXVIII).

## II. Texts from the Context of the Preparations for the "Systematic Work" (Summer 1930 to Spring 1931)

### No. 6. On the Theory of the Experience of Others. Intuitive and Non-Intuitive Forms of Fulfilment of the Perception of Others (August 1930)

(81) The human being in the world, myself as human being in the world: this is given and confirmed in natural-normal world-experience. How is the human being given within the constantly experienced world as one object of experience among others? I who experience this human being M have my perceptual field, the world presenting itself to me in this perceptual present with the sense "objective spatio-temporal universe of realities," of which certain things are directly perceived, M among them. Husserl starts in the natural attitude, with the other as a perceived thing among things. The question is posed from the side of the object: how is a human being given as such? This is the descriptive base of the planned "systematic work," which was to replace the Meditations with a full system of phenomenology.

(81) Every real thing I perceive I experience with an objective sense of which only one side is actually presented. As perception changes new sides come to self-presentation, so I come to know the object better or return to what was already known. Throughout, the "general" of this sense remains identical: the individual experienced as such, which becomes known and determined. In the most general way I know this thing by perceiving it with the sense stone or organism, as this stone. Generally I know how it will behave, how it must look, what strata of features it must show, what changes it will or can undergo. M in his bodily being has his objective sense, but only as contained in the sense "human lived body" and "human being in general." How do I perceive M in his specific humanity? Every perception is typifying: the individual is perceived as of a type that prefigures its behaviour. M's bodily being is perceived within the type "human body," which already exceeds the physical. The question is how the surplus beyond the physical is given.

(82) I experience his "hand" as the organ hand, what M "uses" to touch, grasp, push, his "eye" as organ, what he sees with and can move so that things on its line of sight "come into his view." I experience him as ruling in his body, as sensing when touched from outside and experiencing the touching things, and from there much else belonging to his experienced sense beyond bodily properties. Organ-hood is the first stratum of the psychic, and it is perceived in the body itself: I see a hand as grasping, not a shape to which grasping is added.

(82–83) How far does "proper" perception extend over everything that makes the human being human beyond the bodily, the one who rules "psychically" in this body and has manifold "capacities" in relation to it, organs of psychic acting and undergoing toward the surrounding things and himself as worldly thing, those things being in his perceptual field, his own body first, which is most directly affected and mediates outer relations of acting and undergoing? The issue is first a "psychophysical" one: M's acting and undergoing toward his surroundings as psychically conscious to him. I, the psychological or anthropological observer, experience M as experiencing his surrounding world, while he is affected in his body and acts in it, his organs standing in spatial relation to the objects. The question is precise: not whether others have minds, but how far perception, in the strict sense of self-presentation, reaches in my experience of another's psychophysical relation to his world. The observer is characterized as anthropologist, the role No. 30 develops into an "anthropology" as universal human science.

(83) How do I experience M's psychophysical being beyond the physical, which I perceive directly in original bodily presence? For the body I find everything that belongs to it actually perceivable in particular perceptions, even the sides now hidden. And the "psychic"? M, in his bodily position, sees the house I also see, from a side invisible to me now. I say so because I "see" it. But what falls within my proper perception when I say this? Not the other's seeing, his seeing of the other side, the house as given to him from there. Have I then no proper perception, only an intuitive presentification? Perceiving M I "understand," as a component of this perception, his being directed to that side, his looking. I see not only his body but experience his posture, the position of his eyes, his facial play; I experience the bodily expression as expression of something psychic, the bodily as significant in its psychic meaning. The answer emerges as a third option between proper perception and intuitive presentification: understanding as a component of perception, analogous to reading meaning off a word. Expression (Ausdruck) is perceived as expression, the body as "significant" (bedeutsam).

(83–84) It is as in speech, where the heard sounds are understood in their sense, and writing too, not merely as visual signs. This understanding is not an annex to my perception of M's body but my perception of M: as long as "perception" keeps its normal sense, I must speak of perception here. An object is perceived when I am conscious of it "immediately," evidently, as self-present, in the original. I perceive human beings; I cannot think of experiencing them more directly, as present in their own presence, than as I experience them as they suffer and live. But now I notice that the other's "psychic life," whatever makes him a human being and not a mere body for me, is given only "significatively," not properly perceived. Nothing psychic, neither the whole person nor any particular experience, doing, undergoing, having of appearances, is perceived in its own right. Can the psychic "really" be perceived? Yes, but never the other's, only my own. Husserl's footnote, added later, states the decisive contrast: the significatively co-present is appresented, but this appresentation can never become an actual presentation as that of a thing's back side can. Two descriptions collide, and Husserl keeps both. In the ordinary sense the other is perceived, since there is no more direct way of experiencing him; in the strict sense only his body is perceived and his psyche is "meant." The resolution is that the other's mode of perception is constituted by this unfulfillability: it is perception of a being whose psychic side is given precisely as never presentable to me.

(84) Moreover, I can make the other's psychic life intuitive, not perceptually but in intuitive presentification. I can, but I need not, and in general I do not. It is easy to see that non-intuitive significative understanding precedes any making intuitive of the meaning already functioning in perception of the other. This holds for every appresentation. Empathy in the narrow sense, intuitive imagining of the other's experience, is optional and secondary. The primary achievement is an empty, significative understanding. This corrects the common picture, Lipps's included, of empathy as an imaginative re-living, and is the thesis Beilage IV states as "empathy as perception."

(84–85) In perceptual understanding of others we have original perception, consistently confirmed in originality, as regards the other's body and its natural properties, which come forward side by side (in their being-for-me, not in the "objective sense"). Interwoven in the course of this primal-original perception, which really confirms itself through original fulfilment, are "apprehensions," "meanings." These have their own style of agreeing and disagreeing in the further course of "perceiving" the other; in the first case they run on as fulfilments for the actual existence of the psychic they "mean," which yet never comes to original, proper perception. This needs clarification. Husserl's note names the structure: fulfilment of the perception of others is two-layered, its lower layer being the process and horizon of fulfilment of mere body-perception; hence the continuous appresentation is two-layered too. Fulfilment of the psychic layer is possible without intuition of the psychic. The meaning confirms itself in the concordant course of the bodily layer, much as a sentence's meaning confirms itself in its coherent continuation. "Primal-original" perception of the body is the founding layer.

(85) Even when a thing-perception proceeds concordantly, not only is the "meaning" of the newly arriving side confirmed, but the being of the thing itself in all its sides, which are only ever pre-meant to infinity. In the constant change of the total horizon and its positings lies a process of fulfilment: the positings run on in fulfilling fashion because proper fulfilment, which still has components of empty intention, runs according to style within its limited stock. Horizon-fulfilment is rooted there. Proper fulfilment is at once fulfilment of the whole perception in its whole meaning; it is not isolated. The thing-case supplies the model: even in thing-perception, the bulk of what is meant is fulfilled only "in style," never intuitively. Fulfilment of the horizon through the stylish course of proper fulfilment is the general structure the perception of others specifies.

(85) In perceiving another, body-perception is not isolated but only an abstractively separable layer of the whole. It is continuously significant as expression. Fulfilment proceeds thus: what now expresses, positing something psychic, thereby demands a new expression, a new appearance-structure in the body as now significant; if something enters in the body for me that does not fit this demand, the positing is cancelled. The two layers interlock: the bodily appresentation continuously appresents the psychic, and this, turned backward, continuously appresents the bodily. The interlocking is circular and mutually predictive. The psychic meaning projects demands on the bodily course, and the bodily course confirms or cancels the psychic meaning. This is the mechanism of "concordant behaviour" that the Fifth Meditation (§ 52) names, here worked out as reciprocal appresentation.

(85) But can fulfilment of the kind we considered in the psychic layer, as empty indication, be the whole story? How far can an empty understanding of the other, an empty positing "on the basis" of body-perception, reach? Does all "really" experiencing perception of others not demand an intuitive, empathizing presentification? Is it not like recognizing perception? I see a town again after years: the old streets, new houses here and there, the restored church. I perceive what is present as partly unchanged, partly changed; the recognized refers to what was seen before, but something is "other." Motivations run from perception into memory and back. Yet I perform no intuitive recollection; I remain in the non-intuitive; at best fragments of momentary intuition break through. Recognition shows that a perception can be shot through with past-reference without any intuitive recollection. The analogy prepares the result: empathy may stay empty, as recognition does, while a fuller form is available.

#### Two Forms of Fulfilment of the Perception of Others, Which Are at the Same Time Levels of Fulfilment

(86) We must in any case say that perception of others has two forms of fulfilment, which are also levels of fulfilment. In the first intuition is absent; in the second it is added to empty understanding confirmed in its course. It must be shown that this peculiar presentification really brings the other as other to the most original, perceptual givenness for me, as recollection gives me my past as intuition in the mode "again." The two forms are not alternatives but levels: empty understanding is complete as perception yet surpassable by intuitive empathy, which gives the other in the highest originality open to him. Husserl claims for intuitive empathy what recollection is for the past: the mode of self-givenness proper to its object.

(86–87) The stratification in perceiving another ego and his psychic life must be noted and studied first. The natural core, his physical body, has a first stratum of meaning, "body as organ," and so a first psychic element, the "organizing" ego that animates the body's parts as organs. This stratum founds the full psychic subject: the ego that has its surrounding world, knows itself related to this world in current modes of appearance, behaves toward it as a personal ego and has long done so, has its convictions, habits, ends and means, and its acquisitions to which it can return. Husserl's note adds that in this stratum the ego is appresented only so far, and otherwise undetermined, though from the outset as another ego, "my like." The layering runs: physical body, lived body as organ with its organizing ego, full person with surrounding world and history. The organizing ego is already an ego, but an indeterminate one, "my like" in general; personality is a further, founded determination. The stratification answers the behaviourist objection in Beilage IV by locating mind already in the perceived organ-hood of the body.

(87) First I understand the lived body as lived body, together with the perceptual field in which the other is bodily present and which is oriented around him. I understand this from myself and my perceptual field, in which that body has the meaning of an analogue of my body, as if I stood there. The lowest psychic stratum is the other's orientation: his body as centre of a perceptual field. The "as if I stood there" is the formula of Hua I § 53, here placed at the base of the stratification.

(87) On this the higher sense is built, determining more closely the still indeterminate sense "ego," beyond what the lower stratum determines. In this higher stratum the pre-meanings often need "actual" confirmation. But that needs closer study. The personal stratum is less secure than the organic one: its anticipations, about convictions or purposes, are often wrong, and need confirmation that bodily behaviour alone may not give. The deferral marks the problem of understanding persons, taken up in Beilage IV.

#### Supplementary Remarks

(87) To the perception of a real being such as a human being or animal belongs as essential component a co-meaning of the psychic, not an intuitive presentification of it. But what more precisely is such "co-meaning"? It should be a co-presentation, appresentation, by which the psychic is itself there in one with the bodily. In thing-experience the back side is appresented, and the course of experience is progressive confirmation, fulfilment by particular perception: the appresented becomes actual by presentation, presents itself. Here that holds only of the body, not of its psyche. What is this strange kind of confirmation? M really is a human being: the body changes in a style that is always a manifold of expression and unity of a total expression, each new expression agrees with the previous ones, and the unity of the "meaning" "human person" is maintained. The bracketed supplement restates the paradox and gives the answer: confirmation of the psychic is the continuing unity of a total expression across manifold expressions. The person is confirmed as a coherent meaning is confirmed, by the agreement of its parts.

(88) Compare linguistic expressions. In understanding an expression I have in a sense a perception, the perception of word and sentence as meaningful expression. But it can be a deceptive perception: clarifying, I see that the partial meanings do not really make up a unity of meaning. Single meanings point ahead, have horizons, and must have connections of fulfilment in their course. As we know, something else is realization in the unity of a possible judgment, the non-contradictory uniting in which the theses cohere into one thesis, the "being able to think" in the sense of "being able to judge"; and only then, as further and new, the unity of self-giving, evidencing of the expressed meaning: is it "truth" or "possible truth"? Husserl's note: "the familiar 'threefold layering'!" The reference is to Formal and Transcendental Logic (1929, §§ 13–22): the logic of mere consequence of grammatical form, the logic of non-contradiction, and the logic of truth. Applied to expression, it yields three levels of fulfilment: grammatical unity of sense, logical compatibility, intuitive evidence. The analogy suggests that the perception of persons has corresponding levels, coherent expression, consistent personality, and intuitive understanding.

(88–89) Much needs adding. I "apperceive" something sensuous "as expression." It may "accidentally" look like a word or an algebraic sign, and I understand it with meaning. The difference: I grasp the accidentalness, I then imagine myself into it; it is not really a word but as if it were. A word is really a word only as spoken, written or printed with the intention of being understood, an intention that can vary. A motto in a coat of arms says: this belongs to the arms of this family, instituted once as motto and to be referred by others to this situation. Or the notice forbidding walking on a lawn, or print on paper: newspaper, so news or entertainment, or scientific communication, textbook. The situation is grasped, determined or left indeterminate; the words have "the same sense" in the most various situations and situation-senses, yet modify their sense, as a sentence I utter as my judgment and "the same" uttered by another, the same sentence as motto, in a prayer, in a real or a fictional context, related to real persons, imaginary ones, or to "the city," which through its officials issued the sentence as ordinance, in lasting validity and addressed to passers-by. An expression is an expression only as issuing from an intention to communicate; what merely looks like a word is a quasi-word. Expression thus implies a speaker, often anonymous or institutional ("the city"), and a situation. The sense of the words is constant while their situated meaning varies with speaker, addressee and mode of validity. Husserl's examples, especially the lawn notice issued by the city, anticipate the analysis of anonymous institutional communication in No. 29.

(89) Here intuitions often play their fulfilling role, but how necessarily? I understand at once, read the paper, read on, and the unity of connected meaning organizes itself. Suddenly clarity fails, understanding has become vague, I reread the last lines and it becomes clear. I can repeat "at will" and recognize the same unity of meaning. If I judge along, it is the same judgment, mine. But it may become questionable whether it is true, even possible; inner resistance arises: it conflicts with earlier judgments still mine. Which is right? Reading shows understanding fulfilling itself without intuition, through clarity of articulation, and its passage from understanding to co-judging to critical evaluation. The parallel for persons: I understand others emptily, can clarify, can adopt their convictions, and can come into conflict with my own, the structure of "taking over" others' validities developed in Beilage XIV.

(89) We have a change of the "meaning" of the sense relating to the situation and to the meaning-determining subjectivity, while the words in themselves have one and the same "meaning," which the change does not touch. Hence an ambiguity of talk of sense or meaning, and different directions of confirmation. Two concepts of meaning are distinguished: ideal sentence-meaning, invariant, and situated meaning, varying with subject and situation. Each has its own confirmation: the first by logical clarification, the second by understanding the situation and the subject. The perception of others belongs to the second type.

(89–90) We have various interwoven intentional achievements founded on each other in the context of intersubjectivity. Wherever egoic intentionality functions, there is being-directed-at, but also intentional connections and horizons, and conscious life constantly advances from intention to fulfilment in very different senses. Perception passes into perception, and pre-meanings and horizons pass into one another, fulfilling in their way: the pre-meaning of the front side continuously into fulfilment by the new side, and in each phase of proper fulfilment the whole perception, horizon included, is fulfilled, the horizon of each phase by that of the new. So for the perceptual field and the passage to new fields, partly with the same things in changing perceptions. Little becomes properly intuitive in these transitions, yet there is continuous fulfilment of the non-intuitive sense, the co-meant horizons. Concretely there is constant agreement in the continuity of the streaming presence as streaming world-presence for me. When agreement is broken as illusion, this happens on the ground of universal agreement, disturbed only at one place or line. But presence has "meaning" beyond presence, as recognition with the sense "perceived before," reference to a field of memory I can actualize by recollection or by meanings like linguistic ones, all the "meanings" that accrue in the primal presence. The text ends by generalizing. All conscious life is fulfilment, mostly non-intuitive, within a "streaming world-presence" that holds universal agreement as ground; illusion is always local. And presence always carries meanings beyond itself, of past (recognition) and of others (expression). The perception of others, with its empty fulfilment, is therefore not an anomalous form of experience but a case of the general structure of experience as horizonal, largely empty fulfilment.

### Appendix IV. Experience of the Spiritual in the World, Above All of the Psychic. Empathy as Perception. Behaviourism (Turn of the Year 1930/1931)

(91) Husserl's table of contents: the experience of a human being in his individual typicality and the strata of this typicality; comportment (behaviour); the mode of givenness of a human being in mere behaviour clarified as mere "inductive" indication of the personal, "perception" of human beings that understands them in their doings without genuinely empathizing appresentation in intuition of the other; hence clarification of the twofold "perception" of others and of confirming experience, concordance in mere indication, but higher grounding value through making intuitive in genuine presentification. The summary states the result of No. 6 in the vocabulary of contemporary psychology: what behaviourism describes is real, the first level of perceiving others, but it lacks the second, intuitive level that gives the higher grounding value. "Inductive" indication means association by regular connection, not inference.

(91) The problem of descriptive science, especially of man, as the foundation for a critique of behaviouristic psychology. The critique of behaviourism will not attack its method from outside but show that its descriptions presuppose an experience of the human being they leave unanalysed.

(91) Human spirituality from outside. The individual typicality of a human being in its levels of generality, characters. The normal human being. Types of anomaly, the individual type of a mentally ill person. Individual types of normality: within the genus man the normal man, but also man in general. Types of persons are given "from outside," through their comportment; normality appears here as a typological, not merely a constitutive, category, nested within the genus.

(91) The researcher, scientist, psychologist in particular, as a normal human being with the faculty of scientific reason, a researching subject who is to describe all types rationally, the scientist's own included, already finding himself in a community of researchers doing scientific communal work and gaining theory and science as communal product. The describer belongs to what he describes: the psychologist is himself a normal type, and science is a communal achievement, the self-inclusion that makes psychology's relation to its object reflexive.

(91) The world in which we find ourselves, the "outer," spatio-temporal world. We as subjects who find each other and our world "inwardly," intentionally in consciousness, and our life. The pairing of "outer" and "inner" frames the problem: subjects are both in the spatio-temporal world and the place where it is found.

(91) In the "outer" world, in spatio-temporality, human beings among spatio-temporal realities. The real human being (animal) as body with spatialized, naturalized "spirituality," co-existing with physical corporeality. Naturalization of spirit is a constituted sense: the psyche is apprehended as located where the body is. This is the "naturalistic attitude" of Ideas II, Part II, now presented as a mode of experiencing others.

(91) The naturalized spirit. The lived body with its specifically bodily comportment and the personal worldly comportment manifested in it. Man experienced from outside, in his organic nature, in his somatic and personal comportment. Its typicality, the individually typical. The individually experienced man as this man in this comportment. The problem of comportment as determination of man in his individual and general typical exteriority. "Comportment" (Gehaben) is Husserl's rendering of "behaviour," and he gives it two strata: somatic (organic) and personal. The individual is grasped through his individually typical comportment, a "style" in the sense of Ideas II § 61.

(92) Understanding comportment. This man counts, says numbers aloud, writes them on the board in front of him. We spectators, observing psychologists, "see" this; we share his surrounding world, where we too act, and the board is a board we too can write on. We understand that the physical of his body, movements, and causality in relation to the board has an extra-physical meaning. For me: I move, intend, experience my body in certain modes of appearance and so the board; but I am directed not at the changing appearances but at the thing. Turned subjectively: I move my hand, use it as "organ," a physical moving that is at the same time directed by me, striven for, aimed. I experience the other: he is physically there, but I am directed at him as a human being; I experience his using his organs, aiming at achievements, acting, also his deliberating, doubting, striving to resolve doubt. I understand this, can make it clearly intuitive by "living into" the other as if I did it myself, I who live through the spiritual in my comportment and can observe it in original experience. The counting example shows that even cognitive acts (counting, doubting) are understood from comportment. The understanding rests on my original experience of my own comportment as directed movement. "Living into" (Einleben) names the second, intuitive level.

(92–93) In observing and describing human beings as they present themselves from outside, recognized in their comportment and confirmed as actual (establishing their general and finally individual typicality), do I need "intuition," intuitive clarity of the "spiritual meanings"? In experiencing people in my surrounding world, who remain the same through changing comportment, this comportment is understood singly and as a whole by an experiencing apperception. It is experiential manifestation of moving hands touching and eyes seeing, as originally perceiving things this person has before him as near or far; then of activities founded in this, grasping the pen, throwing the stone; higher still, of a manifold yet unified occupation with the world according to unified goals, finally belonging to the unity of self-preservation. These people may be related to others whom they experience as bodily manifesting themselves too. These manifestations are certain as experiences and agree with one another, or abnormally conflict, so that a manifestation is cancelled as merely apparent. Experiential apperception of comportment has its own levels, perceiving, acting, goal-directed occupation, self-preservation, and its own concordance and cancellation. It is experience in the full sense even without intuitive clarification.

(93) Even in mere non-intuitive, merely indicating manifestation, what manifests itself agrees or not, in the whole course of indicating experience. It is a distinct stratum of experience, which shows agreement or disagreement, confirms and maintains certainty or cancels it, as in the streaming experience of the physical world. Walking through hill and valley, what we see is seen in ever other modes of appearance, which normally refer to one another concordantly; it points to the new as expected, which generally confirms itself; or it does not, and without reflection reinterpretation and correction follow, even before actual perception. Description of nature by familiar types, intuitive genera and species, and of a single natural object in its individual typicality, always experienced as specification of pre-formed generalities, takes place on the ground of universal experience presupposed as concordant and exercised with self-evident correction, without questions being asked about this natural method. One follows the natural evidence that lies in successful concordance. Likewise in the spiritual sphere: one follows indicating experience and its own concordance, restored by occasional correction. One "understands," and is certain by virtue of continuing agreement. Indication has the same evidential structure as perceptual experience of nature, which also works almost entirely through non-intuitive anticipation corrected in passing. Husserl equates the natural evidence of the naturalist and of the behaviourist: both are legitimate experience, both unexamined.

(93–94) Can experience of persons, their activities and deliberations, remain wholly without intuitive presentification of their inwardness? We have an immense realm of familiarity with what empathic indication indicates, in its original intuitive self-presentation in our own life: our bodily experience of ruling in our organs, our immediate experience of things presenting themselves "through" this ruling, our thematic occupation with them, explicating, judging predicatively, theorizing, transforming them in action, weighing practical possibilities and our abilities, deciding doubts and questions theoretical and practical. Our possible occupation with ourselves is also originally familiar. In this sphere we have nature in its most original appearance and with it our psychic being as interwoven with our body in a particular way, using it as organ and occupied from there with other physical things primordially experienced. Here possibilities of concordant self-experience as human being are originally prefigured from the actual one, anticipated in original certainty. This yields a realm of most original, primordial apperception of nature and of nature animated by the ego, the "psychophysical" nature of one's own and nature "spiritualized" by one's occupation. Here we abstract from others and what they contribute to my apperception of the world as objective, common world. The primordial self-apperception as psychophysical being is the reservoir from which every indication of the other draws. Even empty indication presupposes the familiarity of my own original experience of acting; so empathy, however empty, is not independent of first-person experience.

(94) The apperceptive field of primordiality enters into empathy. The question is how empathic apperception of the other comes to function as mere non-intuitive indication and yet as an experience. Is empathic intuitiveness really first, with intuition lost only afterward, as it fades? Husserl raises the genetic alternative: perhaps intuitive empathy is original and empty indication its faded form, as empty retention is the faded form of the present. The next paragraph answers by the general structure of experience.

(94) But is not all experience of objects "apperception"? Does it not necessarily include a domain of "protention" in the widest sense (as of retention), a horizon of indicated co-presence, future, co-future, in the mode of ability (with its own modal variants)? These horizons function in experience before they become intuitive, and then only as fulfilment along certain lines. Clarification, possible in all other directions of sense, is a making intuitive, a picturing in advance or after, which has no function for experience while its course is concordant. Only when the synthesis of concordance is inhibited in a way passive experience does not resolve by itself does clarification of the indicated possibilities come into play. Intuition has a repair function: it is called on when passive concordance breaks down. The general law of horizon-intentionality, empty functioning with clarification on demand, settles the genetic question against the priority of intuitive empathy.

(94–95) Is it not the same with empathy? As long as indication functions normally within the whole psychophysical experience, whose physical core of most original experience is the core of the horizon of indication, and the concordance of concrete apperception continues, experience reinforces experience, and certainty of this human being's existence as perceiving, occupied, in this mood, reacting emotionally (through the higher indications) remains unbroken, as psychophysical perception. This enters into perception's modifications, such as recollection, which "repeats" the concordance; in this repeating presentification too the horizons need not be made intuitive but function in memory as "empty" indications, repeating the former normally functioning perception and being at once experience of the past. The analogy is completed: empathy, like thing-perception and like recollection, works by empty indication as long as concordance holds. "Psychophysical perception" is Husserl's name for this complete, unclarified experience of the other.

(95) If I understand another as other and as acting, but not in particular what he is doing, from what motives, toward what ends, an inhibition arises on this basis. It may vanish "of itself" as I keep watching: the newly experienced acts back on what was just experienced and retentionally held, and the inhibited understanding frees itself, perhaps with corrections; the new horizon-sense, grounded in certainty, reacts directly on the earlier, already inhibited validity, crossing it out as illusory. But where further experience does not or cannot achieve this, if he breaks off or I cannot wait, I may, interested in his doing and intention, reach clarity by empathizing intuitively in him and his whole situation, as if I stood in it and were motivated as he evidently is in respects I already know from experience. Intuitive empathy enters when the passive course of understanding stalls. It is imaginative self-transposition into the other's situation and motivation, guided by what I already know of him. The account makes intuitive empathy an epistemic tool for resolving uncertainty, close to what the Verstehen tradition (Dilthey) called re-experiencing (Nacherleben), but subordinated to perception.

(95–96) We must say that the experience of human beings (of animals, and of all objective spiritual realities) is self-giving even where the specifically psychic and spiritual is only appresented by indication. But the more perfect self-giving we have only when these empathically experienced contents come to "proper" experience, when I also "see" what I co-mean non-intuitively, though concretely in experiential function, and so bring its meaning to fulfilment. This "seeing" is no immediate perception of those sides of the supra-physical concretum. But the making-intuitive is a genuinely fulfilling, originally experiencing appresentation, an analogue of intuitive recollection, whose intentional mediacy does not prevent it from being self-giving of the past as having been perceived. Here it is self-giving of the other's subjective life, an originally experienceable mode of variation of the immediate perceptual self-giving of my own subjective life. As without my perception no recollection is possible as intentionally modified certainty of a self-given past, so without the constant perceptual self-appearing of my own spirituality no experience of others is possible as perception of others in a modified sense, as originally fulfilled appresentation of the other or originally fulfilled intuition of the other as analogue of myself. The paragraph is the theoretical core of the appendix and justifies its title, "empathy as perception." Intuitive empathy is self-giving of the other, as recollection is self-giving of the past: both are intentionally mediated, both are modifications of my own perceptual self-presence, and neither is deficient for that, since each is the only possible original mode of its object. The "modification of my perceptual self-givenness" thesis is the doctrine Beilage L formalizes as secondary and tertiary originality.

(96) Not to be considered here is the far more complicated experience of objects of objective spirit, which rests on psychophysical empathy, other-experience and an already pre-given human world. But for them too their full experiential sense unfolds, and they are properly given, only in a corresponding experiencing intuition of the objectified spirituality, which includes reference to the pre-given world as field of human experience and action. Cultural objects, books, tools, institutions, have the same two-level structure: empty understanding and intuitive realization of the spirit embodied in them, which requires reference to the human world in which they function.

(96) For the simpler case of psychophysical realities we thus have a two-level "perception" and experience: one that grasps the psychic merely in indicating expression in the physical, and one more perfectly self-having and self-confirming through fulfilling appresentation. The summary sentence, with Husserl's emphasis, fixes the doctrine of the two levels that No. 6 had called forms of fulfilment.

(96) This belongs to the experience of psychophysical realities, or of realities with objective spiritual sense, apart from the objective spirituality lying in intersubjective worldliness in general. The qualification separates the particular spiritual sense of objects (a tool's use) from the general intersubjective sense of the world as "for everyone," which belongs to everything worldly and is not an expression of anyone.

(96–97) All objective experience of the worldly is not merely experience of individuals with generality coming only later as logical concepts. The individual is experienced already typified, as object of use, forest, river, stone, man as man, animal as animal, man with typical human traits. In a sense the general, the classificatory, lies before classification, at any rate before conceptual grasp; even naming, which normally sets in, does not have its generality of meaning in a concept arising from comparison and identifying abstraction. Pre-predicative typicality is the doctrine of Experience and Judgment §§ 80–85: types precede concepts and even names. Husserl draws the consequence for descriptive science, whose concepts must be formed from these pre-given types.

(97) This holds in the spiritual sphere too, for psychophysical realities and their properties explicated in experience. A special role is played already in the organic sphere, and in a new and more manifold sense in the specifically spiritual one, by the difference of normality and anomality, of perfection, of truth and genuineness as norms of reason. They attach to the type "personal ego-subject" with its traits of character, talents, skills. Continuing experience always rests on experiences of types, but it is also always formation of new types through the ever newly formed and widened horizons of similarity-spheres that each apperception founds by repetition in similarity. This is the foundation of general naming, which is also a special horizon-formation: every real thing in the surrounding world already has some name and is apperceived with it; if it is "new," it has an unknown name. Even "a" name in general is a typical property. Two claims stand out. Normative types (perfect, genuine, normal) are already at work in the experience of persons, before any ethics. And having a name is itself a typical property of things in the human world: the new thing is apperceived as having a name I do not know, an elegant mark of the intersubjective, linguistic constitution of the surrounding world.

(97) All this, so far as it is spiritual, is subject to that externally indicating or genuinely fulfilling self-giving experience. The question is how descriptive science proceeds, or should proceed, in forming concepts and fixing descriptive truths. Experience is the primal method on which all methods rest. How must scientific experience be constituted as scientific method, and how the method of description and then of theory? "Experience is the primal method" (Urmethode) restates the principle of all principles for the empirical sciences: method in science is a refinement of experience, so the theory of experience is the foundation of scientific method.

(97) After our analysis of the experience of spirit it seems self-evident that science from description must rest on full intuitiveness, on producing fulfilling appresentations. Admittedly, perfect intuitiveness, an experience explicating originally on all sides, is impossible, as analysis of experience of the real shows. In this respect descriptive theorizing of any real domain, natural or spiritual, is subject to general questions of legitimation. No descriptive science intends to go to the end; it presupposes that one need not go on to infinity, but that a finite confirmation in experience and a logical concept-formation based on it suffice, despite finitude, to become certain of the real's existence and individual or general type and fix it conceptually. Descriptive science, of nature or of persons, relies on finite confirmation within an infinite horizon. The demand for intuitive fulfilment is therefore always partial; science settles for "enough."

(97–98) From here it must be shown that such experiencing faces serious questions of justification: what the achievement of finite limitation within open infinity consists in, and in what sense, by what "rational" presumption, its right lies. Instead of the naive, unexplicated method of experience and thought of the positive sciences, a systematic investigation of method must come. Since experience is here the method, the essence and achievement of experience of the real, and especially of the spiritually real, must become a theme in its own right. The justification of the finite "enough" is a transcendental problem: what makes a finite confirmation rational relative to an infinite horizon? Husserl's answer elsewhere is the theory of presumptive evidence and typicality; here he only demands that the method be thematized.

(98) Here a critique of behaviourism would have to start. Behaviourism clings to symbolically empty expressiveness and does not even systematically produce an intuition of the spiritual, still less make the modes of experiencing the spiritual a theme, not even roughly as we have done. The critique is precise and moderate. Behaviourism is not wrong to start from comportment, since that is the first level of perceiving others; it is wrong to stop there, without the second level, intuitive understanding, and without reflection on the experience of the psychic that its own descriptions use. Its error is a failure of method, not of fact.

### No. 7. Primordial Core and Experience of Others as Strata of the Transcendental Present (Summer 1930)

Kern notes that the text follows, in the manuscript, analyses titled "the living streaming present" that examine the perceptual presence of the world, from which Husserl clearly set it apart. His cover note summarizes: the mode of givenness of the world in the primal-phenomenal present is two-layered, first the primordial layer as a layer in the present, second the others and the world's mode of givenness through them; concept of the transcendental ego and of primordiality. The primordial reduction is thus approached here through the analysis of the living present, not through abstraction from others' contributions.

(99) So far we have considered the world-phenomenon in the transcendental present exclusively as perceptual world-presence, and from there extensive descriptions would have to radiate concerning the spatio-temporality of the world in the mode of original spatio-temporal presence. The paragraph closes the preceding noematic analysis of the perceived world, the "transcendental aesthetic" that No. 4 had called world-aesthetic ontology.

#### Turn from Noematic, Onto-Phenomenological Description to Noetic-Phenomenological Description. Self-Constitution of the Transcendental Ego-Monad as Self-Temporalizing

(99) Instead of going further in the ontic direction, consider: in the concrete transcendental now of my transcendental ego, "the" objective world presents itself as transcendental phenomenon in the form of what appears originally to me hic et nunc. In streaming, my transcendental present of being and life constitutes itself with a streaming horizon, and so the "consciousness" of continuing being, being that becomes from past through present into future. This consciousness of unity has its closedness; it discloses itself, again within the streaming self-presence, in self-present presentifications and especially through an "I can" that becomes conscious in them and can be exercised "again and again." Difficult analyses must follow here: how in the streaming present, through such capacities of presentification, transcendental past and future come forth originally as an existing stream of experience and become evidently self-given as stream, so that "present," "past" and so on indicate a typology in a streaming relativity. Further, how in this relativity there is constituted a continuous unity of immanent time, by which one and the same past can be seen evidently through the constant change of relative pasts and modes of memory. Husserl's note specifies that this self-disclosure, as evidence of the streaming present and of the ego's being in its temporalized modes of life, is the work of the transcendental spectator's reflection. The stream is not simply given; it is disclosed by presentifications that the "I can" governs. The living present is "closed," a unity of consciousness that becomes a stream with past and future only through the capacity to remember. Immanent time is a synthetic identity across relative modes, the result Hua X had reached and the C-manuscripts re-ground in the living present.

(100) The same holds for the constitution of the transcendental phenomenon world as regards the world-time it contains: in the immanently temporal streaming of modes of givenness of the phenomenal world-present there are constituted not only the world-present but world-past and world-future and finally world-time, which designates a synthetic form of identity across the changing relativities of what is present, past and future for me. World-time is constituted in parallel with immanent time, as identity-form across relative temporal modes; it is not a container presupposed by them.

#### Methodological: Account of the Procedure from the Epoché Onward

(100–101) The natural procedure, which should probably have come first, can only be this: starting from the world as phenomenon, always concretely given but not yet explicated, we ask about its most general transcendental-aesthetic ontological structure, and from there ask back to its transcendental modes of givenness, its modes of appearance. We arrive at ever new modes of givenness-of, ever new syntheses of the same in different modes, all transcendental occurrences of the transcendental ego. Husserl's note adds that a change in the direction of the spectator's gaze is then required: a reflection on the transcendentally constituting conscious life in which the world-phenomenon becomes streamingly conscious in the primal stream of experience, and on the constituting ego of activities and passivities. The method has two steps: noematic regress from the world's ontological structure to its modes of appearance, then noetic reflection on the constituting life. This is the "way through ontology" that Hua VIII and the Krisis develop, and it explains the order of No. 7.

(101–102) A new fundamental difference must now be introduced, first in the transcendental present and within the transcendental perceptual field of the world as it appears originally to me (in the first, noematic-ontic direction, Husserl notes). As transcendental ego I have myself as human being as a worldly phenomenon belonging to every mundane perceptual field; whatever things are originally given to me, I as human being am present among them. All transcendental analysis concerns me as human being too. If we now consider other human beings as perceptually given in the world-phenomenon, a fundamental difference strikes us in how worldly contents come to original givenness as "appearing" for me. The other is originally given only from one "side," like everything real. But there is an unbridgeable difference between realities which, passing from side to side and through the activity of the confirming ego that brings the horizonal to properly experiencing givenness, essentially disclose finally all sides, all determinations ascribed to them; and realities with "sides" that in principle cannot be given in actual, most proper originality. This concerns the "psychic life" of others, human and animal, which I have as self-present in perception and as past in presentifications. Bodies, including lived bodies, are given side by side so that each side is originally perceived and every bodily feature can come to perception; the horizon of each side points to purely perceptual connections. Every presentification here means having perceived, going to perceive, possible perception, belonging to my transcendental field. But every side in which another's body is given carries another horizon whose realization leads to presentifications of a fundamentally new kind, which can never become direct perceptions in the flow of the present nor have been perceptions, that is, cannot have the character of memories. Husserl now restates the distinction of No. 6 within the transcendental present itself. The criterion is the type of horizon: thing-horizons lead to my possible perceptions or memories; the other's psychic horizon leads to presentifications that are neither possible perceptions nor memories of mine. The heading that follows names the two layers.

#### 1) The Core Layer of What Is Primordially Experienceable in My Transcendental Present; 2) the Worldly Contents Experienceable Only Through Empathy

(102–103) Starting from the transcendental world-phenomenon, a fundamental division opens. The world experienced by the transcendental ego, taken purely as such, has a continuous core layer of mundane features that are actually and properly experienceable in actual and possible experience, perceived or perceivable, having been perceived or perceivable, for me. Over against this, what is experienced as worldly (we leave open whether throughout or in particulars) has a further layer of essential contents that, realized in my field, lead to presentifications which carry possible perceptions, but perceptions of a perceiving ego that is not me. In my immanent field these presentifications occur as my experiences and as experiences of another's psychic life, presentifying perceptions as simultaneous with my present perceptions but as another's, given to me with certainty of being. Further I have presentifications of memories that are not mine but the other's; even phantasies in my field, presentifications of perceptions-as-if, have parallels in experiences of another's phantasies, or in phantasies in which I imagine others' experiences and their phantasizing. The layering is iterative: I can presentify another's memories and phantasies, imagine another imagining. This iterability shows that empathy is a full parallel to my own presentifications, a whole second system of intentional modifications, not a single act.

(103–104) It can be foreseen that I as transcendental ego can also appropriate in transcendental reduction what falls under "other human being" or animal, sociality and the worldly formations of communal activity, and make transcendental findings about their spiritual side, not only their bodies. Without going into this, we posit the division and point out that a general cut can be made through the transcendental world-phenomenon. For this phenomenon it meant: the world purely as world of experience appears with the epoché as mine, the transcendentally oriented ego's. This ego and its "I abstain" is the first thing transcendentally positable for it, and all transcendental modes of givenness of the world, actual and possible, which together make up the world-phenomenon inseparable from my epoché, are transcendentally mine, inseparable from this ego and its "I abstain" as first positable and as remaining in abiding validity. Here there is of course no other ego, only in the world-phenomenon the phenomenon "other human being" (and myself as human being), which as transcendental phenomenon is "my" phenomenon, belonging to what is given at the start as inseparable from me. At the start of the reduction there is only one transcendental ego; the others are phenomena in it. The heavily stressed "my" phenomenon states the initial solipsism of method that No. 7 will then try to overcome.

(104–105) So, as with passively running mundane givens transcendentally taken, so with the active ones running in "my" capacities, all modes of "I can" and "I do," proper perceptions, sides, perspectives, their horizons, the presentifications explicating them: all are mine, my intentional experiences, always taken transcendentally. Under "appearing thing" or "appearing side," the being of the thing is not posited in the natural way. Every human being as part of the experienced world is my actual and possible transcendental phenomenon. Another ego as transcendental other, a second transcendental ego, has as yet no intelligible sense. Now I am the only transcendental ego, and everything I have as transcendental ego is my "experience," content of my living and being conscious, belonging to my transcendental total present, posited through presentifications as transcendentally past or future. The phenomena of the mundane are the conscious, the "meant" as such in constituting life, inseparable from it as its objective sense, identified synthetically in the noematic modes of appearance through continuous and discrete syntheses, which are themselves syntheses of experience bearing the identical as objective sense. The spaced sentence, "Now I am the only transcendental ego," is the methodological solipsism of the first stage, which No. 7 accepts provisionally. "Experience" (Erlebnis) is in quotation marks because it covers both lived experiences and their objective senses: the meant as meant is inseparable from the meaning.

(105) What I have as transcendental ego, what I can look at, is on the one hand the bracketed world, the world-phenomenon, with its single mundane phenomena in their modes of givenness. On the other hand, in noetic reflection I find the primal sphere of constitutive temporalization, the stream of my modes of consciousness and egoic modes of activity in which everything mundane and its manifold appearances come about, "constitute themselves." The transcendentally constituting life, in which the world is constituted for the ego in its changing actuality, and in further reflection this life itself is constituted as the ego's transcendental life, is the ultimate concretion in which everything positable as being is contained: the absolutely concrete noetic life, the all-living (allerlebende) life in which whatever is conscious and posited is "experience." The ultimate concretion is self-constituting: constituting life is constituted as such in further reflection. "All-living" is the term the late manuscripts use for the absolute flowing life that contains everything as its lived content.

(105) Let us now consider what the stratification in the transcendental mode of givenness of the world, in the world-phenomenon inseparable from me, means, to which the distinction between myself as human being and the other human being draws our attention. The single sentence turns from the noetic to the stratification problem announced in the heading.

(105–107) Where other human beings and anything human appear in world-experience, in immediate perception, within the worldly taken as mere phenomenon and transcendental "sense of being," there is evidently a stratification in the perceptual sense of the other: only the body, not the psyche, is properly perceived and can be, however much the concrete human being, bodily perceivable with his psyche originally experienced as "co-present," is called perceived, and rightly. Wherever animate worldly being is experienced, there is necessarily a lower stratum of sense that is directly perceivable, a synthetic unity of direct perceptual modes of givenness. This stratum is distinguished by being inseparable from the transcendental ego, which appears after the epoché as the first transcendental being, given in constant apodictic identity and absolute originality: these are my perceptions, my streaming perceiving, my perceptual syntheses, and the perceptual sense building up in them is inseparable from the transcendentally perceiving ego, the only transcendental being so far. This carries over to recollections: my present consciousness of the mundane "having been perceived" posits my having perceived. Not so for the founded stratum, the new presentifications belonging to experience of others, through which the other's psyche is there for me with his body (empathy). This experience is my intentional experience, but a presentification that is not my memory or anticipation; what it presentifies is not in my perceptual field, a perceiving and perceived, remembering, expecting, possible experiencing, presentified with its perceptual sense, but not of my identical ego, experienced as the experience of another ego, from whom, if he exists, this living is inseparable. Whatever becomes of my transcendental positing of another ego, I have "at first" myself originally, in validity as transcendental ego, with a closed circle of intentional experiences and their inseparable meant contents. To this belong all proper perceptual givens of the worldly, the basic stratum, and also all presentifications through which the spiritual world constitutes itself for me with experiential sense in the whole of the world experienceable for me. Husserl's note: "at first" does not mean temporally first, without any validity of others that would be added afterward as a second, separate thing; we only establish that the primordial ego and its original actual and possible conscious life is a first that constantly founds the validity of others in possible empathy and confirmation. The criterion of primordiality is now positional: what is inseparable from me. My perceptions and recollections posit my having perceived; empathic presentifications posit another's perceiving, which, if it exists, is inseparable from him. Yet the empathic experiences themselves, and even the presentifications that constitute the spiritual world for me, belong to my closed circle. The note against a temporal reading of "at first" is the strongest statement so far of the non-genetic character of the primordial founding.

(107–108) I can say generally: from the first "I am" and "I practise epoché" there opens an endless realm of what is my own, the universe of what is perceived and perceivable for me in the first and most proper sense, and further of what is accessible to me through original modifications of my perceptions. Husserl's note adds: reducing my being to the perceptual yields my primordial transcendental present. I myself, my streaming present with all its contents, am perceptually given, transcendentally, and so transcendentally judgeable. Likewise the endless past constituted in present recollections and acts of recollection, original modifications of my actual and possible perceptions. In a certain way I can call these original modifications, as a domain of possible and evidently legitimate transcendental positings, perceptions in an extended sense: perceptions of my transcendental past and future (the latter with a margin of possibilities). A special sphere of perceptions are the mundane ones, which reduced lead to connections of my transcendental experiences carrying a transcendent objective sense, inseparable from them as their transcendental sense and co-valid, while the straightforward natural positing must be withheld. This differs from my past transcendental experiences, a past act for example, which I can posit straightforwardly as transcendental without positing it as merely meant by its presentifications. Ownness is now defined by perception and its original modifications (memory, expectation). Two classes are distinguished within it: my past transcendental acts, posited straightforwardly as having been, and mundane objects, posited only as meant. The distinction is the transcendental version of the immanent/transcendent difference.

(108) The reduction of world-experience to the pure mundane perceptual sphere and its derivative, to "pure" world-experience, within the transcendental reduction of the experiential world in general, we call reduction to the primordial world-phenomenon as basic stratum of the transcendental world-phenomenon. Husserl's note: but then world-experience means primordial experience. "Pure experience" is experience free of empathy; the primordial world is the world as given to it. The note concedes the terminological consequence: the world of pure experience is not the world in its full sense.

(108–109) This fixes a basic concept of the transcendental ego: the transcendental ego and everything inseparable from it from its own constitution. The ego is the identity-pole to which all its capacities, acts, passive experiences, intentionalities, meant contents, objective senses, characters of being and of value are related. To it belong the meant others as such, the other's experiences as meant contents of my experience, but not they themselves in their own being. The other is not merely a sense of being I have formed and put into validity, not merely valid from my validity with a content stemming from and proper to me. The egological sphere is the one accessible to and theorizable through original perception. I, the transcendental ego, am the theorizer; the theory is from me and for me, with no "objectivity," no address to a human community, as when I pursue positive science as human being. As transcendental ego I reflect on myself and my own. I make descriptive statements fitted to simple self-evidence, and that is already "theory," science. I judge and ground these judgments as evident truths, set out by me and for me, concerning only my own.
