This transcendental egology is the phenomenology first in itself, consciously "solipsistic," but it proves the fundamental domain of phenomenology in general, which reaches much further and finally encompasses all philosophy properly so called. The concept of the transcendental ego fixed here is the primordial one. Two consequences are striking. The other in his own being is not part of my ego, however much his meant being is; the paragraph explicitly rejects the reduction of the other to a sense I have formed. And egology is a science without addressee, an incommunicable science, which Husserl calls "consciously solipsistic." The tension, a science that is science only for me, is the problem Fink's Sixth Meditation and No. 21 (the community of phenomenologizing) take up.

(109–110) An extension of egological phenomenology would be conceivable and necessary if I, the ego of the epoché, could become convinced that in the transcendental reduction of the other human being his ego and ownness can be posited and confirmed as necessarily valid for me, just as in the reduction of my own human existence my psychic being is reduced to my transcendental ego, in which my body becomes a transcendental phenomenon in me. Especially, if the transcendental consideration of the presentifications in which the other ego and its life are experienced could be equated, as to what is positable, with that of recollections, in which I relate to my non-present being. Then we would speak of the transcendental ego over against transcendental others, of transcendental communities of egos, and all worldly contents that in their natural sense refer back to human thinking, feeling, willing and acting, as human achievements giving the human surrounding world its spiritual sense, would yield a pervasive transcendental content that would extend the transcendental sphere enormously. Husserl's footnote is a striking self-assessment: "The transcendental reduction of empathy has therefore not really been carried out." The condition for the extension is precise: empathic presentifications must be shown to posit their object as legitimately as recollections posit my past. That parallel, the memory-empathy analogy, is the bridge from egology to intersubjectivity, and Husserl admits it has here only been named.

(110) Finally every world object, even a mere material body, has as "object" the sense of something there for everyone, experienceable by everyone and confirmable by recourse to others' experience. Every object thus refers to the community of those living and experiencing together, and becomes a phenomenon with this sense in the transcendental attitude. So the world has a pervasive spiritual sense, which must itself receive transcendental significance if transcendental subjects are to be assigned to human beings, and so to human communal being and life, by a deeper reduction. Husserl's note: since the world has intersubjective sense for me, the world-phenomenon must lead back necessarily to the transcendental validity of others in the universe of monads. Even "mere" nature has a "spiritual" sense, the sense "for everyone," which is the trace of others in every object. The note draws the conclusion the main text leaves conditional: the world-phenomenon itself demands the reduction of empathy.

(110) The method of primordial reduction can also be staged by questioning the experiential world as phenomenon about all its sense of being that presupposes human subjects and their life. By showing that the world has a core of sense that is "pure experience," presupposing no experience of others, one reduces to transcendental primordiality. The final paragraph gives the method of the published Fifth Meditation as an alternative staging: subtract the intersubjective layers of sense, and the remainder is the primordial core. Both stagings, from the living present and from subtraction, arrive at the same stratum.

### No. 8. The Transcendental Problem of How Transcendental Others Are for Me (December 1930)

(111–112) If I consider as "spectator," even quite generally, how as a human being I stand in communication with others and what my empathic experience grasps of them and their consciousness of the world, and also uses, I notice that the use consists not only in my becoming certain that they have the world and take it as existing, but in their validity also being valid for me, so far as mutual agreement of objective opinions reaches, and in any case being "taken into account" under the titles of being, as concordance for us and everyone, and of illusion. Through the validity I grant others in my conscious life, my life and theirs communicate and come to a synthesis by which I become certain that we have one and the same world as existing, under mutually supporting validities and with mutually complementing contents. Without pursuing the mediations by which others are for me and function as others of others, something remarkable follows for the transcendental sphere. My transcendental life is the absolute life "purifying" itself into the transcendental through the epoché. My others are for me through empathy, which, purely taken, belongs to this life. The others experienced in it with a certain intentional mediacy are other ego-subjects, like me in the most general respect, subjects of perceiving, remembering, expecting, imagining, valuing, willing consciousness, with consciousness of the worldly appearing in modes like mine. Empathy does not only inform me that others have beliefs; it transfers their validities into mine. Co-validity (Mitgeltung) is the transcendental mechanism of communication: the world's being for me is partly the being-valid-for-me of others' validities. That raises the problem of No. 8: what happens to these co-validities under the epoché?

(112) If I lift my conscious life out of "impurity," "naivety" and restriction (restriction because the universal apperception in the world-presupposition does not become thematic and the psychic is considered only as soul in a pre-given body, while the universal pre-giving life remains undisclosed), it becomes my actual life as transcendental ego. And the other's being and life grasped in transcendentally taken empathy, what becomes of it? Does my "purification" not transfer to the other human beings of my world-phenomenon? As a human being reflecting universally I have myself and the co-existing others in the self-evidently pre-given world. But I know that this world has its particular sense of being for me, all its content of reality, not only from my experiences and knowledge but from my communication with others, who also have the same world only as theirs, for me as theirs, so that they, communicating with me in turn, owe me for the world's being and being-so. The reduction is a purification of my life from its self-apperception as worldly soul. The question is whether the purification, performed by me, also purifies the others. The world's content is reciprocally indebted: I owe the others for my world, they owe me for theirs.

(112–113) If I reduce myself transcendentally and world-being becomes phenomenon, do I not inhibit the world valid for me and so everything others contribute to my world through their life and validity? They contribute by my co-meaning the world in the determinations that have arisen from empathic experience of them and co-validity of their validities. The others have a say in the existence of my world, the world with all its content of reality in which it is this world for me, valid in its relative particularity. I must at times correct my opinions and even experiences through others; this can concern me myself, I can be wrong about my soul and body, and they can teach me. But about my being in the ultimate, transcendental sense they cannot teach me; it precedes the being of the world existing for me and also the being or non-being of the others I experience. Putting the world out of play cannot put me out of play. In that putting out of play lies the putting out of play of the determinate others and their modes of consciousness as contents valid for me for world-being; the epoché also puts out of play other egos as co-constituting my determinately contentful world. Husserl's note asks whether "putting the world out of play" means putting out of play everything I hold singly as worldly. Then the reduction would say: everything of the world I can inhibit, only not my opinions, experiences, acts, through which I have the world as determinate. My contentful validities are bracketed; but the world? It is only put in question universally as to content. Two levels of epoché are being separated. The epoché of particular contents brackets others as co-constituting the content of my world, and with them their transcendental contribution. But the world as such, the universal horizon, is only questioned as to content. The others can correct my worldly self-knowledge; my transcendental being is beyond their correction, which is the first, asymmetrical result.

(113) But if I perform genuine epoché, that of the universal world-apperception as ground of all particular themes, does it also mean putting out of play the others in general? If I recognize the others too as bearers of a world-apperception, of the world pre-given before all determinacy, and put their world-apperception, into which all their particular apperceptions are entered (as with me, since I apperceive them as my likes), out of validity for me, then I transform them into transcendental subjects: this epoché does not cancel their modes of consciousness and them, but lets them stand as the life in which the world is for them and for me. For me they are transcendental subjects of a transcendental life, only they have not become aware of themselves as such by the method of the reduction; that they can, I easily recognize in my sphere. Kern notes that Husserl struck the latter half of this passage in pencil. The unstruck question remains: how do things stand with the being-for-me of transcendental others, who at the necessary starting point in the natural attitude appear as my fellow human beings? How with the communicative conscious life opened by my empathy, with its mutuality and connection of validities? Can it be posited transcendentally, free of world-validity, recognized from me as the being of transcendental others existing for me? The genuine epoché, of the universal world-apperception, does not annihilate others but transforms them, as it transforms me, into transcendental subjects, that is, into the life in which the world is valid. Husserl's struck sentences said exactly this; his striking them shows he thought the transformation asserted rather than shown. The remaining question is whether others' transcendental being can be posited from me without the world-validity that first gave them to me as fellow human beings.

(114) The world for me is, roughly, the world meant in me (confirming too is meaning), but meant as meant through others, receiving sense of being also from them. As I as subject of meaning precede what is meant, the world I mean, so the others, as co-helpers in meaning the world meant by me, as co-bearers of the sense I have of the world, precede the being of this world. As I am transcendentally prior to everything worldly, my others must be transcendentally prior to the world in being. For me they are, from my empathy, meant by me and confirmed. So I am transcendentally first; I precede their being-for-me. Yet I must say that the being of others for me, presupposed for my being transcendental subject of a world consistently confirmed in my life, cannot be grounded in the being of the world, since the world's being for me requires the others. Thus a transcendental path must lead from my transcendental ego and my conscious being to the others as transcendental others and to the transcendental community, whose synthetic content makes the world conscious and confirmed as existing for me and then for my others. The argument is a transcendental regress: since the world's sense includes others as co-bearers, the others cannot be grounded in the world but must precede it, as I do. Yet they are given to me only through empathy, which I perform. The order is therefore: I, then the others as transcendental, then the world. The task is to find the path from the first to the second without passing through the third.

(114–115) The transcendental problem of how anything like an existing world comes to validity for me, through which achievements and capacities it gains its sense, leads to the transcendental problem of how transcendental others are for me, how they are for me as transcendental egos without the co-validity of the apperception through which they are for me in the world. If I am a subject finding myself as a human being in the world, apperceiving myself thus and, living in this apperception, able to experience "the" world and myself in it in concordant confirmation, then I am by essential necessity, if the world is to have intersubjective sense, a transcendental ego in a transcendental we-community disclosed from me, living in a community of transcendental conscious life that constitutes a common world for all. The title of No. 8 is stated in its precise form: how others can be valid for me as transcendental egos, independently of their apprehension as human beings in the world. The conditional conclusion, that I am necessarily a member of a transcendental "we," follows from the intersubjective sense of the world.

(115) This is an argument, not an actual demonstration that shows the essential connections and so produces real understanding. The task of transcendental philosophy is not to create convictions by argument, which would remain mere pre-convictions, but to bring the realm of the transcendental to knowledge systematically in systematic experience and its theory. Husserl's self-criticism is methodological and characteristic of his position: transcendental regress arguments of the Kantian kind establish only "pre-convictions." Phenomenology must exhibit (aufweisen) the connections in transcendental experience. The previous paragraphs are thus demoted to a statement of the problem.

(115–116) What path is prefigured? Transcendentally, in the epoché, I have nothing but myself as conscious existence, nearest my conscious life as stream of passive and active consciousness, and what receives validity and confirmation in it in the transcendental attitude, a validity untouched by the epoché. I have a being prior to the others; if others are to be transcendentally accessible from me, I precede with my own being, and accessibility would mean that my own consciousness performs as other a transcendent positing, exceeding my being, a meaning beyond myself that can yet come to confirmation, transcendentally. What exists must ultimately confirm itself through experience. So there must be an experience transcending me, of the other, and that can only be transcendentally reduced empathy. As my experience it belongs to me; how it achieves transcendence will occupy us much. First, if I contrast my own and the other's transcendental life and already envisage that the other comes to my experience as existing in me, but as not-I, the question is how transcendental self-experience, as experience of what is my own, is to be characterized over against experience of others and of the transcendent in general. Since all experiences of the latter kind are first of all my experiences and belong to the domain of my own, I try to mark out the circle of ownness and the corresponding self-experience. Self-experience is in a certain sense immediate relative to all experience of the other, since the other is experienced in my experiencing, which is in me as prior in itself. But what kind of immediacy is that? Within my ownness too there are differences of immediacy and mediacy. The path is fixed: transcendental empathy, confirmed in experience, is the only access, and the theory of ownness must come first. The closing question about kinds of immediacy within ownness is answered in No. 9 by the distinction between presentation and appresentation, and in Beilage L by the gradation of originality.

### Appendix V. Psychological and Transcendental Empathy (Early 1930s)

(116) First, psychological empathy: understanding a body in my surrounding world of a familiar type as an animal body, a ray, a human being. Seeing the human being before me, I can consider his empirical bodily nature for itself and grasp it as "expression," apperceptive indication, "appresentation" of the human person with her personal life. This appresentation, the experience "here is a body of the familiar type, and in it the co-existence of a human 'soul' is indicated with certainty," remains within the general pre-givenness of the world. Psychological empathy is an apperception of a worldly body as ensouled, presupposing the world. The ray, an odd example, shows that the type can be animal and quite unfamiliar in behaviour.

(116) Second, transcendental "empathy," which from primordially constituted corporeality and nature achieves the "appresentation" of the other's transcendental primordiality, is a structure of constitution through which the pre-givenness of the world and personal existence in it, and personal acts of psychological empathy, first become possible. The psychological apperception, in which a soul is conscious as soul of a body, is quite another appresentation than that by which a primordial body pre-given in my primordiality becomes the turning point for the "appresentation" of another primordiality, another ego and ego-life, through which we constitute a common nature, a common world and ourselves in it and as we. The distinction removes a pervasive confusion: transcendental empathy is not psychological empathy performed in the reduced attitude. It is a constitutive structure that makes the world, and so psychological empathy, possible. Its object is not another soul in the world but another primordiality, another constituting sphere, and its achievement is the common world. The term "turning point" (Umschlagspunkt) for the other's body states its function exactly: the place where my primordiality turns into the givenness of another.

### No. 9. Primordial Reduction (Abstraction) to My World of Experience, First of All to My Perceptual World. Presentation and Appresentation. Toward a Better Clarification of the Concept of Primordiality (Probably December 1930)

(117) I as having the pre-given world in natural validity. In the epoché: I as putting out of play the natural performance of validity through which the world is for me the ground of being on which I find myself and live as a human being, the premise of all judgments, the prejudgment they all presuppose. The world is the prejudgment (Vorurteil) of all judgments, and the epoché suspends this function, not a particular belief.

(117) My interest in the epoché: my own being as constantly living the world as existing and living in it as human being. The world's being is outside the theme; correlatively this pre-givenness and life in the pre-given world as phenomenon is the same as before, yet the performance of validities is modified by abstention. I do not give up the world's existence but make no use of it; I make my world-consciousness and the nexus of its validity my theme: "I am" and my life, in which the world becomes valid and existing for me, and I am posited as existing without the world being presupposed. I now investigate how my having-the-world-in-validity, my world-life, looks, and how in the phenomenon abiding validity arises for me from my own sources, how step by step abiding validity founds new validity, what exists for me on what already exists for me, until I have fully reached and made intelligible my having of the world. The epoché leaves the world as it was, only unused. Its theme is the stepwise founding of validities, a genetic-static "archaeology" of world-possession, the term later texts use.

(118) I can proceed in two ways. I keep exclusively to describing my transcendental being and life, its experiences, confirmations, evidences of various levels, modalizations, cancellations, corrections; or, as a subsequent second step, I co-perform the validities step by step and so have their objects step by step as abiding knowledge. The second way is not a return to naivety: co-performing validities within the epoché lets the phenomenologist possess the world's content as theme without positing it naively.

(118) Then I shall extend my theme and again come to posit the world as existing. The re-positing of the world is the end of the reduction, not its abandonment: the world is regained as understood.

(118) This happens in various ways. I ask first what the general style of my whole world-life is and how it persists as long as I am a world-meaning, world-experiencing, world-living ego. My exact memories of the world do not matter; I may vary them freely and imagine things otherwise. If only the world's identity as world in general remains, and I as conscious of it, this yields a style of consciousness and validity, an essential structure of the world as world in general (ontologically) and correlatively the style of my world-life. The eidetic structure of the life-world is reached by free variation of my world-memories, keeping only the form "world." The correlation of ontological style and style of world-life is the program of the Krisis's life-world ontology (§ 51).

(118) I extend this theme, putting into validity what offers itself in my constitution of the world as non-ego and finally as world. Now I can let myself be carried by the general structure of validity already exercised, belonging to the style of having a world; then I come to understand how, by this style, the unity of a pre-given existing world constantly comes about for me, so that this world simply exists for me and I am in it as a human being. Understanding the pre-givenness of the world means understanding its style, which carries every particular experience; the regained world is the same world, now intelligible.

(118) Consider the experiences belonging to world-experience, which by their outer horizons are themselves modes of world-experience, and especially perceptions: all are naturally appresenting, but there are differences in appresentation as to possible confirmation by presentation (physical things, perception of others). Every perception appresents; the difference between things and others lies in whether appresentation can be redeemed by presentation. This is the thread No. 9 follows.

(119) But can one begin so simply? Transcendental reflection begins with the epoché regarding the world valid for me, the world in its all-temporality, valid as present in my present, as past in my past. Through my whole surveyable transcendental life, in my periods of wakefulness, the world is conscious to me as experienced, perceived, remembered, to be perceived. But I also have world-related certainties beyond this experiential certainty: much knowledge of the world that I owe to others' experience and, by taking over, to their knowledge; much from intersubjective science co-determines the world for me. Reflecting, I see that there are foundings of validity here: the others, determinate ones or an indeterminate manifold as in tradition or science, must already be valid for me as existing for me to be spiritually connected with them and take over from them. Husserl identifies a layer of the world's validity that rests on others' testimony, tradition and science, and notes that it is founded on the prior validity of the others themselves.

(119) I consider further that what I attribute to the world in non-experiencing, logical thought presupposes experience in its grounding, and only what can be grounded in being belongs to the world. I will not presuppose even this as a prejudgment, but it may motivate me to reduce the world existing for me first to the world purely as world of experience, my experience, in which others are experienced like other worldly things. The reduction to the world of experience is motivated, not presupposed: the founding of thought on experience is a hypothesis to be confirmed.

(119–120) If I take into account others' experiences as co-valid, I can direct the question to the world experienced in general intersubjective experience, how it is to be described purely as world of experience founding everything that is valid for me of the world. But if I start so, I come back to saying: the world meant by me as one and the same throughout my transcendental life is, at a founding level, universal intersubjective world of experience; but this level is itself founded on what of it falls within my own universal experience. That leads back to my meant world as my world of experience, of my perceptions, present, past and future. I must perform a reductive epoché, abstractively reducing the world concretely valid for me, the one world of my whole surveyable life, to my perceptual world, to what I have actually perceived of the one world that persists through all change of experiences and opinions. The intersubjective world of experience is itself founded on my own perceptual world. Hence the "perceptual world" is the narrower basis. This is a further reduction within the reduction, analogous to Ideas II's reduction to the solipsistic thing.

(120) I shall have previously explicated my transcendental self-being in a first fundamental way, as is necessary. In the epoché I am subject for the world but also for myself as conscious subject for the world, and I investigate myself first so far as to leave the meant world aside and explicate myself in my most general structure: as identical ego of a conscious life in positings that partly, in a universal closure, posit the world, and as transcendental ego also transcendental-immanent positings; as finding my immanent life temporal, or rather self-temporalizing; as being in the form of a streaming living present in which past, past present, temporalizes itself through present memories. I am for myself originally as self-perceiving, self-presenting; I can come to know myself actively because I already am passively in original self-presence and, affected from there, can look at myself and grasp my original properties. Active self-knowledge rests on passive self-presence: the ego can reflect only because it already "affects itself" in its passive self-presence. This is the doctrine of the late time-manuscripts (pre-reflective self-awareness of the living present), which Michel Henry would radicalize.

(120–121) In passing to "transcendent" worldly experiences, worldly temporalization must be contrasted with transcendental-immanent self-temporalization: the worldly present is given perceptually in the immanent present, the worldly present perceived in the outer perception that exists immanently in the present. Two temporalizations are stacked: the immanent self-temporalization of the stream and the temporalization of world-time within it.

(121) I reduce the valid world to what of it "falls within my perception," and if this world belongs to my whole surveyable life in its temporal modes, being in every immanent phase a perceptual world (perceived, now perceived, to be perceived) and so as a whole a perceptual world, to be taken in abstracto purely by its perceptual contents, method still demands that I question some immanent present, the current living present, about what it offers as actual perceptual world in abstracto. That would be a further, narrower dismantling (Abbau). But we look to the essential, which is the invariant form for all past and future perceptual world-presents. The reduction narrows twice: to my perceptual world over my life, then to the perceptual world of one living present. The second narrowing yields an essential form valid for every present, so nothing of generality is lost.

(121) My world-present, and every conceivable one, is meant in my immanent present as present of its past, having become from it, and of a future that will become from it. Within my immanent present I have a double "horizon" of positional co-meaning, not only of my transcendental immanence as past and future, by which these are constantly co-existing with my perceptual present. The horizon is generally "dark," undisclosed; but at times recollections and anticipations arise, partly passively, partly in the conscious "I can" and "I do," the latter as previews or pre-imaginings. The same holds for the world-present in its mode of givenness as existing, which has worldly past and future as co-existing in its positing. Temporal horizon has two dimensions, immanent and worldly, both "dark," both disclosable by the "I can." Past and future are co-posited, not merely co-intended: they are part of the positional character of the present.

(122) A differently directed horizon-positing crosses this. Every world-present has a horizon of co-presence. Taking the world-present purely perceptually, I find a perceptual field in the special sense of a manifold of single realities, each appearing perceptually, in original self-appearance. In the streaming immanent present this manifold does not stay identical: perceived things leave perception, new ones "enter." But what has left is still co-valid, still co-present, not merely past; and what has not yet entered is valid in advance, as entering perception in the immanent future. The specific perceptual field is no independent sense of being in the perceptual present; what is in validity singly and as group is a domain of originally appearing things carrying inseparably a horizon of co-present reality. I have a total perception of my full perceptual world-present, and within it a specific perceptual field of realities actually originally perceived and beyond it an open, indeterminate domain of co-perceived, perceptually co-present realities not actualized in self-giving. The horizon of co-presence, spatial and simultaneous, crosses the temporal horizon: what I turned away from is not past but still there. Perception is "total perception" of a world-present, of which the perceptual field in the narrow sense is only the self-given core.

(122–123) To characterize this further: to every world-present given through perceptual field and horizon of co-presence my lived body inevitably belongs, constantly as "centre" of the perceptual field, always properly perceived and in peculiar "function." The "perceptual field" means not only the realities I am actively occupied with but those present to me in original self-appearance, so that I can look at them at will. The body belongs to every world-present twice, as perceived and as functioning centre; the perceptual field is defined by availability to attention, not by actual attention.

(123) Note this "I can" and "I do," essential here, especially as part of the sense of being of the body belonging to every world-present. The body is distinguished in that in it, in the form "I exercise kinaestheses" and so "I move," I can put perceptual doing into play, touching, seeing, moving organs or my whole body, walking. Through this my explicating and extending perceiving takes place, an ever new perceptual field given originally, in such a way that again and again something of the co-present horizon comes to proper self-presence and proper acquaintance. The body is the organ of the "I can" by which co-presence is converted into presence. Kinaesthesis is not a sensation among others but the practical condition of the perceptual field's openness.

(123–124) Here we notice a new horizon. Every properly perceived reality is perceived only "one-sidedly"; through changing bodily perceptual behaviour, or correlatively through the object's own motion, new sides enter perception. To observe a real thing is to pass actively from perceived to unperceived through bodily doing. The perceived reality is meant as more than the side perceived; the side is meant from the start as side, co-positing co-present sides. Perception of a reality consists of what is properly perceived and what is meant as co-present of the same reality, a domain of perceivability, what I can or could perceive by further bodily activity. Conversely, what has come to knowledge generally leaves proper perception in its course but remains in the circle of the determinate "I can," of perceivability and abiding knowledge, the abiding acquisition of perceiving. The inner horizon is a domain of perceivability through my own bodily capacity, and what I have seen remains as "known," an acquisition of the "I can." Husserl defines the horizon practically, as a system of abilities, which is the key to the contrast with the other's psyche: no ability of mine can make it present.

(124) The same holds of the "outer horizon," as opposed to the "inner horizon" of the single reality: a horizon of potential perceiving leading to proper self-grasp, a realm of accessibility as accessibility of the co-posited, though not determinately co-posited singly. The outer horizon of the world is also a realm of my possible access, only less determinate. Inner and outer horizon (Experience and Judgment § 8) are both defined by accessibility.

(124–125) After this rough description of the immanent mode of givenness of the worldly present, our distinction between presentation and appresentation is clear, to be understood first as relative and finally as absolute. Every presentation is proper presentation only under co-presentation, appresentation. The total presentation, the consciousness of the world-present, properly presents the perceptual field, but only as co-presenting an open horizon of potential fields; a single reality of the field then counts as properly presented, yet the same distinction recurs in it: it is properly presented as to the side actually seen, and that only under appresentation, since a side has sense only through the co-being of other sides. (The relativity goes further: visually the far thing has appresented in it its near aspects; but this "inner horizon" concerns a mediacy within self-giving perception, not the worldly present, since the far thing is not a reality of the world-present but only an appearance of the present reality.) Presentation and appresentation are relative terms, nested at every level: field within world, thing within field, side within thing. Their "absolute" sense, to be reached, will be the difference between what can and what in principle cannot be turned into presentation. The parenthesis distinguishes appresentation of appearances (far and near aspects) from appresentation of realities, an important precision for Beilage XXXVIII.

(125) To the domain of presence, presented or appresented, belong various realities, among them our fellow human beings. As we know, they are co-existent, actual or possible co-experiencers, co-determining the sense of being of our present world as valid for us. We orient ourselves by one another, and that belongs to the perceptual present as mine. Others belong to my perceptual present not only as objects but as co-experiencers, and their co-experiencing shapes the sense of my present world, the "orienting ourselves by one another" being itself perceived.

(125) The primordial reduction of the world to my world of experience, and of the present world to my perceptual present world, means that I put into validity only my own proper presentations and those appresentations that I could realize as my own presentations. The definition is now exact: primordial means presentable by me. What is appresented but cannot become my presentation is excluded.

(125) The first reduction: to my world of direct experience, to what of the world is, was, will be, and could be perceptually mine. The first step is temporal and modal: my actual and possible perceptions across time.

(125) The second reduction: dismantling the appresentations that could not become my proper presentations. This yields the primordial "world." Other human beings are perceptually realities, they belong to the perceptual world, but not to the primordial sphere. The distinction answers a confusion common in readings of the Fifth Meditation: the perceptual world includes others, perceived as such; the primordial world is the perceptual world minus the unredeemable appresentations. Others are "perceived," so the perceptual reduction alone does not reach primordiality.

(125) Now we determine the concept of primordial transcendental subjectivity, gathering together the following. The sentence announces the summary in the next paragraphs.

(125) In the epoché I as ego and in my world-conscious life am absolutely given to myself, my present in wakefulness; as living this immanent present I am apodictically given and have apodictically, unmodalizably, my immanent temporal horizon, however indeterminate and ambiguous its filling and however doubtful as to determinate retro- and pro-certainties. The certainty of experience I then have contains apodictic certainty of being; only the being-so is not apodictically certain in its determinacy. To this sphere apodictically my own belongs also the world as valid for me, as such; this validity is bracketed. The apodictic includes the temporal horizon as form, not as content, and the world-phenomenon as meant. Husserl's distinction between apodictic being and non-apodictic being-so recurs from No. 3.

(126) Within my own ego I can first show a founding of validity: my immanent being-present founds my being past and future; more exactly, the streaming living self-presence is a streaming founding, the now founding, roughly, the just-past and just-coming in streaming temporalization. The concrete present field includes present, just-past in a continuum, and coming. In the streaming every just-past, having issued from the now (and each later just-past from the earlier), is founded in the being of a now, as a primal genesis of becoming-being from what already is. The concrete present founds as concretely lapsing its concrete just-past in a streaming, continuously mediated founding; finally the whole horizon of past and future of each living present is a not-yet-explicated unity of validity from continuous founding, itself changing its sense in the streaming. Founding of validity begins within the living present: the now founds the retentional. Husserl calls this "primal genesis" (Urgenesis), the becoming of being from being, the formula the C-manuscripts develop as the "primal stream."

(126) We can further say: the being of the concrete streaming present, as conscious in the mode of originality, presentation, precedes what is conscious in it retentionally and protentionally, in the manner of founding. The consciousness of the just-past exists now and is conscious as perceived, while what is conscious in it is a just-past, a not-now. The validity my past and future have for me is an achievement in my present. My present being bears my past and future being; cancelling the present would cancel all past and future. Yet the past lies included in the present as necessary validity constituted in it. The asymmetry is strict: the present bears past and future, but includes them as its own necessary validities. This prepares the analogy: my primordial present bears others' presence as a founded validity.

(126–127) I am transcendentally the ego that, in its conscious life, is conscious of itself in this temporalization and founding as having a world in validity. More precisely, in an original temporalization the world "appears" and is valid in my self-present as present, in my self-past as past; in my concrete living present my immanent temporal being and worldly (spatio-temporal) being are valid together and interwoven. The validity of the world is founded in my being, and we ask about the further levels. Clearly the world-present valid for me, valid in my immanent present, founds world-past and world-future for me. The founding of world-time on the world-present parallels the founding of immanent time on the immanent present.

(127) Further, what delimits itself as perceptual world in the present, and in every past and future present, precedes in validity what is valid of the world non-perceptually, and the primordially reduced world, the abstractively reduced, has an in-itself earlier validity than the rest. So I advance to what is in itself earlier of the world, within the constantly full world as it is always valid for me as existing. The primordial world is "in itself earlier" in validity, but reached by regress within the full world, which remains constantly given.

(127) I now consider more closely my primordial portion of the world, as it gains its sense of being in my transcendental immanence, my world-consciousness. Husserl's note: I describe the "modes of appearance" of the same and the synthesis of concordance in which it has the character of the same continuing to be valid and confirming itself.

(127) What does a presentation that gives a worldly reality itself look like, which foundings of being lie in it, when I hold it primordially reduced? I come to sensation-data as adumbrating, in the apprehension of which I immanently have an appearance of spatial being; to kinaestheses and kinaesthetic motivation. In this abstraction from the sphere of achievements founded in the validity at issue, which first yield the objective world, I come to the constitution of spatio-temporal nature and of the uniquely distinguished body, apprehended in quite another way and constantly valid as my lived body. The primordial analysis descends to the classic constitutive layers: hyle, apprehension, kinaesthesis, primordial nature, own body, the Ding und Raum program of 1907 (Hua XVI) now re-inscribed in the primordial sphere.

(128) I consider what unities come to self-givenness thereby and how they stand to me, the transcendental foundation of their constitution. I show that they transcend my conscious life and my being as, so to speak, immanent person, but are analogous to the hyletic unities, which over against ego, abilities, acts and consciousness are ego-alien and yet concretely inseparable from the ego as occurring in conscious life and belonging to its full concretion. At a higher level the concretion of the ego, which constantly has a world in concordant validity, and its apodictic being-for-itself include concordance in the primordial layer of world-validity regarding primordial appearances and a potentiality of the "I can," an ability to produce concordant presenting appearances of the same with respect to the horizons of co-presence. To each perceptual object belongs concordant actual and possible experience, a system of possible experience as a potentiality of the ego presupposed to exist. In the concordance of the "manifold," which wholly belongs to the ego's own being, the synthetic unity is intentional object in the mode of "itself," constantly confirming itself, in a sense immanent, inseparable from it, though as unity-point of the confirming synthesis "ideal," irreal. It is immanent to the concrete ego in a well-founded new sense, as a potentiality of synthesis inseparable from its concreteness, the ability to experience the same again as itself, the sources of its being lying exclusively in the sphere of apodictic being-for-itself. The primordial thing is "ego-alien" yet inseparable from the ego, like the hyle; this is the second sense of immanence that No. 4 distinguished. The analogy between primordial things and hyle is exact: both are non-egoic yet belong to the ego's concretion. The primordial thing is an "irreal" pole of syntheses grounded entirely in the ego's own capacities.

(128–129) In forming the concept "primordial transcendental subjectivity," this subjectivity is taken concretely. It is I myself, having entered the epoché as human being and having become aware of my absolute essence in performing it. Transcendental subjectivity is thereby fixed as world-constituting for me, as having in validity the world I speak of in the natural attitude. My concrete transcendental ego includes the conscious life in which the world exists for me, is experienced and perceivable, but not the world itself. The world is valid in my transcendental subjectivity but distinguished from it as the other, the transcendent over against the transcendental, if one takes the word purely in this contrast within the transcendental ego's field of knowledge. Of this other, bracketed in its being, nothing falls into the own being of my transcendental ego; so reducing the world abstractively to my perceptual world does not ascribe any piece of the world itself to my own being. Husserl corrects the impression that primordiality is a piece of the world annexed to the ego. The ego includes the life in which the world is valid, not the world. The heading that follows signals a supplement to clarify the point.

#### Supplement on the Clarification of the Concept of Primordiality

(129) The matter is not different at first if I retreat to the primordially reduced. If I leave the further world-validity untouched, it is what I get to see of the world itself, this green lampshade as I really see it, green, in the form of a spherical shell, or as I feel it smooth. The naive reading of the primordial is the "seen aspect" of real things, a piece of the world as it shows itself to me.

(129) But one must be careful and avoid a misunderstanding of our concept of the primordial. It does not comprise what I really perceive of the thing, which as perceived is always valid as belonging to the thing, its colour, form, and the unperceived co-meant perceivable properties. Rather the primordial reduction is not an "abstraction," a restriction of the describing gaze to what is actually perceived of the thing and of the rest valid as world, but a real epoché regarding co-validities under the aspect of the analysis of foundings of validity, which are eo ipso foundings of worldly being-for-me in preceding beings-for-me, beings for me from my validity. If I abstain from performing the relevant appresentations, including all horizon-validities founded in them, I come to primordial transcendence as, so to speak, a world for itself, prior to the objective world, and not a layer of the objective world itself. As soon as the founded validity is restored, the primordial layer becomes self-presentation of the worldly, taking on fuller sense in the higher apprehension and validity. Likewise the hyle, which emerges in reducing the primordial transcendent to the immanence of consciousness, is no longer seen as hyle once apprehended and valid as adumbration; through it objective colour is seen. This is one of the most important clarifications in the volume. The primordial reduction is not an abstraction of the perceived aspect of worldly things, which would still be the objective thing's colour; it is an epoché of the appresentations of others and their founded horizons, yielding a "world for itself" that is not a layer of the objective world. The objective thing's colour is the colour-for-everyone; the primordial green is a different object, which becomes self-presentation of the objective once the founded validity is restored, as the hyle becomes adumbration of an objective colour once apprehended. The analogy reverses the usual picture: the primordial world relates to the objective world as hyle relates to the perceived thing. Husserl here corrects his own vocabulary of "abstraction" in Hua I § 44.

(130) The kind of "apprehension" is different here, however. The appresentation of others and their primordial spheres founds the co-valid synthesis of my and the others' primordialities and mediately the universal synthesis of my primordial thing-appearances with theirs, by which we gain an intersubjective manifold for every reality, possible only through synthesis of primordialities. My primordial sphere gets its companions, and each is transcendentally immanent in the second sense; every transcendental subjectivity I have as transcendent-immanent. The analogy with hyle has limits: the objective thing is constituted not by apprehending primordial appearances alone but by synthesizing several primordialities. Every primordiality is "transcendent-immanent" to me, the formula Husserl later develops as the intentional "in-one-another" (Ineinander) of monads.

(130–131) How then stands the intersubjective world as a world of realities in intersubjective space-time, as against the first, primordial space-time? Of universal nature as basic layer of the real world one can say that it is immanent to the community of transcendental subjects (which become valid in me as transcendental egos), as immanent transcendence, through the community of their transcendental life and functioning capacities. But what of human beings and their psychic, psychic-personal being? One must be very careful, also in speaking of the immanence of nature. The primordial spheres, the monads, do not lie side by side and merely connected. First, in my primordiality I have "lived body" and "nature," and understanding the other's body I already have an ego expressing itself in it and, in the unity of expression and expressed, a human being, appearing in my primordial space in his bodily corporeality, bringing his primordial space and spatial surroundings with him, and both coincide. Then he is understood as experiencing me; my body as appearing to him becomes expression of my primordial being, first of my ego as ego of its (at first primordially immanent) surrounding world. Only thereby is genuine being-human-for-others, being-centre-of-a-surrounding-world, constituted, where the surrounding world is the world in subjective givenness and the ego belongs reciprocally to the surrounding world and to the world itself. Must this not be considered also for common nature, which for each is environmental nature? Nature is immanent to the transcendental community as I's primordial nature is immanent to me. The warning that monads "do not lie side by side" rejects any picture of separate spheres linked externally; their interpenetration begins with the coincidence of his space and mine. My humanity, my being a centre for others, is constituted through my being understood by the other, the reciprocal self-objectivation of No. 1 § 10. The closing question implies that even common nature is never purely objective but always environmental for each.

### Appendix VI. The Phenomenological Epoché. What Belongs to Myself and the Transcendent (Probably December 1930)

(131–132) The way of introducing the phenomenological epoché. I exclude the field of judgment "existing world" as basic field. What remains? I, who put this world into validity, the subject of its appearances, its meaning as being, its confirmation. And what can I posit? World-experience implies levels of experience, of originally founding and founded positings of being; the positing of the world as founded in this sequence, or the world as habitually pre-given in it and posited anew in the same style. The world as phenomenon is the world founded thus, existing for me, its sense constituting itself in this sequence. I as theme, subject of this founding and final validity; I in my life, in my consciousness of the existing world, the existing world as non-ego; I and all consciousness, all having in validity, as inseparable from me, as that in which I am, I as having the world. I, my conscious life and what is conscious in it as such. But what is conscious is what is valid for me as such, if what is valid for me exists. What belongs to me myself: what is completely experienceable, so that what the sense of experience contains horizonally is perceivable by myself, belongs to me, including ideas, ideal unities over against my experiences constituted in them on all sides. Transcendent is what is appresented as not experienceable in its own self. In compressed notes Husserl gives a new definition of ownness: completely experienceable by me, all horizons perceivable by me. This makes even ideal objects (ideas) my own, and it defines the transcendent strictly as the appresented-as-unexperienceable-in-itself, the other's psyche and what depends on it. Transcendence in the full sense begins with the other.

(132) Two things: first, the world as valid for me, with the mediating or founding validities included; not what is meant as world in a single act, but in the whole of life as a unity and as actually existing, confirmed and to be confirmed from my ability to disclose horizons and direct experiences. Of course I can abstain from this world and its validities and consider my subjective life of validity. But that is not the "epoché," which only refuses to have any ground in the world. The distinction separates abstaining from the world's validities (a reflective turn to subjective life) from the epoché in the strict sense, which is the refusal of the world as ground. The former can be performed piecemeal and leaves the world as ground; the latter changes the attitude as a whole.

### No. 10. The World of the Normal and the Problem of the Participation of the Abnormal in the Constitution of the World (10 January 1931)

(133) The issue is not only understanding others but understanding the common surrounding world and the human community for which it is its spiritual-practical world, into which they live and which they have shaped in its spiritual form. The theme widens from empathy to the correlation of community and surrounding world. Understanding a community means understanding the world it has shaped.

(133) I who disclose this already have a "full world," which I must finally reach and understand fully in its constitution, but in levels of validity, levels of the structure "human being" and "humanity," correlatively levels of possible relative surrounding worlds. The family's home, home town and country are relatively separable unities that I can think independent by "dismantling" (Abbau). Historical-anthropological examples ease or exemplify the dismantling in my re-understanding. The method of Abbau, dismantling the full world into relative layers, is guided by historical and ethnographic examples: the closed homeworld of an archaic community gives an empirical model of a layer of my own world. Husserl had read Lévy-Bruhl's La mythologie primitive in 1935 (letter of 11 March 1935); here, earlier, ethnography is already a methodological aid.

(133) It is a problem what "role" the various types of psychic beings play in the constitution of the pre-given world. But first, what exactly is the problem? The question of the abnormal's contribution is posed, then immediately subjected to clarification.

(133–134) I, reflecting on the absolute ground of the reduction, have "the" world pre-given. How is the finished achievement of my conscious life, which carries the world's sense as meant and continuing valid, to be clarified? How is the intentionality of pre-givenness built, and in it the identity of the world continuing valid and yet further determined? It is already built; I live as waking ego in actual world-possession, but I can question it about its "origin"; as intentionally founded, containing actual and potential implications, I can explicate it; from it as living genesis I can lay out the genesis running and run, and so understand this living pre-given world in its living all-temporality, the sense of its being and being-so as achievement of my consciousness of it. Pre-givenness is a sedimented achievement that can be questioned back as to its origin. Husserl speaks of "living genesis," the static structure of the pre-given world being itself the present form of a genesis.

(134–135) In my method of primordial reduction I come correlatively to the primordial ego and build further: I put an empathy into validity (in first originality, perception of the other), a primordial empathy one might say, and gain "primordial others." I keep to experience, first to "perception of others," following what in the pre-given world is first founded from my primordiality as perception of others. I could have assumed several others at once, but I would have to bring each singly to progressive confirming experience, to see what each contributes alone and how their togetherness contributes to my pre-given world, subjectively for me, as I had to form the sense of this other from my primordiality. One perceived other determines a layer of validity of the pre-given world, an already intersubjective worldly layer, constituted purely from me and from him as existing for me exclusively from this motivation. The next I consider is already the third, who, with the sense I give him as an ego that already shares a layer of world with the second, helps develop this world of the first "we two" into a world for us three, and so on. The sense of being of the others changes, and my own sense as ego among my likes. Once I have the other in the world-field, which abstractly is a world for us two, I also have him as valuing and practical co-subject, and as object of my cares and activities; so new habitual layers arise, and the world to which I belong gets new layers, among them my "significances" for him and his for me. Husserl reconstructs the intersubjective world as a series: me, the second, the third, each adding a layer and reshaping the others' sense. The third person matters because he meets an already shared "we two"; with him the world becomes a world of a plurality. The account is explicitly abstract, a validity-genealogy, not a history.

(135) We have let all this happen in the mere perceptual field. A further question is how full temporalization is clarified, the constitution of human beings as persisting realities through time, not only as bodies but as persisting psychic subjects, and with it the further building of the existing world for and through all its subjects. Nor must we forget the constitution of the persisting togetherness of human beings, of open communal humanity and its articulations, through which a unity of a lasting practical surrounding world, a life-world, first arises. "Life-world" (Lebenswelt) is defined here as the lasting practical surrounding world correlative to a persisting human community. The perceptual-field analysis is only its abstract base.

(135) A presupposition I have unwittingly made in this founding sequence must now be emphasized. I take the others in "normality," and must consider what that means, and what abstraction from the abnormal co-subjects, and from abnormal stretches of normal subjects' lives, both belonging to the pre-given world, means: how these are co-constituted and whether by their psychic life (transcendentally turned) they participate in constituting the pre-given world. The question of the title appears: do the abnormal contribute constitutively, or are they only constituted?

(135–136) To come to "normality," consider first: as transcendental ego experiencing the world (though bracketing it as ground), I have my perceptually appearing others through "empathy." I, with my whole habitual structure and the world already valid for me as intersubjective, function, by my being in the form of being-for-myself and being-in-connection-with-others, as primally instituting for the apperception of the other as my like. My surrounding world belongs to me as conscious to me, as common world of fellow humans, as field of my habitual and actual interests, my and our instinctive needs and satisfactions, other needs, cares and efforts. Since I have always been in connection with others as far back as I remember, my whole life of interests relates to a surrounding world that contains others and has formed itself in intercourse with them (others being sometimes objects of my practical world). Every newcomer is apperceived after my image, and he is called normal if the general horizon-prefiguration he must have for me as my like agrees with me in general essential style, confirming itself concretely in further experience (concretion including the whole style of being in which I have form and the correlative style of my world). If further experience runs so that closer and even different determination occurs within the concrete prefigured form, the other is normal. Normality is defined from the empathizer: the other is normal if the prefiguration "my like" is confirmed in style. Abnormality is thus disappointment of the prefiguration at the level of style, not of detail. Instincts appear among the elements of the surrounding world, a sign of the drive theory of the 1930s.

(136–137) Going back to constitutive origin, the same holds analogously for every level of founding. If I abstractively isolate my primordial being and world, I shut off higher motivations and consider what would be motivated from there as "others" and what enters as an intentional lower layer, a layer of validity, into the concretion of my ego, my world and my human others. I first get an abstractly reduced, so to speak first in itself, other, to whom my reduced structure of being and world-life transfers, varied in correlative perspectives, and who is normal if empathic perception really keeps to the relatively concrete style that would be called concrete in this reduction. My primordial being only now becomes an ego that has another ego, enters possible social connection with him, becomes a personal ego with its personal "thou," an ego for itself whose "thou" I become or can become. Normality is level-relative: at each level of the founding, the other is normal relative to that level's style. The "I-thou" relation, and with it personal being, arises only with the second ego.

(137) If we thus construct a history as genesis of humanity as humanity for me and of its world, in the succession of others entering my first primordial sphere, we have historicized the stepwise structure of motivational causality, and for each new level we co-define a higher normality and correlatively a constituted surrounding world as normal world of experience, related to the normal of the higher and universal level. Husserl's note asks for more precision regarding the spatio-temporalization of others as persisting, progressively understandable as my likes, and of the identical world as equally understandable for all of them, so that what grows from each one's living into the world is understandable for everyone and the common world is homogeneous in its relativity to this totality of subjects. A further moment to be treated is the constitution of a persisting "we," whose members are reachable for one another and keep within a common, actually experienceable sphere that becomes their persisting life-world, including themselves as objects: the constitutive problem of the concrete personal surrounding world as a finite field of persons' interests. The "history" is a methodological fiction (Husserl says "construct"): it turns the static order of founding into a narrative. The note sets the next tasks: homogeneity of the common world (everything understandable by every member) and the persisting "we" as finite field of interests.

(137) It is not said that every newly considered ego yields a new world, that is, changes the formal essential structure of the world, which prefigures the horizon-sense of the pre-given world and every possible one so that all actual experience, however it extends knowledge, brings only the world already familiar in general sense into explicit acquaintance. New others enrich the world's content but not its formal structure. The formal structure is fixed with the first constitution of a common world; later others fill it.

(137–138) But the structural levels must be distinguished. Through empathy in general, and quasi-genetically already through the first empathy, a common world is constituted. A further thing is being-in-connection-with-others, living constantly together (and against one another, which is only a form of togetherness) into the same world, caring for it, so that it becomes a world of interests, our practical life-world, with the "we," I and the others, belonging permanently to the constituted world of objects while being the subject of the world, the "we" experiencing, knowing and acting in the world, with results that enrich its objective content. Husserl's note: permanently being there for one another as a connected "we," living in the same near world as practical field with interwoven interests, with and against one another; the constitution of a practical life-world and its "we" is a special matter. Two levels: common world (from empathy alone) and life-world (from lasting connection, Konnex). The second requires interwoven interests. "Against one another" as a form of togetherness is a precise sociological point: conflict presupposes a common practical field.

(138) Something essentially new arises with the extension from two to many and to an openly endless plurality anticipated in the open "infinite" world: communalization, which builds the surrounding world, extends from directly experienced others through others' others, iteratively, and this mediacy conditions the mediacy of its "results," the connected communities and their communal achievements. The mediacy of community (others of others) yields mediate institutions, which do not depend on direct acquaintance. The analysis of indirect sociality anticipates the Krisis's "community of horizon."

(138) Again essentially new, though constantly interwoven, is the constitution of the openly endless generation, and with it the constitution of birth and death, not as occasional contingencies but as general structural features of all psychic living beings: to be born and to die belongs to human beings as such. Husserl's note: the constitution of a persisting fellow humanity correlative to a practical surrounding world can be treated abstractly before generation, and so, through the temporalization of this practical world as personally significant, an abstract historicity already lies in it; when generation enters, the progress in concreteness is also a concretion of the abiding human relations, mother or parents and child, and we have a more concrete, generatively formed temporalization and historical world. Generativity is a distinct constitutive level, introducing birth and death as essential structures. The note distinguishes abstract historicity (any persisting community with a significant world has a history) from generative historicity (history through succession of generations), and names the mother-child relation as the concrete core of the latter, the theme of Beilage XLV.

(138–139) Connected with this is the historicity of human existence, or the constitution of the world for me and for us as historical surrounding world of historical people and communities. A human being is historical, in a widest and unpretentious sense, as member of a historical community. This is constituted as being in the world (which is always world for "me" and "us") as a communalized humanity, a persisting reality of higher order with its particular persistence through change of persons, through being born into it and dying out. That yields a particular sense of present (the historical present of the community, political or scientific, and of its surrounding world), of historical past and future, and a particular sense of communalization, historically hidden and perhaps explicitly conscious on the basis of hidden traditionality, of those now living with those long dead (forms of historical tradition and life in tradition). The historical community is a reality of higher order persisting through generations. Its present is not the instantaneous present but a historical present, and it includes community with the dead through tradition, often "hidden," that is, operative without being known. These pages are the earliest sustained text on historicity in the volume and anticipate Origin of Geometry.

(139) The surrounding world of the normal human being belonging to a historical community is itself historical. It contains as real objects not only persons but communities of various forms and levels, and besides the formation, transformation and dissolution of communities a developing historical world of things, formed as communal achievement. The historical world contains communities as objects and a "world of things" (Sachenwelt) shaped by communal work, the cultural world.

(139) If every normal human being is historical, constituted as existing in a historically persisting community, then his surrounding world, the world concretely constituted for him, is delimited as historical, notwithstanding an open horizon of "empty" space-time and world; so if we take the total historical community, articulated into special communities. The concrete world is bounded by the historical totality; beyond it lies only an empty horizon. This is the first form of the homeworld/alien world distinction.

(139) The problem of extending the world by filling the empty world-horizon with another historical totality, a foreign one of totally alien, in this sense abnormal people of an abnormal surrounding world. Connection to the total "earthly" humanity. Conversely the disintegration of a totality through "forgetting" of tradition, through rupture of tradition. Abnormality is now applied to whole cultures: the alien culture is "abnormal" relative to the homeworld's normality, which is the generative sense of normality. Integration into "earthly" humanity and disintegration through loss of tradition are the two directions of historical change. The theme returns in No. 27 and Beilage XLVIII.

(139–140) Essentially hand in hand with the constitution of a historical surrounding world goes the constitution of an endless, open nature exceeding its finitudes, which, always running ahead, makes possible the constitution of the open possibility of ever new, though factually inaccessible, historical totalities. Husserl's note: but from another side this possibility becomes a problem. Can the whole world be such: infinite nature, historical communities distributed in it, progressively communalizing, so that ideally infinite nature becomes the unified territory of a universal humanity, or the infinite world a single historical world in which all-humanity could gain practical control over all being and spiritualize everything to infinity? Infinite nature is the condition of possibility for other unknown humanities. The note voices a Fichtean or Hegelian idea, the world as territory of a universal humanity spiritualizing everything, and marks it as a problem, not an affirmation. Its echo is No. 23 and Beilage XXIII on teleology.

(140) But what of the constitution of anomalies? Reflecting transcendentally I started from myself and my valid world. In my temporality, as far back as I remember, I always had a world, the same world, which took on ever new sense and yet held the unity of an existing world through all changes. In childhood the sense of the generative existence of humanity and of history was closed to me; I knew nothing of tradition; for me there was none. Only others knew that my games and fairy tales were handed down from ancient tradition through others who stood in it. I had no notion of birth and death, even if I had the words. I knew nothing of literature, science, art, historical culture, though I had a surrounding world with pictures and objects of use. The sense "world" I had was in continuous reshaping, not merely extension by filling horizons. The world-horizon had no determinate, at least no endlessly determinate, inscription, though it had a certain openness. It is a problem what kind of sense-formation occurred, how to interpret it constitutively, and how afterward I can understand that, grown up, I say: I had an idea of "the" world, but very imperfect; only gradually did I gain wider knowledge of it. Childhood is the first anomaly considered: a world without tradition, history, birth and death, in which sense is reshaped rather than merely filled in. The child lives in tradition without knowing it; tradition is operative through others. The question how the adult reinterprets the child's world as an imperfect idea of "the" world repeats Beilage I's retroactive reinterpretation of the solipsistic world.

(140–141) As a mature person among the mature and normal, I and all in at least mediate community with me develop our experience of the world, but its style, as it is for the mature "rational" person and, what usually means the same, "as it really is," no longer changes fundamentally. Of course "world-views" change in the course of time, their sense of being, even within the unity of our historical cultural community. That gives further problems. The mature world's style is stable, but its content as world-view (Weltanschauung) is historical. The distinction between stable style and changing world-view is the theme of Beilage XIV.

(141) Going back into my childhood, as far as I have fairly clear memory, I perform not only recollection but retroactive interpretation and correction, mostly also an unconscious falsification of memory through apperceptive reinterpretation of earlier experience, retroactive apperception from the present one. Access to the child's world is distorted by the adult's apperception. This sets a methodological limit to the genetic phenomenology of childhood, which must therefore proceed by reconstruction (Beilage XLV) rather than recollection.

(141) The normal human being in the sense of the definition is an idealization of the mature human being, who is normal in another sense yet to be determined. The definitional normal is an ideal type; the empirical mature person is normal in a looser sense. The distinction prevents identifying transcendental normality with an empirical class.

(141) I want to understand how my world constitutes itself as human surrounding world, as surrounding world of a communally living humanity, more precisely of my "we," my practical surrounding world or life-world, bounded by a wider non-practical world-horizon, or more concretely a world-horizon containing other humanities and their life-worlds and finally passing into an "empty" world-horizon. For this I need abstractions, and one level of abstraction is the community of the mature, at first those assumed to be really "my likes." The life-world is the practical world of a "we," surrounded by a non-practical horizon containing other humanities and ultimately empty. The community of mature equals is the first abstraction for analysing it.

(141) Every stratification of the world that is demonstrable for me belongs to the problem. Here the community of the mature in my normal "we," and then every such we-community, must be followed back generatively, while children belong only to the world of what is present and to be handled, as do the insane and the sick. But it is difficult to do justice to how all this co-determines the concrete world, how the concrete cultural world is stratified accordingly, comprising first, as science, art, craft and state, only the normal, relatively rational and mature, and then to be taken together with the excluded in concrete historicity. The mature normal community is the constituting "we"; children and the mentally ill appear in it as objects, "to be handled." Husserl sees that this is an abstraction and that the concrete world includes the excluded, whose participation remains the open problem of the title.

(141–142) Finally, how does a life-world constitute itself although mature people are anything but seriously equal? It constitutes itself as an individual one (e.g. as European cultural world, that of the English nation) through an individual-typical universal structure that every "normal" member of the culture has, in his time and its time, consciously and habitually as his spiritual living space, as a "form" he concretizes according to his individual abilities and personal life, imperfectly, in countless degrees of imperfection. Husserl's note: first, every cultural world has its form, its persisting shape through change. The equality of the mature is replaced by participation in a shared form: each member concretizes the culture's form imperfectly. Life-worlds are individual (European, English) and their form is individual, not essential, the theme of the "individual form" in Beilage VII.

(142) How is this persisting structure constituted, on which the persistence of the cultural world depends? It too has "its time," it is only "of its time," and changing it changes only within the unity of a historical style that is the "historical substance" of a universal-temporal culture, like the European from the ancient Greeks to the present. The person living now knows nothing of this, but he can disclose the history that is his as that of his culture, and only from the structure of his historical present, the concretely living temporality with its presently living past. The cultural form is itself historical, yet persists as a "historical substance," a style through changes. History is disclosable only from the present. This is the hermeneutic principle of the Krisis and the Origin of Geometry, here stated with Europe as example, four years before the Vienna lecture.

(142) Normal as a human being is whoever concretely understands himself with the word "everyone," who belongs to an open human community of fellows having the same historical life-world, determined by the same formal structure, familiar to all but not explicated. The normal person is normal in and through the normal community. The final definition makes normality communal and historical: to be normal is to belong to an "everyone" sharing an unexplicated form. Normality is not a property of individuals but of their membership.

### Appendix VII. Normality in the Realm of the Personal World (Custom, etc.) (July–August 1930)

(142–143) Husserl's table of contents: I, we human beings in "human existence," in human life. Goals in life and life-goals, life-ideals. The life of the adult in normality. Breach of normality as itself part of normal existence. Striving for the most willed possible extension of normality, of the domain one can count on. Normality of nature. The normal in the realm of the personal and cultural world. The kathekon; reflection on the kathekon itself belongs to it. Custom. History as reflection with its purpose. Description of the normal style of the human surrounding world (individual form "our" surrounding world), individual form, not essential form. The appendix, written half a year before No. 10, gives the practical and ethical side of normality. "Kathekon" (the Stoic "fitting," appropriate action) names the normatively customary. The two key claims are announced: breach of normality belongs to normal existence, and the description of "our" world yields an individual, not an essential, form.

#### Goals in Life and Life-Goals, Life-Ideals

(143) The human being, I, in the life of humanity, living into the world in common with others, I with my "life-goals" with and against others with theirs. The starting point is practical: life as a field of competing and cooperating goals.

(143) First, self-reflection on the best ways to a goal. Changing purposes in life, so for each purpose such a reflection, perhaps repeated. Purposes of the will regulating a stretch of life as prefiguration of something wished and willed.
