There are generally many possible ways, the way is ambiguous and undetermined, also because it is unclear which imagined possibilities are real possibilities, possibilities of "I can." Reflection on means: the first level of practical reason, concerned with the real "I can."

(143) Second, self-reflection on the purposes themselves, on the whole possible life-horizon as horizon of my ability, generally thought as successful purposive life: the question which whole life, from now on, filled with achieved purposes, would be happy, a life I could affirm as a whole. Reflection on ends leads to the idea of a whole life that can be affirmed, the concept of "life-vocation" in the Kaizo essays and the 1920s ethics lectures (Hua XXXVII).

(143) First, the ideal of "happiness": a life filled with positive goods enjoyed, the highest goods or the humanly best order of goods, the highest good. The eudaimonist ideal is listed as the first form of the life-ideal, the Aristotelian starting point Husserl criticizes in his ethics lectures as insufficient.

(143) Second, reflection on accidents, failures of ability, subjective inhibitions of freedom as belonging generally to human life, on inhibitions by others, on conflicts, how all this looks and how to take it into account. Life includes the failure of plans as a general structure; practical reason must reckon with it.

(143) Third, life in normality, which makes planning and purposive life possible; life in the breach of normality, life that has learned to count anomaly as belonging to the general form of life in a higher normality. Normality is the condition of planning; breakdown can itself be integrated into a higher normality by being anticipated. This is the "breach of normality as part of normal existence" of the table of contents.

(143) Fourth, striving for the greatest possible extension of normality, normality as to some extent controllable. Practical reason strives to widen the domain of the predictable, the root of science and technology.

(143) a) Normal nature: natural science, the presumption of general lawfulness, progressive knowledge of the presumed lawfulness; scientific knowledge of nature as means to practice on nature. Natural science is presented as the extension of normality in nature, its laws as presumed regularities, its purpose practical, the account the Krisis gives of Galilean science as a method for prediction.

(143–144) b) The normal communal world: normal everyday life with others in normal custom, in the normality of the state order, of the common ground of experience, of common tradition, of common ways of judging and valuing, of the expected reactions in each typical situation. Man in normal existence does not merely behave typically alike under typical conditions like a thing in inductive facticity; man lives in the norm by becoming conscious of it as norm. The normal style of communal life is not only a fact for him but an ought and a being from the will to live according to the ought. Life in its form is affirmed, and though the form is not thought of, the particular is approved in its form and for its form's sake. The child is educated into the form of tradition, and later transgression of the form by individual "egoism" is disapproved and punished, the manner of punishment itself belonging to the form. The normal form is what one counts on, like nature's normal form, in its environmental givenness, but it is a form from the will: it is and ought to be, and it was always willed, in the will of the fathers and forebears, which belongs to its sense of ought and will. It ought to be so now because it always was so, because one lived in this norm. So far as the form has an undetermined open horizon, it is determined more closely by asking the eldest how it was in such cases before. The key distinction is between natural regularity and social normativity: social normality is lived as norm, an "ought" affirmed by the will. Its authority is tradition, "because it always was so," and its interpretation proceeds by consulting the eldest. Husserl describes a traditional society in which the normative and the customary coincide, the archaic form of the generative homeworld.

(144) This is the frame of human communal normality and of the "ethical" in the widest sense, the kathekon. Within it are the types of normal, fitting personal life, vocational goals and forms of vocational life. "Ethical" (sittlich) is taken in its root sense of Sitte, custom. The kathekon is the frame within which particular life-forms, vocations, have their place.

(144) A correlative normality, correlate of fitting personal life, is the fitting form of the communal surrounding world, shaped in traditional form as the fitting by individuals and partly by communal work: the building form of houses, stables, the form of "communities" and the position and form of community houses, places of worship. In the life of persons and communities running in normal forms, reflection plays its role, itself belonging to the form: reflection on the fitting, mediately connected with consulting the eldest. The built environment embodies the kathekon; architecture is custom made visible. Reflection on what is fitting belongs to custom, so traditional societies are not unreflective.

(144) Yet traditional custom is known in some parts as having come about historically; certain usages were instituted on a specific occasion in the past. Tradition knows itself, in part, as instituted, the opening for historical consciousness within custom.

#### The Egoic Individual Form "Our World" and the Kathekon

(144) Description of the normal style of the human surrounding world, of the world as world of experience, of normal nature with its regularities, its "habits" so to speak, likewise of the normal personal and cultural world, the world of cultural things in which "one" lives, the world of means and ends in the style of the usual, in the general form of the "ethical" ought. It is an essential part of the human individual form "our" world and its formal articulations and levels. Nature has "habits"; the cultural world has usages with the force of an ought. Both belong to the individual form of "our" world, the style familiar to "one" (man) living in it.

(144–145) The human world as world of persons, as communalized personality or a plurality of persons interwoven in family, community, tribe, with graded personal interweaving. It exists as personal present, has personal memory, its past, communally its history, awakened only occasionally and piecemeal, directly and indirectly, in the narratives of the old, through memorials of material culture, but with a horizon of continuing awakenability and disclosability. Communal memory is the social analogue of individual memory: piecemeal, awakened by narrative and monuments, but with an open horizon of disclosability that history as discipline can explore.

(145) The description of environmental nature (empiriographically), natural history, likewise that of the specifically personal surrounding world with its personally formed world of things: cultural history and history in general, so far as it aims at the historically essential, the individual form of the world in which we live as persons, and the co-founders of this form in historically reachable time, so far as they are such and live on in memory as tradition, determining it in a double sense. History's object is the individual form of our world and its founders, who determine tradition "in a double sense," as having instituted it and as living on in remembrance.

(145) Custom and history, history reflectively re-opened, of communal life in its communal surrounding world, are inseparable. The fitting includes reverence for parents, forebears, the "great" men of the past and how they lived and intervened in the community. History is narrative: what the old and the eldest tell, as their elders told them, mediately, unless they were present themselves. History begins as part of custom, as reverence for ancestors and their deeds, transmitted by narration.

(145) One lives in community and has experience of others' lives in stretches, of one's own life as life with others, richest of family members; the older one gets, the larger the self-experienced stretch of life and community. In life one judges others by the kathekon and is judged, and is early motivated to judge oneself: one's past, perhaps one's whole remembered life, one's career, the past since maturity, when "serious" life began. Self-reflection and self-judgment serve the regulation of life; they go into the past for the sake of the future and into the future for the sake of its possibilities, the possible teleological forms among which one chooses and ought to choose by evaluation. Self-evaluation arises within the communal practice of judging by the kathekon. Its temporal structure is practical: past for future. This is the root of the ethical self-reflection that the Kaizo essays elevate to "renewal."

(145) Within the wider individual form we had chiefly in view the form of the kathekon, the personally usual that also has the character of what ought to be, with deviations, the "not as it should be," which as deviation again belongs to the usual. Deviation from the norm is itself usual; the normal includes its typical violations. This applies the breach-of-normality principle to ethics.

(146) Regarding the distinction between the humanly usual and what corresponds to "custom," much needs considering. The personally usual includes "professional" thieves and swindlers, sham honest men, sham citizens who in truth break the ought-form of custom, and robber bands openly opposed to it and to the state order, which belongs to the ethically required. So we would have "proper" and improper vocations, honourable and dishonourable (executioner, usurer). What is essential here and what merely common can remain undistinguished for now. The usual is not identical with the customary-normative: there are usual forms of life (crime) that break custom, and vocations that custom tolerates but dishonours. Husserl declines to sort essence from fact here, which is consistent with the appendix's claim that it describes an individual, not an essential, form.

(146) At first the general talk of person suffices. But more closely: what belongs to a "person" who lives in these "forms" and has form from them (the form of an official, an officer, a private man, a functionary of the state or not)? In another but related sense everyone has a private sphere in which he is not a functionary. Persons receive their form from social roles, and the private sphere is the domain where one is not a functionary, a distinction between role and private person that structures the personal world.

(146) Now come the character-types of persons and human personal life as purposive life, the surrounding world as my field of purposes, my realm, domain of rule in which I prevail, set and realize my life-goals, which I want to shape according to myself. The surrounding world as "domain of rule" (Herrschaftsbereich) gives the personal world a practical, sovereign structure: the person shapes her world.

#### Our Life-World as Individual Form

(146–147) The "form" of the world in which the factual human being consciously lives personally and to which he belongs, for himself in his personal life, is an individual form. More precisely: I, the inner psychologist or phenomenologist, describe originally, as factual human being, myself in "our" fellow humanity, in my family, my town, my open community, in our world: this world, including ourselves, according to its individual form, the form this world (life-world, not understood as outer world) has. Everyone belonging to this world, to my "we," describes the same, necessarily the same, individual form. A Chinese, so far as he does not belong to it, describes another. World as individual form is not world as essential form for every conceivable human being, for man as "essence." But every conceivable human being is individual and lives within an individual form. We must distinguish the essentially general belonging to man and his world, including the essential form "individual form in general," from the determinate individual form a factual human being and his factual community ("all of us" in the widest sense) have. Described in essential generality, it presupposes or includes the essential form "human community in general," which can be described in a generality short of the essential generality "individual form." The essence of the single human being, which intentional psychology pursues into its deepest structures, and the intentional essence of a human community, purely inwardly seen, in which a personal world is constituted for it, are presupposed and need not be explicated. The individual form of personal being in community and of community itself can be described naively, in essential and not only empirical generality, using the eidos person, without first working out the concrete pure "soul" or monadic and intermonadic subjectivity. They can remain undisclosed, however much that remains a deficiency for understanding. There is a naive ontology of personality and the personal world as individual form. The distinction between individual form and essential form is decisive for reading Husserl's later talk of homeworlds. "Our world" has an individual form shared by all members and not by outsiders (the Chinese example); every human world has some individual form, and that it must have one is an essential truth. A descriptive science of individual forms, a "naive ontology" of the personal world, is possible without transcendental grounding. Husserl here licenses cultural anthropology and history as eidetically guided sciences of individual forms, and he guards against two errors: taking one's own individual form for the essence of humanity (ethnocentrism) and denying that there is an essence (relativism).

(147) This form is for me and every human being the most familiar of all, not as theoretically set out or abstracted, but as the familiar horizon-structure into which every particular activity fits. The individual form is the horizon of everyday life, unthematic and most familiar, the "life-world" in the strict sense of a lived horizon.

(147) Everyone has this form individually oriented from himself and filled as his personal surrounding world, experienced "from him" as a more or less undetermined, known-unknown personal world. It can be described in greater or lesser generality and then with its most individual content. The common individual form is lived perspectivally: each has it as his surrounding world, centred on him. Description can proceed from the general form to the most individual content, closing the appendix with the same layering of generality No. 10 had used for normality.

### No. 11. Apodictic Structure of Transcendental Subjectivity. The Problem of the Transcendental Constitution of the World Starting from Normality (Probably End of 1930, or 1931)

(147–148) Husserl's table of contents: the pre-given world and the world of the normal with the abnormal. The modes of abnormality. Man, animal, the insane. Personal abnormality. The world of animals, of primitives, of the insane, and the world simply, our world. The text combines two themes the title names: the invariant (apodictic) structure of the ego, and the question whether the abnormal, animals and "primitives" co-constitute "the" world. Husserl uses "primitive" in the ethnological vocabulary of his time (Lévy-Bruhl), and the commentary reports it as his term.

(148–149) The ego as subject of intentionality. The waking ego. Intentionality in passivity and in action; "passivity": instinct and association. The activity of the waking ego, striving in waking self-consciousness through its acts. The ego as specific subject of instinctive drives (as drive-habitualities), of drive-intentionalities running through every living present; and as subject of act-habitualities, which live themselves out in waking affections and acts and are instituted anew with them. The distinction of the unmodalized: drives toward acquisitions that remain unmodalized; a particular synthesis of all such drives. Drive runs through act-life: the affected ego turns toward, aims at, strives for something. Acts as "achieving"; the ego achieving on the basis of a habituality by which it keeps its old achievements as abiding, uncancelled acquisitions, abiding capacities. Through associative passivity acquisitions are actualized as apperceptions. Waking life is apperceptive, grasping the new according to the old, assimilating it. The intentionality by which I have a worldly present implies that of the past; the present world of experience implies my experiential pasts as assimilating them in intentional continuity. Striving toward certainties, toward lasting validities, toward having. But all this is too general. The sketch gives the late Husserl's anthropology of the transcendental ego in compressed form. Drive and instinct are not added to an intellectual ego; they run through all acts, and the striving for unmodalized certainty, for "having," is itself a drive. The thesis that cognition is the fulfilment of a drive toward abiding validity anticipates the "universal teleology" of No. 22 and the drive-intentionality of No. 34.

(149) We thus consider the world as achievement: the world for me as my achievement, from my intentional activity on the ground of my passivity. In this achievement I acquire for myself the being of others as co-achieving the being of the world for me. The world is an accomplishment (Leistung), and the others are acquired within it as co-accomplishers, a formula that keeps both the egological origin and the co-constituting role of others.

(149) The total achievement, when I reflect, is always already performed, so that I always already have a pre-given world. It continues as an ongoing achievement of my and our subjectivity; the pre-given world develops, acquires new sense, yet remains the same world and is pre-given in every present, future and for everyone with the sense it has come to have. I investigate the general style of the pre-given world, the style of its modes of pre-givenness in surrounding worlds of various levels, of its structure of modalization, of the correction of its sense in the course of experience, of its way of holding identity through change. I can describe this and gain a structural truth about pre-givenness, which leaves immense problem-horizons open. Does the being of the world and of constituting subjectivity not lead to actual infinities? If being is a constant process of genesis of achieved results from ever new achievements, and looking back was always so, actual infinity seems necessary. Husserl raises the antinomy that haunts genetic phenomenology: if every achievement presupposes earlier achievements, the regress seems to require an actually infinite past of constituting subjectivity. The emphasis signals that he takes the question seriously; Beilage XXXIII and No. 33 take it up as the problem of beginning.

(149) The transcendental reduction leads the naive being of the world back to pre-giving subjectivity, which constitutes the world as pre-given and as experienced and experienceable in ever new experiences. To this subjectivity the world belongs as constituted, as habitual acquisition and as constituted field of activities, especially of self-preservation. Self-preservation (Selbsterhaltung) is named as the practical sense of the world for constituting subjectivity, an unusual and important note: the world is not only a theoretical correlate but the field in which the ego preserves itself.

(150) Suppose the systematic intentional explication of the world in this constitution, and correlatively of constituting subjectivity, were carried out; we might say that over against the world, which exists relative to constituting subjectivity, this subjectivity exists absolutely. But does transcendental subjectivity really exist absolutely; has it not itself a temporariness, unless we assume being in an actually infinite time, a time to which we already give transcendental sense without being able to change its form of infinity and order? The absoluteness of transcendental subjectivity, the thesis of Ideas I § 49, is questioned. Either constituting subjectivity is temporary, beginning and ending, or it exists in an actually infinite time. Both options are problematic, and Husserl leaves the dilemma standing.

(150) The transcendental reduction and the explication of the constitution of the experienced world, and of the scientifically constructed and idealized world, may be a wide field of new and fundamental insights. But they bring further riddles. We understand ourselves and the world more deeply: our deeper being, from which our human being first springs, is being as transcendental "I" and "we"; and yet we do not yet understand ourselves and our world-life as human beings. The transcendental clarification leaves the human being, born and mortal, unexplained; the gap between transcendental "we" and human existence is the next problem, the one Fink formulated as the problem of the "mundanization" of transcendental subjectivity.

(150) Beginning and carrying out self-explication, I find: I am, however unknown I am to myself; I can come to know myself as an ego that experiences itself objectified as human being in the world, that has the world pre-given and lives into it with its current sense, has ever new purposes in it, instincts related to it, recurring needs, abiding structures of ends and means acquired by its own achievement and in communication. Self-explication begins with the bare apodictic "I am" combined with radical self-unacquaintance; it finds the ego already objectified as a human being with needs and instincts.

(150–151) The transcendental ego finds all this as transcendental phenomenon, finds itself and such things as its formations, finds itself as subject of original and acquired capacities, in a structure of habituality. Despite unacquaintance in many respects, I find myself as an ego who can and cannot do such things, who cannot possibly do them. I find myself as apodictically existing for myself, in an apodictic structure of which I must say: so I am and otherwise I cannot be; it would be "unthinkable" for me; an invariant structure filled with the varying, contingent and particularizing, as it must be filled somehow. If I am mistaken about my being-so, there necessarily corresponds another within the fullness that belongs to me "in advance" as apodictically necessary structure. The apodicticity of the "I am" leaves nothing open as to the particularization of the structure; it leaves open only whether I really know it. My own being is "definite": every statement about myself explicating myself from my own experience is either true or false, decided "in advance." Two theses are notable. The apodictic structure includes a domain of impossibility, what I cannot do, which defines the ego's essence. And the ego's being is "definite" in the sense of Husserl's theory of definite manifolds (Ideas I § 72; Formal and Transcendental Logic §§ 31–33): every proposition about it is decided in itself, even if I do not know which way. Self-knowledge is fallible, self-being determinate.

(151) How far does the definite structure enclosed by apodicticity reach? Does it not include my being as subject for a world, entering into all self-variations of my ego as far as they are conceivable, as essentially general? And does that not include the community of transcendental subjects as constituting a common world? The question of No. 2 returns with the vocabulary of definiteness: is the world, and intersubjectivity, part of the apodictic structure of the ego?

(151) But how so? Can I not think myself or my world varied so that it loses objectivity for everyone, can I not imagine all presumptions of experience dissolving so that not even a reduced world remains for me? Is it not thinkable that my body and things lose their validity, that in my sense-fields every contrast and every constitution of identifiable sense-data ceases, and so every stimulus for my active ego? Or can I not re-think myself as never having had sense-fields with distinct data? How could an ego-life with its affections and actions proceed without data? But if sense-data are necessary, why must they run so, together with kinaestheses, that objective apperceptions can form? Husserl's note: freely varying me and my world, the fact: the problem of world-annihilation (Ideas). The reference is Ideas I § 49, where the annihilation of the world was argued to leave consciousness intact. Husserl now asks whether the hypothesis is coherent: a consciousness without world would be one without differentiated data, and it is unclear that ego-life could proceed at all. The questions descend from objectivity, to the reduced world, to hyle, to kinaesthetic correlation.

(151–152) It seems I could be worldless. But is life a field I can re-think arbitrarily? Is it not the life of an ego? Am I thinkable as an ego that could never wake, never be affected, never come to acts or lasting achievements? Are sleep and unconsciousness not possibilities of the waking ego, thinkable only through a waking ego that passes into sleep and, waking, understands sleep? And is an "empty" visual or tactile field perhaps thinkable only as a modification of a field filled with data, from the side of an ego that already has a world in some restricted sense, and is an arbitrary modification of all fields at once over the whole of life excluded by the structure of ego-life, life in this sense being unthinkable without an ego? Husserl's note: and has it been considered what free variation means, that it essentially involves a "covering over" of experience, of actual experience and of experience possible under the idea of true being? That raises problems. Husserl now argues against the world-annihilation thesis of 1913. Ego-life requires wakefulness, wakefulness requires affection by differentiated data, and empty fields are modifications of filled ones. A worldless ego may be an empty possibility. The note adds a methodological point: variation covers over the actual experience it varies, so its results are bound to the varied fact. This is a significant self-correction of Ideas I.

(152) Much depends on the method of constructing evident possibilities of different sense and their presuppositions, and one must not so easily turn evident but restricted possibilities into evident universal ones. On the other hand, it is very hard to make thematic the evidence, the self-givenness of the ego as subject of capacities, habitualities, abiding acquisitions, abiding types of apperception in which the worldly is given, of abiding "innate" instincts preceding all possible genesis. The fallacy named is the extension of local possibilities (I can imagine this thing absent) to global ones (I can imagine all things absent). "Innate" instincts, prior to all genesis, belong to the apodictic structure, a striking admission of an a priori of drive.

(152) It is a great problem first to explicate systematically, from the fact "I am subject of my world of experience," this world in its pre-givenness through actual and possible experience, and to note that its explication includes the explication of the "souls" belonging to it, personal subjects in the concretion of their psychic existence, according to their invariant structure, by which they are subjects psychologically and intentionally related to the world in which they are. The explication of the world includes the souls in it, and their invariant structure, a task for intentional psychology.

(152–153) Turned transcendentally, the whole inner-psychic structure of the person with her life and capacities enters into the transcendental ego for me as transcendental subject who finds itself as human being in the world. If I vary myself according to eidetic possibilities of myself as human being in the world, transcendentally turned, the essential moments of the purely psychic structure remain invariant: the person and her conscious life, with the structures of passivity and activity, immanent temporality, capacities, habitualities and centring. But it belongs to the invariant essence that this structure is invariant only as the structure of a streaming life, and so of a changing yet persisting person and of her surrounding world, reshaped from her, in which she exists and which is, corresponding to her temporality, the current "mode of appearance" of "the" world. So transcendental subjectivity, noetically and noematically, is persisting in a genesis of persisting form. The invariant of the eidetic variation is not a static structure but the form of a genesis. The formula "persisting in a genesis of persisting form" is Husserl's answer to the antinomy of (150): transcendental subjectivity is neither timeless nor merely temporary; it is the persistence of a form of becoming.

(153) But then the being of other transcendental egos, objectified and co-given through the ego's world, and the being of all in the form of generation, bring new problems of genesis and the problems of birth and death. Generation introduces genesis across egos; birth and death are the limits of the genesis that the eidetic invariant had described within one ego.

(153) Advancing from myself in the transcendental, the question of the infinity of temporalization opens the question of the infinity of the transcendental manifold of subjects. If the ego's temporalization is not infinite, the infinity required for the world must be supplied by the plurality of subjects in generative succession, the move Beilage VIII develops.

(153) Transcendental philosophy answers the question what am I, what is man, humanity, by its deepest explication of subjectivity as constituting itself and the world. But as said, ever deeper problems arise, the problem of the infinity of transcendental subjectivity as infinity of individual transcendental subjects and of their mundanization, the problem of how their totality is to be thought. The Kantian question "what is man?" is assigned to transcendental philosophy, but its answer opens the problem of the totality of subjects, the monadological theme of No. 21 and Beilage XXII.

(153) In another direction: the explication of a human being as related to his pre-given world, as actively living into it, experiencing, thinking, valuing, and intervening practically to transform it according to guiding intentions and purposes. The second direction is practical anthropology, the theme of Beilage VII and No. 30.

(154) The human being in the world, and human beings together, have through all their pauses of sleep the unity of a waking life in the synthesis of their wakefulnesses, in manifold single acts. Further a special unity, through all pauses of illness and every abnormality, the unity of a normal life. A single person may be abnormal, but we count it as belonging to humanity, the total one or a separate one, that not all can be abnormal. Abnormality is a modification of the normal, stands out from it and is added to it as an occurrence necessarily entering under occasional, possible and recognizable circumstances. But is wakefulness itself not a basic mode of normality? Normality is a unity of life spanning interruptions, as wakefulness spans sleep. "Not all can be abnormal" is the thesis of No. 2 now stated of humanity. The closing question proposes wakefulness as the first normality, so that sleep is the first anomaly, which ties the theory of normality to the phenomenology of wakefulness and sleep (cf. Beilage XLV).

(154) A humanity has a particular unity in that, as normal, it constitutes a normal world through its normal individual subjects, as field of normal human practice in which normal culture grows, and the individual subjects maintain their normal style, at least on the whole, in all their ways of life. The individual subject too has its normality, within which abnormality appears as a certain unity of style, in particulars and occasionally disturbing. Human normality designates not only a style describable from outside, but an inner unity, the unity of the person in her life, the unity of a humanity as analogue of a person. Husserl's note: a people's humanity (Volksmenschheit)! Then in the idea: total humanity in genuine personality. A state-nation within a super-nation can count as analogue of a single person. Normality is personal unity, and since a humanity is a person of higher order, its normality is its personal unity. The note sketches a hierarchy: people, super-nation, ideal total humanity as genuine person, the ideal of the Kaizo essays.

(154) All this, transcendentally clarified, of course concerns transcendental subjectivity, individual-monadic and collective, but not merely taken together collectively: communalized within the monads. The qualification "innermonadically communalized" excludes the picture of a collective as a sum of monads; community exists within each monad as intentional implication of others.

(154–155) But normality has various forms and levels that belong essentially to the constitution of the human being and of the human being in humanity, with which he is himself communalized, becoming from child to normal mature person. The human being is not only in community but, as becoming, grows into communalization and forms himself according to it and with it in mutual motivation, so that as having become he bears in himself the genesis originating from community, or, what is the same, bears his human formers intentionally in himself. This also concerns formation by tradition, the effect ("after-effect") of fellow humans of the same humanity in earlier times. The mature person carries her formers within her intentionally: her parents, teachers, tradition. The formula extends the intentional implication of others from co-presence to genesis, a genetic Ineinander that underlies the later thesis that the ego is always already generatively communalized.

(155) How does one gain the sense of the normality that belongs to the constituted world itself, the objective world for all, and that is in turn presupposed as structure of the one reflecting on the world? The levels of normality and abnormality correspond to the levels of constitution of being, from relative being in relative appearances up to the objectively true being of the truly existing world. Normality is graded like objectivity: each level of relative being has its normal subjects. The reflecting philosopher presupposes his own normality, as No. 2 showed.

(155) The world constituted in a normality is constituted as containing abnormalities. The world of experience simply, constituted as common to all, is constituted as containing a normal humanity experiencing it normally, and also abnormal people experiencing the same world abnormally, so that normal and abnormal reach understanding about the same world and things. Every normal subject has occasional abnormal deviations from its normal experience, yet identifies the same thing as appearing differently, occasionally abnormally; likewise intersubjectively. This points to an objective basic structure guiding identification as form, spatio-temporal individuation. The first and universal normality is that transcendental intersubjectivity constitutes a world as normal world of experience of normal humanity; but in it abnormal people occur, and animals, which we do not call abnormal. They are understood as modified from the human norm and then as having constituted, in their own togetherness and normality, a normal "world" for them, for each species separately. But that is no world in the proper sense. The common identifying form is spatio-temporal individuation, which survives all sensory anomaly. Animals are not abnormal but modifications of the human norm with their own species-normality; their "world" is a world in quotation marks. The claim that the animal world is "no world in the proper sense" is the thesis Heidegger would make in 1929/30 (GA 29/30, "world-poor"); Husserl reaches it from the theory of normality, not from the analysis of captivation.

(155–156) The more concrete explication of human normality has its difficulties. It leads to stratifications into particular normalities. First, the normal world has as founding stratum normal nature, the realm of merely sensuous experience. Explicating the normality of nature constituted by normal human sensibility would be ontological explication of the normal thing as essential form and of normal nature in general; correlatively, explication of the subjective givens of nature, its modes of appearance for everyone sensuously normal. The harmony of all normal people as to their sensuous experiences and courses of appearance, by which, though each has other appearances and none simultaneously the same, they see the same. Sensuous normality is the founding normality: the harmony of appearance-courses that makes "seeing the same" possible.

#### Characterization of Human Normality

(156–157) "I, apperceiving the world, apperceive it as a normal person." That is first said privately. How is the phenomenon characterized generally? I find myself first in my perceptual present among fellow humans with whom I understand without further ado that they perceive and confirm the same natural objects I perceive, in the same sensuous qualities. Further, I have a surrounding world of co-presence, with fellow humans in it, and everything confirmed for me by sensuous experience, its properties, spatio-temporal relations, causal characters within sensuous experience, has the same for others, so that each of us has his sensuous phantoms, changing with his kinaestheses, his own and distinct, but in exchange of places we would exchange these appearances. Now the correction: abnormality also belongs to the surrounding world, and my own experiential life and private surrounding world include abnormally experienced things, abnormally appearing, which are not really illusion: seeing otherwise with a diseased eye, blurred seeing, multiplied contours with the onset of presbyopia in the normal person. The abnormally seen becomes a mode of appearance of what is normally to be seen for me myself; it is apperceived as "abnormal" deviation from its normal primal object, not as the thing itself, nor as an image, but as a modification referring to the primal proper (Ureigentliche). Normality is first lived from within: I apperceive the world as normal without noticing. Abnormal appearances are apperceived as modifications that refer back to the normal "primal object," not as illusions, and not as pictures. "Phantom" is the technical term of Ideas II § 10 for the purely sensuous thing-schema.

(157) So I also find among others those who experience abnormally, even consistently abnormally compared with me and my likes. I find that they are subjects of a concordance of their sensuous experience by which, in their private world, they can have no private abnormalities appearing to them, apart from relation to others, as abnormal deviations. Consistent abnormality, the colour-blind person, cannot be noticed from within; it appears only in comparison with others. Abnormality of this kind is intersubjective in its very sense.

(157) We must add: "I am normal" means phenomenologically from within: I am in a surrounding world with people who "all, with isolated exceptions," experience this world as the same and identically determined, or have it in certainty as experienceable and identifiably so for one another. The world before science means for me and them all (in their open, also generatively "openly infinite" manifold) this common world, and nature which "everyone" who can experience it in all it "is" in his own possible sensuous experience apperceives in relation to an open horizon of "all others, with individual exceptions," who could experience the same world and have it as common world of possible experience; so they also have it as world of possible common experience, experiencing together and taking over experience from one another. Husserl's note: "as a rule," "usually," "always, with actual, known or possible individual exceptions," is one of the logical forms, that of the empirical universal. The normal is defined by the "everyone, with exceptions," the logical form of empirical generality. The note is precise: normality is not universality but the empirical "in general," a form with built-in exceptions.

(158) The excepted individuals have the character of abnormal co-subjects. As objects of the surrounding world they are there for our normality, experienceable for all in the same determinations. But they are not "normally" co-experiencing, since by definition they lack the capacity to reach the same world in all that it is for us in their own possible experience. Yet we experience them as experiencing the same world (if we experience them as humans at all, even as "bestialized" humans), perhaps as concordant with themselves and with some others, but unable to experience their private world in all its features as common world for "all but a few," only as common world in particular strata. The abnormal co-subject reaches the common world in some strata but not in all. "Bestialized" (vertierte) humans marks the extreme case; Husserl's vocabulary reflects the psychiatry of his time.

(158) We also understand that, growing up among an endless plurality of the normal, who can always expect to meet their like and agreement in experiential givens, they may adapt and see themselves as exceptions, as abnormal, and must interpret what would privately be normal for them as abnormal modification of what normally is, provided they are abnormal only in a particular stratum and otherwise in complete harmony with the normal, and so normal in everything else. The abnormal can understand themselves as abnormal, by adopting the community's normality. Self-interpretation as abnormal presupposes partial normality, the colour-blind person reinterpreting his colour experience by the community's colour-equations (Beilage II).

(158–159) There are further problems. First, how does identification of the same things and nature for the normal come about at all, and which abnormalities are possibilities for each subject, which types of relative or permanent anomaly are thinkable or understandable? Experience of others presupposes that one, say I, experiences the other's body as lived body of another ego, so that the physical thing in my sphere and the body the other experiences as his are the same. Thus the conditions of the possibility of empathy lead to a world of experience of the other, co-understood as identical, in a certain extent. How far must this common world, grounded in the mediacies of empathic understanding, reach, and how far is abnormality (not illusion) possible, which abnormalities are open a priori as typical and usable for interpreting others (as abnormal not only in bodily sensibility but as insane)? Everything depends on the phenomenological understanding of the structure of transcendental (or purely psychic) being and on the basic fact that the one explicating can gain every abnormality as experienceable possibility only by intentional modification of his own individual inner-psychic structure. This concerns not only the "otherness" of other people and a possible typology of them, but also the understanding of animals and the typology of animal-psychic being in ever more remote, mediated intentional modifications. Empathy guarantees a minimal common world, at least the identity of the other's body. Beyond that minimum, the range of possible abnormality is limited by what the interpreter can generate as modification of himself. This is the principle of all understanding of others, animals included: understanding is self-modification, and its limit is the limit of my possible variants.

(159) Basic differences also arise. First, the abnormal person like the colour-blind, who may be otherwise very normal, a unified personality experiencing his concordant world, which agrees completely with that of the normal except for his individual colour-equations. Second, the abnormal like the "insane" in their various forms, who are no longer human and yet not animals. The animal too, as normal animal of its species, has its unified concordant existence, and this unity, concordant wholeness as against disintegration, the radically abnormal, must be made intentionally intelligible. But both types of abnormality are possible for animals too. The decisive distinction is between partial abnormality within a unified personality and disintegration of personal unity. Husserl's remark that the insane are "no longer human and yet not animals" is the harsh form of a structural claim: unity of concordance defines both human and animal normality, and its collapse is a third condition. The language is that of 1930 psychiatry.

(159) There are anomalies of memory, intelligence, valuing, will, emotional life, instinctive drives and needs. There are personal anomalies within a unified personality and human life. A vast body of pre-scientific and scientific material, past and present, exists; but as transmitted and described it shows only an external and unintelligible typology. Everyday interpretations, and those working from outside with everyday or "modern" psychology, give no scientific understanding, no reconstruction of the abnormal psyche, no possibility of an inner psychology of abnormality. That requires an already very advanced phenomenology. Husserl demands an "inner psychology of abnormality," a reconstruction of the abnormal psyche from within by intentional analysis. This is the program phenomenological psychiatry (Binswanger, Minkowski, Straus, later Blankenburg) would take up; the criticism of "modern" psychology as external is the same as that of behaviourism in Beilage IV.

(160) What do we learn for the task of phenomenological explication of the world as pre-given to us as scientists and philosophers, and especially as world of common experience? Husserl's note: time is not considered, only the present, the primal modality of temporal modes. And the fundamental distinction: man knows himself as person of his personal generative, historical temporal nexus, that of his humanity, and this as living in the real world with natural time, so that persons are at once psychophysical humans and humanity a psychophysical humanity belonging to the world of realities. In this double world the animals, which have no generative, historical world, hence no real world and properly no time, no space, no universe of realities with ontic structure; such things are constituted not for them but for us. The note goes further than the main text: animals lack historical time and therefore lack the real world as such, since space, time and an ontic universe are constituted only for beings with a generative history. World in the full sense is a correlate of historical humanity.

(160) So as transcendental ego I investigate my own logos, together with that of my world and my others. I create a norm, a completely concrete one, for all judgment of the factual world, first my judgment and that of my transcendental companions manifesting themselves in me, who function as transcendental co-bearers of our world. It is the concrete universal eidos of a possible transcendental subjectivity, the mathesis of transcendental subjectivity and its world: concrete transcendental logic. "Formal" logic, transcendental-apophantic, relates to the transcendental ego and others as judging subjects related to any transcendental sphere of being, since beings of various kinds are constituted transcendentally, the transcendental ego and its life but also the world and worldly ideas, all with transcendental sense in different ways, but all constituted or constitutable as existing. The eidos of transcendental subjectivity, with its world, is called "concrete transcendental logic," a "mathesis" of subjectivity. This is the program of Formal and Transcendental Logic's conclusion: logic in the full sense is the eidetic science of constituting subjectivity, of which formal logic is the apophantic part.

(161) The transcendental concrete and formal-apophantic logic arising from my self-reflection, which, transcendentally understood and ordered, comprises all a priori sciences, is nothing but fully developed eidetic transcendental phenomenology, which presents to the transcendental fact, and within it all worldly positivity, the transcendental a priori sense through which it gains concrete and absolute significance. It is the absolute ratio for all empirical knowledge. Eidetic phenomenology contains all a priori sciences as its parts and stands to empirical knowledge as reason (ratio) to fact, the Leibnizian ideal of a universal science of possibilities.

(161) But that is not all. Transcendental phenomenology or concrete logic is itself a transcendental fact, mine as its builder and my co-workers', whom I find as transcendental companions of my work. Like everything transcendental it has its mundanization and appears in the world historically, factually, in the present of the twentieth century. It is now a fact for me, its developer, and was before a "real" possibility determining my fact as transcendental ego, as it is for each companion, who, as co-bearer of our world, even without any notion of it, has the transcendental capacity to co-understand and develop it. Phenomenology is itself a historical fact, mundanized as a twentieth-century philosophy, and a possibility of every transcendental subject. The thesis grounds the later claim (No. 23, Krisis) that phenomenology is the self-awakening of a capacity latent in humanity.

(161) Showing what sense this has and may have is itself a transcendental a priori theme. Transcendental subjectivity ego knows itself as subject of its capacities and thereby its co-egos as subjects of the same capacities; it knows their innate capacities as correlates of the transcendental logos. Husserl's note: not actual capacities as actual ability, but as an "ideal" possibility of developing such ability, a possibility that is not nothing. The capacity for phenomenology is "innate" in the sense of an ideal possibility of acquisition, the formula that avoids attributing transcendental insight to all humans while affirming it as their possibility.

(161–162) Transcendental logic is evidently not something first my own in my transcendental individuality, and likewise each other's own for himself, then knowable as the same for all. The others, co-bearers of my world, are where they are co-workers also co-bearers of my transcendental logic, as I of theirs. We all contribute, but with all these contributions, re-understood by me and taken up with my own validity, it is wholly mine and so each individual's logic or phenomenology, perhaps with a horizon of yet unknown contributions to be re-understood and tested. Husserl's note: but that is too hasty! The communal character of phenomenology is affirmed: it is a common work, yet wholly mine as each contribution is re-validated by me. The self-criticism shows Husserl dissatisfied with a solution that makes the common possession consist in many private re-validations; No. 21 takes the problem up again.

(162) My transcendental-logical insights hold always, essentially, for all others, so far as they are experienced or presumed from the start as my likes. But the "insane" and animals are also "other" subjects, transcendental-monadically understood, in manifold essential types. All beings accessible to me through "empathic" experience are intentional modifications of myself. But not all are, transcendentally reduced, co-bearers of the world I have as mine and "we" have pre-given, "we" meaning the open plurality of co-bearers constituting the same world in community (by no means the animals and the insane "humans," though they too are experienced by us as related in their inner life to the one "actual" world). The spaced thesis separates two classes of others: all are modifications of me, but only my likes co-bear the world. Animals and the insane are monads but not co-constituting monads. This answers the question of the title negatively, provisionally, and the next paragraphs test it.

(162–163) We human monads, all of us, co-constituting subjects, co-bearers of our world, co-experiencing and co-thinking, not each in general, but in a certain harmony conscious to every co-bearer and experienceable as yielding not only the same results but determining mutually the same objects in complementation, in a validity that propagates by taking over from subject to subject. So we are also co-bearers of the world of values and of the practical world there for us all; we act together, for and with one another, toward the same purposes whose sense we agree on, complementing one another reflectively, and in whose realization, as work, something common arises with the same sense and ought-validity for us co-agents. As outsiders we understand the work in relation to its particular practical bearers in its personal significance. Likewise we are fellow citizens, members of the same church, fellow children in the kingdom of God. We are all this as humans in various respects: in a most general respect that makes the world common for all humans as such, members of humanity in the widest sense; in other respects as co-bearers of our world as special, related to us in particular, as members of associations, churches, cities, states. Communities and their surrounding worlds become particular, up to a European or earthly humanity, which yet does not fulfil the idea of humanity in the most universal sense, since an essential concept of man leaves open whether outside the earth humans live as co-bearers of our world, humans who, once in factual relation of understanding with us, would at once be called and able to co-constitute the world. Co-bearing is not mere agreement but complementation and transmission of validity. Communities are nested, and the universal concept of humanity is essential, not terrestrial: a rational being on another planet, once in communication, would co-constitute our world. The "kingdom of God" mention is Husserl's example of a religious community, not a theological commitment.

(163–164) How are these co-bearers characterized, and correspondingly, how is the human being characterized in the world constituted for me and us, we humans as persons, rational egos, who know ourselves as such, over against the animals, for whom this does not hold? How are those psychic beings distinguished whom I regard as co-functioning for the sense of being of my world and always presuppose? My world, the one I speak of, has always been meant as "our" world, the world of us humans; not determinate ones, though only determinate ones were in direct or indirect intercourse with me and contributed experiences and knowledge; nor only these actual takings-over; the world as world of possible experience and knowledge. The whole indirect tradition of unknown generations counts. And if it turned out that "humans" live on Mars and we found means of understanding them, they would at once count in the human "we" correlative to the world as common to us all. Husserl's note: the other human being is for me a person; that already means he is for me a person in my personal "humanity." This must be stressed at once. The "we" of the world includes unknown generations and possible Martians, a "we" defined by possible understanding, not by acquaintance. The note anticipates the homeworld problem: the other is a person within "my" humanity, which is a particular humanity.

(164–165) I have "the" world pre-given in my intentional life. To its pre-given sense belongs that fellow humans belong to it and that I myself am objectively real in it as human being. Whatever else makes up the general content of the type "human being," it means above all that he is experienced or experienceable by me with the sense that he can enter into a community of understanding with me. In it I experience him in advance as related to the same world, moving in the same possibilities of experience, able as I am to determine the experienced in thought, to value it and treat it. As my own experiences and thoughts are valid and stay valid until discordance in my own sphere forces cancellation and correction, so the experiences and thoughts I re-understand from others, however expressed, are valid for me like my own, as long as they "fit" mine and go together with them in one concordant context. The concordant courses manifested in the individual subjects I experience do not yield separate validities apart from the concordance constituted in me, but come with mine and among themselves to a unity of concordance spanning all, in the first form groundable for me alone in me, since the others are valid to me not only as co-existing subjects but their validity, the "world" valid to them, is co-valid for me, subject like all my validity to modalization within the context of my validities and abiding only as long as the universal context remains concordant. The human type is defined by capacity for a community of understanding. Others' validities are taken over as one's own conditionally, as long as they fit: a principle of coherence governs testimony. And the universal concordance of all is constituted first in me, the egological form of a common world.

(165) It lies in the sense-giving of my experience of others that the other is apperceived as my like in such a way that he in turn is valid as standing in a community of understanding with me from his side. Having him actually valid as existing and understanding me, and he me, I can turn to him and understand that he, my like, can turn to me and perhaps does. I can respond, and intercourse of person with person and community in the pregnant sense of social connection in action and reaction can arise. Social community proper begins with address and response, the "social acts" of No. 1 § 11, made possible by mutual apperception as able to understand.

(165) All this I find already there as always present and constantly re-forming, as belonging to the world that is mine and is understood eo ipso as the same world of the others existing for me and entering into such "relations" with me, known and unknown, in open endlessness of spatial distribution, existing for me in advance as subjects with whom I share the world in which we all live, so that this world has received and receives sense for all from communalization. Husserl's note: but further: I already find myself as personal member of my "people," of my humanity as universal home-humanity. The note introduces the decisive concept: my humanity is a "home-humanity" (Heimmenschheit), particular even when it claims universality.

(165–166) These people, who have come to validity from me in my life as co-functioning for the constitution of the sense "world" in its whole known and unknown concretion, as a community of world-validity constituting for me and all others the one world of everyone, these "we humans" are evidently the "normal people." The distinction in the "we" and "everyone" is normality, which stands out as such only through the co-occurrence of the abnormal; or rather the abnormal stands out from the normal as first in itself and appears as its intentional modification. The normal, within the wider sphere of beings understood empathically as psychic, as modified humanity, becomes a distinguished class, humans in the full and proper sense, fully mature and fully "rational," the correlates of the actually existing world. The constituting "we" is identified with the normal, and the normal with the mature rational. Normality is noticed only by contrast with the abnormal, but is first in itself.

(166) Other psychic beings "relate" intentionally to the same world, but as my modifications. They are "in 'the' actual world." Our common experience, of full humans, which constitutes our world, finds them in it, and, as far as re-understanding reaches, as having "their own 'world'," if they can experience concordantly, a "world" we call their "mode of appearance" of the actual world, an intentional modification, but by no means one of the "modes of appearance" that co-ground the being of the world itself or can help in the originality of experience as its confirmation. Animal and abnormal worlds are modes of appearance of the actual world, but not constitutive ones: they cannot confirm or disconfirm it.

(166–167) The world must already be for me, must already have the sense "world," for animals or the insane entering my horizon to be understood as subjects experiencing the world abnormally, and for animals to be understood as having, in themselves and with their kind, a concordant world of experience that is not a world beside "our" world but their mode of appearance of "the" world, an intentional modification of the world we humans have. Ours cannot be corrected by theirs; it is not an apparent or appearing world that could conflict with ours, since it is not and cannot be co-constituting for what we call world. World simply, actual world, is the exclusive correlate of us humans as unity of our human, normal and abnormal human, modes of appearance. The order of founding settles the question: the animal world presupposes ours, since it is understood as a modification of it; therefore it cannot correct ours. This is the strongest anthropocentric thesis of the volume, and it is immediately put to the test.

(167) But is this really right, since one might object that if animals are understood as relating to the same world, they could occasionally function as co-constituting? If the dog is understood as scenting game, he teaches us what we did not know; he extends our world of experience. The dog has its own concordant world-experience, originally and mediately. Understanding it so, does that not mean establishing a synthesis between it and our human experience, so that world-actuality is the unity of experience extending synthetically through all human and animal experiences? The hunting dog is a counterexample: the dog's scenting gives us information about the world, which is co-constitution in a minimal sense. The objection is serious and Husserl states it without immediate rebuttal.

(167) The same holds, it will be objected, of "primitives." They are even humans; why not count them constitutively? Admittedly, as we learn to understand, they have their own logic and categories, guiding their identifications and their sense of realities, and so their world-apperception. But are they not related in their way to the one world, the world for all, whether primitives, animals or "normal" humans? Each animal species and each kind of primitive has its way in which the same world appears to it. Can we not, re-understanding their way, translate their world-apperception into ours and so establish which things and events of the world given to us as demonstrable reality correspond to their apperceptions? The second objection generalizes: if alien world-apperceptions can be translated, they become contributions to one world. Husserl's report of a "primitive logic and categories" reflects Lévy-Bruhl's "pre-logical mentality"; the possibility of translation is the possibility of a single world across mentalities, the problem of Beilage XLVIII.

(167) But where do I, reflecting, get what is pre-given as world, this world as being and being-so, and in its explication its identity and actuality over against the changing apperceptions of normal people, animals, primitives, and finally the insane? The reply to the objections is a methodological regress: the identity of the world against all these apperceptions has its source in my own constituting experience.

(167–168) I explicate, questioning myself, the world always pre-given to me, which in the course of my experience "appears" anew, offers new fields, is judged anew, and despite its changing sense counts as the one world and must confirm itself in the concordance of experience and knowledge founded on it, with occasional corrections. For an existing world is and contains only "beings" from the achievement of confirmation, whose lowest level is mere concordance. Being is the result of confirmation, whose base is concordance; this principle will decide the objections.

(168) If I reflect on what confirms itself as existing world in my streaming life in actual and possible evidence, and on the order of immediate and mediate evidences and so on the founding order of propositions and validities, I come to my actual and possible perceptual fields, fields of memory as former perceptual fields, future fields, all connecting into the unity of my original experience and constituting the world as experienced, having been and to be experienced by me. The world that constantly has sense from my own experience contains other people, whose bodies are really experienceable, whose psychic and personal being is experienceable in a secondary, no longer original way, through "experience of others," confirmable in the concordance of this secondary mode. The founding order runs from my original experience to the secondary experience of others. Since animals' experience is accessible only through a further modified empathy, its confirming power for the world is derived from ours.

(168–169) In my extended experience, which brings the pre-given world to the evidence of self-presentation, there occur birth, ageing, illness, death, the generative, and accordingly I understand myself as someday dying and once born. I experience older people born before me and expect new generations after me and my contemporaries. I understand humans in the world as generatively connected in open endlessness both ways, and understand that the same world I experience was experienced through the endless chain of generations, confirmed in connection by concordant experience and mutual correction, and will be. Through the life-units of humans bounded by birth and death extends the unity of a human life as unity of a mediated but connected all-human experience and tradition founded on it. So I understand humanity as historical, world-time with its worldly events reaching beyond my lifetime and that of my contemporaries. The world's identity across generations is the identity of a tradition of experience. I know my own birth and death only through the generative world, as events I infer from others' and that I "understand" about myself, the theme of Beilage VIII.

(169) However incomplete and rough this explication, I gain a first understanding, to be explicated in its horizons, that the world which is for me in advance, always pre-given, is co-constituted by the other people of my and their humanity who gain validity in my conscious life. Husserl's note: but the peculiarity of a humanity, a world, concretely a cultural world shaping itself from and of persons, is not yet clarified! Chain-wise communication would give no tradition that would be tradition for all, a community of spiritual acquisitions accessible to all, already horizonally prefigured for all as "the actual world." What is missing: homeworld, alien homeworlds that are valid not for us but for them. From there the way to relativization, but also the problem of a new world for all, all "humanities." Perhaps the achievement of method for an unconditionally objective science, exact natural science first, and the problem of objective human science. The note is a programme for Part III. A tradition common to all requires more than chains of communication; it requires the homeworld as a shared horizon. The relativity of homeworlds then poses the problem of a world for all humanities, answered by the method of unconditionally objective science, which the Krisis will analyse as Galilean idealization.

(169–170) I also find primitives, animals, the insane in my world, but I find that I do not experience them as my likes and "our likes." Those I have in mind in saying "we humans" I understand starting from the narrower and narrowest "we" of my acquaintances, whom I had determinately before me, more or less perfectly. But I understand them without further ado (and this understanding enters, determining sense, into the open horizon of the unknown who might become known) as co-constituting my world, fellow persons of my "humanity." Starting, as said, from my nearest, fully familiar, with whom my life unfolds, first in a layer of everydayness: experiencing together, deliberating, working together, being considerate, discussing, quarrelling, agreeing, but also eating together, living together, playing together. Husserl's note: in levels of homeworldliness; so far as we do not understand one another or remain "strangers," we do not have the same world. The phrase "humanity" in quotation marks is now marked as a home-humanity; the note makes the decisive move: the same world extends as far as understanding. Strangeness is non-sharing of world. The note continues: I and my fellows are always already constituted in the horizon of my home-humanity; the world is pre-given with the sense of the world of my, "our humanity," which is itself real in it; this horizon is correlative to the horizon of my humanity, first home-humanity, then super-nation and earthly humanity, open, but in life there are, according to the human attitude, "different we's" and different "existing worlds." The "we" is built from the most intimate, everyday practices, eating, dwelling, playing, the concrete stuff of a homeworld.

(170) Living thus naively in the horizon of my humanity, I experience the others of my nearest everyday surrounding world as co-experiencing the same or as able to co-experience and confirm everything I experience. I experience them as co-determining my "world-representation" through expression, especially their communications. This concerns all their doing that, affecting what we have already constituted in common, transforms it in practice, gives it purpose or means significance or other sense. In this community of life I understand without further ado, so far as I attend to the conditions of understanding (being near enough to perceive their expressions and communications), their tools as tools, their works as works, the begun and the finished work with its purpose. I understand purposes partly as having individual significance for them, partly as addressed to "everyone" and understood by everyone as generally purposive. I understand that what I understand in my surrounding world everyone understands, so that this surrounding world, pre-given for me, is pre-given for everyone, understood by everyone in the same sense, and constantly re-shapes itself in each one's life and in the life of all as a mutual working on one another mediated by mutual understanding, with a sense of being to which all contribute. Husserl's note: that is relative: we Germans, we Europeans. The text ends with the naive homeworld: everything is understandable without further ado, and every purpose understood as for "everyone." The marginal "we Germans, we Europeans" punctures the naivety: this "everyone" is a particular people. The text thus ends where Part III's theory of homeworld and alien world begins.

### Appendix VIII. Problem: Generativity, Birth and Death as Essential Occurrences for the Constitution of the World (Early 1930s)

(171) It must be shown that birth and death must count as constitutive occurrences enabling the constitution of the world, or as an essential part of a constituted world, that is, generativity with birth and death. In building up the achievement of empathy as experience of others and of myself among others, I first exhibit others' actual and possible experience as a mode of presentification that has validity of being and is modified relative to my primordial-original experience. The thesis to be proved is that generativity is eidetic, not factual: a world without birth and death is not a possible world. The starting point is the constitution of the other's experience as presentification.

(171) As long as "other" memory coincides with my possible memory, as long as my memory has only the limit of forgetting, my inability to remember being mere forgetfulness that leaves open the potentiality of remembering the forgotten, the constitution of a world is not satisfied. That would be as if generativity, with birth and death, were a contingent fact of the world. If the other's past were only my possible past, the world's past would be bounded by my possible memory, and history beyond my life would be impossible. The world's time requires memories that I could never have had, not merely forgotten ones.

(171) Empathy originally yields only the other, perhaps with experiences actual and possible that I partly have or could have had. His past of memory reaches further, as I can understand, but in re-understanding it has a sense for me that does not exclude its memories being possible for me. So too the future entry of others into my circle: they are others I will or could experience. My "human" being among humans is my anticipated and certain being with them. Now death and birth enter experience at this level, forming sense for humans and world. The future certainty of my own being as world-living human among humans and of each other's being gets an insurmountable limit, and correlatively the certainty of memory concerning human past existence. Empathy at the first level preserves the illusion that everything others experienced I could have experienced; birth and death break it. With them the world acquires a past before me and a future after me that are not my possible experience, and only then is world-time truly transcendent to my life.

(171) But now world and birth and death, generativity, must be put seriously into essential relation, showing how this is not a fact, how a world and humans without birth and death are unthinkable. The appendix ends with the task, not its execution; the proof is attempted in No. 22 and Beilage XLII, where the eidos of the transcendental ego is shown to imply generative intersubjectivity.

### Appendix IX. Important Consideration on Constitutive Genesis. Essentially Different Concepts of Empathy (Probably Early 1931)

Kern notes that Husserl later dated the manuscript 1933, probably mistakenly, since he worked on analogous problems in August and September 1933 (Nos. 34 and 35); the text probably dates from January 1931.

(172) Husserl's table of contents: first, the unwinding of the founding levels of world-validity lying psychologically in the ego (which is "human" and has a human surrounding world). This leads to childhood development, but not into early childhood and its embryonic development. Second, the biological problems from there. They are at the same time problems of "empathy": first, the psychic that can be intuitively re-understood and from which the world for all is understandable to all; second, modified empathy, yielding analogues of the human, first of the mature; modified empathy that also modifies the developmental stages childhood, maturity, early childhood. Limit of modification. "Symbolically" constructed stages. Where does analogy end? Limit of analogizing. And all this included in my, the ego's, constitution of the world. Species development, primal cells. The outline separates two geneses: the static-genetic unwinding of validity within the mature ego, and the biological genesis of the human (early childhood, embryo, species), accessible only by modified, ultimately "symbolic" empathy. The limit of analogy is the methodological theme.

(172) Constitutive genesis in worldliness, the genesis lying in the phenomenologizing ego as human: psychological genesis. Second, biological genesis, going back to generativity and the genesis of generativity as species development; on the other side, going back to early childhood. That leads to essentially different concepts of empathy. Two directions of regress, ontogenetic and phylogenetic, each requiring a different form of empathy.

(172) First, the world is constituted as universe of beings. Constitution is in levels: higher-level being founded in existing being. Constitution here is thematizing achievement, proceeding in theses. "Genesis" of the existing world, which continues to constitute itself; its being is constant continued constitution in ever new achievement of constituting subjectivity, but it is always world, the world, and all newly constituted being is worldly. The genetic lower levels are active achievements that constitute pre-worldly being, in this sense pre-being (Vor-sein). The first genesis is the genesis of validity within the world: thetic, active, building being on being. Its lower levels are "pre-being" relative to the full world, but still within the frame of the world.

(172) Second, over against this, another genesis and another pre-being and "constitution." The second genesis is non-thetic, pre-active, the genesis before the world, whose "constitution" is in quotation marks because it does not constitute objects.

(172–173) In the first genesis we have the world for us humans, the genesis in me, the ego that is "human" as having world-certainty. Dissolution of the levels of validity as levels of founding of being and genetic regress to preliminary stages of being demonstrable in the ego: what can be shown as psychologically constituting, which itself lies in worldliness as psychic, as acts and act-theses presupposed as founding world-validity. The first genesis remains within psychology: its levels are acts, demonstrable in the mature ego by reflection.

(173) Worldly: we stand in generativity as normal humans and in the childhood and development of every human ego and its world from childhood. But "human childhood." Actual childhood and its psychic development do not belong here: this psychic life is not that of the empathy that gives humans for us and the world understandable to all humans intuitively, as subjective correlate belonging to the constitution of the existing world. Human activity, from which the world "is," is there for us with its content, but also always was and, in every active grasp in which something is actually for us, is presupposed as it was (being that always already was). The childhood relevant to the first genesis is "human childhood," the childhood the adult can understand and remember, already world-having. Actual early childhood belongs to the second genesis, which empathy proper does not reach. The world is always "already been," presupposed by every activity: the "always already" of pre-givenness.

(173) Early childhood in its stages, up to the childhood that has a world. Early childhood and animal being. Animal species, higher and lower animals in stages of distance and understandability from man, each with its mature-animal normal world for the species, parallel to the mature-human normal world, with subordinate stages of childhood and early childhood: animal generativity parallel to human. The universe of animals that are still psychophysical analogues of humans. Husserl's note: as against this, plants? Then before the born child the unborn, its psychic genesis back to conception.
