Limit of the modification of interpretation from empathy. Stages of development to be reconstructed symbolically. Parallel in all animals that are analogues of humans, having bodies with sense organs like humans. But where does analogy end? Are single-celled organisms not psychophysical too, do they not have bodies as organs of their "ego-pole"? There the analogy ends in the limit. Another parallel: the whole order and development of species, phylogenetically back to the primal organisms. The second genesis is reached only by "symbolic" reconstruction, an empathy modified beyond intuitive limits: the embryo, the single cell, the evolutionary series. Husserl allows a limit-concept of an "ego-pole" even in protozoa, but marks it as the limit where analogy expires. Plants are left as a question. The passage is the textual basis for later phenomenological biology (Hans Jonas) and for the idea that evolutionary history is a constructed extension of empathy.

(173) Pre-being, pre-worlds, pre-subjectivity as pre-constituting, pre-genesis. But all this ordered into our human world, and all pre-constitution itself belonging to the universal unity of world-constitution as founding. The final formula keeps the transcendental priority: pre-worlds and pre-subjectivity are constituted within our world as its founding past. The genesis "before" the world is still a genesis for the world, the position No. 23 develops as the "veiled" history of transcendental intersubjectivity in natural history.

### Appendix X. World and We. Human and Animal Surrounding World (1934)

Husserl's note on the manuscript: continuation and deepening of the problems of No. 11; also a deepening of the doctrine of the temporalization of the world, which is only a human world and has sense as such. All important. The final section, from the questions about animal memory onward, is an addition of July or August 1934.

(174) First, the natural world-life of the human being in his familiar surrounding world, his homeworld as horizon of life; natural world-life in the first sense, in which humanity still lacks theoretical life with its bearers, scientists and scientific communities, and the transformation of humanity and its world by scientific acquisitions taken up into it. The first stage is the pre-scientific homeworld, the life-world in its original, pre-theoretical form.

(174) Second, scientific life, first instituted in individuals as habitual "theoretical interest," working out as theoretical action aimed at lasting acquisitions that become premises for higher ones; its personal effect in founding widening communities of "theoreticians." Natural interest-life in its typical variety, with its typology of personalities and groups (vocations among them), changes in its general typology as a new vocation, that of theory, of science, enters the circle of vocations with its typical interest. Natural world-life in the second sense, influenced by positive science. Husserl's note: "science" at first in my home-humanity, without regard to alien humanities, which may be regarded as madmen, fools, abnormal and left out of account. The emergence of theory is described as the founding of a new vocation, the narrative of the Vienna lecture (1935) on the Greek origin of philosophy. The note records with irony the attitude of a home-humanity toward the alien: they "may be regarded as madmen," that is, abnormal, which ties the theory of normality to ethnocentrism as its natural, pre-reflective form.

(174) Persons already had their personal temporality, in the form of various "dominant" interests, each working out in "its" time. The person's life is unified so far as all her interests are at one in her, but not merely collectively and externally alternating in personal time, perhaps periodically (working hours, weekdays). Interests interweave in content into complex, higher-level ones and influence each other, so that each finally transforms the whole course of life and the habituality of the whole personality. Personal time is structured by interests, which are not merely scheduled side by side but integrated. The theoretical interest, once instituted, therefore transforms the whole person.

(174–175) To this must be added: interest-life is life toward goals of the will, practical life. That is life in success and failure, which motivates deliberation on possibilities, choice. To it belongs reflection on being and non-being and the removal of modalities of certainty, a doxic life in the service of practice as far as it demands: here appear "it is true" as "it really is so," "false," "mere illusion," "possible," "impossible," but not in the theoretical sense. Practical life has its own concept of truth, serving action. Husserl's claim that the modal vocabulary of truth exists already in practice is the thesis of Formal and Transcendental Logic's appendix on "situational truths" and of Beilage XII.

(175) Even practical life in the ordinary sense, excluding theoretical praxis, has its truth, its correlate of what is actually in and for itself over against changing opinions and apprehensions, even in perception or recollection. Practical truth already has an "in itself": what is really so, as against opinion.

(175) The theoretical in-itself, correlate of theoretical-predicative truth, is a heightening of extra- and pre-theoretical truth. It springs from a theoretical interest, interest purely in being and being-so, not in the singular but in the total: for a single real thing, in complete knowledge of its whole being-so or of its type, first its inner determinations, then its real relations, spreading without limit over the world. Or it is interest directed unpractically from the start at the whole surrounding world without limit, at the totality of types of beings and the universal forms concerning this typology and the realities and configurations that keep presenting themselves, proceeding from generality into concrete particulars and then determining the individual newly entering view on the basis of acquired general knowledge. On the other side, overcoming the relativity of opinions, even of experiencing, confirming opinions, by methods of observation, and the disclosure of inaccessible distances by induction and its methods of confirmation, to reach secure certainties or at least probabilities. Linguistically: formation of a theoretical language that serves not only communication but the securing of acquisitions objectively accessible and testable by everyone, in a testable method itself documented and secured in language. Theory is defined by universality of interest (total, not local), by method overcoming relativity, and by a language that secures testability for everyone. The account is the core of the Krisis narrative of the theoretical attitude, and the role of documenting language anticipates Origin of Geometry.

(175) All this as science, theory of the world or of its regions: science in the first sense, correlatively theoretical interest, truth, true being (being in itself) in the first sense. Through it already the natural first surrounding world and human world-life are transformed, taking the theoretical into their apperception at various levels. It is ready to be taken up by everyone who learns science, as far as he can, conditioned by his subjective misunderstandings. Science feeds back into the life-world, which "apperceives the theoretical," the sedimentation the Krisis describes (§ 34 e).

(176) Third, this theoretical interest, science, truth, is in one respect still relative-subjective. The world is a world for everyone, identifiable by everyone in connection with everyone. It is pre-given horizonally to me, who speak of the world and have passed from practical attention to particular beings into a universal attitude toward the universe; a horizon starting from me, my body, its core of perceptual presences, into ever more remote and less intuitive perception, endlessly into a non-intuitive domain of possible experience of realities. All this as a domain of possible experience, individual and common, for everyone in possible communication with me, experiencing and thinking with me, exchanging acquisitions, in mutual correction. Even theoretical truth remains relative to the communicating community and its horizon centred on my body. The third stage, announced here, will be transcendental science, which reflects on this relativity.

(176) I have an open horizon of immediate and mediate actual and possible community with others, who are themselves in possible community with one another and have an open horizon of others. So I have in constant certainty an open universe of my others, my universal "we," as one that every other ego of this "we" has as his "we." It is the universe of subjectivity, which as universe of community, of persons in "communication," is the universal subjectivity to which the real world existing for me is related as mine, ours, everyone's, existing for everyone from his own primal-original experiences, but not from these alone, rather from appropriation of others' experiences under constant complementation and correction, and existing as long as further own and communicative experience demands no correction. To the world in this constant horizonality, anticipated as existing universe but never gaining final total sense through factual experience, belong the subjects themselves as constantly experienced and experienceable by one another as psychophysical objects. Husserl's note: my homeworld, my people. The universe in its first form as homeworld only stands out when other homeworlds, other peoples, are already in the horizon. The life-world in the horizon of alien life-worlds, my people surrounded by alien peoples. The universal "we" is the correlate of the world. The note insists that this "universal" "we" is at first a homeworld whose particularity becomes visible only through the alien: the homeworld is constituted as such by contrast, the thesis of Part III.

(176–177) The correlation of our world and us as the subjectivity knowing it, related to it in consciousness and shaping its current sense, is itself a "developmental" member of an open transformation of the sense of this correlation, or of transformations of the "we" and thereby of the world's sense as existing for us, as the world in which we live as humans. Husserl's note: "development" can mean a) historical development of an open horizon of known alien peoples, who may have their alien peoples in turn, up to the last? That may remain unconsidered, or other motivation leads to infinity. b) Development of concrete intercourse. In a), already the constitution of a "nature" extending beyond one's own territory (soil, sky, plants, animals) on which the alien field of life also lies. Through b), in concrete intercourse, the need for a synthesis of validity of one's own and the alien (only partly understood) life-world. The world-"we" correlation is historical: both terms change. The note gives two mechanisms: extension of the horizon to known alien peoples, which constitutes a common nature beyond territories, and practical intercourse, which compels the synthesis of one's own and the alien world. Nature appears as the common ground first constituted by the extension of horizons, an observation central to No. 27.

(177) To clarify: "we" means we humans. As humans we are and count to ourselves as persons, and that means persons of their humanity. Not all persons belong to one and the same humanity, and yet they do, depending on how humanity is understood. If the world is world for all of us, then depending on how "all of us" is understood, the world is concretely the same or not, since humanity sometimes has a sense including uniqueness, sometimes a pluralizable sense. In the latter case each humanity has its world (its life-world, its surrounding world), and if all these surrounding worlds "form" one totality in development or reflection, or rather count as "modes of appearance" of the one world for these humanities, then this one world is in principle not surrounding world for these humanities in the same sense, as if it were correlate of a "we" presupposed as actual. But this is only a necessary first indication. The distinction is precise. "Humanity" is either unique (all humans) or plural (humanities); correspondingly "world" is either the one world or plural life-worlds. The one world is not a life-world of a super-"we"; it is the unity of life-worlds as their modes of appearance. This blocks reading the one world as the homeworld of an all-humanity.

(177–178) Let us build an understanding of humanity and surrounding world step by step. Animals are, like us, subjects of a conscious life in which a "surrounding world" is given to them as theirs in certainty. Subjecthood relates to their anima. Their animal conscious life is centred, and in speaking of a "subject for consciousness" lies an analogue, or something more general, of the human ego of its cogitations of cogitata, for which we lack a suitable word. Husserl's note: generality arises as assimilating apperception, as a basic mode of intentional modification. The animal too has something like an ego-structure. But man has it in a unique sense over against all related animal ego-particularizations: his ego, the ego in the ordinary sense, is personal, and so man is a person for himself and all fellows. Being a person among persons he is, as the word already means, a person of a humanity closed in itself, yet conscious for each person as an open-endless horizon, a totality "all of us." Husserl's further note: this "open-endless" does not mean an absolute iteration, infinity in the pregnant sense; it corresponds to a set with no last member specifiable for the subject, no "countability" (not in the mathematical sense); such a naturally constituted humanity is for its persons not a factually given totality of acquaintances but an indeterminately countless totality. The animal has an ego-structure without being a person; personhood implies membership in a humanity. The notes are exact: the general concept "subject" covering animals is formed by assimilating apperception; the openness of humanity is not mathematical infinity but indeterminate countlessness, the lived infinity of an open horizon, not an actual infinite.

(178) Every human being, so far as he lives in his world-consciousness, as ego-subject for the world certain to him, is for himself a person in the endlessly open generative nexus, in the chaining and branching of generations. In it he is, and knows himself, as child of his parents, matured through upbringing by them and by communicative mature co-subjects, now himself functioning as co-educator, co-determining their personal being directly or mediately, perhaps himself father or mother. The generative nexus includes newborns, "early children," so to speak embryos understood as preliminary stages of children proper. Children proper are pre-persons in stages before maturity, which is a point of completion in the type person. They already have something of a real surrounding world (unlike the embryonic stage), but not yet the world related fully to an "all of us," to humanity. It is hard to express sharply this being a person that is not yet fully actual personhood, which it is only in maturity. It will be clarified from another side. Personhood is generative: one knows oneself as someone's child and someone's educator. Children are "pre-persons," with a real surrounding world but not yet a humanity-related world, the developmental counterpart of the levels of No. 10.

(178) But first it must be stressed: "we and our world" does not refer to the concretely full (psychophysically understood) generative nexus, which is more certain for us; excluded are children, and the mentally ill and the sick generally, as long as they live in abnormality. At least they no longer count fully, only so far as their co-living is partly co-functioning with the fully normal. Only the mature, as normal human persons in the unity of their communicative life with the unitary form of their personal temporality, are the subjects for the world that is theirs. Each counts in its temporal being, the stretch of this generative-communicative time, only as regards its normality, in which alone it functions constitutively for "the" world, the one universe of beings for "all of us." The "old" too, so far as retired, no longer counselling and active, count here as abnormal, like the sick. The constituting "we" is restricted to the mature normal, and even the retired elderly fall out of it, a striking extension that shows normality here to mean active participation in the communicative life of world-constitution, not health.

(178–179) How then is the unity of "one" humanity and its surrounding world circumscribed? How the personal totality constituted for me and my fellows as inner purely personal connection in the form of totality, and how does the world itself, in which all subjects are embodied mundane realities, psychophysical realities in which the personal is localized as soul and temporalized in nature's space-time, contrast with this total subjectivity for the world? How do personal time and real natural time contrast? The questions oppose two aspects of humanity: personal totality connected by communication, and psychophysical population in nature, with two times, personal-historical and natural.

(179) Transcendentally, this corresponds to monadic time as the form of the universe of monads and to the time of the world constituted in it, with its remarkable duality of personal and real. But note that, correlatively to the connected totality of persons, which does not include all human persons, distributed as they are over various humanities and surrounding worlds "alien" to one another, the transcendentally corresponding universe of monads is also a special totality. Each humanity corresponds to a "special universe" of monads; the transcendental universe is articulated as the human is, into homeworld-totalities.

(179) But a transcendental problematic not yet considered opens here. As humanities are not side by side when viewed personally rather than as realities, as the relation of home-humanity and alien is an intentional relation of validity that must lead to union in a super-nation, so in transcendentality there lies a problematic intentional articulation in monadic being (itself already constituted) and in every universe of monads functioning as homely for itself a tendency toward progressive constitution of a supra-homely universe uniting its aliens with it, a home-universe of higher level. And with it the constitution, progressing through territory, of a supra-homely nature with an organic-animal world. Above all this concerns the constitution of an all-monadic time and of real time, space-time, both in their constituted infinity. And the ultimate sense of this infinity and of these transcendental and human-personal processes? The relation of home and alien is a teleological tendency toward union at a higher level, nation into super-nation, homeworld into higher homeworld, and this tendency exists transcendentally among monadic totalities. Infinite time and space are its constituted correlates. The final question about the "ultimate sense" points to the teleology of No. 22–23.

(179) Humans as "highest animal species," zoologically, subjectively. The phrase records the naturalistic self-apprehension of humans within zoology, which the next paragraphs contrast with personal generativity.

(179) The generative nexus of humans like that of the animals of a species, and phylogenetically all animal species connected into a generative unity of descent, finally organic beings in general; followed biophysically, but also biopsychically. Husserl's note: but also purely psychically, purely "monadically," the word now not taken transcendentally. Biological descent can be followed as a psychic, "monadic" history, an idea Husserl's late texts entertain cautiously (No. 35 and Beilage XLVI): evolutionary history as a history of monads.

(179–180) Over against this, another generativity or "descent," exclusively proper to man, the personal being. Man living as person among persons, deliberately connecting himself with them and always already naturally connected as mature person; natural personal associations as parts and layers of higher natural associations culminating in a total association, a people. Husserl's note: from the outset every mature person has the total horizon "people." In this horizon all purposive connections and agreements take place in the world-life of man as member of a people. All "social" acts and associations are within the horizon of the people. Personal generativity culminates in a people (Volk) as total association, the natural horizon of all social acts. The concept is descriptive and generative, not political; the note's point is that social acts presuppose a total horizon of belonging.

(180) Here the fundamental problems. Animal community, animal social life, considered purely spiritually, living in their "surrounding world," each species in its specific one. Each animal has its "spiritual" development from embryonic beginning to maturity, and in it builds up the surrounding world "existing" for it. But it does not mature into a person, and its surrounding world is not human; a human surrounding world is not just a more differentiated animal one, as differences between lower and higher animals are. One can only say that in the human surrounding world and in man as its subject there is an abstractly distinguishable layer that may be singled out as the animal in it, common with the animal (which requires closer investigation). The human differs in kind, not degree; yet an "animal layer" in man may be abstractable, the problem of a stratified anthropology.

(180) Even a cursory comparison of animals and humans (both occurring in the human surrounding world, yet understood as subjects of their own) shows: man as person is subject of a cultural world, correlate of the personal all-community in which each person knows herself and knows herself related to her humane world. The animal does not live, knowing itself, in a cultural world. To this belongs that man is a historical being; he lives in a "humanity" that exists in historical, history-creating becoming; it is the subjectivity bearing the historical world, an expression that then means not the historically living, history-constituting life, but the environmental correlate, humane surrounding world bearing spiritual meaning from man and total humanity, as a title for ontic properties of realities and their ontic historicity, having this meaning from human action, interests, purposes and systems of purposes. Culture and history are the distinguishing marks, and "historical world" designates the ontic historicity of things, their sedimented purposive sense.

(180–181) Each animal generation in its communalized present repeats its specific surrounding world with the typology proper to the species. A human cultural world is in constant development; the culture of each human present is ground, premise, for the new cultural creation of the new generation. Purposive formations carry their purposive sense as a property; understood in it, they motivate new purposes on the basis of the old or their fulfilment-forms. The cultural face of the world has a typology that repeats itself concretely, or seems to, but for man tempora mutantur et nos mutamur in illis. The times are the times really filled in unitary human time with realities purposively shaped in each case. The concrete typology changes in repetition; some concrete types drop out, though the changes leave a general total type of the surrounding world and of social human existence. Animal generations repeat; human generations accumulate. Culture is premise for further culture: the logic of sedimentation and reactivation. The Latin tag (times change and we change with them) states historicity compactly.

(181) Human works are effected by action according to possibilities represented in advance and valid to man as within his ability. Man has projects (Entwürfe), choice among valid possibilities, and decides for what is recognized as best. He joins others in communal action by communalizing volitions and their goals, which are purposes for him, private and communal. Projection, choice and communal will characterize human action. "Projects" (Entwürfe) is a term Husserl shares with Heidegger's Being and Time, here used in the ordinary practical sense.

(181) The animal realizes "instincts" in community; its doing is instinctive, the communalization of its striving instinctive. The bee does not act, has no purposes; the beehive is no community of purposes, does not stand in the unity of a life that is human purposive life, borne by subjects who put their purposes habitually and by renewal of intentions into function again, identifying them as the same. Husserl's note: these are only indications for the real clarification of person and humanity with its surrounding world. There must follow the clarification of the finitude of a humanity and of the distinction of home and alien, our humanity and people and others alien to us, and the relatively unified groups within a humanity, "we" and others. Then the anticipation of the chaining of surrounding worlds into the "infinite," the constitution of super-nations and worlds on the basis of "empathic" worlds, always from one's own; the problems of infinity and the idea of the one infinite world as idea of a universal in-itself, universal exact nature infinitely, and humanities related to it infinitely on the way to one humanity and one culture as lying in the infinite. But over against this, actual, earthly humanity, and fate, chance. Then: a) science in the first sense; b) science in the second sense, related to infinity, as naive construction; c) science in the third sense, absolute science. Likewise life: natural-first finite life; second, under the guidance of first science; third, in the horizon of infinite nature; fourth, in the transcendental horizon. The bee lacks identification of purposes across time, hence purposive community. The note is a synopsis of a whole philosophy of history: finitude of a humanity, home and alien, the idea of infinite humanity and one culture, set against the contingency ("fate, chance") of actual earthly humanity; three sciences (finite, idealizing-infinite, transcendental) and four forms of life. It is the plan of the Krisis in miniature, and the contrast between infinite idea and earthly fate is a rare admission of tragic contingency.

(181–182) An animal does not create, in the unity of its life, a system of spiritual acquisitions it experiences as development; it has no unity of a time spanning generations as historical time and of a world running through it; it does not "have" these consciously. It is we humans who hold the chains, the endless successions and branchings of ant generations, in validity in our world. The animal itself has no generative world in which it lives consciously, no conscious existence in an open infinity of generations, and correlatively no existence in a surrounding world proper, which we humans ascribe to it by humanizing it. The animal's "world" is our construct: the generative chain of animals exists for us, not for them. Husserl's position is close to Heidegger's "world-poor" thesis but grounded in the absence of recollection and generative self-understanding rather than in captivation.

(182–183) We find animals in our world through an empathy that is an assimilating modification of empathy with fellow humans. Assimilation to human subjects first concerns the understanding of animal corporeality as such, and so the basic layer of empathy that constitutes fellow humans as living into a common surrounding world. What must belong to the empathized ego for empathy to occur at all is the "basic layer": assimilation of the other's physical body as lived body like mine, the apprehension of its organ system as perceptual and practical organs through which the perceptual surroundings are there for the animal, as a unified field of identical things, each a unity of modes of appearance; but such that these modes are only similar to mine and every human's, as they are exchanged communicatively among humans and yield in the lower layer common human nature, a universe of appearance-unities that are unities of all our communalizable modes of appearance, actual and possible. This whole human system and human nature is presupposed for us to experience animals as animals and gain empathy as perception of animals. The primal-generative development of man, in which he grows into first self-consciousness and consciousness of surroundings, the first "I and surrounding world," yields this first ego already as ego of a "we" and the surrounding world as common to this "we." Others are not mere reduplications of the ego; assimilation requires constantly correcting modification, and others are understood in advance as analogues in undetermined generality with an individual-typical core. The modes of appearance of things belong to this, and the horizons have their concrete typology and are "projected" in it. This already changes in a way when empathizing with animal existence. The animal (in its species) does not sense quite as we do. Yet it perceives the same things in its way; assimilation still holds: appearances of the same, a ruling ego in the analogous body, and whatever else of the required analogy survives without being modified away. For each species a modified type of "ego and surrounding world," for me already "we humans and surrounding world," hence of the animal ego in the "we." We humans stand out in the "we" by having concretely typically the same corporeality, and in our constitution as ego and "we" we are already constituted, by generative origin, in this typical corporeality: I am already in self-consciousness the ego of this typical body; I already have my human horizon in the typology of my family, and my body already has the sense of a body typical for all of us. Animal empathy is a modification of human empathy and presupposes the whole human system of common nature. Two theses are remarkable. The first ego is "already ego of a we": in primal-generative development self-consciousness arises together with a communal world. And the typical human body belongs to the self-consciousness of the ego from the outset: I know myself as a body of the human type, in the typology of my family. Generative typicality is part of the constitution of the self, which undercuts any picture of a pre-social primordial ego in genesis.

(183) The problem of the animal: the animal is a new, another subject, but other than us humans, yet in being other analogous to us, as lions among lions in a generativity of life analogous to ours. Otherwise, living in the world, the lion world constituted in appearance for lions, living into it, having needs and satisfying them, hunger and thirst, eating and drinking, sexual intercourse, all this is assimilated without further ado, human life in human generativity and surrounding world, as far as analogizing goes and confirms itself in experience. The "lion world" is an analogue of the human world, constituted by projecting our life-functions, needs, sexuality, generation, as far as experience confirms the analogy.

(183) But essential differences arise. Do bees live in their surrounding world generatively as we do? Do bees develop like us as "children" growing spiritually into the world of the mature? Or take animals nearer us, mammals: is a fawn, a foal, a child that develops as a human child does? Biophysically no great difficulty, but psychically. We meet the instinctive, but instincts play a constant role in humans too, not only in childhood. Instinct is first a title for externally characterized facts, but from within it has its unintelligibilities. Where is the limit? Are the bees' buildings in their "purposiveness" real purposive formations, rational in the sense of goal-setting and calculating means? The "bee state," the ant state, ant slavery and wars: how do the analogical interpretations in these words stand? The words of human sociality applied to insects (state, slavery, war) carry an unexamined analogy. Instinct is an external concept that phenomenology must understand from within, for animals and for humans.

(183) One can ask (the addition of 1934 begins here): do animals (pets excluded) have genuine recollections, intuitively repeating, and intuitive phantasies in the same sense as we? The criterion of the human is now sought in recollection and phantasy, the capacities of re-presentation.

(183) Do they have horizons they can clarify intuitively as we can? Ideas of goals and purposes as representing models of the future as satisfying, as end of a practical way itself intuitively represented? Without presentification there are no explicable horizons and no represented goals.

(184) Can dark drives with drive-fulfilments that do not become representations take their place, namely in renewed actualization of the drive? Is the original temporalization in the period of human primal childhood of this kind, this animal kind? How is world-temporalization built in stream-temporalization, first as temporalization of hyletic contents? Is it already, in the primal child, a real temporalization of beings? Drive-intentionality may replace representation in animals and in early infancy. The question whether the infant's time-constitution is already constitution of beings anticipates Beilage XLV and the C-manuscripts' "primal child" (Urkind).

(184) Does the animal level have "innate" representations of things and world, manifold perceptual appearances normally combining into concordant unity, occasionally discordant and inhibiting, so that each striving acquires the character of the inhibited: intentionality understood as purely drive-like, directed by drive toward concordance? Concordance itself might be the goal of a drive, the animal "world" a drive-correlate, not a represented world. This is the idea of a universal drive toward concordance that No. 34 develops.

(184) Do animals then know nothing of the surrounding world we ascribe to them in naive empathy? They have the past only as retentionality and sameness of things only in the form of primary recognition, which does not yet know a going back into the past in recollection (as conscious re-perceiving) or an identification of temporal and spatial positions that makes the individuality of things as beings possible. Without recollection there is no identification of positions, hence no individuals, hence no world of beings. Animals have retention and primary recognition, the minimal identity of the familiar.

(184) The animal lacks the capacity for consciousness or knowledge of an existing world, a world of persisting things persisting through time and change, with causality, singly and unitarily through universal space-time, identifiable by temporal and spatial position, by past and anticipated-presentified future. Getting to know, projecting possibilities, willing, producing, effecting, works, purposive formations, communicative formations that make the same communicable again and again: all excluded. Animals have no "proposition" in the narrower or widest sense. Animals communicate, understand utterances, and yet have no language. Language requires the identical, repeatable proposition, which requires recollection and identification; animal communication lacks the ideal identity of sense. This links language to the "again and again" of ideal objects (Origin of Geometry).

(184–185) In man passive intentionality is constantly transformed into activity from the capacity of repetition. Is this sharp division correct? How can it be made intelligible why the animal has no genuine memory, no repeating intuitions as repeating perceptions and with the capacity of "again and again," and so no constitution of beings in the form of temporality? Man has "reason"; is what was just said the lowest level of "rationality"? Man, the animal rationale, necessarily interprets "blindly instinctive," purely drive-like intentionality first as constituting a surrounding world, as if animals were a kind of lesser human, as if they too had beings, connections of being and purposes directed at a pre-given world, representations of what ought to be, as if instead of their feelings, mere modes of blind drive-life, they had human valuing directed at values and goods. The capacity of repetition, the "again and again," is the lowest form of reason; Husserl questions the sharpness of the division but keeps it. Anthropomorphism is a necessary first interpretation, which must be corrected.

(185) But domestic animals? Are they not real analogues of humans or already in human, though very low, personality, only unable to develop beyond their beginnings as our human children do? Domestic animals, living in human community, are the exception that blurs the line: perhaps persons of a very low level, arrested children. The exclusion "pets excluded" at the start of the addition is thus motivated.

(185) Psychology is therefore in principle human psychology as the first psychology properly resting on experience. Animal psychology is purely constructive; it presupposes for the legitimacy of its construction a genuinely intentional human psychology. The final thesis is methodological: animal psychology is a construction by modified empathy, and its legitimacy depends on human intentional psychology, which rests on experience. The order of founding in psychology mirrors the order of founding in constitution: animals are modifications of humans, and the knowledge of them is a modification of self-knowledge.

## III. Texts from the Context of the Second Revision of the *Cartesian Meditations* and the Conception of a "Systematic Work" Arising from It (July 1931 to February 1932)

### No. 12. On the Fifth Meditation: Constitution of Realities in Primordiality as "Formations" of the Ego, and Constitution of Others, Not as Egological Formations but as Transcendent to All Such Formations and Coexisting with My Ego (1931 or Later)

The manuscript bears Husserl's annotation "Fink." In spring 1931 Husserl first entrusted his assistant Eugen Fink with revising the German Cartesian Meditations; this text belongs to the material produced for or with him.

(189) What then is to be said about the being of a primordial natural object and of primordial nature as a whole? The question asks for the ontological status of the primordial world, the point No. 9's supplement had clarified negatively.

(189) This being has the sense of a being of such and such content for me: it is primordially constituted in my life and is actually and potentially the unity of manifolds that belong exclusively to me as primordial ego of capacities. This primordial ego has its own concretion, its own essential real and ideal properties. It has them for itself: it is given to itself originally and is referred back to itself in its potentialities; it is for itself. Its concretion includes primordial nature as constituted in it and purely in it. This nature is also a being, determinable and knowable, but in this being it is a unity of my appearances, the identical of my actual and potential identifications. Not something outside me, separate and somehow joined to me by an incomprehensible rigid law, but nothing but intentional correlate, only sense of my, and exclusively my, sense-giving. The primordial thing is wholly sense of my sense-giving. The rejected alternative, a nature outside me linked by an "incomprehensible rigid law," is the realist picture of psychophysical parallelism, or Leibniz's pre-established harmony, which phenomenology replaces with intentional correlation.

(190) What about transcendental others, other egos, other filled immanent times, other experiences, acts, another primordial nature? My primordial nature exists in its space-time (a primordial one, whose sense is experienced in it and to be further explicated) and is "transcendent" to my immanent temporal sphere, my sphere of experience. Primordial nature is transcendent to the stream but immanent to the ego, the "immanent transcendence" of No. 1 § 5.

(190) My other, and everything immanent and primordially transcendent belonging to him, is transcendent to my primordial (immanent and primordially transcendent) sphere in a new sense. Two transcendences are distinguished: the primordial thing's transcendence to the stream, and the other's transcendence to my whole primordiality.

(190) He is appresented in my primordial sphere as other, as being for himself, as ego of his primordiality, and is in my primordial sphere also a unity of experience of "appearances," perhaps, and normally, concordantly confirming itself and prefiguring confirmation. As this unity of my appresentation belonging to my primordiality, he exists for me and is appresented as existing for himself, not merely for me. Since my primordial nature contains "other bodies" and prefigures their being, I cannot, as I am, do otherwise than posit others; though posited by appresentation and confirmed in it, they too are inseparable from me. As I am and am for myself, I am not only ego of a primordial nature that is a unity from my own life in itself, but also ego for which others are as coexisting with me. Nature as my primordial one has no real inexistence in me in the sense of immanent experiences, but it has constitutive inexistence in me, inexistence as constitutive correlate. Here it would be wrong to say this nature coexists with me, the ego constituting it; rather it is an ideal formation in me (primordiality as a special way of "ideality"). The other, by contrast, is not constituted as a "formation" dwelling in me in my own way; rather in certain primordial manifolds (my understanding, animating experiences) the being of another is constituted for me, who as another ego is for himself. The distinction of the title is drawn: primordial nature is an ideal "formation" (Gebilde) in me, having constitutive inexistence; the other is not a formation but coexists with me. "Coexistence" applies to egos, not to egos and their correlates. Yet the other is "inseparable" from me in that my primordiality, containing other bodies, necessarily posits others. The scholastic term "inexistence," Brentano's for intentional containment, is split into real and constitutive inexistence.

(190) The other has in me not real transcendent presence, we can say, but real appresence and through it ideal compresence. The formula is exact: what is really (reell) in me is the appresenting experience; the other himself is ideally co-present, compresent, through it.

(191) This grounds an inseparable being-for-one-another: neither am I for myself, as I am, separable from the other, nor he from me. Each is for himself and yet for the other. Each as existing has sense at once from himself and for every other, and this belongs to each one's essence. It is no powerless mirroring; if we call an ego an absolutely real being, it belongs to such a real being that its being is inseparable from every other's being and that each intentionally encompasses every other and bears it in itself in an intentional mediacy that is no empty gesture. Husserl corrects Leibniz again: monads do not "mirror" one another powerlessly; their mutual intentional implication is constitutive of each one's being. Being-for-oneself and being-for-others are co-essential.

(191) As my living (primal-phenomenal) present bears my past in itself, always attesting it in the form of the present, and as my immanent time is thereby constituted, so my primordial spatio-temporal present bears my primordial past and future, and primordial nature is constituted as filled universal space-time. The model of the living present bearing the past is extended to primordial nature, preparing the analogy for others.

(191) Likewise my primordiality bears in itself another, his primordiality, and he again another. For me, and for every other existing for me, there is constituted a compresence and consuccession of primordialities, a transcendental all-time, thereby a transcendentally grounded objective nature and world. The coexistence of transcendental subjects, of their immanent temporalities and primordialities, is no empty (on closer view unthinkable) being-together but a being-for-one-another, an appresentative accessibility to one another and thereby an inner, intelligible, self-intuitive unification and connection. Mere coexistence of monads without intentional relation is "unthinkable"; coexistence is being-for-one-another. The "transcendental all-time" is the time of compresent and successive primordialities, the theme of Nos. 19–20.

(191) Thereby intersubjective constitution becomes possible; my stream of consciousness and the other's stream appresented in me, in which mine is appresented likewise, become one connected stream of consciousness (as my past and present life become one stream), and association, which is first primordial association, acquires the new sense of intersubjective association. The striking thesis is that two streams become "one connected stream," on the model of past and present life. Association extends across subjects: intersubjective association is the passive synthesis by which the other's life is linked to mine, the concept that grounds Husserl's later talk of an intermonadic "stream."

### No. 13. The Constitutive Structure of the World and Constituting Intersubjectivity. The Self-Explication of the Ego Leads Within the Ego to the Alter Egos. On the Transcendental Theory of Monads (16 July 1931)

(192) The constitutive explication of the ego in the first originality of the streaming egological present leads into the apodictically (though only as range of play) prefigured past. In the present, enclosed in its apodictic horizon, is the past; the present ego "implies" the past one. The future too is prefigured as that of my ego. Can one continue: the horizon of the present (mediately and implicitly the horizons of past and future) implies the world as phenomenon, implies first others too? First in the present the content of primordiality, the primordially reduced "world," with my own body. So the world-phenomenon is subjected to the disclosure of validity. The apodicticity of the past is that of a "range of play" (Spielraum): the form is apodictic, the content open. The question is whether others are implied in the present as the past is.

(192–193) Apodictically I am, as present ego of my horizon, the ego that has the present world as phenomenon with its constituted temporal horizon. The explication (ultimately of my full transcendental present horizon) leads to the transcendentally and actually co-present others and their horizons. An apodictic universal structure is prefigured here, in my ego and in every ego, an egological intersubjectivity prefigured in each ego in its own structure. The ontological a priori of the world as an ideally possible world in general is traced back (through "clarification of origin" as clarification of the constitutive sense of valid world and the scope of its validity) to the absolute universal a priori, the a priori of possible and actual being in general, the universal transcendental a priori. The answer is affirmative: intersubjectivity is an apodictic structure of every ego, an "egological intersubjectivity." The world's ontological a priori is grounded in the transcendental a priori of being in general. The claim goes beyond the Meditations, where the necessity of others was not asserted apodictically.

(193) But the clarifying achievement, as clarification of the content of apodicticity, must actually be carried out. It must be shown that absolute being has the apodictic essential form of an absolute totality of monadically (egologically) articulated being, in the form of intentional being-for-itself and being-for-one-another; that to the monadic universe essentially, apodictically, belongs the intermonadic constitution of a world in which the monadic totality appears in the apperceptive form of human and animal beings; that the transcendental universe of monads has the form of transcendental temporality, so that to the world in the ordinary sense, the universe of mundane realities, there corresponds transcendentally the absolute "world," the universe of transcendental realities. The concept of reality ("substance") doubles: constituting absolute reality and constituted relative reality. "Substance" in the other sense, independent being of a full concretion, doubles too. The mundane universe alone is independent; every single real thing and every finite whole is dependent, being merely part of the "whole" of the world, relative to other worldly things, with a world-horizon on which it "depends" mutually. As long as transcendentality is undisclosed, the world, the totality of finite substances and as totality the only unconditionally mundane being, is infinite substance. The program of a transcendental monadology is stated in three theses: absolute being is a totality of monads in mutual intentional relation; this totality necessarily constitutes a world in which it appears as humans and animals; it has transcendental temporality. The doubling of "substance" is Spinozist in form: mundanely, only the world-whole is substance (independent); finite things are modes. Transcendentally, there is a second, absolute concept of substance.

(193–195) Likewise every ego, every monad concretely taken, is substance, but only relative concretion: it is what it is only as socius of a sociality, as "member" of a total community. A wholly new concept of dependence, connection, being-with, coexistence, being in the unity of a temporality appears here, and being-real, being ultimate substrate, has a quite different, absolute sense. The monad exists as identical ego of an intentional, constituting life, an actual and potential conscious life, hence as ego of capacities, relatively persisting states of capacity related to the experiences (acts especially) that actualize them. The ego as intentionality and unity of capacities centred in the ego-pole is a self-constituting, self-conscious unity shaping itself passively and actively, and at the same time constitutes "objectivity" (transcendence) in its conscious life. But each monad constitutes not only itself in levels (from the streaming living present) but all other monads, actually and potentially (expressions whose sense varies with the intentional level, though encompassed by a unity), and so, potentially if not actually, all possible being in its horizon. Each monad is not only for itself but for every other. As being-for-itself is no empty (basically senseless) image in its own being, so being-for-one-another is no mere "mirroring," "representation." It is not as if each monad were for itself (created for itself) and had being without the others; each, having intentionally "constituted" the others in its being (as each has constituted its past in its present), cannot be without them. Its own being "implies" every other and is "implied" by it; each is "related" to each, coexists so that its existence conditions the existence of all others, each is "really connected" with each, real connection understood in the sense proper to monads; another has no sense for them. Every mundane reality and real relation (causality) leads back, through the constitution of the world, to absolute, monadic reality and monadic-real relation. The monad is substance only as socius: its absoluteness is relative to the totality. The decisive anti-Leibnizian claim is explicit: monads are not created each for itself; each is constituted with the others as the present is constituted with its past, so that none can be without the others. Monadic implication is the transcendental ground of worldly causality, the thesis of Beilage XXII on "monadic causality."

(195) The problem of the totality of the world, whether it must be thought finite or infinite, corresponds to the absolute problem of the monadic totality. Kant's first antinomy has its transcendental counterpart: is the totality of monads finite or infinite?

(195) But how far does such prefiguration reach? It is presupposed: I am subject for a world valid to me in which fellow humans already are, my parents. I construct what this presupposition absolutely presupposes. But I come to birth and death and the questions of the "entry" and "exit" of monads from actual monadic temporality, which exists only in the modes of living present, past and future, the present being actual in a primary sense, in which also mundanely the present is "real" in the proper sense as long as it continues. The regressive construction from the given world meets birth and death as entry and exit of monads, the problem of Beilage VIII now posed for the monadic totality.

(195) The past and no longer present as enduring is "nothing." Hence the attempt to reduce the world to "eternal," eternally present atoms. The aei on: infinity of the present world, infinity of the all-temporal world, but finiteness in every present. "Immortality" of monads; "immortality" of the universe of monads with mortality of individual monads. Questions of the ultimate sense of the monadic world. What conceivable variations does the "I am" as ego of a world continuously valid to me permit? What are the ultimate conceivabilities? What ultimate necessities of being are implied in the apodictic fact of the "I am," only within a horizon of possibilities? The notes sketch the metaphysical questions the monadology raises: if only the present is real, the past is nothing, hence the old temptation of eternal atoms, the Greek "always being" (aei on). The alternative Husserl entertains is immortality of the monadic universe with mortality of individuals, a position No. 33 and Beilage XXXIII develop under the title of the "primal ego" and the "transcendental immortality" of the ego.

### No. 14. The Pre-Given World in Intuitive Disclosure. The Systematics of Extension (Mid-August 1931)

(196–197) Husserl's table of contents, first part: a general preliminary consideration, in rough generality, of the normal style of experience of the world: the world in its living noematic temporalization as pre-given in it, its spatio-temporal (geometrical, mechanical causal-natural) structure. Style of familiarity as pre-given, always already known structure of the experiential world. The style of horizon: finitude of the known surrounding world actually self-temporalized, iterable extension into past and future as the iterative style of the experiential world, the extended always already belonging to the pre-given world as capacity for actually living temporalization. Correlatively constituting subjectivity. The riddle of primal being, a regressive path. Everything that is for me is from my universal temporalization: primordial being, others, the identical common world, constitution of the "we" as "we-persons" in personal communal relations, socializing personal acts, the human world as field of common purposes, humanizing itself through personal-social humanity. The generative: identical human world through the generations in open temporality. Finite human worlds. Homeworld and alien. Territory. Variations of this concept. Variations of the alien (alien homeland, alien of the alien). The question what is essentially necessary for the possibility of an infinite world of experience. The generative synthesis of life-worlds does not yet yield the constitution of an infinite world as world of "possible experience." Sense of the general "we" in relation to the surrounding world. "We" as universal form of the community of understanding, enclosing socialities as personal connections, each with its practical surrounding world. All this is pre-given world. From there the transcendental regress: as formation of validity. The regress leads to me. The outline joins the life-world analysis (finitude, homeworld, generativity) to the question of the infinite world of science, and announces the negative result: the synthesis of finite life-worlds does not yield an infinite world of possible experience.

(197) Second, supplement: only the normal world of experience and its modes of givenness were considered, naively. Addition: abnormalities as implications of sense in the pre-given world, up to the collapse of the concrete human life-world: fate. The supplement adds a dimension rarely found in Husserl: fate (Schicksal) and the possible collapse of a whole life-world.

#### Finitude of the World and Living Temporalization

(197) The world from experience in its living temporalization, with its living horizons of past and future as living present world. Original homogeneity of the world, its originally living homogeneous structure. Spatio-temporal structure, its concrete typology, its typology of changes in motion and rest, deformation, qualitative change, its causal style: push and counter-push, the style of free fall and its acceleration, inertia, the connection between oscillating deformation of bodies (by impact) and radiating propagation of sounds in space, between heat in space and the warming of bodies, warm bodies radiating heat, heat and light radiation, also in relation to "mechanical" processes. The life-world already has a causal style, the pre-scientific physics of everyday experience, from which Galilean science idealizes. The examples (falling, impact, sound, heat) are those of the Krisis § 9 on "the prescientific world of experience."

(197) This general typology belongs to the world of experience as ours in common at every time and place; it is there wherever we find ourselves, in every spatio-temporal world-present, the familiar, pre-given, known style anticipated for what comes and for every change of situation we bring about. The typology is invariant across times and places, the life-world's universal structure.

(197) To the style belongs also, as style of givenness of the world as world of our experience, given in itself yet meant with a horizon, the indeterminacy of what is to be counted as being in it. For what is actually experienced we have the style, a typology of relative finitude, of the generally familiar, surveyable surroundings, the world we already know and live in. The actually experienced world is finite, a surveyable environment within an indeterminate horizon.

#### Living Streaming Extension and Iterability of Extension

(198–199) But this finite world of actual experience and familiarity is in constant flux of coming to be for us, coming to experience and knowledge, not only in our occupation with what is already there taking new shapes within familiar typology. The world, we say, reaches further than our present experience; we can move or be moved, and our knowledge extends so that the finite manifold of known things increases. The world was always known as a finite manifold, but always also a world that extends and remains extendable in extension of experience. I and everyone have the world with a horizon of extension; I can recur to others, ask my parents and companions about their surrounding world. This style of extension of knowledge into unknown spheres is anticipated, and anticipated as iterable. Correlatively the world for us is not only the finite manifold actually experienced with its living style of extension, but also a world iterable in its iterable future horizon in the form of continued extension. This also belongs as potentiality to our past: we could always have come to know new things in other directions, as the extending progress now always has various possibilities and directions. The life-world is finite in actuality and infinite only in the iterable "and so on" of possible extension. Iterability is a style of capacity, not an actual infinity. The past too has unrealized directions of possible extension, a modal enrichment of the horizon concept.

(199) What is described here concerns the extension of our knowledge of the world that is for us; what extended knowledge offers is not newly created by us but belongs in advance to the world and world-time. So the world is temporalized in streaming experience, streaming subjective-intersubjective temporalization, such that the capacity, individual and common, of a grounded system of extensions of knowledge has the character of an iterable one. The world is actually experienced as a finite set of realities in living temporalization in the change of temporal modes, a finite set of already known realities, but in the flux of self-extension, of new things coming into view that were not actually there at all. Yet we have and had the capacity to go further and seek the new, and it is anticipated that there is always more to know in the various directions. Anticipated is a world-future, first as ours, but also an accessible co-present, and then iteratively, with the capacity to go on at every point in time and in every direction. What extension discovers was already there: discovery, not creation. The world's being-in-advance is the correlate of an iterable capacity.

#### Infinity and Generative Intersubjectivity. The Generative Nexus Itself Iteratively Constituted. Iteration Through the Others

(199) Here generative intersubjectivity must be considered, belonging to the sense-formation of the objective world as "infinite," extendable by each only finitely in his finite temporalization; but in intersubjective communalization the world extends in extended and extending intersubjective time. But is the generative nexus of subjects not constituted iteratively from me, and then for everyone, from finitude? Life and its temporalization proceed in continuous presumption, which iteratively contains presumption again. This includes the continued presumption that my others not only have the same finite surrounding world as I but more that I do not or cannot have; that these others may have others in turn, finally others inaccessible to me in fact. Likewise generatively: I have parents, I knew my grandparents, but they had parents, and so on, whom I could never know. This "and so on" belongs to my presumption, to the horizon of my presumptive existence. To me belongs the capacity to "disclose" this horizon and make one of the possibilities intuitive as possible actuality, and then the horizon belonging to that possibility, and so in infinitum. The infinity of the world is generated by iterated presumption through others and through generations. Each "and so on" is a presumption I can disclose, which yields a new horizon. Infinity is thus constituted as iterative presumption, not given; this is the precise sense in which the objective world's infinity is founded on intersubjectivity.

(200) I in my life of validity have the interpenetration of actual validity and implicit validity implying further validity, and so iterably. Iterating in the actuality of disclosure, validity arises again and again, the existing arises again and again, what already exists for me temporalizing itself into new contents and new beings coming forth to be determined. All this in constitutive relation to the intersubjectivity first constituted for me. In me the others are implied as implied in one another, and I implied in them again, and in the living egoic life implied in me and so in egoic interpenetration "the" world is constituted. In me a horizon of availability, of being affected, of purposes and purposive activity, of effecting and creating, of moods, of colourings of happiness and unhappiness. I: before me as horizon my personal existence in my world, in my human environment of manifoldly mediated personal relations, these persons themselves having their own connections. All this as dark horizon whose implication I can illuminate, explicate in its familiarities and its empty horizons of unknowns. Mutual implication is iterated: I imply the others, who imply one another and me. The horizon includes moods and the colouring of happiness, which makes affectivity part of the world-horizon, a rare emphasis in Husserl that No. 14's supplement develops.

(200–201) I as subject of knowledge, knowing with the help of others, performing cognitive achievements that join the "world," the horizon of being, as lasting, accessible to fellow researchers, meant for them, as I can find theirs in my horizon in possible "experience." Cognitive achievements become part of the world, accessible to others: the objectivity of science as sedimented communal acquisition.

(201) This liveliness of absolute transcendental being as liveliness of intentional achievement of being in the form "ego" and of a constituting ego-life, which has its direction of sense-formation prefigured from original instinctive habitualities and always already has horizons, lives on shaping its world, gaining determinate horizon-formations, among them those with the sense "others," "co-subjects," co-constituting for an intersubjective horizon with an intersubjective world. Original instinctive habitualities prefigure the direction of constitution: instinct is the ground of teleology in constitution (Nos. 22, 34). The constitution of others is one of the formations this instinctively directed life gains.

(201) The riddle of primal being, mine, the transcendentally questioning one's, primal riddle: my primal-phenomenal stream of temporalization. In it I, questioning, find already pre-given the sense-formation "world," valid to me, existing for me in the stream, streaming in itself in temporal modes and constituting being in itself. As waking ego I always experience "the" world, but as a streaming living present phenomenon, as meaning, as appearance, as it now appears in perception, or at once in recollections and anticipations, as it is valid to me, again and again in other what and how and yet the same, with a horizon of validity on which I must reflect to bring out even everything known and momentarily awakenable, to which belongs the universal form and typology always prefigured. In syntheses of disclosing intuition and identification, in a stream of my life, I gain as explicate world as sense of being in this milieu of life, with consciousness of the capacity to determine identically in many ways again and again. My path of identification passes through the disclosure of the others and their identifying life, also in the activity of judging, predicating thought and the practice of knowledge aiming at firm validity. The "primal riddle" is the primal-phenomenal stream itself, the living present, in which the world is already pre-given as a sense-formation. Identification passes "through" the others: even my own determination of the world is mediated by others' identifying life.

(201–202) I thus explicate what I already have as acquisition, but an acquisition that is always being acquired. The pre-given world is not pre-given as a rigid being but as constantly becoming for me in the streaming of life, in the primal stream, a sense of being that becomes for me as containing the sense "other egos" coexisting with me, always already openly presumed, to be shaped by my actual or possible activity, but in this arbitrary shaping (explication) only determining more closely the already valid horizon of being: the directions of ability are in advance directions of possible doing, and if I proceed in an arbitrarily chosen direction I realize something I could have realized before and can realize again afterward, as something that has in itself its duration in objective time, its beginning, modes of change, end, and afterward in this having-endured is accessible again as the same that was; as before it was, it had its ways of being foreseen, at any rate of being something future corresponding to the fixed style of the future, prefigured indeterminately and generally as a style of future real being from every point, every egoic present and its experience, which must afterward be grasped as fulfilment and mere closer determination. The world is an acquisition always in acquisition. Exploring it realizes possibilities already prefigured, so what I discover has objective temporal existence before and after my discovery. This is the constitution of the "in itself" of the past and future through the iterable "I could have" and "I can again."

(202–203) I come to know, in the transcendental attitude, in transcendental predicative explication, with the claim to essential knowledge, apodicticity and logicity, that the first and truly concrete being in itself is my being in actuality and habituality, passivity and agility; that in this being I have a sense-formation as already and constantly acquired (implicitly), a sense-formation that in the validity "world" is sense of being in a constant subjective mode of givenness, constantly valid but constantly anticipation, anticipation in various ways of a possible process of disclosure and confirmation concerning past, co-present and future. I find that this pre-given world, as this formation, this identical in constantly mobile actual and possible identifying confirmation, is manifoldly founded, and especially founded on the sense "other transcendental being," but in a way that, in the interweaving of foundings, always leads to a finite sphere of world related to the transcendental others founding it (who are objectified in this finite sphere as humans, world-objects), and at once to an extended sphere, by virtue of more world-objects originally accessible in these subjects' capacities. Among these objects are the others co-existing for the others, objectified as humans, and their extension of the world with at least presumably expected new subjects, and so on, the world coming to validity intersubjectively being always co-valid for all new subjects (with possible correction), and after the new ones also for the old. Moreover in the generative nexus, through which the world explicates its validity in its successive temporality, and so in the open nexus of communication. The founding on others always produces a finite sphere, extended by each further other. The world's infinity is the open iteration of finite spheres, and validity flows both ways between old and new subjects.

(203) I state what I find as existing, what I hold as sense of being in validity, putting it into actual validity as an index for its horizon, which means for me: capacity to identify again and again on familiar synthetic paths prefigured and walkable for me. My life is thoroughly life in capacities, a life of intentional synthesis, of passive synthesis with many directions of progress, primally temporalizing in every direction realized in the primal-phenomenal stream. This passive structure is directed actively by the waking ego of activities and capacities, all action having its horizon of capacities. Every existent is an index of a system of capacities; passive synthesis carries life, active ego directs it.

(203–204) Under the title passivity I also have secondary passivity, arising from acts: my own and others' apperceived ones, coming to me passively through empathic presentification. I have my own living, functioning, original capacity and activity and the other's, my own achievement as primordial achievement of sense from my own capacity and doing, and the presentified other's, now co-valid to me; this because the other ego is not only passively presentified but valid as existing from my capacity, with the horizon of being able to confirm itself for me. Implied in this is the sphere of being constituted by others from their capacities, mediately existing for me, mediately confirmable as theirs and, in synthetic coincidence, partial perhaps, with mine as mine. The others acquire from my progressing horizon of other-experience their others, generally not mine, and as I, progressing in my primordial or present environment, am familiar with always being able to meet unknown others, I experience others with these possibilities too. Others' achievements reach me as "secondary passivity," sedimented acts received through empathy. Husserl thus extends the concept of sedimentation from my own acts to those of others: what others have achieved enters my life passively, as tradition does.

(204) It must also be noted that what I experience of others and for others has its repercussion on me as regards the sense of being I have for myself. When I reflect, I am always already the person who has, for himself, a sense grown from the being-for-me of others. My action relates to the circle of what already exists for me, my experienced environment and the horizon of the unknown but accessible. So my action relates to the others. But here action on others becomes possible not merely as objects but as subjects, prompting them, motivating them to suffer and do, and this also enters into the others valid to me as their actual and possible motivating of me; from this springs "agreeing" in the widest sense, personal connection. My self-sense is formed through others. Acting on others as subjects, motivating them, is the root of social acts and of personal connection.

(204–205) In disclosing the foundings in which the world is valid to me, I see that my own transcendental being, in which I constitute myself (experiencing myself actively as existing on the basis of the primally temporalizing stream), temporalizes the being of everything that is for me in a universal temporalization that is mine. Explicated: in my being is founded my primordial "world," in it the being-for-me of others, the being-for-them of their primordial worlds, further the being-for-me of the identical world as the same appearing as my primordial and their primordial worlds. Thus there is also constituted for me the mutually understanding "we," actual and possible social relation, mutual or one-sided, as social pairing, social pluralization as personal associations, the human world as field of common purposes, humanizing itself through social humanity. Further the possibility (and for the humans of this world the capacity) to follow the constituted generatively, to understand it as a life-world of successive generations with a unity of historical tradition, as historical cultural world, to question back and forward and so explicate world and humanity in open temporality, having them in streaming temporal modes as present, past and future world (human life-world) and as one and the same (constituting identical time and persisting world-being objectively in the streaming), that is, in this finitude as world for everyone living in it now, for me and then for everyone. The summary of the founding order is the most complete in the volume: primordial world, others, their primordial worlds, identical world, "we," social relations ("social pairing," a striking extension of the term), humanized world, generative-historical world. "Humanizing" (sich humanisierend) names the progressive transformation of nature into human world through common purposes.

#### Finite World. Homeworld and the Alien

(205) To every world so conceived, constituted as normal ordinary life-world, belongs finitude and the possibility of transcending this finitude. As constitutive level, first objective level of the world, it is the homely world of a homely humanity communalized in its concrete present, whose associations arising from generation, and associations of associations, finally form a unity, a mediacy of connection spanning all connecting mediacies. It is conscious to every personal individual and closed association as universe of factually possible accessibility. The homeworld is the first objective world: objective for a home-humanity, finite, and transcendable. Its unity is the unity of mediated connection among all its members.

(205–206) Into this humanity and its unified world "strangers" may occasionally come from abroad, from an alien world that is a world like ours with humans like us homely ones, but other, alien. As I from myself bring transcendental others to validity in experience, so I, already constituted as homely human, member of my homeland in the homely cultural space (territory), bring a new otherness to experience. Or we homely ones with our homeland experience (constitute originally in the progress of world-constitution) an alien as an alien homeland with alien humans who have their homeland, their territory (also the alien of the alien). The cultural territory (the environment actually become practical, already humanized) has its outer domain of nature, openly endless, perhaps to be drawn into use, humanized, but not territory proper. In occasional penetration into this distance, past culture, past human existence may be indicated. Husserl draws an exact analogy: the alien is to the homeworld what the other is to my primordiality. The alien is "an alien homeland," a homeworld for others. Territory is the humanized environment; beyond it lies untamed nature, in which traces of past cultures may appear, the archaeological horizon.

(206) The territory may be merely temporary; the home-people may be a nomadic people meeting other nomadic peoples, other tribes. Many possibilities exist here (which anthropology and history show as actualities). But are there not essential necessities running through them as conditions of the possibility that we can have in experience the full spatio-temporally infinite world, whose worked-out logical ideality we take as truth for the world simply, though it is given to us only in a "segment" as finite life-world? Empirical variety (settled or nomadic peoples) is surveyed by anthropology; philosophy asks for the essential conditions of the infinite world of science, given only as a segment.

(206) The world that is for us, we say, is a world of possible experience. Does possibility not mean possible accessibility (hence confirmation) according to time and space; are these not forms of access? "Possible experience" is interpreted as possible access, in time and in space. The next two paragraphs test each.

(206) a) For temporality we have generation: my parents, our parents, the parents of parents. This is not merely imagined but prefigured in this mediacy, a prefigured past as that of the environments that were, valid to us, however unknown and indeterminately anticipated, an open horizon of possible historical information. Temporal infinity is prefigured generatively, the past being a horizon of possible historical knowledge.

(206–207) b) But spatially? My and our earthly horizon with its distant things fading in the circle of the horizon. We can go there, approach, and having arrived could go toward a new horizon, and so we can think ourselves moving or moved "to infinity." The variation of distant things, perspectivization, concerns not only the earth's surface; thrown stones, birds flying up, tall trees, mountains show it. So we can think ourselves flying up, being lifted. Spatial infinity is first "thought" as iteration of approaching horizons, including the vertical dimension.

(207) But is this thinking ourselves an empirical prefiguration showing a possibility of being? My ability is limited. My freedom of constitutive experience, by which I experience the same being in the change of near and far, is sometimes contingently inhibited, but here I have a normally prefigured, in advance valid general restriction.
