Above all, in this world of birth and death I have a temporally finite life and finite, if growing, powers. So I have, for me and my home-humanity together, an extreme distance, whose reaching would again yield distant appearances, but these no longer indicate objects of actually possible experience of the style considered here, not prefigured as actually existing by these factual capacities. So finally for all of us together, even if we migrate and extend our world. It remains, as world of such capacity of experience and as existing for us from there, in finitude. And now with regard to the distances of the heavens. Finite lives and powers bound the actually possible experience. The iteration of horizons is imagined, not prefigured by real capacity. The heavens are the limit case: the stars are not places we can reach. This is the argument of the 1934 Earth-ark text in its first form.

(207) What if we bring in the chain of generations? Its temporal "infinity" does not yet prefigure a spatial one as spatial reality or even real possibility, that the realities existing for us as generative humanity, experienced and actually experienceable, could extend "on all sides" in space to infinity and be confirmed. Generational infinity does not supply spatial infinity: generations stay on the earth.

(207–208) The synthesis of life-worlds and generations therefore does not yield, as it first seemed, an infinite world as "world of possible experience." If we take world in our sense as "world of possible experience," that cannot mean the universe of beings that universally communicating humanity, generative with its historical time in infinitum, could actually experience in all possible extensions. Husserl notes that more precise material is in manuscript FF, September 1931 (A VII 17). The negative result is important. The infinite world of exact science is not the world of possible experience in the sense of actual human capacities; it is an idealization. The concept of "possible experience" must be revised, toward the ideal possibility that the Krisis analyses as Galilean idealization of "and so on."

#### Sense of the Constitution of the We (Personal-Human). The Universal "We" as Frame for Particular Communities

(208–209) So the world existing for me, always horizonally, is a current world of experience of actual perception, recollection, anticipation, with a horizon of known world-objects not actually experienced, among them other humans, these as human co-subjects with whom I stand in a community of understanding in experiencing, thinking and doing, but also in personal connection by social acts and social habitualities. As a community of understanding I am with my others the unity of a "we," that is, for me this "we" is constituted as I and those in actual or establishable understanding with me, each of whom counts for me as himself an ego and centre of the same community of understanding, of the same "we" as valid from him. To the sense of "we" belongs this capacity of identifying exchange as one I have and must attribute to others reciprocally. Within this frame of the "we" exist particular personal connections, momentary and lasting, which I enter or only others enter. They are relations and connections of persons with regard to their common environment, in which they are for one another, belonging to it as humans and human associations. Acting connected, they have in turn an environment as the whole of what is practically there for them, the field of their connected action. Environment does not mean an external world only, with sense only where the agent has his field of activity outside him, the realm of his purposes. Since each can be object for himself, and an association too, its own personal being included in its purposive sense, belongs to the environment. The "we" is the universal frame of possible mutual understanding, centred on each member; particular communities are connections within it, each with its practical environment. The environment includes the agents themselves, so far as they take themselves as objects of their purposes.

(209) But how does all this proceed transcendentally, this constant process of progressing world-constitution, of progressing formation of one's own and others' personalities, with their changing environments vitally valid to them, determining them, determined by them, taking ever new shape, ever new purposive sense, ever new mood, ever new colour of a hopeful, beautiful or fateful, sad environment? From such processes the world that is for us has already become, and has from the outset a horizonal sense, a hidden validity, which we can elicit whenever we reflect. The world is the sediment of formative processes, including affective ones (hope, sorrow, fate); its horizon is a hidden validity to be elicited by reflection.

(209–210) I, the questioner, reflecting, am the living site of the whole world already valid for me and valid as heritage of my inner tradition, from my own achieving past. I with my primally streaming life go first; in it the validity of others for me is constituted as existing, in a horizon of my activities of possibly getting to know them, entering into relation, bringing their own validities to validity for me, connecting them synthetically with my own primordial ones. In me the validities, the occurrences of being, birth and death of others are constituted and, transferred back to my already living and personal being, my own human birth, my own human death. In me is constituted, in the synthesis of finite environments of actual accessibility with indeterminately open marginal horizon, the infinite world, in whose objective space-time I am an insignificant human child, into which I was born once and will once cease to be, to be worldly, with the decay of my body. My "not-being" is beyond doubt, if "being" means real being, person-in-the-world-being, bodily actuality in space-time. The "inner tradition" of my own past is the egological counterpart of generative tradition. My birth and death are constituted by transfer from others'; I am "an insignificant human child" in the infinite world I constitute. The paradox of human subjectivity (Krisis § 53) is stated in its existential form: my worldly non-being is certain.

(210) But what of my transcendental being, in which I am now living constituter of the world, in which the manifold unified validities are sheltered as instituted and habitual, spin themselves on, are reactualized, interweave with new ones acquired on the basis of the old as continued constitution, proceeding in the open horizon of the unknown yet existing world motivated in me and co-valid in its way? The question whether transcendental being shares the death of the worldly person is posed and left open, the problem of Beilage XXXIII and No. 33.

(210) What of the transcendental others, constituted in me and for me as unities of being, and constituted as transcendentally co-functioning others, co-functioning for the constitution of the world valid to me and thereby world for all of us, for the open infinity of transcendental others? Has their being as being for me from my constituting life possible validity without this life? The parallel question for others: does their being, constituted from my life, survive the end of my life? The question reverses the solipsistic worry: not whether others exist without me in fact, but whether their validity can outlive the life that constitutes it.

#### Supplement

(210) Naively the world of experience was taken exclusively as world of normality. Exclusive weight was placed on the "homeworld," so only the normal life-world was considered, the world in the normal style familiar to me, on which I can always count. Abnormality, occasionally breaking normality, has its own style; the typology of unforeseeable abnormal events can be foreseen. This has its relativity too: the normal day as day of life, the normal periodicity of daily events or activities with their forms of abnormality; the week, weekday existence and Sunday; weekday vocational life and outside it the care of other interests. Abnormality has a style: the unforeseeable is typically foreseeable. Normality is articulated by the rhythms of life, day, week, Sunday, the temporal structure of a homeworld.

(210) The typical of a connected whole normal life, a life that constantly has its normal practical horizon, a practical field, a field of "calculability," of practical possibility with foreseeable consequences and in consciousness of ability. Normality is calculability, the practical correlate of concordance.

(210–211) Breach of this normality. "I no longer know which way to turn." I and my normal ability, the normally existing environment and its practical possibilities at the time. Breakdown is first described from within, as the collapse of the "I can."

(211) Degrees of disturbance of this normality: a particular thing does not fit, does not proceed as expected, but every particular has its horizon of possibilities, and the disturbance is subordinate if adaptation to the other possibilities is familiar and practised and the total system of the practical environment remains undisturbed; the total style of our interest-life runs on concordantly. We need only change our way at the particular point. Minor disturbance is absorbed by local adaptation within an intact total style.

(211) It is different when a "heavy blow of fate" strikes us. The death of a child changes the whole horizon of interests and so the mode of life and purposive system of the parents. Future life in its variety was determined by the child's life and flourishing, by care for its right development, securing its material future. Or the death of the wife for the husband, as co-bearer of life's tasks, mother of the children, caring housewife. Otherwise: a war calling into question the whole future of the community of people and state, a flood destroying house, home and fields. Blows of fate alter the whole system of purposes, since lives are organized around other persons and common goods. The examples (a child's death, a spouse's death, war) carry personal weight: Husserl's son Wolfgang was killed at Verdun in 1916, though the text itself makes no personal reference.

(211) The breach of the normal style of a personal life has various types; we must distinguish the types of abnormality, grave fates deeply affecting life's themes, which are already familiar from acquired knowledge of the environment as acquisitions of life in a community of life. To these belongs the death of those near us belonging to our normal life, as in the examples, the fateful intervention of others in our life, the failure of collaborators, of companions with whom we joined in a common purposive life. The manifold typology of such fates is familiar to us; living in a varied homeworld and generally normal environment, we know what has occasionally happened to others; even if not affected ourselves, we know that such things could normally happen to us. Grave fates are "normal abnormalities," typically familiar from others' lives, part of the homeworld's horizon of possibilities.

(211–212) On the other hand, fates we are not and could not be prepared for, which shake our whole normal environment common to us all. In Germany, for example, we are not prepared for earthquakes destroying our whole environment, unlike regions of the earth where earthquakes are, so to speak, a normal abnormality, though of a kind one cannot generally prepare for. Unforeseeable catastrophe is relative to the homeworld: what is unthinkable in one is a "normal abnormality" in another. The earthquake example shows normality as homeworld-relative even for natural events.

(212) The world as existing for us (for me and my co-subjects, my "we") is not a rigidly existing world. We are subjects of acts, practical subjects in the widest sense, practical in relation to the world pre-given to us with a determined and undetermined sense of being. That means first the outer world. But we ourselves, the practical ego, am always another, though the same ego; my capacities change, not only because the outer world refuses itself. I am healthy or sick, I let myself go or pull myself together. I always already have my peculiarity, character, abilities, and through all change an individual identity as person, an identity in the freedom of being able to intervene and in the style of being now fresh, now tired, now lax, now serious; according to how I am and am disposed, the future world is another and yet individually the same, since it moves in possibilities that are mine, though not practically calculable and controllable. Even if we do not consciously take into account that we are no rigid being, it is clear that every other, in the freedom of his decisions, changes the world, the outer world, which is ours too, and also in that every act changes the other subject himself, who henceforth is changed for all of us. So the world is constantly changing; we change ourselves as worldly beings in our doing, and the world by acting outward. There is no rigid world for us, and a world other than the world for us with all its vague individual and intersubjective horizons has not the least sense for us. The world's mutability follows from the mutability of the subjects who belong to it and act in it. The final sentence restates the anti-realist thesis in practical terms: only the world-for-us, with its vague horizons, has sense.

(212–213) Now the question must be posed: we are, and the world is for us, only in the relativity of normalities and abnormalities. One of these normalities is that in which every human being, I the reflecting one, normally find myself: in the unity of an on the whole concordant life of purposes and interests, in which I act and acquire and so gain a lasting world of acquisitions as fulfilled goals and premises for further goals, and develop myself as a persisting ego concordant with itself in convictions, directions of will, purposes and means, living in a constancy of satisfaction and of striving, working, creating into a horizon of future satisfactions. Normally human existence remains in this normality despite particular inhibitions, disturbances and breaks, which are overcome, and the overcoming belongs to the progressing and self-confirming satisfaction. Practical normality is a concordant purposive life whose overcoming of disturbances is itself part of its satisfaction, Husserl's version of a eudaimonia of activity.

(213) This normality has its horizon of possibilities, its style in these possibilities and anticipations. Does its normal content include coming death and normal fates and finally also the type "unknown fates, wholly unknown in their particular type or thinkable only as the remotest possibilities, the highest improbabilities"? Even death and the unknown may belong to normality as anticipated types.

(213) But is there not also possible a course of fate that totally breaks this normality and brings me into the situation: "I no longer know which way to turn, it cannot be foreseen how life can go on, how it can again take the form of a fruitful, stable existence, a normally human one"? Total breakdown of personal normality is the limit case, the "limit situation" (Grenzsituation) of the next paragraph, a term Jaspers had made current.

(213) Is not a collapse of the whole human community possible, in which not only I but all of us could fall into this limit situation: nothing can be relied on, no human being, not even oneself; the whole environment as our common life-world loses for all of us the character of a world in which one can exercise foresight, set purposes, and in a manifold of purposes live a unitarily integrating human life, each satisfied as human in a gratifying human world in which all normally satisfy themselves, rise, acquire, can be, each normal one in the normal world that each and all together can affirm? What use is it then if the world retains its identity through all abnormalities, even the collapse of the practical human environment, an identity resting on a stylistic form in which natural laws are passively fulfilled? Husserl envisages a collective collapse of a life-world, in which the world remains as nature but loses its character as human world. The rhetorical question devalues the mere identity of nature: a world that persists only as the passive fulfilment of natural laws is useless for human life. Written in 1931, the passage has an evident historical resonance with the crisis of the Weimar years.

(214) The being of the world has only an appearance of firmness; in truth it is the firmness of a normal formation. But precisely from here arises, as soon as this mode of instability is discovered or at least felt, the highest question of the world, the philosophical questionability of the world as such in its totality, understood radically, lifting all horizons and drawing them into the question. The final paragraph turns the possibility of collapse into the motive of philosophy. The world's firmness is that of a normality, and the felt instability of normality motivates radical questioning of the world as a whole. Philosophy arises from crisis, the thesis of the Krisis and the Vienna lecture.

### Appendix XI. Homeworld, Alien World, and "the" World (1930 or 1931)

(214) I and we come to know strangers as subjects of an alien world, experienced concordantly in their communal life. Correlatively to this world, valid for them as practical life-world and as world in general, they are people of other experiences, other natural surroundings, other life-goals, other convictions of every kind, other habits, other practical behaviour, other traditions. For me (or my home-community) my world extends because there is another home-community, living and behaving otherwise, apprehending "the" world otherwise, and in fact having another cultural world valid for them, not for us. Husserl's notes: important for the method of correlative constitutive construction of the transcendental aesthetic, that is, the correlative system of validity of the world as world of experience; and, always in addition, the empty distant world, the unpractical one in their apprehension. The alien is defined by a world valid "for them, not for us." Meeting the alien extends my world, not by adding their world to mine but by adding a world that is theirs. The note situates the analysis in the "transcendental aesthetic," the constitution of the world of experience, and adds that every homeworld, theirs included, has an unpractical distant horizon.

(214–215) So an alien humanity is constituted, an alien people perhaps. Precisely thereby our "own" home-community, community of the people, is constituted for me and us in relation to our cultural environment as world of our particular human validities. I have (understanding it quasi or really genetically-historically) a change of my and our world-experience and of the world itself. In "the" world are we, my people, and the other people, and each has its folk environment (with its unpractical horizon). Environment separates from world. But how? If I say "the world," what is it that exists and confirms itself for me under this title, and for us, when we gain knowledge, at least to some extent, of the alien people? At first we may hold fast to our validities while separating and in a way connecting the world, distinguishing in it our environment as life-environment and that of other peoples. Husserl's note: it must be distinguished more clearly between the personal environment of others as reality valid to them, confirmed for them (taken totally: the world for them, enclosing their life-world), and the "true world in itself," to be known by us scientists through scientific reflection, to which nature, humans and their meant environments belong as more or less one-sided, but also perhaps false, apprehensions of "the" actual world. Can a personal life-world not have its "situational truth," and so not be false at all? Homeworld is constituted as such only through the alien: "our own" appears in contrast. Environment and world separate at that moment. The note's question about "situational truth" is pivotal: the life-world of others need not be false relative to scientific truth; it may be true relative to its situation. This anticipates the Krisis's rehabilitation of the life-world against objectivist science and the theory of "occasional" truths in Formal and Transcendental Logic.

(215) Here is a task: clarifying the founding of validities that I and any subject of a first, mere homeworld must perform to distinguish world and homeworld, or to understand an alien humanity with its alien homeworld as distinct from mine. More clearly: at the first level of validity of intersubjectivity and intersubjective life-world I simply have world extending into open endlessness from the life-world as the only interesting one, the environment of interests; afterward, instead of this world (with its correlate "we," which meant all humans), I have this "we" as a particular humanity and our life-world no longer as the life-world in the old way. Over against this "we" an alien "we" has been constituted, over against our humanity an alien humanity, each finding itself in its environment, which is no longer called the world in general, in the ordinary human sense (life-world). From me and us an extended humanity is constituted, and then, in the same iterable constitutive process, a plurality of folk-humanities forming one humanity (at first as a mere collection of folk-humanities related to their territory and concrete culture) and belonging to one world, which is first a world for them all. But at first in the form of a progressing and ever further progressing world of realities with ever new "we's" and "our worlds." The first-level "we" meant "all humans"; the encounter with the alien particularizes it. "Life-world" itself undergoes a shift: what was "the world" becomes "our life-world." Humanity is first constituted as a collection of peoples, a world of realities containing many "we's," before any genuine unity.

(215–216) What is always common is the natural structure with the most generally understandable humanity to be put into validity: humans existing, communalized, acting, always finding their cultural sphere of being, shaping it on by their human life and work into beings of general validity for them, hence in relativity of such being, while running through it is an all-encompassing valid and self-confirming being as universal world of realities. To relativity belongs that each such humanity has as environment a world simply, but for it, concordant for it, but not therefore concordant in the universal of reality with other humanities, related to their valid world. The common invariant is nature plus the most general form of humanity (communalized, acting, culture-forming). Every homeworld is "a world simply, but for it," concordant internally but not necessarily with others. This is the structural formula of cultural relativity within one world of realities.

(216–217) I, from "we," disclosing my "we." What can I keep in validity in view of the discordance between my and other humanities' universal experience and conviction? I am motivated to constitute a new world, new at any rate in having taken up alien humanities and cultures as facts, but not so as to perform a synthesis taking over the alien validities. My "extended" world is not constituted as a connection of my and the other worlds in the unity of a concordant experiential conviction running through all humans of the extended circle. How does the original world simply permit this transformation? Evidently because the world valid for me and us from concordant experience has a spatio-temporal horizon, that of possible individual-real being, which need not be anticipated as before in the form of open continuation in the previous style, of possible experience that, if humans are given or anticipated as experiencers, always apperceives them as humans of my folk-type and so experiences a corresponding environmental form. Husserl's note: but if no alien humanity is given, mere "nature" is the plus ultra. The open spatio-temporal real horizon receives a different determination, an occupation by the alien people and its environment. Thereby real world in general stands out from particular humanity and human environment, folk environment, including the contrast of culture as such relative to people as such (first home-community as such with homely culture): ours and another. This is understood through the layering of validity already contained in the constitutive founding of the concrete first homeworld: starting from the primordially reduced "world" with "nature," own body, "culture"; then in the homeworld the corresponding layering of validity and being, by which physical nature, the plurality of bodies or humans, the particular human communities, and material culture can be distinguished. That does not mean that for me and my homely "we" a separate layer of universal mere nature and a layer of human subjects and personal communities is split off. The world for us gains new, alien humans, but humans, realities, animate bodies, persons living in particular community, forming culture and particular communities, connected by their peculiar convictions, theoretical, axiological, practical, shaping culture according to peculiar (typically novel) life-purposes. This we understand; that is, it is valid for us with sense of being: realities individuated by their physis and its spatio-temporal places, bodies valid as organs of a ruling psychic subject, mediately individuated in space-time, but identical in and for itself and intentionally related to the nature universally common to them and us and to fellow humans, accessible to them and us. This commonality of experience concerns realities and all fellow humans, including those of the new sphere in their real individuality, but in a horizonality that is now different for us (who were formerly all humans and are so no longer) and for the others. Their horizon of being is known only in the form of strangeness and the understandability of their environment, which is only indicated and can be explicated only indirectly, from the understandabilities drawn from the most general form of culture. The new world integrates the alien as fact, not as validity: I acknowledge that they believe otherwise without taking over their beliefs. The integration is possible because the homeworld's horizon was never strictly prefigured as filled with humans of my type; it is "occupied" anew. The encounter makes the strata of the homeworld explicit: nature, bodies, persons, communities, culture. Universal nature and the general form of humanity are what is shared; the alien's specific cultural horizon is accessible only indirectly. The parenthesis "we who were formerly all humans and are so no longer" is precise: the first homeworld's "we" claimed to be all humanity; the alien deprives it of that claim.

(217) Can I let the mythical convictions of others count, which determine the sense of being of their world, their fetishes, their deities, their mythical causalities? If I keep my belief (they may regard it as mere mythology), their belief is superstition; if I keep my existing world, their world does not exist. The alien humanities are for me as facts and as subjects of factual convictions, factually meant worlds, which I call their mythical world-representations. I call them so because I have already modified my world in its sense of being, essentially keeping my convictions, by putting these other humans into validity within my spatial world, correcting only the earlier anticipation that did not prepare me and us for other humans. The asymmetry of validities is stated plainly: from my homeworld, theirs is myth; from theirs, mine may be. The parenthesis ("they may regard it as mere mythology") registers the reciprocity without resolving it. "Mythical world-representation" is a classification from within my world, not a neutral description, and Husserl knows it.

(217) How do I come to speak of a world of experience for all humans in general, known and unknown, and to hold it in validity? As I, by getting to know other peoples, by successful though limited empathy with folk strangers and their environment, come to an extended world of facts, so does everyone, every person of an alien people. But the factual world is different for each of the different folk circles, yet so that each knows himself at one with each in that the one and same world is experienced, but each particular humanity "apprehends it differently." What is the ground of this sameness, and so far as there is truth in it, what is the common concordant experience to which a common world corresponds, how is it to be circumscribed in a truth binding all? What is true being, from concordant experience in a homeworld, what is formal for every homeworld in general, what at a higher level universal truth, to be produced in the all-synthesis of actual and possible homeworlds? The problem of the one world is posed as a problem of truth at three levels: homeworld-truth, formal truth common to all homeworlds, and universal truth produced by synthesis of all possible homeworlds. The last is the idea that Beilage XII and the Krisis assign to science.

(217) How do I, from myself, performing ever new validity and levels of validity, constitute the validity that bears all others in itself as founding lower levels, the validity of truly existing world, and how is this existing world enriched, extended, elevated in taking up new foundings; where is the conclusion; how is the anticipation and validity of a final form to be understood as one that, within a universal horizon-structure, permits only closer determination and already encloses all possible validities as prefigured? The final question asks whether there is a final form of the world's validity, a horizon-structure in which all future discoveries would be mere closer determinations. The answer the Krisis gives is that such a final form is an idea, not an achievable state.

### Appendix XII. Language, Truth of Judgments, Surrounding World (Homeworld). The Function of Linguistic Communication for the Constitution of the Surrounding World (Probably Summer 1931)

(218) Husserl's table of contents: the problem of the objectivity of predicative truth in relation to the pre-givenness and givenness of the world in levels of environmentality, to which levels of relativity of the corresponding truths correspond, so relative environment as situation. Homeworld and inaccessible distances. The function of linguistic communication in the constitution of a human environment. Very imperfect: no adequate clarification of the achievement of language and of the difference between animal and human communication. Truth is level-relative to environments, and the environment is the "situation" of truth. Husserl's self-assessment of the text as "very imperfect" concerns precisely the theory of language.

#### Objective Validity of Predicative Judgments in Their Various Levels. Their Grounding Through Evidence

(218) Without carrying out constitutive considerations here, I can say as a beginning philosopher the following. The appendix proceeds from the natural attitude, as a "beginning philosopher," before transcendental analysis.

(218–219) What "the" world is for us and that it is, it is from (in and on the basis of) our experience, the connected, concordantly streaming experience of us all. It is experienced by me at every moment of waking life as finitized present world, with a past disclosable piecewise in memory and a future as horizon of future presents. This environment of mine is also world for the co-present, co-past and co-future others, each with his own world-experience. To my environment belong the others as in it and at once in our common world, and belongs that they are born and have descendants, and that humans will be born and have descendants in open endlessness, likewise for the past. Common experience (Husserl's note: more clearly, communalization of experiences, mine and those of the others I experience, who experience one another, so my actual experience communalizing with the empathized experience of the others now experienced, then the mediacies of communication) exceeds my and everyone's finite life and extends through all our lives and the open chain of generations. It reaches beyond the others I know and those I come to know individually through history, into the indeterminately posited human masses determined as national, state and other communities under the title of historical peoples. The common world also carries indirect indications of animal and human being our history can no longer reach, and indirectly grounded possibilities of co-existence of animal and perhaps human-like generations not belonging to our earthly chain. Our environment, historically-generatively connected with us, accessible step by step from us present ones in the unity of generatively bound history, has an open horizon of a nature transcending the nature belonging to this circle and actually accessible, an always inaccessible "astronomical nature," to which generative nexuses of world-experiencing subjects might belong, who could enter into community with us (perhaps even generatively unite with us) only if in a future present the inaccessibility of stellar nature were overcome and it were turned into an accessible near nature. But even then a still inaccessible distant world with unknown subjects would be prefigured behind the stars made accessible. The world of common experience extends by generations and by history, and beyond them by indirect indications (fossils, prehistory). Its outer horizon is "astronomical nature," inaccessible in principle but meant as possibly accessible; possible extraterrestrial subjects belong to its horizon. Each overcoming of inaccessibility would prefigure a new inaccessible beyond, the structure of the iterable horizon.

(219–220) In this way the communal or objectively existing world is given in universal experience as a world first for us humans, and "we humans" denotes an endless chain of generations to which we can assign no beginning and no end (both must be left problematic as to whether and how), within which we have an always mobile, openly extending history that makes us certain not only generally of an environment of our historical humanity but also discloses it to our experiential knowledge in progressive determinacy. Within it relative environments separate for relatively closed humanities, and finally each has his private environment, while all these environments cohere into the unity of one environment (that of the whole chain of generations centred in us). This itself has its horizons: its inner horizon of the unknown within general familiarity (of the individual and of the typology in the structure of each relative environment) in its own open endlessness; and the outer horizon of distance, known in its own style of distance, indicating indeterminately nears that remain inaccessible. The beginning and end of the human chain are left "problematic," against any dogmatic claim. Environments are nested (private, communal, humanity-wide) and the whole has inner and outer horizons.

(220) Universal world-experience thus has a peculiar structure: first, it is oriented for each from himself, offering the world as oriented from here to there, near to far, environment to distant worlds; second, it is oriented as communal world of experience by the way the other experiencers, through whom I also experience, are given to me: as immediately others, as immediate for them but mediate for me, and so on. Two orientations: spatial (near-far) and social (immediate-mediate others). The world is oriented in both.

(220) The endlessness of extending and further extendable world-experience, through which an "infinite" world (first as endlessly open) becomes my own, passes through this openly endless mediation of others for me, and what holds for me holds for each of these others. We subjects of world-experience have the endlessly open world in its known actualities and unknown possibilities each from himself, through the mediation of others and ultimately of their communications. Infinity is mediated by others and ultimately by communication, the theme announced in the heading that follows.

#### More on Communication

(220) To communications belong first the involuntary expressions of experiencing, what one can see in others' bodily behaviour and apperceptively insert into them, as well as the mediate expressions of their life and of the experiencing that goes with it, for example in work, and what was "there" for them, growing perceptually as their product, their work in the same world in which I am. Communication in the widest sense includes involuntary expression and works, which display the experience of their makers.

(220) To this add intentional and especially linguistic communications. Intentional communication, above all speech, is the second level.

(220) Common experience, which gives us the common endless world and, becoming in an oriented order, the infinite world for "all," is not a thing finished in one act but a process of becoming and of a becoming achievement, which always contains achievement already become and grounds new becoming on it, passive and active achievement. The environment, at least at its lowest level already communal, extends, corrects itself in part or determines itself further through linguistic communication, which always shares in building the experiential sense of the world in which we live acting, and in which, especially in acting from theoretical interest, scientific interest, we produce formations of knowledge of a particular kind which we call scientific statements, "propositions," principles, conclusions, scientific "facts of experience." Common experience is a historical process in which language always participates; science is a special product of this linguistic process.

(220–221) Statements of every kind, normal serious statements (not playful phantasy statements and other abnormal modes, among which we now also count monological statements in silent thought), have their function in forming a world-experience and all have their "objectivity"; quite distinguished, of course, the scientific statements, whose clarification is my particular aim, where I must make intelligible to myself what science as a system of statements, or scientific subjectivity forming them, is really aiming at. Monological statements in silent thought count as "abnormal" relative to communication, a remarkable reversal of Logical Investigations I § 8, where the solitary mental life was the paradigm of pure meaning. Here the normal statement is communicative.

(221) Experience springs from experience. In its partly passive, partly active course experience produces knowledge as lasting acquisition, available henceforth in recollection. It prefigures what is to be expected. Future experience of the new is founded in it by analogy with the familiar; it determines for the future; it is apperception and with it the apprehension of the new in a familiar typology, recognizing the new as house (as such things have been experienced repeatedly), as tree, the house's colour as yellow. Experience generates its own continuation through typification: the genetic law of apperception.

(221) World-experience is from the outset always communal experience, however it may widen and the world itself take on extended sense. The oriented structure of the sense "world," in which it has its typology of access and its way of keeping identity while taking up ever new particular sense, has an environmental typology we must describe more closely. The thesis "world-experience is always already communal" is decisive and anticipates No. 34's instinctive communalization. The structure of the environment is the next theme.

#### Typology of the Environment, Homeworld and Experience of the Homeworld

(221) I as experiencer have a nearest environment, a near world, which we may call the homeworld, the world in which I am already at home from my own experience, the well-known, long-familiar environment I originally acquired. The homeworld is an individual-typical identity in the change of manifold modes of givenness. In changing actual experience everyone has an actual momentary present with a horizon, and this mode of givenness is a presentation of the homeworld, which keeps its identity in activity or passivity of such modes. To it belong the nearest others sharing the same "home," who have acquired roughly the same from their own experience. We also count the immediate exchange of experience in which the familiar common home grows (home in an extended sense). Each has his home as that of his own original experience, and in communalization there are differences. All home-things, known and identified for the home-companions in direct intercourse as belonging to the same home, are not without differences: one knows them more precisely, another less, and each "knows" that too from original intercourse. That belongs for each to the inner horizon. The homeworld is defined experientially: the familiar world acquired by my own experience and immediate exchange. It is identical through its modes of givenness, like a perceived thing. Differences of acquaintance among home-companions are themselves known and belong to the homeworld's inner horizon.

(222) For each individual the familiar home also has undisclosed horizons, opened only by occasional events, perhaps intentionally in practical life and its new goals, as when I want to rebuild my house and expose the unknown foundations to examine them. In general one will know things another does not, without prejudice to the commonality of the homeworld. Even the most familiar has unknown depths; the foundation of the house is the image.

(222) The content of the homeworld changes, despite the preservation of a lasting typology and of individual objects (things, workplaces, tools, humans and animals) in their individual typicality; new objects appear, from the experiencer's passivity or activity (perhaps through his productive activities); they become known and remain their time, others disappear, change, and so the concrete type of the homeworld in its temporariness changes. But this mode of change belongs itself to the general type of being of the homeworld. The homeworld is historical in a modest sense: its content changes while its type of change is constant.

(222) We can extend the sense of this homeworld, and it extends of itself in the natural course of experience: one's own home with garden, field, then extended, one's own village, town, and at once our village, our town. Here extension is served, already in the narrowest home and all the more in the widening homeworld, by synthetic complementation of one's own and original communal experience through communicating statements. The homeworld expands concentrically, from house to village to town, and communication is the medium of the expansion.

#### Original Communication as Extension of the Experience of the One Who Understands

(222–223) The most original communication is explication and linguistic expression of what I directly experience or have experienced and what the other, favourably, also has in his field of experience, though not in his actual present field, so that, if the statement is sufficiently complete and determinate and related to the already common and certainly valid homeworld, he can not only understand it perfectly but at once perform it in co-believing. So through communication he shares in an experience he does not himself have originally; he gains a secondary experience (experience of the experiential content of what the experienced other has experienced), which only to a small extent can lead him to original experience of the same content and in any case has its own right, its own modes of confirmation, from the concordance of communal experiential life in which original experiences and the mediate, secondary experiences through communications combine concordantly and, remaining valid, let concordance persist. Where this is not so, where communications, directly or indirectly, conflict with what I experience or others (according to their communications, but well fitting) have experienced, believing acceptance turns into disbelief: what supposedly is or was, is not and was not. Husserl's note: not sufficiently analytical. Testimony is "secondary experience," a mode of experience with its own right and its own confirmation by coherence. The account is a phenomenological epistemology of testimony: neither reducible to inference nor accepted blindly, but governed by coherence within the communal experiential life.

(223) The one who understands gains an original secondary experience when, understanding the communication, he at once intuitively re-enacts it, having what is communicated intuitively "as if" he saw it, as if he were the other. By its intersubjective sense this experience-as-if is at once possible experience of the same for me, what I would really see if I "went there." But statements are also understood emptily without passing into intuitive quasi-performance, and they can be, by virtue of the language already formed in relation to the homeworld and adapted to its generally persisting typology. Proper names, common names, states of affairs are understood in empty recognition of what is meant (our house, father) and generally co-believed unless already crossed out in this determination. Testimony, like empathy (No. 6), has two levels: empty understanding and intuitive re-enactment "as if" one were the other. Empty understanding is made possible by a language shaped to the homeworld's typology.

(223) The statement has a content that can pass from person to person in the community, and it has its objective sense if anyone could state and understand it in the same sense and possibly believe it. It has objective validity or truth if I and anyone can at any time convince ourselves of it, by original experience (perception, recollection, original induction) or by secondary taking over from someone who formed it by original experience, so far as trustworthiness and what was trustingly taken over continue to be confirmed. What is objectively valid for one in this way is so for everyone in the same community, since everyone can be referred to the ultimate source of truth, original experience of the matter and others' original experience. Objective truth at the homeworld level is defined by possible verification by anyone in the community, directly or through trustworthy testimony. Objectivity is community-relative, the "truth for everyone" of a homeworld.

(223) We thus have objective statements related to this homeworld, of the following kinds. The paragraph introduces a typology of homeworld-objective statements.

(223) First, immediate and mediate descriptive statements about individuals, known to anyone, directly experienceable or through the mediacy of communication, and reachable on equally known paths; determination in empirical concepts. Direct experience: perception, recollection, original expectation (primal induction) of the similar under similar circumstances, empathy. Empathy is listed among the modes of direct experience, a classification consistent with Beilage IV.

(223) Second, general description, typical generalities as generalities of induction; inductive inference by means of them. Through "it is generally so under such typical circumstances, such a thing generally occurs under such typical changes of the known circumstances," communicating, I lead my companion to experience secondarily something typical he has not actually acquired in his own experience. Typical generalizations transmit experience the hearer has not had, the epistemic function of practical lore and proverbs.

(224) Third, thought-achievement not only in descriptive statements but in determining individual objects by thought-determinations such as measure and number, by which I and anyone can convince ourselves indirectly of identity and non-identity (individual difference) of objects in discretely connected presents. Measure and number are introduced at the homeworld level as means of identifying individuals across separated presents: the origin of exactness in practical identification.

(224) Production of linguistically documented objective "world"-knowledge as an intersubjective acquisition of judgment, as common knowledge, a common stock one can presuppose and extend, each taking over the others' acquisitions and communicating his own, extending their knowledge of the world in a determinate way, first as foundation for individual and intersubjective practice of life. Linguistic documentation makes knowledge a common stock, the precondition of tradition and science.

#### Human and Animal Communication

(224) We thus do not have, like the animal, a familiar homeworld through mere own and others' direct experience and a common immediate understanding and direct taking over of the other's experiential belief and content from the commonality of synthetically connecting, mutually complementing and balancing experience, nor the animal mode of communication by indicating something directly experienceable, but expression, communication by language, which stamps the typical forms of thought-formations as meanings and can stamp them completely, and at the same time and correlatively indicates the activities to be performed, on the basis of the passive pre-givenness of experience (the synthetic sediments of earlier experience), the activities of explication, conceptual grasp, the whole thinking belonging to the unity of predicative judgment in which the formations would originally arise in evidence. In communication, re-understanding means a quasi-performance of these activities and their initial experiences, which the one who understands in general never lived through, so that through an indication from the linguistic intentions a possible experience is mediated to him, in motivated co-belief, and again mediated what it lacks for him in determinacy and what would be determined for him by explication and his own typification. Husserl's note: all this not really adequately explicated! Animals share experience directly and indicate what is directly experienceable; language encodes the forms of thought as meanings and thereby indicates the activities by which the hearer could originally produce the judgment. Understanding a sentence is a quasi-performance of the judging activity, the theory of reactivation in Origin of Geometry. Husserl's dissatisfaction is recorded.

(224) The function of language in the chain of generations would still have to be discussed. Language as the vehicle of tradition across generations, the theme Origin of Geometry will treat through writing.

(224–225) Human communal life thus becomes possible as the life of a linguistic community, of quite another kind than animal communal life. The human homeworld, which is the basic piece of the structure of the objective world for him, or can become so in higher development in ever more significant forms, is essentially determined by language. Only thereby arises not only a sensuously common world, a concrete present world (in an extended sense including the still living horizon of co-present and past and a piece of living future), but a practical human homeworld, whose incomparably wider circle of experience effectively contains the linguistically mediated experiences of companions, not only those fully intuitively re-understood and taken into belief, but also the incompletely or not at all intuitively re-understood linguistic formations of knowledge, as ones that can be clarified and used when needed. In every present the experiencer has not only his "sensuous" environment, actually present in original experience or becoming his in original "empathy" as secondary sensuous experience, but this present with a linguistic coating and a linguistic-apperceptive horizon; to this world belong manifold own and alien linguistic formations with their novel validity, accessibility, confirmability. This environment is now typically one and the same for all who live together. In it lie each one's practical goals, his formations of achievement; to it relates working life, but also stating life, always serving some practice, in which the environment as linguistically determined enriches and extends itself intersubjectively as the same. Husserl's long note sketches the levels: a common familiar homeworld with near sphere and relative distances inhabited by others who have their distances; familiarity in the individual and in the typical; orientation forms (right, left, near, far, number of steps, measurement by counting, iteration, order with counting); determination of individuals by properties, concrete type, relations, order, measure, number (the last formal, like all forms of thought, the concrete stemming from familiar experience); hence objective, exchangeable judgments understandable and confirmable by anyone, their evidence that of familiar concrete experience, of familiar communication, of methods of division, measuring, counting, determining direction and order, evident in performing these thought-achievements on what is itself experienced, the familiar world presupposed; extension of the homeworld to the world for the people, a communicative humanity, world of the present, past as world of past humanities and generations; objective statements of a new kind: what can be said about distances inaccessible to humanity at all? The formal, which distance as world-distance must share with nearness; the spatio-temporal causal (mechanical). Relativity of all environmental statements, all descriptive ones and those based on description of the occasional. Irrelative: form-statements, essential statements. Rational objectivity; relatively rational statements: what can be stated for everyone in general in truths in themselves. World-statements that are not environment-statements. World-formal statements. Essential form of the world. Eternal truths for the given world and eidetic essential statements for every possible one. The main text makes the homeworld "essentially determined by language," with a "linguistic coating" over every present: a thesis close to Heidegger's and Gadamer's later views, here grounded in the theory of communication. The note gives a compressed genealogy of objectivity: homeworld-objectivity through measure and number; then statements about the inaccessible, which can only be formal (spatio-temporal, causal); then the distinction of relative (environmental, occasional) and irrelative (formal, eidetic) truths; finally "eternal truths" for this world and eidetic truths for every possible world. This is the plan the Krisis executes in its account of Galilean science and of the life-world's a priori.

(226) Communal life extending and tending in "political" form toward universality of a practice, as far as possible communication reaches. Universality of practice as universality of harmonious, satisfying and intersubjectively satisfied practice. Universality of the linguistic community, bridging foreign languages, unity of the practical environment, unity of a homeworld of higher order on the basis of the synthetic construction of homeworlds of lower level. The political tendency is toward a universal homeworld built from lower homeworlds, with translation bridging languages. "Political" here means the practical organization of a universal community, close to the Kaizo idea of a community of renewal.

(226) The theoretical interest. Knowledge in general, to gain power (apart from knowledge from curiosity). Universal knowledge in service of a universal rational, satisfying, hence satisfied practice. Tendency toward a systematic science, a systematic construction of a universe of objectively valid judgments completely spanning the universe, the universal home in its possible extension, spanning its open individual and general typology. This universal knowledge, as objective accessible to everyone, gives everyone universal knowledge of the practical homeworld (a universal knowledge that would have to adapt to continued transformation) and so the foundation of knowledge for all practical plans, especially for all practical possibilities, goals and ways toward the final goal of enabling a universal politically satisfied practice. Here theory is grounded in practice (knowledge for power, the Baconian motive) and directed to universal satisfied practice. This contrasts with the Krisis and the Vienna lecture, where the theoretical attitude is a disinterested attitude born of wonder; the two accounts are not contradictory but distinguish a practical and a purely theoretical genesis of science.

(226) Homeworld and unhomely world. Relativity. And yet a lasting difference: the earthly world and the world of the heavens, the inaccessible, forever unhomely distance, which yet has the sense of distance that could ideally be brought near, transformed into nearness. The heavens are the permanently unhomely, yet meant as ideally approachable, the limit of the homeworld.

(226) Universal knowledge of the world; the world spanning all distance from theoretical interest; how it becomes practical in that the "accidents" of the homeworld become explicable, in analogy to the intelligible causality of the intervention of distances that were only temporarily inaccessible, by "astronomical" causality, and these causalities become practically relevant. The infinities, outer and inner, the physical conception of the world and its confirmation. The construction of the objective universe constituted through linguistic thought, constituted through iteration in thought and in infinitum. Astronomy makes "accidents" (seasons, tides, eclipses) explicable, and so practically relevant; the physical universe is constituted by iterated thought, in infinitum. The outer and inner infinities are the infinitely large and the infinitely divisible, both products of thought's iteration.

(226–227) This novel objective knowledge as exact, the objective world already a formation of thought, a formation of method, and then the theme of objective, "exact" science, in which the objective world does not merely explicate itself like something homely and is then determined more closely. The objective world of exact science is a "formation of method," a construct, no longer the explication of a homeworld. This is the thesis of Krisis § 9 on the substitution of a mathematized nature for the life-world.

(227) Radically insightful science is only a science that makes this method itself thematic. Universal theory of forms of the constitution of the all-world from the theory of forms of iterative homeworlds. The appendix ends with the transcendental demand: science becomes radically insightful only by thematizing its own method, and the universal world must be understood as constituted from the iteration of homeworlds. The "theory of forms" of homeworlds is the life-world ontology of Krisis § 51.

### Appendix XIII. Normal Human Community and the Order of Levels of Relative Normalities and Abnormalities. The Problem of the Identical World for Everyone (Probably Summer 1931)

Husserl's heading note: important considerations on the method of a systematic explication of the world of experience, transcendental aesthetic, ontologically and constitutively-subjectively, mainly with regard to the levels of normality and abnormality.

(227) To a pre-given world belongs a totality of humans belonging to it, acting with one another directly or mediately, to whom it is pre-given as the same, psychologically constituted as the same. Ideally speaking: if really the same world were pre-given to each (Husserl's note: in the sense of actually accessible?), and accordingly the world-life of humans were related to this identical world, identical for them, and if through progressive humanization the identity of the pre-given world remained identically the same for humans (all newly born included), there would be no difference between educated and uneducated, scholars and laymen. So, if we understand pre-givenness in the most proper sense of actual pre-givenness. But not everyone really has access to everything that is; for each the world is conscious with an unknown horizon, but not everyone can gain knowledge of everything unknown by experience, directly or indirectly by taking over others' experiences or their descriptions. The "same world for everyone" is not the same accessible world: differences of education show unequal access. Identity of the world must be understood despite inequality of access.

(227–228) How in our world? Sciences also occur in the world, states and state laws. Not everyone can get to know the sciences, can gain "experience" of their theories, that is, reproduce them, see them, re-understanding and re-seeing the spiritual formations produced in the work of experienced researchers. Religious symbols, dogmas belong to the world too, but direct access to them has only the one who stands in that religion; anyone else indirectly, since in imperfect re-understanding, as irreligious or of another religion, he understands only that such things are what the believer realizes in a certain way in actual belief, as the one ignorant of a science has the indirect idea of "experts" for whom the uncomprehended or half-comprehended propositions and proofs have full meaning and the force of real insight. Likewise art: hearing the sounds is not hearing the music, and hearing music as a chain of single harmonies is not taking in the symphony or quartet in its real sense and peculiar actual being. Access to cultural objects requires capacities that not all have: science requires training, religion faith, music cultivated hearing. The outsider knows only that the insider has access, an indirect representation of others' insight. The religious example treats faith as a mode of access to a reality of the world, not as mere belief about it.

(228) What then does a humanity mean as all-humanity of a world identically pre-given to it, or when I say as phenomenologist: the world is pre-given to me, I am pre-given to myself as a human who has world-experience and knowledge, who experiences other humans as world, as experiencing and knowing the same world, and transcendentally I am related to a transcendental intersubjectivity that has one and the same world constitutively in common? What does identical world and its constitution mean, what characterizes subjects as subjects "for" this world? The question is restated transcendentally: what makes subjects "for" the same world, given unequal access?

(228) I am a scientist. I do not count science, theory about the world, as part of it. The world purely from experience comes first. But what does that mean? Is science not a form of culture, and does culture not belong to the facts of experience of the world? Does its scientific value (the truth or untruth of theories), which appears as result in insightful grounding, belong to it? The history of science, of art, culture in its historicity, we will not banish from the factual world. The scientist's methodological exclusion of science from the "world of pure experience" conflicts with science being a cultural fact in the world.

(228) Certainly, but we will say: if we take the attitude toward the world merely as universe of factual realities, it comprises nature, humans and their real life, their real capacities, real identifications, real evidences and capacities to repeat evidences and in synthetic repetition find the same as evidently existing. Theories themselves do not belong to the world, but the discovered theories as discovered by these persons, so better, the discoveries of discovering persons and others' capacities to re-understand, co-judge, co-know. To the concept of reality corresponds the concept of real experience, experience in the ordinary sense, more precisely experience of the world of bodies, of the human, animal and plant world, of nature in the widest sense (probably the physis of the ancients). Ideal objects (theories) are not realities; their discovery and understanding are. The world of realities is physis in the Greek sense, including living beings, not modern physical nature. This separates the ontology of the real world from that of ideal objects, the distinction Origin of Geometry will reconnect through the concept of tradition.

(228–229) What is it then to have the same world pre-given with a totality of others, the same nature, space, time, filled with the same realities, things, humans and communities, cultural objects, real facts of every kind? But I and every ego have the world only horizonally, and we do not have the same facts actually given, only pre-given. And we are far from being able to experience as fact by re-experience everything a factual person lives through in himself, if he is a Newton, for example, even if this Newton wished to tell us of his life as well as he can. Not all facts are accessible, and not merely because we are inhibited in exercising our capacities. The mature person who never practised science cannot re-understand scientific thought, and his life is generally too short to develop the corresponding capacities. Yet Newton or Einstein is there for everyone in the world of experience, understood by everyone as a human, only imperfectly. The world has not only the general horizon; the single realities already experienced have horizons in experience, by no means fully accessible. The world of experience is an open universe of experienceable concrete realities. So far "we" all have one world of experience as we have access (at least indirectly, by means of all the others to whom we have access) to all realities to which they have access, as the same objects of possible experience, though not to all their objective features. What anyone has concretely given as a real thing in perception or an intuitive modification of it, I can perceive too, or could have, or can make intuitive mediately by taking over his experience (as if it were perceived; Husserl's note: this holds for all who stand in actual connection with me directly or mediately), in an appearance that brings it to intuitive validity properly or improperly-mediately, though with a horizon perhaps wholly inaccessible to me (note: "perception" here naturally including perception of others and its modifications in the analogizing of animals with humans). The sameness of the world is sameness of the objects of possible experience, not of accessible features: I can perceive Newton, not his insight. Access to all realities is guaranteed by the chain of mediated access through others; access to all their features is not. The world is therefore one at the level of objects and plural at the level of horizons.

(229) But then the problem is how the world can be pre-given as determined in itself, hence existing for everyone, even as to what is inaccessible to many. If I gain insight into the psychic character and achievement of a productive mind, perhaps as the only one, how can I count this as part of everyone's world, claim my closer determination of the horizon as closer determination of the human who is this human for everyone? It is not generally communicable, cannot be taken over by everyone like an experience, cannot be confirmed or corrected by everyone through insertion into the concordant nexus of intersubjective experience. The problem: knowledge accessible to one alone, such as an understanding of a genius, seems to be knowledge of the common world, yet cannot be verified by everyone.

(229–230) What I learn in my concordant experience as determining the world already valid to me also concerns the world the other experiences. He is constituted for me as related to the same world. I do not experience him as experiencing everything I experience, though a ground of commonality of actual experience is given by empathy as a domain of common actual accessibilities. This concerns the layer of concrete descriptive physical nature, bodies, and a small layer, structure of the soul, of personal life and activity, especially of instinctive life passing into conscious needs and typical modes of satisfaction. Bringing others in deeper layers to experience and knowledge, I do not transfer the opinion that the other has access to them and the corresponding capacity as I do; likewise for other real facts. We experience the same circle of realities and the particular real thing together; we have a common world of experience. Every experience I make of a real thing remains valid for the common world, as long as not crossed out by emerging discordance, as long as it does not disturb intersubjective concordance. That the other lacks certain experiences and cannot have them, as he is, does not disturb concordance. My extended experience therefore continues to hold for our common world. For others, what I perhaps alone experience, in a way excluding communicability and translation into analogizing intuition, becomes graspable as indirect indication when (as in experience of scientific knowledge) I draw consequences that are confirmed in a way intuitively understandable to others. Then the thought of a "certain" insight the other has, by which he foresees worldly facts, substitutes for the missing experience. The answer: private access is admissible into the common world if it does not disturb concordance, and it can become indirectly accessible to others through its confirmed consequences (predictions). The common minimal layer is nature, bodies and the instinctive-needful structure of the soul; deeper layers are accessible unequally.

(230) A human community in which each experiences each other as co-subject of the same world of experience in the sense that he has direct or indirect access in own experiences to all realities of this world is a normal community. Each experiences the others and himself as normal. (Husserl's note: but that is still imprecise; the community must be grasped as a totality of humans standing, however mediately, in a nexus of experience.) In this sense a people of "primitive humans" is normal in relation to their world, the world pre-given to them, and concordant in mutual taking over of experiences. Normality is defined by mutual access to all realities of the shared world. Every closed homeworld community, "primitive" included, is normal in this sense, relative to its own world: a relational, non-hierarchical concept.

(230–231) That does not mean all are on the same level. There is another concept of the normal. Within the same folk circle there are differences in sense experience and all capacities: subnormal (temporarily or permanently, the former in illness that can strike anyone) or supernormal, and permanently abnormal like the colour-blind, the deaf, likewise supernormally intelligent, subnormal, fools, also abnormal as idiots. This is a typology running within normality in the previous sense. An averageness is constituted (Husserl's note: related to the typology of ages of life as developmental stages, and of estates, vocational strata), in which on average the world is sensuously seen, heard so and so, judged, valued, acted on so and so. To this corresponds the world of experience of the human in averageness, what belongs to it experientially, generally speaking (for each developmental stage), and determines the world-representation for "everyone" in it. But the average world-representation (of each age) is not the world itself valid for this humanity. And finally it belongs to the generally known and co-valid that there are exceptional, supernormal humans to whom much is accessible that is not to the average (note: there is therefore not one but many averagenesses, among them one or several highest). The second concept of normality is statistical: averageness (Durchschnittlichkeit), relative to age and social stratum. Averageness is plural, and the average world-representation is not the world, since exceptional people have access to more. The term "averageness" is the same one Heidegger used in Being and Time (§ 9, § 27) for the everydayness of the "they"; Husserl uses it descriptively, without Heidegger's critical sense.

(231) The world in the experiential form of averageness is of course much richer than the general world of experience of humans who have access to all reality. The child, for example, who "sees" everything but still "understands" so little of the human and cultural world, is not a subject of that averageness of the mature. The world of experience has for every mature person (of his group) a typology, an all-familiar typical shape, constantly counted on in practical action. This too must be constituted. An unusually stupid person, an idiot, can be normal in so far as he shares a pure world of realities with the normal, but he is like a child or still more stupid. The "general world" (realities accessible to all) is poorer than the average world (realities plus culturally understood significances). Children and the mentally deficient share the general world but not the average world. Husserl's vocabulary of mental deficiency is that of his time.

(231) Is everything now in order? Do we not stand in abstraction, and must we not by conscious dismantling gain the right descriptive abstractions and from there, by steps of concretion, lead to the really concrete world and world-constituting subjectivity that I, philosophizing, can call our world of experience? Self-criticism: the concepts so far are abstract; the method of dismantling and reconstruction must produce them properly.

(231–232) Must I not start from the basic fact: I am subject of a world into which I live humanly, which is always already for me a universe of presence (Vorhandenheit), of realities to which I am related in experience and which, as experienced and experienceable, "concern" or "do not concern" me, which are positively or negatively in question for me practically? In this world others are present, but they are at once co-subjects of experience and practice, living into the same world, and in community with me, practically and cognitively. This world as one and the same for us all, into which we all live together in mediate practical community, stands in its being in the correlation of presence for "concerning-us" (singly and communally) and of us as subjects whom it concerns. From the actual concern of occupation with it, it takes on ever new content as present, as pre-given. That is the basic fact. Unity of this world for "us all" is correlatively unity of humans standing in direct or mediate life-connection, an experiencing, valuing and ultimately practical connection, as this world is for them practical environment. The basic fact is practical: the world is the correlate of "concern" (Angehen), of what matters to us. Husserl's vocabulary here (Vorhandenheit, Angehen) is strikingly close to Heidegger's Being and Time (presence-at-hand, concern), though Husserl reverses the priority: practical concern is the correlate of a world of present realities.

(232) How then to reach the homeworld and go beyond it, how determine the homeworld in its constitutively founding shape by dismantling? Should we say: the world as life-environment, practical environment, has an unpractical horizon, an unexperienced, unexperienceable horizon, not merely left "out of play" practically (which would itself be practical), but not in question for practice at all? That can only mean: it lies in the manner of being and life of this humanity and its environment that it keeps within a practical finitude of experience and effect, that practical impossibility of further experience narrows practice and the life-world, and only the empty thought-possibility of further experience remains, disregarded as practically unrealizable, as the life-world of a small island people that has its "world-representation" in complete isolation and its world as finite life-environment. So in various ways a relative geographical and at first insurmountable boundary can exist and condition the life-world. But a homeworld, a practically closed environment, can also remain essentially in finitude while a wider horizon of human and world existence (other life-worlds) has already been formed. The homeworld is defined by practical finitude: beyond it lies a horizon not in question for practice at all. The isolated island people is the model. But practical finitude can persist even when other worlds are known, so the homeworld is not merely geographical isolation.

(232–233) This yields an order of levels of relative normalities and abnormalities and levels of extension of "our" world-experience and of the experienced world as such. More precisely, the world-horizon gains ever new occupation by experiences and corresponding corrections and prefigurations, but always keeps an openness, that is, indeterminacy that can take on ever new determination, closer and other determination, by experience as prefigurations. We say: man learns by progressive experience to exceed his relative environment, he learns to know the world better and better. Conversely we must say transcendentally: he constitutes for himself the world valid to him with ever richer sense, but valid only with an indeterminate horizon. The natural and transcendental descriptions of the same process are contrasted: "learning to know the world" versus "constituting the world with richer sense." The latter does not deny the former but explains it.

(233) In this relativity the concrete world is built up in living constitutive genesis as world for the human being normal in the most general and extended sense. He is no longer merely the normal of his homeworld but the normal of the knowledge of humans and world to be gained by synthetic extension of environments. From my homeworld, as world of concordant intersubjective experience of my fellow natives, every stranger is finally normal for me and us, so far as he is understood like me and us natives as subject of his homeworld constituted in concordant experience, as member of his community of natives connected by concordant community of experience. Husserl's note: what should this mean more concretely? First, as subject of a homeworld I get to know strangers and an alien homeworld as meant by strangers, "unintelligible" to me except for the most general intelligible form of its being-meant. There is commonality and difference, and the difference between the fact of the strangers' coexistence and their "world-representation," their ways of behaving toward the world, and the question of truth. The world of experience is practical world in the sense of the homeworld. And so existing world and homeworld first separate by there being several homeworlds with their different practical faces. The stranger is normal relative to his own homeworld, and the higher normality is that of the synthetic knowledge of many homeworlds. The note repeats Beilage XI's thesis: world and homeworld separate only when there are several homeworlds.
