(233) Understanding and apperceiving the stranger thus is itself a still indeterminate horizon-experience; to really explicit understanding, real experience of the other and a fully established community of experience with him, belongs that I really get to know his homeworld, the one present to him, or the home-humanity in its environmental life, work, creation, in which this homeworld got and still gets its sense of being. This knowledge is provided scientifically and methodically by the human sciences (Geisteswissenschaft), as science of humans in their homeworld and further of humanities in their cultural life related to relative environments yet entering into connection. Husserl's note: natural development of the human into a human of his homeworld from childhood; the homeworld as valid to him, the correlate. Then problems of history, of the development of homeworlds and humanities in correlation and together. Our native nature, our history, in which we became and our environment acquired a historical face, a cultural face. Their (the strangers') homeworld, their history. Can I do otherwise than re-understand, if it really succeeded, transposing myself by historical empathy into them, living with them, deciding, agreeing with them? Is the world a progressive synthesis of historical finitudes? Science, phenomenology, and infinity. Full understanding of the stranger requires knowledge of his homeworld, which the human sciences supply. The note asks whether historical empathy can ever exceed re-understanding to real co-living and agreement, the hermeneutic problem; and whether the world is "a progressive synthesis of historical finitudes," the formula that best sums up Husserl's late concept of world.

(233) But is the primitive human not then human of a primitive homeworld and so not normal in the same sense? Of course. Normality relative to a homeworld is not identical across homeworlds; the "primitive" is normal in his, not in ours. The brevity of the answer marks it as a direct consequence.

(233–234) Now consider: a first existing world I have (in my childhood genesis) as concordantly experienced homeworld of my natives. If I understand strangers as subjects of their home-community and their other homeworld, this is first their meant world for me, and the question is whether and how far I can take over their validities of experience in re-understanding, progress to a synthesis of their homeworld with mine. How do I come, and must I come, to an encompassing concordance? But can I not, from my transcendental ground of my primordiality, my empathy and through it my possible common world, investigate essentially what structure a world must have as homeworld, and what structure a concordant world in general must have in essential necessity, through whatever syntheses of whatever homeworlds it may come about for me in a relative sense (relative by its open horizon), hence the essential structure of concordant, even international, intercultural experience, and the essential forms of possible mutual corrections? Homeworldly, national and other concordance of relatively closed humanities is not yet universal concordance. It lies in the essence of continuously confirming empathy that we participants together are related to the same world, belong to the same home-humanity. That does not exclude, rather includes, mutual corrections in the particular case, with individuals or communities. So it is not said that what I have as concordant world in experience keeps its concordance as part of the grounded concordant synthesis of my and others' experiences. The question is not who has the advantage in the immanence of his experience, but how the community of both sides' total experiences stands in their possible or producible synthesis of concordance. If it is produced, even in synthesis with ever new humanities, the question of the finality of this concordance can still be posed, or rather, concordance is always relative and in motion. Experience, individual and communal, does not stand still. I, explicating the world valid to me and correcting validity from myself with concordance as correlate of being, do not have in view the world of my merely private convictions but the world for all. Re-understanding others' experiences and concordances in me, I let them count as co-constituting my world as long as discordance does not lead to crossing out and their experiences reveal themselves as deceptive illusions. Here I have the possibility, from my immediate, proper, primordial experience, to investigate the essential possibilities of concordant empathy of various levels and of concordances to be produced by synthesis, taking into account the possibly co-valid others and their validities, thereby to outline a possible world of experience as environment of all fellow humans in the unity of a community of experience and of a connected humanity in general, in open infinity. The method for the problem of cultural relativity is eidetic: investigate from the transcendental ground the essential structure of any homeworld and of any intercultural concordance. The decisive move is to reject the question "whose homeworld is right?" ("who has the advantage in the immanence of his experience") in favour of "what synthesis of both sides' experiences is possible?" Concordance is always relative and in motion. The result is an idea of a world for a connected humanity, in open infinity.

(234–235) This will yield the task of a transcendental aesthetic, a transcendental "empiriography," which designs an all-human structure of experience and of the world of experience, to serve as norm for the critique of the relatively concordant worlds of experience and opinion of any humanities. To the empiriographic (transcendental-aesthetic) sphere belong humans themselves and their conscious life, humanities, their meant environments as such and the constant movement of life in which the mobile life-environment of the all-communities, the home-communities, changes in standing-streaming fashion and relatively keeps its unity and inner typology; correlatively the humans' mode of being, their being in becoming, their personality maturing from childhood and developing, community in communities. All this as psychological and psychic in the widest sense. But since the task is transcendental, humans have become transcendental, absolute subjects, subjects that have transcendentally constituted a meant "world" concordant relative to them. At the same time they are in question as constituting co-subjects for the world that in truth of experience is mine and the same for all co-subjects, constituted in a possible concordance of intersubjective experiences; so the question is whether and how my and all transcendental co-subjects' experiences and relative concordances can be brought to a universal concordance to be continued forever in infinitum, in the indicated, more or less realized nexus with alien home-communities, or the open mobility of nexus-formation. "Transcendental aesthetic" or "empiriography" is the eidetic science of the world of experience as such, a norm for criticizing particular cultural worlds. Its object includes humans and their cultures as transcendental subjects, and its question is the possibility of a universal concordance in infinitum. The program is the "life-world ontology" of the Krisis with an explicitly critical, normative function.

(235) We see that the question of the transcendental constitution of the world existing for me has its levels; the next is the level of constitution of a concordant homeworld. The formal character of this investigation entails that the problem is also solved for me of how any stranger existing for me constitutes a homeworld in general, provided this essentially possible, openly endless progress in strangeness and in the confirmation of an alien home-community has previously been shown and clarified. Since the analysis of homeworld-constitution is eidetic and formal, it applies to every homeworld, the stranger's included.

(235) The second is the problem of the critique of a homeworld in the horizon of alien homeworlds, or of the critique of a universal experience that is to produce unity over all homeworlds to be connected synthetically, to produce a true world. The question concerns not the fact but the essential possibility, the essential form of an absolute concordance of experience reaching through the merely relative homeworlds and their syntheses into infinity. Husserl's note: the problem of critique is the problem of the genuine relative sense. Critique of a homeworld does not mean declaring it false but determining its genuine relative sense, what it is right about relative to its situation. The note answers the question of Beilage XI about "situational truth."

(235) A certain structure already belongs to the essential form of homeworld and home-community. Every homeworld has its openness, which makes it seem possible that it keep itself as homeworld and extend spatio-temporally. To the homeworld belongs above all the formal structure of filled space-time, the construction from single realities in spatio-temporal order, in modes of temporalization, in spatio-temporal shape and not only position as to spatio-temporal content: the core structure of mere nature, and so on. Particular questions arise, for example the conditions of the possibility of the identity of a natural thing as object of possible experience, of an experience to be carried out concordantly by all. The essential structure of any homeworld includes filled space-time and a core of mere nature, the invariant through which homeworlds can be synthesized.

(236) The study of the constitution of nature already in the home-sphere leads to the relativity of constitution: nature experienced sensuously normally, nature related to a sensuously normal home-community, and identity of nature for the sensuously abnormal in synthesis with the sensuously normal. But already in the single individual, normal and abnormal constitution of the same. The relativity of sensuous normality, analysed in Beilage II and No. 11, recurs within the homeworld.

(236) This carries over into the inter-homely. It would be thinkable, for example, that the ancient Greeks were really in a certain way colour-blind relative to our modern peoples, though the experienced nature would in essence be the same. Husserl's note: these are evidently problems of historicity, in which humanities exist, how they constitute a single history, how far it reaches. The example refers to the nineteenth-century debate (Gladstone, Geiger, Magnus) on the colour vocabulary of Homer, which some took as evidence of historical colour-blindness. Husserl uses it to show that sensory abnormality can be historical-cultural and still compatible with an essentially identical nature.

(236) True nature as synthetic unity of all normal-human experiences (normal in the previous sense) is an idea; it guides science and its "idealizations," and science then makes possible the anticipation of all relative, merely sensuously experienced natures and correlatively of their constitutive systems. The nature of the normal, abstracting from sensuous abnormality, becomes a merely relative and "subjective" aspect of true nature as idea. Nature from actual and possible intuition, thought in the unity of a universal "picture," is despite its infinity a mere aspect, but through these aspects an intuitive identity is constituted synthetically on the basis of this "experience." Husserl's note: the problem of coexistence, the possibility of an infinity of homeworlds in mediate connection, in relation to the problem of the constitution of an infinite nature from the infinite homeworldly natures, offers further sides requiring great investigations. True nature is an idea guiding scientific idealization; normal intuitive nature becomes one aspect among others. Yet through the aspects an intuitive identity is constituted: the idea is not detached from intuition but is the identity running through aspects. The appendix thus ends with the relation between life-world and scientific nature that the Krisis develops: science idealizes the identity that intuitive aspects already constitute.

### Appendix XIV. On the Problem of the World-View. Transformation of Others' Experience into Possible Experience of My Own (2 October 1932)

(236–237) First, repetition of the primal-modal streaming present of my ego in remembering modification, in continuous iterability. The appendix begins with a constitutive genealogy in numbered steps; the first is the iteration of the living present in recollection, the basis of all further modifications.

(237) This yields primordial egological life in its "immanence" (immanent temporalization). In it the constitution of "transcendent" objectivities, universally of the world, first the abstract layer of the "primordial world" (primordial worldly temporalization). Objective present, past and future as appearing: objective present appearing itself as perception, an immanently self-present experience, the objective present appearing in the immanent present. Past in the immanent modification of recollection appearing as objective past: in the immanent present, past immanent present appears recollectively, but as past perception, and so carries the perceived object in the intentional modification "past object," object of the past temporality. Objective time is constituted inside immanent time by the modification "past perception," which carries its object as past object.

(237) Objective in perception as appearing itself, and its horizon of potential "remembering" presentifications through which the appearing object shows itself in its many sides, could have shown itself. "Possible experience," possible perception, recollection, as modification of memory, immanences to be produced freely and actively in the immanent sphere, so immanent memories in which, by virtue of their immanently arising modifications, objective presents and modified presents constitute themselves in appearance, in concordance of certainty of being. Further possible discordances of objective appearances (as already of immanent memory appearances), modalization. Finally the modification of validity of all modes as phantasy (neutrality modification). Time-constitution again constitution by modifying repetition, iteratively. Every horizon of the thing, its unseen sides, is a set of possible memory-like presentifications. Husserl unifies the whole field of presentification (horizonal, recollective, imaginative) under one concept: modifying repetition of the present. This sets up the next step: empathy as a further modification of the same kind.

(237–238) Second, insertion of the empathy modification and its iteration, again a variant of memory, but of all primordial memories and their modifications: in the sphere of immanence a transcending of the sphere of immanence in the form of primordial spheres of immanence that have the sense of others'. In my own primordiality these alien ones come to "representation," and in certainty of being, through a modification of memory of a higher level that modifies the whole primordial being and brings it in this modification to certainty of being. All earlier iterations and possibilities of iteration are now repeated in modification. In addition the new iteration of this modification itself: others of others. Then correlatively the objective world constituted as world for me together with the others disclosed in my sphere of immanence and others of others. Primordial space-time becomes all-subjective space-time. The primordialities communalized with one another for me as ego are constituted as a communalized intersubjective immanence with ego-subjects actually and potentially there for one another, able to enter into social acts. In this communalization of immanences a communalization of the world-appearances of all individual primordialities and intersubjective constitution of the one objective world for all, temporalizing itself in its actualities and possibilities in the passage from my primordiality to those manifesting themselves in it "memory-like," through motivated co-validity (apart from modality and correction). I dispose not only of my primordial memories; the others' memories too (at all levels: their real perceptions, recollections, expectations, "possible" experiences for unperceived present, for never-perceived past) are available to me as to everyone. Empathy is formally a modification of memory, but a modification of the whole primordial being at once: the other's whole primordiality is presentified as a modified version of mine. All the iterations of my primordial life are repeated in this modified key, and empathy iterates (others of others). Hence "intersubjective immanence": the communalized streams. The last sentence names the theme of the appendix: others' experiences become available to me, and the question is in what sense.

(238) The problem in exemplary formulation: "Of course I and anyone can form in himself a unified world-view, as if the world could be the unity of his own possible experience (in his own primordiality). How can I give it validity; it presents itself already as evidence?" Problem of the critique of this evidence. The problem is the naive universal world-view, in which I imagine the whole world as if it could be my own experience. It seems evident; Husserl asks for its critique.

(238) Remembering, I am with my past ego and its intentional present, what appeared to it in its how. Empathizing in the other, I am with "my" other ego, with his immanence and appearances, what appears to him in its how. If this happens intuitively, I have a certain intentional modification of memory, and as regards his perceptual field it is a co-presentifying memory, similar to a non-empathic co-presentification of object sides co-given to me primordially, when I now make them intuitive: here as possible perception I would have in the future if I "went there," the object thought unchanged being determinative, or if I had directed my perceiving suitably before. If I say instead, I presentify how the object would look if I stood there instead of here, this equally contains horizonally the potentiality in the system of my kinaesthetic capacities and the corresponding syntheses of appearance as possible perceptions. Empathy parallels memory ("being with" my past ego / with "my" other ego) and parallels the co-presentification of unseen sides. The "if I stood there" is itself grounded in my kinaesthetic system, which links the empathic "as if" to my own possibilities.

(238) In empathy the other is presentified as a corresponding ego of capacities in the new modification. As such it is a new "repetition" of what is given in my primordiality. Remembering in my sphere I say: it is as if I were now in the then, but the then is not now; it presentifies a nexus of memory with possible sequences of memories within a possible system of memory. The other is a "repetition" of me as ego of capacities, as recollection is a repetition of my past.

(238–239) What is present to the other is co-present for me, and in a near field I have a synthesis of identity between a primordially reachable co-present and the empathic one. On the other hand, what I experience as determinately empathized co-present is a co-experience by which I synthetically extend my field of primordially known things. My knowledge of my object, the one I experience, or my field of objects, now reaches further, as if I had gone there myself. But I also have mediate empathy, through indirect, for example linguistic, communication, and there I come to extensions that exceed my capacity. Empathy first extends my world by what I could have reached myself ("as if I had gone there"); mediate empathy through communication exceeds my capacities. The problem of the appendix lies in this excess.

(239) Especially not with regard to determinate co-presence but with regard to the past, and from there I gain indirect indications (inductions) for what is in the present world, which, as I become certain in advance, must be demonstrable, findable, though not found in the present and not yet taken from the extended present time into the sphere of familiarity of what is currently perceived. Others' past experiences, communicated, give indications about present facts not yet perceived: inductive extension of the world through testimony about the past.

(239) I can transpose myself into all times, all historical times, related to the generative and communicative humanities in the unity of one humanity. I "interpret" sun, moon, stars, Milky Ways as distant bodies, their appearances as distant appearances to which near appearances would have to belong as in my nearest sphere. Can I transpose myself everywhere? I can think that, as in my near world I am moved or move myself, I could also reach distant and most distant space by motion; my factual capacity is insufficient and necessarily finite. I know that the sun's heat would destroy me if I came too close, but I cannot come too close to a blast furnace either. How can I apperceive all appearances of my primordiality with a sense of being that has in validity an equal system of possible experiences for all bodies, and how can I regard all systems of appearance, all primordial things and worlds, mine and others', as one world from a homogeneous system of appearance, treating what the empathized systems yield exactly as the empathized systems of the near sphere, which I can realize as such in my own experiences? The question generalizes the problem: the homogeneous world, in which every body, near or astronomically far, has the same kind of system of possible experiences, treats the inaccessible as if it were accessible. The blast furnace is a touch of irony: inaccessibility is not peculiar to the stars.

(239–240) How do I understand that I can represent the world intuitively, illustrating it into all infinities, quite as if they were in fact possible experiences for me? What any other can experience and could have experienced, I treat in re-understanding as if I could too (perhaps as if only contingently inhibited); what others once experienced, I treat as if I could have experienced it, if not actually. Yet my finite time is insurmountable, in what I could have actualized before as in what I can realize in future. But I act as if I had wings of time, a capacity of motion through all times, as if I could construct a unity of world-view, of a possible world-experience as my own, in which I could run through all infinities of experience in infinite immanence. Every knowledge of the world I gain by way of others I transform into possible direct experience of my own. The naive world-view is described exactly: I appropriate others' experiences as my own possible experiences and imagine myself with "wings of time" (Zeitflügel), an infinite subject running through all experience. This is the imaginary basis of the "view from nowhere" of objective science.

(240) And yet this cannot seriously be a system of possible primordial experience of my own. The world-view to be formed thus in infinitum by me and by everyone cannot possibly be a true universal world-view. For what about the human subjectivity belonging to the world; the world-view to be constructed relates to it too? The refutation: the world includes subjects, and their subjectivity cannot be appropriated as my possible experience.

(240) If I transpose myself into the bustle of Plato's Academy, I am still the German of today, and the intuition I produce of this bustle has sense only as empathy into a co-present Greek, or into one or another academician himself. My wandering through history and the worlds of the time is a constant empathizing, and the worlds of the time are the environments, to be re-understood, of the people of that time. Historical imagination is empathy, not self-transposition: I remain "the German of today," and past worlds are others' environments, accessible only through their subjects. The Academy example states the historicist's insight within phenomenology.

(240) Empathy itself is, so to speak, a coincidence of my own ego and ego-life with its appearances and those of the other. The other's appearances are intentional modification, contentual variations, in the same sense as my own possible experience is a variation of my actual experience. I, who now am as I am (to which the capacities of this transposition belong), am not in truth the subject that then was, or will be in anticipated future after millennia in the heavenly distance into which I transpose myself, but the present one who "transposes himself." In my past, transposing back has another sense, the sense of "that was I," and I who now am am at once he who was. In the other cases it is I who have these co-subjects and have given their being in me as co-valid for me, but in the manner of others, and these as subjects of appearances that are co-valid for me in the synthesis of concordance but count as appearances I could have myself only in my finite circle. The decisive difference between recollection and empathy: in recollection I identify with my past ego ("that was I"); in empathy, however coincident, I remain the present transposer and the other remains other. Other-experience is a modification but not an identification.

(240) But through them I gain not only their life and the co-validity of their appearances, hence the world commonly valid and demonstrable as existing for us and finally for all, but I can also, as regarding my limited own life and sphere of experience, so regarding theirs and the world for all, gain a "theoretical," disinterested attitude and, as disinterested observer "intuiting," interested in being, run through the world and gain knowledge of its known actualities and its ranges of possibility. To this belong humans and I myself as human of the worldly present. The disinterested theoretical attitude is possible for the world-for-all, including humans as objects. This legitimizes the theoretical world-view, but as a view including subjects as objects, not as appropriating their experience.

(240) But does a problem not remain? The single question opens the final difficulty.

(240–241) Following the synthesis that connects my own appearances with their primordial horizons with those of the immediate other as simultaneous appearances of the same present objects, my total perceptual field and corresponding total (primordially reduced) horizon with the other's; then following the mediacies, the synthesis of the various primordial pasts and futures, this synthesis yields finally a universal intersubjective system of appearance for the world, just as in my primordiality every thing and the whole primordial world has its system of appearance. This system of appearance does not merely add up with the system experienced in universal empathy; we have an objective world, in it every object as unity of its actual and possible appearances, each with its system of possible appearances, and this is not an external sum of the systems of individual subjects but an intentional unity constituted in its intelligible intersubjective synthesis. Here is the remarkable and problematic thing: the system of appearance of every object, and of each in the universal objective nexus, can apparently and evidently be constructed and described by every ego-subject as if it were only a primordial system extended into infinity. So it seems, even in evidence. We constantly use empathy, and as to nature its own natural-historical manifestation of past and future beyond history, but we gain a unified intuition that connects real possibilities of our own experience with possibilities that are not ours, in consciousness of their validity, yet connects them just as if they were. How can they then be valid for us? As to others' experiences, they are at least possibilities valid for others and co-valid for us in connection. But we connect them with our own not as valid from them but as if the being of others were irrelevant. As when we form a remembering intuition, however imperfect, of the growth and successive stages of the fig tree under which the Buddha taught, which persists through the millennia to the present (as is said), or of Rome as it was from Romulus' times to the present. Of course in such a construction, even hypothetical, we mean that Rome was or could have been seen thus by others at various stages. But our unified intuition does not compose others' intuitions, though formed on the basis of their reports. For us it is the unity of an intuitive change no different from that of a finite process seen by ourselves in our own primordiality. The problem is stated precisely. The intersubjective system of appearance is an intentional unity, not a sum; yet we represent it as a single primordial system extended to infinity, "as if the being of others were irrelevant." The intuition of Rome from Romulus to the present is a single intuition of a single process, though it is built from others' reports. The objective world-view erases the others whose experience it uses. The Buddha's fig tree (the Bodhi tree) and Rome are examples of objects whose history no single subject experienced.

(242) As for the universal intuition of nature based on natural history, it does not care about the impossibility of an intuition of the past reaching behind my birth. Natural history intuits a past before any human subject, the extreme form of the problem.

(242) Is this way of making the world and everything worldly intuitive a naive continuation of the world-view within the frame of the normal human community? But here too it is not clear how the intersubjective synthesis of normal intuitions is reduced to an arbitrarily extended and finally infinite intuition in one's own normal primordiality. The reduction of the intersubjective synthesis to a single extended primordial intuition remains unexplained.

(242) How is the formation of such a "world-view" motivated and how can it maintain itself; how is it to be criticized and how can the limits within which it can be useful be made intelligible? The appendix ends with the critical task: the naive world-view of a single infinite intuition is motivated, useful within limits, and to be criticized. The answer suggested by the analysis is that the world-view is a useful idealization that forgets its intersubjective origin.

### Appendix XV. Presentification of Inaccessible Nature and Empathic Presentification (August or September 1931)

Husserl referred to this text in a separate note placed among his texts on empathy (E I 4): do not forget the important final slips in FF (September 1931), in which the intuitive presentification of the world-distances constituted through natural-historical indication (the inaccessible ones) is put on a par as a mode of presentification with empathic appresentation, which also indicates something inaccessible in principle and appresents it made intuitive; on both sides "analogization" with the accessible, where the analogized is in itself inaccessible. The manuscript FF is today in convolute A VII 17, on "world-view."

(242) Astronomy, geology, palaeontology, universal natural history of the earth's time, phylogenesis. The sciences of the inaccessible past and distance are listed as the theme.

(242) The world inaccessible to man, inaccessible because his organic corporeality is bound to a causal milieu whose typical alteration destroys organic existence. Regions of the world where humans and animals cannot live; on the other hand the stars as bodies belonging to the unity of nature reaching into infinity, on which, under favourable environmental conditions, organic beings, animals, humans could live as dwelling places. Inaccessibility is grounded in the bodily dependence of organisms on a milieu. The stars are possible dwelling places, which keeps them within the sense "nature for possible subjects."

(242–243) How are things, which gain their original sense from the experience constituting the near world, "sensuous" experience, experienceable, demonstrable, if it belongs to the sense of being of organic beings that they are environmentally bound, so that every "sensuous" representation of the distant regions of nature is impossible? Do we not nevertheless operate with representations of nearness for these inaccessibles, represent the heavenly bodies like bodies near us, cancel perspectivization, make "models" in physics where there can be no question of visibility? Do we not think the liquid state of the earth at enormous temperatures as we think melting metal from real experience? Solid can become liquid, can melt (Husserl's note: not all sensuous layers are bodily realizable; I cannot touch it), so also the solid earth. I think the melting in the indication as if I were there and saw it; likewise the glaciation of the Ice Age or the ichthyosaurs, as if I swam in the sea in whose temperature I could not live and saw these monsters, and likewise the sea in its enormous depths. I see with the eyes of the indicated animals, re-understanding, one could say. The inaccessible is represented by analogy with the near: melting metal for the molten earth, swimming for the ichthyosaur sea. The phrase "I see with the eyes of the indicated animals" reveals the structure: representing the inaccessible past implies an implicit empathy with a possible observer.

(243) What kind of making-intuitive is this, seeing, experiencing-as-if, in which the as-if-experienced would be constituted, as if? I am, I constitute in constant apperception, constant analogization, by letting the old continue to be valid and apprehending the new by analogy with the old. I speak of shape, size, as if it were a matter of distant points or distant perspectives that I or we could turn into near perspectives, into an optimum. What does this "as if" mean? How is the extended constitution to be understood as constitution, as identifying sense-formation in which every level is "appearance" of the same? The "as if" of scientific representation is grounded in the general structure of apperception as analogization. The question is whether it is constitution proper, the formation of an identical through appearances.

(243) What kind of "indications," analogizations are these? They are indications of melting. So I infer from the known to unknown similar things, from what is intuitively constituted and to be constituted to the similar? As with empathic presentifications. But is not an ego co-presentified there, which would then have to be bodily present? But precisely that is to be excluded. By what, we must ask. A human fireplace: presentifying-indicating I understand it, believe it. But how can I? As if I were there. I have the others first through this as-if-being-there, but I am not there, I am here. The archaeological fireplace makes the structure explicit: understanding it requires "as if I were there," the same structure as empathy, yet I am not there. Presentifying inaccessible nature co-presentifies an observer that must at the same time be excluded, a paradox the following paragraphs clarify.

(243–244) Does this as-if not already play its role in the analogization of my body here with his body there, through which I first get the inner body for the other? I cannot be here and there at once. I can think in advance that I went there and then would be there. I can think a near thing moved there; I cannot really bring the near thing to the zero point, I cannot push my body away, but I can "think" it brought to the zero point and "think" my body pushed there, hence apprehend it as moved in space there, in coincidence with my inner movement. I cannot experience that; it is a peculiar kind of presentification that is a modification of a perception, and again a peculiar kind in the other cases. The "as if I were there" of empathy is a presentification of my body displaced, which I cannot experience but can "think" as a modification of perception. The same modification underlies the representation of inaccessible nature: both are presentifications of perceptions I cannot have.

(244) The fireplace. The presentifying is not presentifying of an actual perception but of a perceptual modification: it appresents (ad-temporalizes) something; it first appresents the empathic appresentation of others' life and in it the appresentation of a world perceived, experienced by it. This too is presentified, and the presentified is as if I perceived it or had perceived it before, and yet it is not perceivable for me. We have here, in a new mediacy, appresented perceptions-as-if, appresented through a motivation anchored in a presentation and a pairing through the presentification. The fireplace appresents ("ad-temporalizes," a coinage) the past life of its makers and their world: an appresentation of an empathic appresentation. The structure is a mediated pairing: present trace, empathy with past subjects, their perceived world. Archaeology is empathy at a remove.

(244) Apperception of what I presently experience with a sense stemming from earlier experience. That holds for every apperception. Original apperception, with the original capacity; on the other hand past, causal result and future. Apperception as tool. But primarily the issuing of this from my action and use for my purpose. This is not so with empathy. How do I remain with an apperception while changing its sense? "Indication of another." Others are given first directly through empathy, and in it their perceptions, their extended actual and possible field. Thereby I have presentifications of a modified kind, the empathies. But in them the horizons of ability, explicated in presentifications of what the other can do. Apperception generally transfers sense from earlier experience; the tool is apperceived from my own use. Empathy differs: it transfers sense while changing it into "another's." The other's horizons are horizons of his abilities, presentified by me. The appendix thus ends on the specific difference of empathic apperception, which No. 15 investigates through the analysis of the body.

### No. 15. On the Theory of Empathy, Also on the Basis of Precise Analyses of the Lived Body. Imagining Oneself Into, Pairing Association, Modification of Memory (August 1931)

(245) To clarify the constitution of the lived body some things must still be considered. I cannot see my body from all sides, fully, but through self-touching it is constituted and appresents a visual shape, also that of the head and eyes, and so the whole body. The text begins from the anomaly of my body as visual object: never fully seen, its visual shape is appresented through touch. This appresentation of my own visible body will turn out to be a condition of empathy (No. 16).

(245) I can imagine myself turned around my long axis so that I see my back as I see my belly. I can also imagine how my back looks while I sit here, as I can imagine how the bookcase behind me looks or the seat on which I sit. I can stand up and look at it; I then see it as it is after standing up. But it is unchanged; it then is as it is now, unseen and yet now already looking so visually: an unseen look. The "unseen look" (ungesehenes Aussehen) is the general structure of appresented sides: the thing already looks a certain way where no one sees it. Husserl asks whether my own back has such an unseen look.

(245) My back looks, unseen, as if I could bend my head back (as if I had a giraffe neck, but one quite arbitrarily conceivable) so that I could look at my back at will. My head, as if I had independently functioning eyes and eye-kinaestheses (eyes on eye-arms), so that I could look at myself everywhere and the eyes could look at each other as I touch myself, as one touching member touches another. The imaginative fictions (giraffe neck, eyes on stalks) show that the appresentation of my back's look is constructed on an impossible extension of my kinaesthetic capacities. Visual self-appresentation lacks the redeemable "I can" that grounds the appresentation of the back of a thing.

(245–246) But must we not consider generally how bodies have their sense-thing layers, and have them whether the senses concerned are in play or not? A thing given in touch is, unseen, what has its look. In touching I have the changing mode of givenness, but I experience it as unchanged, and it has in advance what will later show itself as belonging to it tactually. It is "appresented," and the presented is constantly one, streamingly, with the appresented. This is constitution through potentialities of perception under possible kinaestheses. The optical is anticipated: an object I have in touch, or grasp in the dark, unseen, awakens the possibility of seeing and spreads in front of it an image from the optical sphere under circumstances of possible seeing; a stretch of optical seeing comes alive as a leap ahead into possibility. Cross-modal appresentation: the touched thing in the dark already has a look, anticipated as possible seeing. This is the general structure; the problem is that for my body the possible seeing is not available.

(246–247) But how is it with representing my own body? Here I have developed in it a limited capacity as constituting. I scratch my back and "see" my back visually appresented, though I could not see it. Why does my body not have a limited visual layer, or what gives this anticipated mode of appearance its functional sense, since it cannot be anticipated actual visibility from my experiencing seeing capacity? I have my bodily body represented like any other thing, and this representation is always an experiencing one, bearing validity. But if it is validity from other sense-sources, must I not find it there? I know how things stand with seeing and possible touching of my back, and that this need not wait for going there, that indications constantly issue from the constantly seen and touched front side, so that it only awaits the kinaestheses of turning the touching organs. Is it then so that the intuition of my back, once intersubjectivity is put out of play, is eo ipso to be excluded abstractively as stemming from others? But is not precisely the difficulty for empathy to understand how appresentation can become possible where the corresponding capacity of presentation is lacking for me? The problem is sharp. My visual image of my back cannot be grounded in my capacity to see it. Two hypotheses: it stems from others (I see others' backs), so it should be excluded in the primordial reduction; or there is a primordial appresentation without corresponding presentation, which would then be the model of empathy itself. The closing question shows that the analysis of my own body and the theory of empathy face the same difficulty.

(247) In the manner of "external thing" we have a constitution with its levels. The paragraph announces a four-step genealogy of the external thing, to be compared with the constitution of the body.

(247) First, constitution of rest, rest of the first level; nothing of reality is involved yet, and rest is constitutively related to my holding still my walking-kinaestheses, in this sense my standing still. Rest as rest of the whole perceptual field as resting "thing"-field. Various senses function for rest; touch can "reach further" than sight and conversely. Rest is first constituted relative to my kinaesthetic standstill, the zero of motion.

(247) Second, constitution of rest while I change my standpoint (walking). Rest is maintained through my own displacement: things stay in place while I move.

(247) Third, change of a rest: something at rest changes its mode of appearance while I hold still or not; constitution of motion, into which rest passes, as continuity of possible rests. So in my standstill and in my change of standpoint. Constitution of a moving object in a double sense, with division, deformation and without. Relation to division, constitution of extensive magnitude, of figures as limits, deformation as motion of limits. Division here means the standing out of continuously connected parts in a continuous whole and the arising of new such parts through qualitative change, finally the moving apart of the former parts into a then resting configuration which may itself move and change as configuration. Motion is a continuity of possible rests; extensive magnitude and figure are constituted with division and deformation. This is the program of Ding und Raum (1907), §§ 44–83, compressed.

(247) Fourth, causality of changes and then of changes of qualification. Constitution of the real and its real-causal predicates. Reality is causality: the thing becomes real as a substrate of causal dependencies, Ideas II § 15.

(247) Now the constitution of the lived body. The parallel genealogy for the body follows.

(247) As to the first point: first "rest," what is constituted in my standstill. While I hold all kinaestheses still, I have an unchanged phenomenon of my body as of external things. If I only move my hand in touching function, I have of the rest of the body the same as of a thing. If I only moved my eyes, I would again have, as with an external thing, a phenomenon of rest of what I see. Seeing and touching together: I touch what I cannot reach seeing. But this happens equally with external things as unchanged and especially as at rest. At the first level the body appears like a thing at rest; the parallel holds as long as only a part functions.

(248) But I also have seen "things" "at rest" that I do not touch. A "thing" unreachable by touch looks visually like a touchable near thing. With the latter the ability to touch belongs, and according to "distance" other kinaestheses as motivated. If the distance is greater, this capacity is lacking. Seeing, I have a constitution of its own as "rest" of the "visual thing." But various visual things differ (abstracting from light and shade, illumination) by "distance," the whole visual field of objects a relief of distance. The visual is constituted kinaesthetically, but what remains visually unchanged in this domain of "rest." Husserl's note: evidently typology also plays a role, the familiar way a house near us is perspectivized. The visual field is a "relief of distance"; visual things at a distance appresent tactility by analogy with near things. Typicality (how a near house looks in perspective) mediates.

(248) So the fixed system of places in which I move from place to place I already have through walking. The real being of my body as a body of natural causality through being moved. Two achievements are distinguished: the fixed system of places is constituted by walking; the body's reality as a physical body in causal nature is constituted by being moved (passively).

(248) The achievement of being moved comes about originally by "becoming one" with an object I already experience as moved (at the lower level). What is this becoming one and then passive being-one? A kind of being-together (which I can produce) in which what is "connected" to me keeps its orientation, so seemingly rests and undergoes only changes at its place, perhaps rotations. This change, producible at will in an original sphere, transforms experienced motion into "rest," which at once becomes motion when the "connection" is dissolved from my side. But on a cart I can still move myself, and this motion within motion creates a modification of higher level to be considered too. Being moved is constituted by riding on a moving thing: the cart keeps its orientation to me and seems to rest while the world moves; when I step off, the cart moves. This experience gives my body the sense of a body that can be moved like any thing, the first step toward its apprehension as a physical body in space. The cart example anticipates the 1934 Earth-ark text, where the earth is the "cart" that does not move.

#### On Empathy

(248–249) Does the experience of being moved, and of transitions from being moved to rest or walking myself, also make possible the strange way of representing: my body, if it were there, and the representation of my movement there as perspectival movement? And what of understanding a mirror image? Does that belong here? Or is all this already a consequence of empathy? The representation "my body there," a body seen in perspective at a distance, may be grounded in being moved; or it may presuppose empathy. The mirror image is the test case, analysed in Beilage LV.

(249) How can the similarity of my body with the other's, or conversely, possibly come about so as to become effective? That is the basic question. It must be subjective similarity; it must be association. Two things, two houses, one from one side, the other from the other side, which (let us assume) looks quite different from the front. If I do not know the house from all sides, I do not recognize that it is the same. The immediate effectiveness of association as pairing is only through what is actually "seen," and the variations by lateral shift must be such that partial coincidence occurs together with living awakening of the indicated side-pieces. The basic question of the theory of empathy is how similarity between my body and his becomes effective, given that I see them so differently. Pairing works only on what is actually seen; the problem is that my body as seen by me does not look like his body as seen by me.

(249) Apperceptive transfer: when does it occur, since what I know of the one I need not subsequently find in the other? Is it not habitual superimposition, which holds similarity and commonality while the different cancels itself? And when I already apperceive everything, it is also a habitual anticipation that a common element will remain and differences drop out. Transfer is habitual and anticipatory, preserving the common, discarding the different.

(249–250) If an "other human" is to be seen: how can his seen body and my seen body yield a pairing? My hand and his, my feet and his look alike with suitable position of the other in near space. Is that enough? I can touch the other, "flesh"-like corporeality, I at rest, he there too. Do I need more? I then have the other body at this or that distance with all its changed look, its perspectival sides, and it remains my like, bodily. Is it not right to say that the external representation "my body at some place in space" as perspectival representation first comes about by way of the other bodies recognized as like my body? This representation always gave me difficulty. Is the solution here? A promising answer: pairing starts from parts seen in both (hands, feet) and from touch; once the other body is "my like" bodily, my own body can be represented as at any place, through the others. The representation of my body as an external thing in space would then be a consequence of empathy, the reverse of the initial assumption. Husserl's admission that "this representation always gave me difficulty" points to the circle No. 16 confronts.

(250) My body is constituted not only as a physical body but as my organ. It does not induce as an experienced, perceived, self-given other thing induces something self-givable, perceivable for me; and yet, as if I were there: the body there a body similar as if my body were there. The body there "induces" not what a thing induces (a further perceivable) but "my body as if there."

(250) And as I represent myself there as ruling in a body similar to the one there, and as if I were there, similar bodily movements happen there in the real body now really there, as if I, ruling there, did this and that (had done, if I were bodily there instead of the other body, had gone there before), moving eyes and hand perceptually, busying myself in acting with the things there, presenting themselves so from there, in the self-evident familiar way. The "as if I were there" is coordinated with the other body's actual movements: his movements follow the course my movements would take if I ruled there. This coordination is the confirmation of empathy, the concordant behaviour of the Fifth Meditation.

(250–251) Do I imagine myself into the other body? Can I at all? Do I perform an imagining as-if-I-had-the-second-body-there-as-my-lived-body, can I imagine my body transformed into this other, as if I had it as lived body, this in fact other body? Would that not mean I leave my body and slide over into the second one there? But can my body move off, and another body be substituted for me, or be cut off, amputated, and some other given me instead? Am I not subject of the totality of things existing for me, first primordially, and as such inseparably one with my identical corporeality, as bodily perceiving everything that is and can be for me? Does not the identity of that other body before and after the amputation presuppose my body? There is much talk of thinking oneself into the other, which can also be called imagining oneself into. What can be the good sense of this talk, which cannot mean transformation, but an "imagining," a thinking oneself, in which I am who I am and yet at once, on the basis of the appearance of the other body, become conscious of the as-if-I-were-there, and of the similarity of the body there with the appearance I would have to have from here if I were there in the same corporeality I now have? How do I come from there in our case to a real possibility of another human, another body, another ego? Really possible is that I go where the other is (or am moved exactly to his place) when he, when his bodily body, leaves its place; so I can put myself in the place of every thing, think it moved away and myself moved there, and think my body brought to coincidence with the one there in a certain way, not as possibility of being there now at the same time, but similarly to bringing like and like, similar and similar to superimposition in intuitive comparative transition, as also in identification. Kern prints in a note Husserl's first, immediately corrected version: it said that from mere phantasy no path leads to actuality; that one can imagine a thing changing colour, as a real possibility in its sense; that the real possibility here is that I go where the other is when he leaves; that a complete equality of two organisms is perhaps excluded by the a priori of nature, though from here two things can look completely alike; and it ended: "What then is empathy, since it is none of this and of course cannot be a hypothesis that I could now be there in the sense: now there and at the same time, as I really am, here? I say: the apperception essentially peculiar to all association (original, positional, not running in the as-if of phantasy) — transfer of the sense of being; so as to position it is really ad-position, or better, transposition." Husserl rejects Einfühlung as "imagining oneself into" the other body: I cannot leave my body, which is the condition of all my perceiving. The amputation thought-experiment shows that my body's identity is presupposed by the identity of any other body. What remains is the "as if I were there," a superimposition (Überschiebung) of my possible body-there onto his actual body, as in comparison. The first draft's term "transposition" (Transposition) names the result precisely.

(251) What then is new in empathy? I say: the apperception essentially peculiar to all association (original, positional, not running in the as-if of pure phantasy, for which everything valid for original association modifies itself accordingly), transfer of the sense of being, so real ad-perception, since in perception lies the sense of validity of being. Empathy is not phantasy but "ad-perception," a positional transfer of sense in the mode of association. The neologism "Adperzeption" (or "Adposition") marks a transfer that adds a perceived validity.

(252–253) The pairing of association is mutual "superimposition," and if each of those pairing has in its way presumptive validities with associated capacities, this transfers mutually from one to the other, passively, at once, at a stroke. But that does not mean that the validity taken over must or can maintain itself; it is still validity with presumptive horizon, certainty at the moment of transfer, but presumptive as to the horizon. On this rests all "hasty" apperception by analogy that proves false (which is really a tautology). The course of experience shows that validity, as it has become fully from transfer, as simple apperception, overshoots its "rightful" limits. If one says right extends as far as "real analogy," the answer is: experience first shows what "real analogy" may mean here, the fulfilment and disappointment of anticipation. What I really "know" from experience of one member, what stands as confirmed validity in my apperception of it, may transfer. But in the other case I have only the similar, and on closer acquaintance only part of the presumption may pass over confirmingly (hold) into the analogue. Conversely the back-transfer to the other analogue may lead to new moments of holding analogy despite the emerged differences. Pairing transfers everything at once, overshooting; experience corrects. "Real analogy" is not prior to experience but determined by it. The "back-transfer" is important for empathy: what I learn of the other can modify my self-apprehension.

(253) In our case of empathy I am constituted for myself, familiarly and from my life-experience, with the sense of the ego ruling in this unique bodily body with everything belonging to it. It is a constituted validity with an open horizon; the sense-forming experience constituting me as this psychophysical primal unity goes on. My self-apprehension as psychophysical unity is itself a constituted, horizonal validity.

(253) On the other hand, the bodily body there is constituted for me exclusively with the sense of external body in its open universe of external bodies, taken as it enters into pairing. If it occurs, without further ado, the moment I experience that body, in a mode of appearance that makes original pairing possible (Husserl's note: in general, only with sufficient approach can what is already apperceived as external body enter into assimilation), corporeality and everything belonging to it transfers from it to mine by mere apperception, in the mere variation of similarity, within the frame of the type body in general. This transfer is confirmed without further ado, since my bodily body is really and constantly constituted for me as a body. That is the first decisive point. On the other side, from me the specific lived-bodiliness and the "I rule" transfer to this body, analogized. My ego and ruling do not transfer in the literal sense. Pairing works in two directions. From the other body to mine transfers corporeality, the sense "physical body," which is confirmed because my body is a body. From my body to his transfers lived-bodiliness and "I rule," analogized, not literally. The first direction is the "first decisive point": my body's apprehension as a physical body among bodies is, at least partly, a result of pairing with the other.

(253–255) Here the second decisive point is reached. I am constituted for myself from experience in a horizon, a style of experience prefigured by past experience, and as ego of this style spanning past, future and living present I have my sense as original. The "similarity transfer" of original life, of ruling originally in the body, living and experiencing into the world I actually experience, acting, is at once cancelled. (Husserl's note: I am unique, and in my primordial sphere no external thing can show an ego-analogue, a ruling; that would mean showing it as perceptually experienceable.) How then does it continue to work, how does the impossible presentation become possible and valid appresentation? Every apperceptive presumption I can make intuitive. For external things and those of every individual type it is presumption to be fulfilled or disappointed by original experience. But the apperceptive presumption of empathy cannot in principle be confirmed by original experience. The primordial sphere is the totality of what is originally experienced and experienceable for me; it contains every actual and conceivable originally experienceable ruling, every conceivable originally experienceable, say "originally presumable," act. And everything originally demonstrable for me has me as the one absolutely unique ego-pole. If in this sphere I am primordially constituted as ego of my body, as psychophysical being, this happens in absolute uniqueness, which makes no plurality representable at all. The uniqueness of this ego as ruling in the body, "the" (unique) body, is not a fact leaving other possibilities open. A second ego in my primordiality would mean a second life of which I am primordially aware, a second primordial ruling as psychophysical, whether in my same body or another. However much I can say I have suddenly become quite another, have suffered a break in my personality, or however much a personality can split, all these splittings take place as those of the identical ego-pole persisting through them. The ego ruling in this primordial body is the same that lives in the living primordially streaming life. So in this sphere no second body as lived body, hence no second ruling ego, is presumable by any conceivable primordial apperception with its confirmabilities, whether by actual perception or any primordial presentification such as recollection. The second decisive point is the uniqueness of the primordial ego, which is not a fact but an essential necessity: no second ruling can be presented in my primordiality, not even in cases of split personality, which are splits of one ego-pole. Hence the transfer of "I rule" to the other body is cancelled as presentation. Husserl's appeal to dissociative splitting is precise: multiplicity of personalities within one stream is still multiplicity within one pole, so it offers no model for another ego.

(255) How can there nevertheless arise in the primordial sphere an act, an apperceptive presumption which, as presumption, is presentifying and to be made intuitive, yet not originally confirmed in possible confirmation, yet able to be confirmed? The paradox is stated: a presumption confirmable but not by original experience.

(255) If, so to speak, the assimilation of my body to external-thing being (which is yet essentially differently experienced and experienceable) is already pre-given, so that my bodily body is "to be regarded" for me as a thing-body like any other, and so the individual-typical similarity-pairing of my body with some body has come about, we must first consider what kind of individual-typical pairing this is. My body is constituted for me as a many-membered unity; the members are my bodily organs, constantly set off in a special way by my constant ruling in them, now singly, now together, also as regards pure corporeality. So always a unity articulated thus, most familiar in this articulation and always so apperceived, multiplicity in unity, and so apperceived in the abstractive direction of regard to mere corporeality. The individual-typical similarity with an external body therefore means that it is apperceived as such a many-membered unity, not just any many-membered unity like a tree in its branches; my body is individual-typical for me in its hands, fingers, arms, legs, and we presuppose similarity, individual-typical similarity of the whole and of the set-off parts, for the second body that is to be apperceived as lived body. Pairing requires a specific typology: articulation into organs of the same type as mine. Organhood articulates even my body's pure corporeality, and the other body must match it, not merely be many-membered like a tree.

(255) Further belongs the individual typology of movements, of changes of my own body and its members singly and in groups, as they typically belong to the modes of egoic psychophysical ruling. With me this typology belongs to my ruling, unseparated. Ruling is only ruling as familiar unity of belonging together, from a unity of constitution that is actual in every living waking present as original experience, as immanently original streaming appearing of the body with its members in such and such psychophysical situation, in such and such originally proceeding egoic ruling, and together, in constituted or apperceived (perceptual) belonging, such and such modes of bodily configuration with such movements, deformations in their typology, so that everything runs perceptually as typically familiar. Husserl's note: what kind of setting off or layering does the body have in itself, as spatial body and as egoic bodiliness? From constitution, the body is constituted in a special layer. The typology of movement belongs to ruling as its perceptual expression: I perceive my own movements as typical. The note raises the question whether body-as-spatial-thing and body-as-egoic form distinct layers.

(256) Such a typology, modified relative to the one I now live perceptually in my bodiliness, but one of the modifications belonging familiarly to my unique body (so horizonally co-apperceived from the outset in my self-body-apperception), I find in the body there: an analogue, a living (perceptual) analogue of my own, perceptual analogue of my physical corporeality. The other's movements are modifications of my own possible movements, already contained in the horizon of my body's typology. The other body shows what I could look like.

(256) Now "association" occurs, which is nothing but this similarity being alive, pairing, superimposition and apperception. But my body is perceptual body only in one layer; concretely it is lived body, body of my ruling ego, which is ego of my universal primordial sphere. The apperception cannot here be indication of something existing outside it (in primordial external thinghood, among which the other body is), nor run as indication or induction from it to something to be expected beside it, nor in temporality to an earlier or later primordial thing usually connected with it in primordial "worldliness." And yet the typical shape and "comportment" of this body there "reminds" of my ruling in my distinguished body, by which it is unique in originality. The association cannot be ordinary indication (something else in space or time). It is a "reminding" of my unique ruling.

(256) It reminds, and yet not in the original sense of my memories (recollections, co-memories); it is an analogue, a modification of memory, and then has, as "memory," its horizons of memory, its anticipations, its confirmation and disconfirmation. Empathy is a modification of memory: it "reminds" of me without being my memory, and has the structure of memory (horizons, confirmation). This is the "memory-modification" of the title.

(256–257) The comportment of the body there, its bending with the hand stretching out, "reminds" of me as if I were there, would bend kinaesthetically, stretch my hand to grasp the thing there, the stick. The further movement for me would be to straighten up again; that transfers apperceptively, and the body there indeed makes a movement of that type. In this mobile, progressing presentification, with constant fulfilment of the presentifications constantly prefigured, there is constituted a unity of manifoldly interwoven and mutually founded presentifications as presentifications of a ruling and thus of a ruling ego, in whose life that in which it rules as its body is constituted spatio-temporally, but which is necessarily only presentified and is not I myself with my life, I who have this presentification and bring about in my life the constitution of this ego as existing for me. The confirmation of empathy is prediction of movement: the body there does what I would do. The presentified ego constitutes its body spatio-temporally in its life, and is not I.

(257) The body there, experienced originally by me, in appresenting a ruling ego in it, does not appresent it really as a psychic annex to this primordial thing, but as an ego that constitutes this same external body in itself as inner body. The other is not a psyche attached to a thing in my world, but an ego that constitutes that same body as his "inner body" (Innenleib). The heading that follows marks a further attempt.

#### Renewed Consideration (Another Time)

(257–258) From myself I now have constitutively the others existing for me. A body of my primordiality, constituted there first only as external body, assimilates associatively with my body as to its bodily corporeality and now appresents in its own way. A ruling in this external body cannot be merely appresented; originally constituted in my primordiality, ruling can be present only as my ruling in original perception (or, the same, presentation). The body there resembles my body as if I were there. By its optical mode of givenness, or already as haptic near thing, it presentifies the self-appearance I would have if I went there and were in this thing's place, and so the being-outside-me of a body in whose place I could be there, a similarly articulated body behaving bodily in a similar typology. Like every external body, it presupposes that, as long and wherever it is, it is outside me, outside my body, and that my body must be able to function perceptually so as to bring this external body to optical self-experience, my ruling body and this thing being in the togetherness and bodily mutual externality of touch. Thereby, I tried to say, the apperceptive transfer is cancelled in its validity (Husserl's note: but that is wrong, for the assimilation does not mean that I am now there). The thing there "reminds" only of my possible being there and ruling there; my being as bodily ego ruling in the body coincides in this "memory," this representation of possible being-there (if I went there, had gone there), with the body there and its bodily comportment, so that the sense-layer of ruling is co-reminded and so to speak laid upon this body. But that naturally yields no validity, at most cancelled validity. How then does perception of others come about? The renewed attempt ends in aporia. The assimilation "as if I were there" yields only cancelled validity, since the body there is outside my body and I cannot be there now. Husserl's note corrects the argument's premise: the assimilation does not mean "I am there now," so its cancellation by my being here is not decisive. The final question, "how then does perception of others come about?", is left for No. 16, which attacks the problem from the apperception of my own body as a spatial body.

### No. 16. The Apperception of the Spatial Corporeality of My Lived Body as a Presupposition for Empathy (Probably August or September 1931)

Kern notes that Alfred Schütz had already published this text in 1946 in Philosophy and Phenomenological Research (vol. VI, no. 3).

(259) Apperception: apprehension of something similar according to the similar already existing for me. The new I experience, have in validity without further ado, with the experiential sense stemming from similarity with the similar already existing for me as earlier acquisition. The sense of apprehension is a validity of being with an anticipated horizon of possible confirmation. Apperception refers to an earlier primal institution, but it is also a constant process in coexistence, in the sphere of the living present. If in original association I have a plurality of equals (sensuously similar) in my present field, every new sense-formation, every new apperception of one of the equals, transfers passively-associatively, while the equality persists, to all others: according to similarity, analogically. Apperception has two dimensions: diachronic, referring to a primal institution, and synchronic, the constant transfer of sense among similar co-present objects. The synchronic dimension is what pairing in empathy exploits.

(259) Objects of experience and pluralities (equalities, similarities) are normally already very complicated formations of apperceptions, containing, as intentional modification, anticipations of sense-building apperceptions that are not actually performed but implied. For example the equality of two things, one given as near thing, the other as far thing; apperception continues to work through intentional modification and assimilation; motivation by similarity itself produces intentional modification. Even perceived equality is mediated: a near and a far thing are "equal" only through apperceptive modification of the far thing's appearance into a near one. Similarity motivates by producing such modifications, the mechanism that empathy will require between my body here and his body there.

(260) Association or apperception often constitutes being and type in levels, such that the apperception of the similar according to the similar remains in force in a certain way when the typical similarity is broken, if one member has, beyond the preserved typical similarity, a layer of sense the others lack, and the others all have an apperceptive community among themselves that the exceptional member lacks. A formal model of the body's exceptional status: within a class of similar things, one member (my body) carries a sense-layer the others lack, while they share a community it lacks. Apperception remains in force across this break.

(260) But that is probably too formal. I am concerned with the formation of apperceptions by intentional modifications of already formed apperceptions, the formation of perceptions, presentifications of various levels, or such as are, in their higher sense-formation, peculiar intentional modifications of lower, more original experiences, but always experiences, constituting being in their higher way. Empathy will be such a modification of a lower experience, the experience of my own body. The heading that follows names the theme.

#### Empathy as Experience of Others, Perception of Another Human Being

(260) The body there experienced as lived body according to the apperception of my body is apperception of the similar through the previously experienced similar, according to it. But it is not as in the already constituted field of things outside me, where what I learn of one I transfer apperceptively to another similar one. My body has no equal yet and gains one only through the new apperception of the body, which presupposes the apperception of external things but transforms it into a new one. In other words, the constitution of my body as a bodily thing like another is presupposed for the constitution of others existing for me. That first makes possible the experience of things equal to my body and then that such a thing equal to my body-thing be a lived body, but another's, experienceable as such for me. That seems simple. The thesis of the title is stated: empathy presupposes that my body is apperceived as a physical thing like others. Only then can there be a thing "like my body." The problem is that this apperception of my body is itself difficult to constitute, as No. 15 found.

#### Constitution of the Primordial World and First of the "Extra-Bodily" External World

(260–261) First comes the constitution of the extra-bodily primordial world as spatio-temporal world, my body with its kinaestheses being the functioning body. In the change of kinaesthetically motivated modes of appearance every external thing is constituted as the same, and every togetherness configuratively as the same, persisting in rest and motion, qualitative unchange and change. The extra-bodily world is constituted first, with the body functioning, not appearing.

(261–262) Presupposition here, first in itself, is the constitution of motion (with deformation) and rest, within the spatial field as invariant system of possible positions. This constitution in two levels. First, the kinaestheses of walking are still out of play; we abstract from "I walk" and from its sense "I am moved in space," and from what belongs with it, "I am moved 'mechanically' in space without walking." Only my other kinaestheses are in play: arm, finger, eye movements, foot movements that are not walking, jumping. Second, only after considering the achievement of this sphere does walking come into question. "I rest" precedes "I move" constitutively in that the latter must first gain its meaning as a continuum of possible points of rest. Here we consider, in the system of "I move," taken purely in its subjective kinaesthetic sense, the special rest of standing, sitting, "not moving forward," as it is called in an already objective apprehension that is here first to be built up. All other kinaestheses are in play, in free course as "rest" and "motion." The appearing external world of things is constituted in their motion and rest, but my body too, through its bodily self-relation, is constituted as mobile in these or those organs: the hand stretching out is spatially in motion together with "I stretch out," the eyes rolling spatially in their sockets together with "I move my eyes." Kinaestheses and spatial movements are unified by association, with combination of movements in a double sense: at once the head moves and the eye in the head, the hand, upper arm, fingers. Abstracting from kinaestheses, the organs and their unity, the body, are a body like others, constituted as body in relation to modes of appearance motivated by the functioning kinaestheses, which as functioning do not belong to spatial things but to their subjective modes of givenness. So too for the bodily, thingly body. The body is constituted through functioning kinaestheses with their modes of appearance, which, by a change of attitude, are experienced as kinaesthetic courses localized in hands, eyes, members, and as parallels of the outer spatial movements of the members. At the first level (no walking), the organs are constituted as moving spatially, in parallel with the kinaestheses that move them. So the body's members already appear as bodies in motion; but the whole body, without walking, does not. The doubling of kinaesthesis, as functioning (subjective) and as localized in organs (paralleling spatial movement), is the key to the body's half-thingly character.

(262) In this constitutive context we have things given as near and far things, motions and changes given in different modes of appearance related to the different special kinaesthetic systems. For each such system we have a system of appearances of rest and motion, change and unchange. But all this varies when other kinaesthetic systems co-function. In their totality they constitute, in a way to be clarified, a "world" of persisting things, though with very limited content. Each kinaesthetic subsystem (eyes, hand, head) constitutes its own system of appearances; their totality constitutes a first "world."

(262) Here I have a core sphere of fully originally constituted things, so to speak a core world, the sphere of things I can reach by my kinaestheses, can experience in optimal form, touching, seeing, so that I have available systems of appearances I could convert into optimal modes. Here I would also have a possible practice, a core sphere of possible practice by my pushing, shoving body. The "core world" (Kernwelt) is the sphere within reach: optimally experienceable and practically available. It is the first, near world.

(262–263) But I have also apperceived things outside this sphere. What comes into question here?
