"Things" recede in perspectivization to the outermost edge of the horizon, and approach from there in perspectivization, perhaps entering the core field. They behave in perspectivization like things. Very "distant" things in their unusualness relative to the familiar and first-familiar near sphere (core sphere) are like near things of reduced format, and yet not quite, since they do not perspectivize noticeably when the kinaestheses are set in play, and only when they move are finally lost in the horizon: they can then be seen like picture-book pictures. That does not arise if what is experienced plays out in "closed space" (a room), where the "unusual distance" with the circle of the horizon is lacking. Not considered are the phenomena not constituted through perspectives in the interplay of all kinaestheses: constellations, clouds, rainbow, sun, moon, northern lights. Far things are apperceived as things by analogy with near ones, though they do not perspectivize with my movements; they look like "picture-book pictures." Celestial phenomena are set aside because they never perspectivize at all: they are not yet things.

(263) But walking already plays a part from the outset, already in closed space, in which normally everything becomes accessible and so everything is constituted in the same way as real mutual externality in space and time. But closed space is closed by things and has its exits into open space. And now the apperceptive extension of the near sphere (the more original core sphere) into a homogeneous endlessly open spatial world takes place. Walking makes everything in a room accessible; the exits lead to open space, and the near sphere is extended apperceptively into homogeneous infinite space.

(263) First in walking, interplaying with the other kinaestheses and the things of a "core world" already constituted by them, distant appearances are modified perspectivally into near appearances and are therefore apprehended in advance in the capacity of being shown as things, which includes dealing with them "themselves," directly and actively. Walking converts far appearances into near ones; hence far things are apprehended in advance as accessible things.

(263) So for all extra-bodily things. The body has from the outset a constitutive exceptional position, already when I consider constitution in standing still. The body (like the wall of closed space) is not experienced as possibly and capably now resting, now moving as a whole body, but only its members, and those only in a limited way. I can experience my hand moved and in the perspectival way like a similar thing-motion, and yet it cannot be moved and be moving at will like any other thing in all directions of orientation, arbitrarily far; I cannot throw my hand away so that it flies off. As for the whole body, its mode of appearance, despite all movements of its members, resembles rest, as a tree rests as a whole even when its branches move. And yet every thingly rest is experienced in its sense as rest only with the possibility of the change of appearance in which thingly motion is constituted. But that is not the case for the body. Apart from its being unique as my body, experienced as my total organ, articulated into organs in which I am the functioning ego, so that in this functioning all thing-perception takes place and the perception of the body itself, through itself, the body is also uniquely constituted in that it is so to speak half experienced as a thing, and yet thingly motion, as originally constituted in perspectivizing phenomena, is not experienceable for it. The body is "half and half" a thing: its members move like things, but within limits (I cannot throw my hand away), and the whole body appears always at rest, yet a rest without the possibility of motion, which is no thingly rest. Husserl's comparison with the wall of a room is exact: like the wall, the body is a fixed frame, not a movable thing. This is the deepest form of the difficulty of No. 15.

(264) This does not change when we add walking. This kinaesthesis first produces something new in that the first "world," constituted in constant standing still (as stillness of this kinaesthesis), is a world fixedly oriented around my bodily body or a zero-point constituted in it; when walking begins, it remains that everything worldly there for me appears oriented around my phenomenally still, "resting" body, here and there, right and left, with a fixed zero of orientation persisting as absolute here. But now that is "appearance," such that everything that shows itself as resting object when walking is interrupted or reversed (going back) and proves resting in standing still now gives itself in a change of orientation and then in an apparent motion, while motion in standstill gives itself either as rest or phenomenally as modified motion. Walking acquires the meaning of a modification of all coexistent subjective appearances, by which they first receive the intentionality of appearance of things, as constituting themselves as identical things in the oriented things and the change of orientation. Walking does not move the body phenomenally: the absolute here persists. Instead, the world's orientation changes, and the changes are apprehended as appearances of resting things. Walking is the kinaesthesis that turns all perspectival changes into appearances of identical things.

(264–265) As every kinaesthesis functions as sense-giving by having its modes of holding still and keeping in motion ("I move"), and kinaesthetic moving has become constitutively a continuum of places of possible holding still, so also the concluding kinaesthesis of walking, which has its particular mode of functioning by its back-reference to the already synthetic achievement of all other communalized kinaestheses, each of which has already taken on the sense of being combinable capably with every other in holding still and moving, and so a functional member in a functional whole, a unity of organized functions. In walking new motion and rest (and all other unchange and change) of the world of experience (perceptual world) are constituted in their streaming manner. Now the fixed system of places with fixed distances, fixed configurations and groupings of resting things is first constituted, and the "objective" change of these configurations by change of distance in motion. Walking is the "concluding kinaesthesis" because it presupposes the organized unity of all others. With it the fixed system of places (Ortssystem) is constituted, the objective space of distances, as against mere orientation space.

(265) I ask myself: is the homogeneous objective world, the homogeneous space-time over against which the oriented is mere appearance, constituted only through the interplay of walking oneself and being driven, being moved mechanically? Let us try with mere walking. Can one not say that in the first system, the rigidly oriented spatial world, each has its changing or fixed distance from each? My body too, like its members, has changing distance from oriented things, only it cannot change position; it remains a rigid zero-object, and only other objects change their distance relative to it. In walking I change my distance from myself to objects resting identically through the change of orientation, and as two external objects can touch so that their distance at the point of contact becomes zero, so too my body and an external object. Can I not thus come everywhere within the reach of my capacity to walk, and is not space thereby constituted as a system of places (no longer as orientation space), and my body as an object like others, occupying a part of space, having a place in space and moved, spatially moved like other objects? The hypothesis: walking alone suffices to constitute my body as an object in space, since I change my distance to things and can touch them, making distance zero. The question whether being moved (riding) is also required is posed.

(265) The fixed system of places of all external things perspectivally accessible to me is evidently constituted, and also that I can approach every thing and place bodily (on the "surface of the earth"; indirectly, by way of empathy with birds, I understand flying and then have the ideal possibility of being able to fly and fly ever further idealizingly before my eyes, but that does not belong to the present constitutive level). I can come to every place and be at it, and so my body is also a thing, a res extensa, and mobile. Husserl's note: but is that really correct? The conclusion, that my body is a res extensa because I can reach every place, is immediately questioned. The parenthesis shows that the idealized infinite space requires empathy (with birds): flying is understood through others. The doubt is justified, since reaching places is not the same as being seen as a moving thing from outside; the gap is what Appendix XVI and No. 37 address.

(266) But does being moved in space (being driven, carried) achieve nothing for constitution, nothing essential for the possibility of empathy? The closing question returns to the riding experience of No. 15 and is taken up in Appendix XVI and Appendix XVII.

### Appendix XVI. On the Systematic Constitution of the Lowest Physical Nature. Lived Body and External Thing in Correlation (End of May 1932)

(266) If I am to make clear in primordial abstraction, "before" the co-validity of others, how, given the essentially different modes of givenness of my own bodily body and of external bodies, an apperception can come about in which this bodily body is experienced as a body like any other, various difficult problems must be solved. The problem of No. 16 is restated eight months later, with the same primordial restriction.

(266) From the outset, for us living in constant world-experience, our own body is experienced as a spatial-bodily thing among others, and every other thing likewise. In general experience all things are experienced as things, however different their special and individual sense. A regional generality lies in the apperception; it is "thing"-apperception. It already has in its sense world-space as universal horizon, in which each thing is among others, infinitely as to experiential actuality and possibility. So in the unity of a total apperception of a perceptual present of the world I always find both, body and external things; my body always there, other things changing in their appearance. In the natural attitude the body is simply a thing among things, under the regional generality "thing." The analysis must reconstruct how this regional unity is achieved.

(266) I first consider for themselves the particular apperceptions, the typical ones of external things and that of my body. If I look at them in concreto, as my self-body-perception and my perception of another thing, and consider the systematic course of the continuity of experience in which the perceptual sense "bodily body" and "external thing" comes to progressive fulfilling self-givenness, I must state that considerable differences prevail on both sides, which make it hard to understand how both can count in the same regional sense, as spatial objects in the same sense. The difficulty is the asymmetry of fulfilment: the body's perceptual sense is fulfilled quite differently from the external thing's.

(266) For all external objects (non-bodies) the manifold of experiences and their unification into self-givenness of one and the same existing object is completely alike; but it is quite isolated and different for self-body-perception. All external things share one type of fulfilment system; my body has a unique one.

(266–267) I keep to what actual and possible perception offers and can offer in the unity of a universal continuity of perception, or I survey the total manifold of actual and possible perceptions, all connectable singly and already as experience of the same. The method is restricted to what perception can fulfil.

(267) First external things (and these before all change, at rest, qualitatively unchanged, neither deformed nor changing in their extended qualities; so change by division and joining is also excluded). Asking what of the apperceived real things comes to real self-givenness, I gain the surface phantoms as intelligible unities of "sides," of the current phantom-presentations; and it is pointed out that the validity of unity in this course of validities of sides is motivated by the functioning of bodiliness as perceptual bodiliness, in which I am functioning and active. From there one would have to proceed to the further regress of thing-apperception, the question of the possible perceptions in which what else is proper to the thing as such would come to self-givenness. Husserl's note: inadequate, hasty! The bodily thing purely as originally experienced: ontological description. The aesthetic essential structure of the thing: guide for the phansiological structure and the egoic-noetic. The first stratum of the external thing is the surface phantom, unified through the functioning body. The note reorders method: first the ontological description of the aesthetic thing, then the "phansiological" (appearance-) structure, then the egoic-noetic. "Phansiological" (phansis, appearing) is Husserl's term for the analysis of modes of appearance.

(267) Certain ontological distinctions are guides, and their order is determined by the founding of perceptibilities and systems of perception, as systems in which confirmation alone can run in prefigured order, itself implying a prefiguration of validities by validities, but such that in the total system to be gained relations of founding prevail: the system of the phantom founds that of motion, deformation, division. The in itself earlier and later in validity is sought. The founding order of thing-constitution: phantom, then motion, deformation, division, a sequence from Ding und Raum and Ideas II.

(267–268) The first is the regress from the aesthetic-ontic to its self-presenting appearances. Then consideration of the peculiarity of aesthetic-ontic bodiliness and its mode of appearance. We come at once to the "I am involved": my perceiving, my functioning in the body, in the perceptual organs. Turning in this direction, the body itself is given, even constantly, as experienced thing, but as more than a thing; and its functioning, as the motivating presupposition for other things to appear to me in modes of appearance and count as existing with their presented ontic content, offers itself as an ontic worldly occurrence, yet not as a mere natural occurrence. If I study how this ontic togetherness of bodily body and objects of perception outside it comes to self-givenness, what its perceptual system and mode of appearance looks like and the foundings and motivations of validity belonging to it, I come ontically to the articulation of the body into mobile, kinaesthetically mobile organs functioning as perceptual organs in these movements. And these organs have, as I always apprehend them, the particular ontic structures as eyes, touch organs. But the question is only what perception really brings to self-givenness, what in the course of external perception is itself given on the side of the body in the perceiving consciousness of it, as motivating, founding the validity of being inherent in thing-appearances. Perception of everything real, reduced to what is properly perceived, refers back to perception of my body; even reduced experience of nature refers back to my body, which is itself given perceptually as "thing," but as more. Every perception of things refers to the body, which is perceived as a thing and "more": its functioning is itself an ontic occurrence in the world, yet not a mere natural one. The body is both condition and object of perception.

(268) But is the body, counting as a perceived bodily thing, not itself perceived in the same way as other things, through seeing, touching? The question whether the body's thing-perception is like any other.

(268) I thus come to the body's functioning for itself as perceptual organ, or to the ways it is built of manifold organs and, functioning in them, I can use each organ now as perceptual organ for other organs, for the rest of the body, now as object of perception, other organs functioning perceptually toward it. The reversibility of organ and object (No. 1 § 3) is the specific structure of self-body-perception.

(268) I meet the difficulty: the surface of the external thing is constituted both haptically and optically; the surface of my body I can touch all round but not see fully, and yet I have a complete optical representation of my surface look for myself. How is the "complement" of the unseen conceivable as "possible perception," which can never be possible for me? The difficulty of No. 15 recurs: my unseen back has a visual representation that is not a possible perception for me.

(268) Further, I find that my body in the layer of surface, in pure experience, has a double-layered mode of experience no other spatial object has (the "aesthetic" of the body goes beyond the sensuous-aesthetic of every other thing). Its surface is continuously "sensitive," and in it I function in my perceiving, but also originally practical, activities. In this functioning it moves doubly, in spatial moving and kinaesthetically egoically; and this inner "I move" contains nothing of motion in the spatial sense. But this also implies that an external thing in all-sided experience in the layer of surface experience is experienceable as unmoved, unchanged in general, and then concrete experience necessarily has the sense of the existing thing persisting unchanged (Husserl's note: which is not merely surface). But explicit self-body experience necessarily shows the body, as to its "outside," in processes of motion and accompanying deformations. Two asymmetries: the body's surface is sensitive (sensings localized on it), and experiencing it requires moving it, so it is never given at rest as an external thing can be. The "I move" has nothing spatial in it, so the body's spatial motion is not given in the kinaesthesis.

(268–269) Further, every external thing has its total system of oriented presentations, of ontic perspectives, belonging to its total mode of givenness. On the other hand, only single organs of my body have a perspectivization, an incomplete one. My total body cannot perspectivize, cannot have modes of givenness of "nearer" and "farther." Connected with this: external motion in original experience, which gives the basis for all mediate experience (all mediacies of understanding the sense of spatial motion and indirectly supposing a possible motion not directly experienced), external perception of motion has no analogue in self-motion as locomotion of the whole bodily body. How does the inwardly experienced kinaesthesis come to its "objective" sense as change of place in space, the same space as that of other things? How do I come to apperceive what I really experience from my continuity of self-body-perception as my spatial shape, first the surface shape, as a piece of a universal space? The whole body has no perspectives and no perceived locomotion. The question is how the kinaesthetic "I walk" acquires the sense "change of place in objective space."

(269) Every external thing is given in the possibility of its exchange of place with every other. Can I see, perceive in the same way, the exchange of place between another thing and my body when I "move"? Indeed, when a thing rests, no other can occupy its place; in moving toward it there is impact; the resting one resists and keeps its place until pushed away or otherwise moving. While I hold still, kinaesthetically keep rest, an external thing can come up to my body until contact, and if I stay at rest, "resist," "perhaps tense my force," the motion is ended. If I "give way," the thing finds empty space it can traverse further in motion. But the holding still, the resistance, the forces I tense, are inwardnesses and not givens of my bodily experience as external experience. Resistance and impact between body and thing are experienced from within as effort, not perceived as physical impact. The body's place-exclusiveness is felt, not seen.

(269–270) Must one not say: the space belonging to external things from actual and possible perception, the space of primordial perception, is constituted as a unity in such a way that I am at every moment and constantly the zero-centre of the system of places of orientations in which the spatial places present themselves, experienced as such? What appears in the mode of oriented place, as to its content, is the apparency (the qualified surface), whose distinguishable parts are themselves given oriented, relatively oriented among themselves. The bodily body in its mode of givenness as zero-body, taken as a whole, is not accessible to a change of position, an orienting motion; in oriented space it is neither at rest nor in motion, unlike all other objects. But it has its apparency, its extension, and in it in a particular way inner differences of orientation. In the kinaesthetic change of walking in this or that subjective direction, the apparency of the oriented place is identified as spatial place in continuous perceptual fulfilment, and the apparency of the zero-place and zero-extension, despite its unchangeability as zero, again as a spatial place for the one spatially extended body. My own apparency of corporeality indicates to me, for every sensuously perceptual field of orientation and its directions, the oriented spatialities to be experienced in familiar kinaestheses, and in their apperceptive synthesis of identity the freeing of spheres of orientation-space, orientation places, which in this apperceptive identification would drop out with corresponding walking, because my body as centre of all orientation and its changes, awakening these changes with its central kinaesthesis, cannot itself be given as a unity in changes of orientation. It constantly indicates, in the way it itself is, and correlatively, constituted, possible original perception of spatialities, and in this indication it puts a spatiality indirectly into validity as one of possible experience, which is indirectly confirmed in its walking actual and possible kinaesthesis. The body as zero-body is "neither at rest nor in motion" in oriented space, an exact formulation. Walking identifies oriented places as spatial places, and by the same identification the zero-place is identified as a spatial place too, indirectly, as the place my body vacates and occupies. The body's spatiality is "indicated," put into validity indirectly, not perceived.

(270) As my body in its walking kinaesthesis constantly frees oriented space and the constitution of an identical spatiality presenting itself as immediately perceptually oriented becomes possible, it is intelligible that there is a universal space and a spatial world in which all things given in orientation have identical spatial places in the change of their orientations, which they now keep, now change in rest or motion. But that concerns only all extra-bodily things, as those standing in possible change of orientation, a change that becomes presentation of something identical because it is I who, in my capably mastered kinaesthetic system, can produce every change of orientation, transform every one that occurs of itself, and ideally also cancel it. On the other hand it is not yet intelligible that my body itself exists in space, is to be experienced and experienceable as a spatial thing that has its place, physically moved and at rest and so a real object, res extensa, constituted in its externality. Universal space is intelligible for external things; my body's being in it is not yet.

(270) I apperceive myself as moving myself inwardly kinaesthetically and apperceive that as staging from within a mechanical motion, a motion in objective space. I apperceive myself as pushing, shoving, experiencing and offering resistance inwardly, and understand that externally as a physical process of impact and counter-impact, of resistance, taking place between the thing body and the other thing exactly as between any two things: so my body is a natural object. The apperception is stated: inner kinaesthesis is apprehended as "staging" an objective mechanical motion, inner effort as physical impact. The question is the right of this apprehension.

(270–271) The question is how far the constitution of sense of being in primordiality reaches, which I must bring out abstractly, though I notice and can convince myself amply on reflection that the sense of being of extended nature is not built up purely primordially and that others constantly help, so to speak, as soon as they are experienceable and experienced as others for me. But if I want to understand how the validity of the sense "others" is constituted in its foundings and how far primordial validity and unity of validity founds the possibility of perceiving others, I must first stubbornly follow the reach of primordial constitution as founding of validity from the systematic structure of fulfilling identification in the system of possible experience thought as running in concordant synthesis of self-confirmation. I must thereby bring out that two-sided "psychophysical" unity of primordiality which, as self-enclosed sense of being, carries an "outside," a special unity of validity, bodily body, which, in its typical outer comportment, pairing associatively with a similar external body showing similar comportment (other body), leads to the appresenting transfer of the "psychic" side. Husserl admits that the sense of extended nature is co-constituted by others, so the primordial reduction is an abstraction; yet he insists on "stubbornly" following its reach, because empathy requires an "outside" of my body already constituted primordially. The method requires the abstraction even if the result is not purely primordial in fact.

(271) But it must also become intelligible, precisely from the peculiarity of the original constitution of bodily corporeality and from its overlayer of appresentations, which already have the peculiarity of not being able to become proper presentation, why the psychic appresented in empathy cannot be converted into original presentation for me. The insight sought: my own body already carries appresentations that cannot become presentations (my back's look). This unfulfillability within my own body is the model for the unfulfillability of the other's psyche. Empathy's structure would be prefigured in self-body-constitution.

(271) In the layer of apparency of the real, primordially: there is always experienced within perception the real present as mode of real temporal apparency and the real at its place, presented in spatial presence, in here and there. This presentation is orientation. However orientation changes and whether the experienced is experienced as resting or moved, it has its bodily extension, and in experience at every moment its surface, its apparency, is filled as what is properly experienced, and at every moment only from one side. The apparency in what is properly perceptually given is presented in two layers, immediately, visual-tactile. Apparency is two-layered: visual and tactile.

(271–272) Seen and touched appresent each other, and the appresentation is original in the sphere where it happens in consciousness: where I do not see but touch, I can actualize the corresponding seeing or the visual belonging to the touched as presentation of the same. I restore here what original genesis instituted as possible. But my back I cannot see, and yet it is co-experienced quite so, originally apperceived like the back of my hand experienced by touch, for which I can at any time gain the appresented visual side. The different sensibilities have their sense-giving function. It may be that the typical analogy of the tactile back with the tactile back of the hand leads, so to speak frivolously, in "blind" association to appresenting the visual back. But does that say anything other than: I habitually expect, in associative transfer of validity, and that means transfer of capacity, that what I can do here I can also do there, namely realize the analogue of the visual I anticipate? The visual back is appresented by association with the back of the hand, a "blind" transfer of capacity: I expect to be able to see my back as I can see my hand. Association transfers capacities, not only contents.

(272) How is it that I now, since every attempt fails, still assign the corresponding visual properties to touching the back as parallel instead of crossing them out? Is the right answer not: being is not constituted visually or tactually for itself, as if two beings were constituted for themselves, but one being; and the capacity belonging to it is that of possible experiencing confirmation, which has possibilities of illusion beside it. But crossing out, the "null," as illusion can have its original confirmation only in conflict with persisting being. In our case: the appresented visual back is the back, the same that is tactually experienceable in the unity of the experience of the back. That I cannot in fact "get there" in seeing yields no crossing out as illusion; for I can get there; in the constant contact with clothes I am really already in this experience; I progress in the sense of greater fulfilment by reaching there with my hand. Having made the transition into visual appresentation and from given motives anticipated future visual presentations, they are nothing fictive; they have validity, they confirm themselves through touching activity or prove incorrect. But while otherwise fulfilment can take both paths and in general the visual near sphere yields the optima, as to inaccessible parts of the body I cannot have this advantage. With the coat on my back I can convince myself visually by taking it off and holding it before me or laying it down (the tactile co-counting in its appresentation and having to agree in its confirmation, otherwise the visual is illusion). But it is evident that I cannot do this for the body itself. The answer to the puzzle: the visual back is not crossed out because illusion requires conflict with confirmed being, and there is none; the visual appresentation is confirmed through touch, since the thing is one, not two sense-objects. Unfulfillability in one modality is not cancellation. The coat example shows a mediate visual confirmation impossible for the body itself, since I cannot take it off.

(272–273) And what about movements? Walking, I apperceive myself as moved body in space. But originally I am experienced only as to the kinaesthetic "I walk." Of my spatial motion? Original experience of spatial motion I have only of other things as oriented. Here too I can well imagine how my walking would "look," how it would present itself with closed eyes in the touch-object. Do I here act as if my body were an external object, as if it perspectivized visually like one in moving away from me, in change of orientation in general? But how is this way of representing to have a sense of validity, which it does have for me, since it is absurd to let it count as possible experience in the way the representation of possible motion of an external thing counts as possible experience realizable in possible perception? How can an intuitive representation be null as possible experience and yet carry a validity, a validity of experience? That would be possible, if at all, mediately, in that this representation takes on a function of validity in the context of the possible system of perception of the same, so far as it indicates possible experiences in it, though not the one that presents itself here like an immediate parallel belonging to tactile perception. The representation of my body moving away from me is "absurd" as possible experience, yet valid. Its validity must be mediate: it indicates other possible experiences, not the one it pictures.

(273) The example of the coat shows the possibility of such mediate references. The visual parallel image of the touched back of the coat I am wearing has no immediate validity as immediate appresentation, as something to be realized in immediately possible presentation, merely by looking. But the intuition, devalued in this sense of validity, refers me not only to the co-valid tactility but in the visual sphere itself to the manifold of visually producible perceptions of this coat and what is to be discovered visually in them, which I can produce by taking off the coat. A representation can be devalued as immediate appresentation yet valid as reference to other producible perceptions. This "mediate reference" is the structure the body's spatial self-representation needs.

(273) This leads us to the point that the synthetically connected or connectable total system of possible experiences, possible self-presentations, in which one and the same thing is experienceable in its original self-existence and demonstrable in every respect in synthetic traversal, falls into two essentially different groups standing in a nexus of motivation that co-determines the sense of being. First, the system of appearance in which the external thing (not being my body) presents itself as outside the body, appearing in the system of orientation by the dimensions right, left, above, below, front, back, experienced, experienceable and confirmable as such, and in all these dimensions with gradations of near and far. That gives differences in the modes of givenness of experienceable realities as to their experience of places in space, differences of presentation also expressed as relative differences of here and there; the more distant in the various directions is "there," the nearer "here." And the whole relative near sphere is also addressed as that of the here, as for all gradualities within experience (as self-givenness) the total domain divides vaguely into regions with the general characters near, far, intermediate "neither near nor far." The total system of a thing's appearances has two groups; the first is the oriented system, here and there, near and far.

(273) In everything lies the relation to the body, "outside" which, as in the widest sense there, external things are experienced. It itself, taken as a whole, is the zero of all orientations, in this sense the absolute here for every there, everything outside it. Husserl's note distinguishes changes of orientation that occur of themselves (only the kinaesthesis of traversal) from those due to my intervention, which change the orientations of the whole field. The body is the absolute here, the zero of orientation (Hua I § 53; Ideas II § 41).

(273–274) Second, we have a correlative system of self-presentations of the same external things (as things that are not my body): those appearances the same bodies take on by being "connected" with my body, or which they have and can only have in already existing "connectedness." What is this "connectedness"? The peculiar mode of union that occurs, for example, when I take a thing standing "freely by itself," moving, into my hand, or only lay my hand on it, and which it has as long as I hold it (perhaps running along); or which unites my body and the cart as long as I am on it, while it now drives, now stops. To this belongs the unity of my clothes with "me" as long as I wear them, the unity with my desk as long as I sit leaning on it writing. "Objectively" these things remain separate from my body. How is this union characterized? Evidently in that now not my mere body is the zero-object, in zero-orientation (which as zero is the opposite of orientation, but presupposed in all orientation), but my body together with the object "connected" with it. Every object that is not body can, through a certain behaviour of the body, move into zero-orientation, but only by gaining share in the body's original and proper mode of appearance. The body has so acquired the object that was before there, right, left, near, far, before me, that it loses its there, forfeits the modes of orientation and enters the zero mode of experience, which is unitary for the whole body together with what is acquired. The second group is the system of "connectedness": a thing held, ridden, worn, leaned on enters the zero-orientation of the body. This is the phenomenological theory of incorporation, the extension of the body's here to tools, vehicles, clothes, which Merleau-Ponty would develop (the blind man's stick).

(274) Included in what was said is running along with a moved thing, which not only changes the modes of orientation of things like every walking but, as constant being-with the thing, unites the modes of appearance of the body and of the other thing. Taking into hand is, when I walk, letting the previously perhaps resting object appear constantly as zero instead of letting it appear in change of orientation through my walking, and thereby it is experienced as "moved with me." If it was moved, running along is no change of its motion but of its orientation into zero. Running alongside a moving thing gives it zero-orientation; a held thing is experienced as "moved with me." This is how the body acquires the sense of being moved: the held thing is moved, and I with it.

(274–275) The essential and most general is: between my experiencing-functioning body and the totality of current experiences, in relation to the system of orientations essentially belonging to it (in the fixed persisting form), there is the connection that through certain kinaestheses I can freely change the orientations, however they factually run. I have capacity, mastery over change of orientation, only bound to the form and law of continuity of change; and that implies I can finally turn the orientation of every thing into zero and keep it lastingly in zero form. This concerns certain kinaesthetic stagings in the kinaesthetic system, the bodily functional correlates of what is experienced as orientation. To every passive orientation belongs a kinaesthetic situation in holding still; every orientation in my in this respect "walking" holding still (standing still) stands under the already familiar possibility of changes. Husserl's long note distinguishes orientation in the ocular field (kinaesthesis of looking all round, the ocular thing with its optimum "itself," an absolute ocular nearness) from orientation in the aesthetic spatial field (the apparency, the surface-thing as distance-thing, the ocular thing degraded to an adumbration of it); observes that looking at a thing all round without turning it changes the orientation of other things; and states that apperception always grows in the formation of capacity and its anticipated horizon as constituted practical possibility, a systematic founding of practical anticipations as always attainable acquisitions, the identical as "always again" available; and that in the aesthetic sphere the apparency is relatively existing, itself appearance in the system of distance and nearness, the near being the optimum, while the identical in the manifolds of oriented apparency is being from the systematic capacity to change the system of orientation freely and keep identity in this change. Mastery of orientation is the essential correlation of body and world: I can bring any thing to zero. The note makes the principle explicit: being, at the level of apparency, is what remains identical under my free changes of orientation, the "always again available," a practical definition of identity.

(275) I should have said in advance that in this fundamental consideration we want to deal exclusively with objects purely from actual and possible perception, according to what comes to self-presentation in it, and again exclusively with the circle of bodily objects, or set them off abstractively, that are perceivable on all sides and completely. Excluded is the ground-body on which all our walking, all our natural and original perceptual life plays out: the earth as ground-body. For travelling in an airship we cannot traverse the air, which we otherwise count with the earth, by "walking." If we were birds it would be different; their flying is a kinaesthetic "I move," and as soon as they settle on an aircraft, the reversal in all modes of appearance occurs for them as for us on our ground-body. The restriction to what is actually and capably experienceable for us excludes our ground-body, because in its endlessness it is not experienceable for us in the total system of its modes of experience. Of course this points to extensions of primordial apperceptions through indirect sense-givings as extensions of sense, which cannot yet come into question here. The earth is excluded because it is not a thing perceivable on all sides: it is the "ground-body" (Bodenkörper) of all walking. This is the thesis of the 1934 Earth-ark text: the earth does not move, since it is the ground relative to which motion is constituted. The airship and bird examples show that "ground" is relative to the kinaesthesis of locomotion: for the bird the air is ground.

(276) The task arises of explicating the intentional mutual reference of the apperceptions of the two groups of the total system and the founding of validity occurring in it, which makes it belong to the sense of being of every single experience of a spatial body, correlatively to its ontic sense, that its current mode of appearance, if oriented, also refers to the counter-modes belonging to it as zero modes. Since all modes of appearance in the system are conscious as convertible into one another capably and only so achieve sense of being, the reference means that I can self-evidently perform the conversions from myself, in my kinaesthetically free activity. So deceptions are also clarified. If I do not think of having boarded the cart or the ship whose oriented motion I saw before, I experience rest, or, as regards objects on the ship, motions other than the true ones. Every oriented appearance refers to a zero appearance (when held or ridden), and conversely. Illusions of motion (the train that seems to move) are explained by forgetting a "connection." The structure gives the phenomenological basis of relative motion.

(276) The distinction is also not unimportant between union with a bodily member and thereby extension of that member, by which it becomes mediately an extended perceptual organ, impact organ (hand tool), and union with the whole body, with the special case that I make what is appropriated a vehicle. "Tools" (Zeuge) in the original sense are physical objects through which the most original, immediate bodily doing and the next mediate bodily working on the external receive the new form of working, with the inclusion of a body-extending thing, more effectively and purposefully. The appendix ends with a phenomenology of tools: a tool extends a member; a vehicle extends the whole body. "Zeug" in the "original sense" is the body-extending physical object, a definition close in vocabulary to Heidegger's Zeug in Being and Time § 15 but grounded in the theory of the body's zero-orientation rather than in a referential totality.

(276–277) Once such clarifications are achieved, especially those presupposed as explications of the zero modes of appearance and in them that of the body itself, the clarification of the peculiarity that, in the zero-form of orientation, belongs to the body and its modes of appearance as against the non-bodily things co-comprised in this zero-form (that they are not kinaesthetic members, have no kinaestheses of their own, but gain a certain share in the kinaestheses when "connected" with a "bodily member," belonging to its co-stock), once all this is achieved, one can approach the question how the body itself can be apperceptively equated with non-bodily things, as if it too were one of the zero-objects, which only through its bodily-egoic ruling can take on zero modes of givenness beside their oriented external modes. The appendix ends by inverting the problem: held things enter the body's zero; the question is whether the body can be apprehended as if it were a held thing, an external thing that entered zero. Appendix XVII answers by this inversion.

### Appendix XVII. Psychophysical Apperception. How Does the Lived Body Come to Be Experienced Like Another Thing, as Moved and at Rest Like Others, Hence in Space? (Probably June 1932)

(277) In primordiality there is an essential difference in apperception or constitution between my bodily body and other bodies. Reducing both to pure experience, to the pure bodily apparency as unity in the manifold of "modes of appearance," of sides and of the perspectives belonging to what is properly perceived, what originally comes to intuitive confirmation as my body is essentially different from all other bodies, the external bodies, all constituted alike. To them belongs ontically, as co-constituted, originally experienced and experienceable, motion, presenting itself as "near and far things," changing modes of givenness of "orientation." Correlatively, intervening actively in the change of these modes, I can modify them so that in the limiting case what was experienced as rest without my intervention now presents itself exactly as motion did, and conversely. This intervention is characterized as perceiving bodily doing, seeing with the eye, moving my organs from myself while seeing and otherwise perceiving, now moving, now holding still. If I consider my body as it gives itself in perception as a body, purely perceptually, or confirms itself on all sides, I find that my hand, single members functioning as perceptual organs, are experienced, though very limitedly, in their bodily motion and rest (the hand moved on the table) like external things, so that motion, change of place, really shows itself perceptually in them, but not the whole body in any of its motions or rests. The difference is restated: external things show motion perceptually; my members partly do; my whole body never does. Rest and motion of external things are interconvertible by my intervention (running alongside turns motion into apparent rest).

(277) Nevertheless my body is apperceived as a body like another. The apperceptive institution of my body, constituted at the level of purely sensuous experience, which cannot yet have generic commonality with what presents itself in sensuous external experience as external body, as a body like all others, first creates general corporeality in general space, the form of all bodily coexistence in motion and rest, in change and unchange of shape, which in all circumstances makes up a piece of space in the common space and makes it, in its qualification, the real shape at its place. The apperception of my body as a body like others is not a recognition of an existing similarity but an institution (Stiftung) that first creates the genus "body" and common space. Before it, my body and external bodies have no genus in common.

(278) Together with it the institution of the bodily body already experienced as res extensa in common spatiality takes place, as the body of nature in which I constantly rule and with which I am constantly and immediately. In other words, the institution of my psychophysical being takes place with it and from it. With the bodily corporeality, now at rest, now moved in space, deforming and otherwise changing, I am constantly, and so constantly localized in space, in space-time, hence experienceable and experienced as unity of body and soul, as ensouled body. The same institution makes me a psychophysical being: localized in space through my body. The soul's spatial localization is a consequence of the body's apperception as a spatial thing.

(278) In further consequence the institution of the region man is made possible by way of the apperceptive transfer of the sense of psychophysical being to external bodies, so far as they meet the conditions of transferability, that is, have the similarity with my bodily existence that can lead to this apperceptive transfer. Empathy, the institution of "man" as region, follows from the psychophysical self-apperception.

(278–279) On closer view, however, before the psychophysical apperception proper, in which I am experienced as ensouled body in all-nature, in space-time, there lies a founding body-soul apperception by which the apperception "my bodily body, a body like every other," and so spatial apperception in general, is first made possible. This requires that the effective similarity of my body, sensuously experienced in looking at and touching myself, with the sensuously experienced other bodies can lead and leads to an indirect apperceptive transfer of the motion and mobility experienced in these external bodies to my body, in the following way. The motion of my members, which I really see as motion, like that of external objects (though motion on both sides is an ontic occurrence that cannot yet have the full sense of motion), has its continuous and inevitable parallel in the inwardly ruling "I move" of kinaesthesis. One sees already that this must play a role where in an external body a member resembling my hand and presenting itself in typically similar motion is apprehended as hand movement, a spatial motion indicating a parallel inner movement, an egoically ruling accompanying moving. But that presupposes more. It must first be shown by analysis of sensuous self-experience of my body how this experience brings the body as unitary corporeality first sensuously-appearingly to experience analogously to the externally presented bodies, articulated bodies, analogous in their members to the corresponding members of my body, but as bodies that do not bring to experience those sensuous modes of change that, in a first, more original sense, make up their moving there and back, approaching or receding, moving right or left. According to this analogy my body is apprehended as an "unmoved" body on which only its members move. Now every sensuously experienceable motion of my members is consistently accompanied, associatively one with a parallel "I move," an egoic kinaesthesis. It is therefore always experienced in a "psychophysical," two-sided way. On the other hand this experienced motion of members is in constant function in every external experience, at least in all courses of perceiving external bodies, and as basically essential moment of this functioning belongs the inner "motion," the kinaesthesis, which constantly carries its outside with it. This functioning can have a double consequence. As continuously belonging correlate of the kinaesthesis it may condition those courses of appearance (co-courses of the kinaesthesis) in which "rest" as experienced is constituted. In the inner, egoically staged traversal of the kinaesthesis, as often as I return to "the same" kinaesthesis, I regain the same mode of appearance, and in arbitrary traversal I traverse a manifold of always the same corresponding modes of appearance in which one and the same resting thing is constituted. This identically remaining, unchanged correlate-system separates from what changes in the modes of appearance not from me, not as pure consequence of egoic kinaesthetic moving: what makes up the object's qualitative change and unchange occurring without my doing. If the modes of appearance return entirely the same in the change of kinaestheses, complete unchange is experienced, which only in concurrence with other cases separates into kinaesthetically motivated and unmotivated change. But we do not go into that. A founding "body-soul" apperception precedes the psychophysical one: my members are experienced two-sidedly, as seen motion and as inner "I move." This psychophysical doubling of the member is the hinge: when a member of an external body resembles my hand and moves like it, its motion indicates a parallel inner movement. My whole body, by analogy with articulated external bodies, is apprehended as an "unmoved" body on which members move. Rest is constituted as the return of the same appearance with the return of the same kinaesthesis.

(279) Accordingly motion is experienced when modes of appearance run in a regulated way such that they do not form, without further ado and in all kinaesthetic courses, one and the same manifold of appearances belonging together and to them, but form it only when the bodily ruling ego exercises a particular function in a corresponding kinaesthetic "going along" and so functions kinaesthetically in a double way: by a walking intervention of a certain kind, speed, tempo, the modes of appearance again run as in rest, and the otherwise free kinaestheses, put into arbitrary function, have modes of appearance as consequence, quite as in rest. Motion is the deviation that a compensating "going along" turns back into rest: motion is defined through the kinaesthesis that cancels it.

(280) We thus have the remarkable thing that a functional division takes place in the kinaestheses: bodily movements as ruling of the ego and at the same time appearing externally as "walking" in an extended sense (functioning locomotively), and bodily movements as functioning (here exclusively perceptually) but without locomotive function. Thereby motion can be experienced, with a sense that takes it as a continuum of possible rests, and on the other hand as a phenomenon of change continuously convertible into apparent rests, since at every moment the going along, running along, can set in that can turn the phenomenon of change into a phenomenon of rest. The functional division of kinaestheses into locomotive and perceptual is the key to the constitution of motion.

(280) If the experiencing gaze and active experiencing turn to the body, it differs essentially from all external things as to experience of motion and rest. All external things need, for their sensuously experiencing apperception of motion (or transition from rest to motion and conversely, as the constant possibility that gives rest its sense as zero case of motion), bodiliness constantly co-functioning as motivating, namely walking bodiliness. What else is to be seen and sensuously experienced in external objects is provided by the other co-functioning kinaestheses (eye movements, touch movements while I walk here or there, perhaps hold still, walk again). On the other hand my body, experienced in this latter respect like external things, is by no means experienced as moved or at rest through the walking, locomotive kinaesthesis. For it motion and rest originally have no sense, namely no sense such as with external things as motion and rest in their spatial field (which at first is not yet real space). The walking kinaesthesis has only inner functional sense, but no sense of a real change of place of the body in space, and so no space is constituted yet in which the bodily ruling and body-experiencing ego, by virtue of this experience purely as sensuous experience, would as this body have spatial shape and position in space and so spatial coexistence. Correlatively, the space of external things is not yet real space, the corresponding external world no real world, as which it would encompass everything coexisting, body and psychophysical ego included, as its form of coexistence. Without the body's being in it, external space is not yet real space: a space that does not contain the perceiver is only a field of orientation. Real space requires the body's inclusion.

(280–281) How does the higher-level apperception of the body as body like all bodies, founded in sensuous experience, come about, and with it space and world become experienceable? There is a not difficult answer. If we already have external things and body as natural objects, have already experienced ourselves as psychophysically real, as humans, it is an intelligible fact that we connect extra-bodily objects with the body, take everyday objects of use in hand, lift, carry them, board a cart, jump onto a moving cart and be driven. If we attend to the modes of appearance, the system of possible sensuous experience transforms for every object as soon as it becomes, from one separate from the body, one united with it. The object taken in hand at once loses the capacity to appear as resting or moved like an external thing; it becomes so to speak a bodily member, only lacking the particular kinaestheses. It can never yield, through my walking, the change of modes of appearance of near and far. Precisely this reversal evidently founds the possibility of the apperception of the body as a body. If I take a thing in hand and walk, it moves; when I let it go, it is at another place of the already constituted "external space," as if it had moved there without my walking. In jumping on and off, in the example of the cart, I can see motion at will and make motion disappear as phenomenon, in reversal into a body-piece phenomenon. One and the other are apperceived as equivalent, indicate the always ready possibility of reversal, and that transfers to every part of the body, every member and the total body. It is moved in space as part of the moving cart; it itself receives an equivalent sense of motion, that of a thing-motion. The answer: the reversibility between external thing and held thing (or cart) founds the apperception of my body as a thing. A thing in hand becomes a "bodily member"; let go, it reappears elsewhere in external space, as if moved. Since held thing and external thing are equivalent, and the held thing is like my member, my member and finally my whole body acquire the sense of a thing that can be moved, as the cart moves me. The body is apprehended as a thing by being apprehended as a held thing is apprehended when it is let go. The circle of No. 15 (does the body's spatial apprehension presuppose empathy?) is resolved within primordiality through the experience of being carried.

### No. 17. My Primordial Being as "Human Being" and Its Transcendental Constitution. The Problem of the Separation of Ego and Non-Ego, and the Lived Body. The Possibility of a Non-Worldly Ego in Primordiality (Beginning of September 1931)

(282) The primordial ego in the living streaming primordial present, existing in its primordial constitution of the world. What is constituted in this liveliness: unity in the streaming perceptual appearances, as perceptually realized capacities of a horizon of capacities. The starting point: every constituted unity is a realized capacity within a horizon of capacities.

(282) The unity "external world" constituted in the streaming, as streamingly extending all-unity of the current single things, each the unity of a constitution embedded in world-constitution, but constituted inseparably from this unity and itself constituted together with it, all this said in primordial reduction. My body as spatial body, alike to external bodies in this, but as body in which and through which I rule, am perceptually occupied and can be occupied with extra-bodily things and with it itself, practically, intervening causally in pushing, shoving. Single things are constituted only with the world-whole. The body is a spatial body (the result of Appendix XVII), but the body of ruling.

(282–283) What of the unity of the ego as ruling in the world in and through the body? What distinguishes the bodily body worldly is that I am, continuously identically in the streaming present, the one ruling and able to rule in this body. I am identically one and inseparable from this body. But I am not myself as body, but in a unique way, what I am as ego, ruling: in the body and through it externally. The ego is inseparable from the body without being the body: its relation is ruling (Walten), a unique relation.

(283) As ego of the body I am ego for the world, ego that is now at this, now at that spatial place, and so has its spatio-temporal place. But I am not in space as a thing is in space, nor as my body is in space; I am not in motion as a thing is in motion. I have place not as a thing; my bodily body has it; but so far as I rule bodily and only am as that, as the body only is as body, I am one with the body; I am bodily, as the body is body as egoic organ. The ego's localization is derivative: it has place through the body, not as a thing. Husserl rejects both Cartesian separation and materialist identification: the ego is "bodily" as the body is "egoic organ," a reciprocal determination.

(283) In primordiality I am thus a worldly ego, psychophysical unity in the sense that I can always consider my body abstractively as a body among bodies and treat it so and then tell myself: I am not a supplement to this body, as if it could be like other bodies, but am continuously ruling in it and through it related to everything external; through it everything existing is there for me, accessible. The ego is not a "supplement" to a body that could be like others. The body is not a body plus something; it is a body as organ of access.

(283) So the external world, the world of mere things, mere bodies, separates from my primordial being "as human," with a bodily body distinguishable in me, which is yet not a body like others and does not differ from them by something being added to it that merely factually is lacking in other things. The difference of the body from things is not an addition. This anticipates the critique of the "layer" model of the body (body plus soul) that Merleau-Ponty would make central.

(283) This primordial world is the world of my experience, the experienced unity of many special unities constituted in my streaming experiencing, among them I-human myself, "in" it my bodily body as a central unity in a certain way; I bodily in the world, bodily with the capacity to perceive all things, approach them, be practically occupied with them, thereby changing the world of things and perhaps myself as human by my human doing. The "I-human" (primordially) is among the constituted unities of the primordial world.

(283–284) But this world with everything described as worldly is a unity of experience, a world of perception, memory, anticipating meaning of various kinds, a world synthetically constituted in the streaming of "appearances," of various meanings, as unity of validity and as unitary-manifold unity of unities of validity, which I as transcendental ego bring to validity through my active and passive "transcendental" life, have in habitual validity as valid from me and maintain in validity in manifold foundings of validity as my achievement, through my activity on the ground of my passivity, my validities instituted earlier, which continue to be valid for me in the form of a hidden "tradition" conditioning the unitary sense of being, on the ground of a hidden horizon that everything experienced as unity of sense implies. So the psychophysical "ego" too, this primordially reduced human (far indeed from being a human, as the ego is from being an ego in the normal sense that already refers to a thou, a we) is a constituted unity, acquired sense of being, acquisition of the transcendental ego and its transcendental "achievements." The primordial human is constituted by the transcendental ego. The parenthesis is important: the primordial human is not a human in the full sense, since the full ego refers to a "thou" and a "we." Validity has a "hidden tradition" in my own past, the egological analogue of cultural tradition.

(284) So if we perform the phenomenological reduction regarding the world existing naively for us, bring ourselves out of the naive attitude in which, living through the stream of experiences, we activate the validities of experience so as to be directed straightforwardly and exclusively at the world and its single objects and events, we direct our gaze to the fact that these unities are synthetic unities of a constituting life and constitutive acquisitions. The reduction is a redirection of the gaze to unities as acquisitions.

(284) I can gain an analogue of my ego with analogous ruling in analogous bodiliness by re-thinking myself as if I had a modified body, perhaps in a modified environment. Self-variation produces an analogue ego: the imaginative basis of the idea of another ego, though not yet another ego.

(284–285) I then modify my possibilities of thought in conflict with my constantly persisting actuality. But I am as sense of being from an essentially belonging constitution; I am experienced and experienceable as such in current modes; transcendentally reduced I am as ruling in the body, as affectable by external things through contact, as seeing them (with open eyes), hearing them, moving and changing them practically, as bodily being in space bodily, bodily moved and bodily changeable. Transcendentally I am all this, with this my external world, a unity of sense of a streaming life of appearances, of a streaming egological being in general, centred in an ego-pole, an ego-pole of capacities, of kinaestheses which, when I set them in play, bring into course from me the associated appearances in which the current sense of being valid to me comes to self-givenness and self-confirmation, the single sense and each as moment in the universal sense "bodily ego as ego in the world and this world itself." Going back from the unity bodily ego (ego concretely as primordially reduced human) into the transcendental concretum, I find the transcendental streaming constitution centred in the transcendental ego-pole, in which everything is primordially-transcendentally enclosed. Imagined variations conflict with my persisting actuality; they are not another actual ego. My actual being as bodily ego is a unity of sense centred in an ego-pole of capacities; the transcendental concretum contains it.

(285) I find in it, running together unitarily and inseparably, the streaming transcendental world-appearance, all single worldly appearances ordered into it, as perceptual world, the world as now living-present to me. In it the world "appears" as identical sense of being in the change of appearances, single and total, identical in this course also in its horizon of course, index for systematic capacities which, when I set them in play kinaesthetically (I, the transcendental ego as pole), let the same world appear from ever new "sides," in ever new modes of appearance, perhaps showing ever new such unities of sense that would come, could come, as co-enclosed open capacities in the change of the total appearance. The world is an index of capacities, the leitmotif of the late texts.

(285–286) In this stream of world-constitution is at the same time enclosed, inseparably, as constitution of the primordial spatio-temporal natural world, the constitution of extra-bodily things (external things) and that of my bodily body. Both constituted as res extensae in the unity of space-time and natural causality, though constituted differently, since the optical modes of appearance of my bodily body cannot let this total body appear perspectivally as approaching and receding, and the perspectivization of members (reduced purely bodily) is only limited. Further, in the modality of appearance belonging to the constitution of nature, near world as not only immediately visible but graspable world (and farther world, given only through transition, in natural motion "of itself" or "directed from me," into the near modality as "immediately there"), in relation to this modality the bodily body has the peculiarity that it is necessarily "always there," always the body "in" which I am or "with" which "I myself" am, which here means: I, the transcendental ego, with my transcendental kinaesthetic, appearance-governing activities, live here alone in an immediate activity, while with things experienced in the far modality I only mediately bring other thing-appearances into course through the immediacy of governing my bodily-body appearance-courses and in the bodily body constituted in them in bodily being-moved, transforming them into the near modality, in which I am then immediately active with them. The body is "always there" in the near world, the one place of my immediate activity; everything else is reached mediately through it.

(286) Further, when an external body joins another into the unity of on-each-other or by-each-other and remains one with it in rest and motion, only their modes of givenness become siblings, running parallel and simultaneous. But when an external body joins my bodily body, when it is carried by it or carries it, the modes of appearance of the external body (and of all things so united with "me") change optically and haptically and in every other way. Union between things is parallelism; union with my body is transformation into zero, the result of Appendix XVI.

(286–287) These different modes of constitution are correlative, since they belong together essentially for the constitution of a nature, first a nature, a universe of res extensae in the individuating form of space-time as uniformly perceivable in the living present. In it a body must also be constituted, a res extensa like others, in that distinguished way, since it is to be constituted supranaturally as lived body, body in which the ego senses, in which it rules immediately, in which it has its organ, with whose special organs its kinaestheses are one. It is not as if nature and world in general were constituted as unity of sense in an ego-centred stream of modes of appearance and capacities of appearance, so that in this correlation we would have world and the ego that is supra-worldly, the ego that constitutes world, that as ego of its transcendental life has world as non-ego opposite it, only in constant contact with the world so far as it is the appearing, streamingly presenting world in its non-worldly modes of appearance, its experiencing life and its pre-worldly experiential formations. Rather the world is so constituted that in it the ego itself appears as embodied. As bodily ego, ego that lives bodily in the world, it is itself worldly being, real object among other real objects. Or: the world is not merely nature but human and nature, in a certain way human within nature, since by "having" a body that is also nature, but having it as its organ, he is mundanized and can only be mundanized so. Husserl rejects a model in which a supra-worldly ego merely faces the world through appearances. The world is constituted with the ego embodied in it: "human and nature." Mundanization occurs through the body, and only through it. This is the thesis the title calls the "problem of the separation of ego and non-ego."

(287) But this primordial human ego, concretely embodied ego or egoized bodily body, is itself a unity of transcendental constitution, and so we finally come, in questioning back after the constitutive formation of the sense "ego-human," to the deepest separation: the transcendental-primordial ego of its streaming transcendental life, constituting the world in it, and the non-ego, the world, including the embodied ego. But can one separate so? Husserl's note: cf. the following page! In the transcendental ego we then distinguish it in its full concretion, comprising the whole transcendental life (with the world meant in all phases as meant), and the ego as mere ego-pole of the pole-synthesis necessarily occurring in act-life and of the constitution of the ego of capacities following from it. The regress seems to lead to a dualism: transcendental ego versus world including the human ego. The question "can one separate so?" and the note introduce a distinction within the transcendental ego: full concretion (including the meant world) and mere ego-pole. On the first, the world is inside the ego as meant; on the second, the pole faces the world.

(287) Must we not say: primal being is the totally streaming absolute life, in which a correlative synthesis necessarily prevails, the synthesis constituting the ego (a constitution of a totally different sense from world-constitution occurring in levels, achieving through appearances), and on the other hand precisely this ontifying constitution or synthesis? Primal being (Ursein) is the absolute streaming life, in which two syntheses are correlative: the ego-constituting synthesis and the "ontifying" world-constituting synthesis. Neither ego nor world is primal; both are constituted in the absolute stream. This is the doctrine of the C-manuscripts, and it relativizes the ego-pole as a constituted unity.

(287) This opposition is so to speak prefigured already in original passivity through the separation of hyletic and noetic (function). If we oppose ego and non-ego, world, as two spheres of being, that can only mean that I can judge consistently about the world, experiencing and thinking "naturally oriented," in the manner of knowledge of true being; and on the other hand, that I can judge consistently about my transcendental being and judge everything at all levels in it as sense (as existing, valid) and so about the world as transcendentally constituted sense of being, as it is valid to me and confirms itself under corrections, correctable again and confirmable again. The "idea" of the world as true in itself is then a transcendental formation constituted by me at a higher level and made explicit, perhaps clarified in its kind of evidence. Transcendental subjectivity then encompasses the totality of everything subjective, which finally includes the world itself as subjectively constituted, so that all truth of being gained straightforwardly for the world appears again here in a changed manner of consideration of the formation. The hyle/noesis distinction in passivity prefigures ego/world. "Two spheres of being" means only two consistent directions of judgment, natural and transcendental. The transcendental sphere includes the world as constituted sense, so all worldly truth reappears in it under a changed regard: the formula of transcendental idealism as including, not opposing, realism.

(288) Can we not then oppose in a good sense the existing world to the whole of the transcendental-subjective in which it is constituted as unity of validity? The opposition is legitimate as correlation, not as separation.

(288) For every level of ontic constitution of being we then have the corresponding distinction between the ontic itself in its own being and the constitution achieving sense of being as the transcendental-subjective that is its correlate. The transcendental subjectivity that is being-constituting in ego-acts is then correlate of this ontic sphere, and to it belongs the ego of the capacities formed, its kinaestheses. But there are not many egos side by side; there is only the one transcendental ego and one ego of capacities, which can contain in itself coherently unitary egos of capacities, as at the worldly level the relatively closed scientific ego, family ego. Correlation holds at each level; the egos of particular capacities (the scientist, the father) are sub-unities within the one ego, not plural egos.

(288–289) So this correlation runs through all levels of ontification, objectivation, reification, "thingification." In the manner of "constitution" one must distinguish between ego, transcendentally taken, and "thing," "world" (to which in lower levels the "world" of oculomotor data and similar "merely subjective" worlds belong, finally also the primordial as still "merely subjective"). In acquiring its habitualities, its capacities, the ego does not present itself, does not perspectivize. The ego's self-constitution differs from thing-constitution: it does not appear in perspectives. This is the reason the ego is not a thing even when constituted.

(289) But the human is himself a world-object; he is mundanized ego, and yet ego. The ego-structure and with it its constituting life, the appearances themselves and the relative onta, in its whole transcendental fullness, this whole mundanizes itself; that is, transcendental subjectivity embodies itself. Mundanization of the whole transcendental subjectivity, its whole constituting life included, occurs through embodiment.

(289) How can embodiment achieve this, or how can transcendental subjectivity, which in embodiment performs a particular achievement belonging to its own essence, embody itself, and so have this embodying achievement embodied too? Does that not lead to an infinite regress? The regress: if embodiment is an achievement of transcendental subjectivity, and transcendental subjectivity is embodied, then the embodying achievement is itself embodied, and so on.

(289) In a certain way yes. But first, every objectivation concerns the constitution of a particular sense of being, a particular identity-pole identifiable again and again in the life of transcendental subjectivity, always habitually ready. Husserl concedes the regress, but limits it: each objectivation constitutes a particular identity-pole, so the regress concerns iterated objectivations, not a vicious infinity.

(289) The ego here is the counter-pole of the rays of direction with their whereto, their goal-pole; in the change of goals, the identical "objects," all the rays have their starting point, their from-me. So the ego, the identical pole from which all directing goes out as my directing, and this pole is necessarily not object, at which the direction goes, during the directing. The ego-pole is necessarily non-objective while it functions, the "anonymity" of the functioning ego (Krisis § 54).

(289) The world is always there, a universe of the there, there for me, first perceptually. It is the universe of what I am directed at and can be directed at variously, in the spatio-temporal horizon the universe of spatio-temporal objectivity, perhaps itself becoming unitarily objective. I am the identical ego-pole, but not identified by an identifying directed at it, which would mean an objective pole. That I am as this pole I know subsequently through a certain methodical reflection, which is already objectifying but not yet mundanizing. This ego as counter-pole of the world is the transcendental. The precise distinction: reflection objectifies the ego-pole without mundanizing it. The transcendental ego is the counter-pole of the world, known by reflection but not a world-object.

(289) I as world-having, world-directed ego, actually directed and able in egoic potentiality to direct myself from the actual through the habitual system of directions to the anticipations of the world-horizon, can direct myself or am directed at external things, at this and that, and I can direct myself or be directed at my body. At a thing I am directed by looking through familiar kinaesthetic paths and further modes of appearance motivated by them at the appearing unities, the things. Direction at a thing passes through kinaesthetic paths.

(290–291) If I direct myself at my body, it is likewise constituted as a body. But in every presentation of a thing it is constituted and experienced as involved. Every kinaesthetic process of presentation of a thing, every touching, seeing experiencing, runs parallel and together with bodily movements of my (corresponding) members under rest or motion of my whole body: hand and finger movements, arm movements, eye movements, bending forward, walking. But these bodily movements in space have at the same time the character of egoic processes, movements set in play, done by me as ego striving and doing.
