The same "I move," the same kinaesthesis, which occurs in "I experience a thing," say in "I see it," is the egoic moving of the eye; in touching experience it is touching kinaesthesis and means for the hand a spatial motion as egoic motion. A specifically egoic, an "I do," is thus localized in the eye, the hand, in its spatial position of rest and motion, both fused in the form "I move my eyes." Further, the eye has, in it "lies," the "capacity" to traverse a certain manifold of spatial positions, a certain system of movements, in the "function" of seeing. That concerns both eyes together; they have a coordination of movements, are one in this function in every temporal phase of seeing, an "I see here and there," spatially a simultaneous motion of two eyes. So in the various bodily members of the bodily body both "capacities" of motion and actual kinaestheses are localized in the current motion, whenever I set these movements or rather kinaestheses in play in the function of this or that perception, these members then functioning as perceptual organs and their bodily moving then necessarily having the layer of kinaestheses localized in it. Kinaesthesis is localized in the organ: the "I move" is felt in the eye, the hand. Capacities too are localized: the eye "has" its range of movements. This localization of the ego's doing in the body is the core of embodiment.

(291) More belongs to the eye. The seeing of the eye is not merely performance of the kinaestheses in which the eye's being directed is experienced; if the eye is open (with its own localized kinaesthesis of opening and closing), it sees; with closed eyes it sees nothing. I see a thing, a whole visual field of things. But I do not always see the same; even continuously seeing the same thing I have a different "image." Objectively, bodily speaking according to the bodily-objective sense of experience, I say I now see this part of the surface (this "side") of the thing, now that, now at this distance from my bodily body, now at that. But all the manifold contained in bodily spatial motion and change gives itself in seeing in the form of changing "aspects," "views" ("images," as one also says in a dangerous way of speaking). If I close the eye they disappear, though objectively everything goes on as it is and may be experienced through other senses and their "images." The changing course of these aspects, these visual appearances of things and processes, is regulated by my kinaesthetic moving of the eyes, but also by my other bodily movements, likewise as egoic movements accompanied by kinaestheses. These movements or rests correspond to movements and rests in the spatial field such that a correlation exists: changes of aspect with objective rest can be produced in the kinaesthetic system in the same kind and sequence as objective movements otherwise appear in aspects. Aspects are subjective appearances regulated by kinaestheses. Husserl warns against calling them "images," the picture theory of perception rejected since Logical Investigations V § 11.

(291) We thus distinguish these aspects as merely subjective from the seen things; they are the things in the how of their visual modes of appearance. They are not experienced as localized in or on the eye, since they do not always belong to the eye, whether it functions perceiving bodies or not, as the kinaestheses do, at least normally, since it is abnormal for the eye to be moved mechanically without egoic participation. The belonging of changes of aspect to the eyes as bodies in space and as seeing spatial bodies is experienced as a causality. Bodies, which are even when not seen, in their motions and qualitative changes in space-time, must be in a certain spatial position to the eye to be seen, and there must be daylight; on the eye's side too, it must be open, must move kinaesthetically in certain ways for visual acquaintance, pass into certain distinguished positions, be accommodated (by its own kinaesthesis of accommodation into an optimum), have its normal, healthy structure. The thing affects the body and especially the eye in a stimulus process whose closer properties one learns by experience, by research into the bodily conditions of seeing on both sides; as a result of this affection the egoic processes in the body and especially in the eye and other co-functioning members are triggered and set in course by the ego, and there takes place subjectively, as causal consequence, the course of the seeing apperception of the appearances of things in their how, a course in which progressive seeing and visual getting-to-know come about. The interweaving of bodily events of our world of experience with bodily events of the always co-experienced body and the subjective processes localized in it or regularly related to it leads to psychophysical experience. All "outer" experience is interwoven in psychophysical experience. Aspects are not localized in the eye, but their dependence on the eye is experienced as causal: the thing stimulates the eye, and the aspect follows. Psychophysical causality is thus a constituted, experienced structure, the phenomenological basis of physiological psychology. "All outer experience is interwoven in psychophysical experience" is the thesis of Ideas II § 18.

(292–293) The eye is affected by illuminated objects; it "senses" them, I sense them in the eye, though no differentiated localization of the various simultaneous images and of the whole image field occurs. On the other hand, haptic experiences are so related to the surface, or chiefly the surface, of the body that it is a unified field of localization of all contacts and all tactile data have in their difference their different localization. Nor is the "auditory field" related in this way to a part of the body, the ear, but indifferently unitarily to the ear "in" which I hear. Localization differs by sense: touch localizes each datum on the body's surface; vision and hearing are related to the organ as a whole without differentiated localization. This is the asymmetry of Ideas II § 37 between touch and sight in the constitution of the body.

(293) It is something different to experience the body as body and to experience a bodily sensation. Sensation is nothing bodily in the sense of the experience of corporeality. Not the body senses, but I sense; I am affected by the body of an external object existing for me in such a way that the sensation is given localized on or in the body, at this or that place. The body has the uniqueness that when it is physically touched I am at the same time affected, and that when I set kinaestheses in play, bodily movements run together in the spatial-bodily sphere of experience such that they coincide in every phase with phases of the kinaestheses and so are organ movements in the mode "I move," "I actually act on 'nature,' moving it immediately in my members and perhaps thereby acting mediately on the rest of the spatial-bodily world." This can happen in a mediate continuous "I move," "I act," "I strike with the stick," "I push the cart," or I may have further consequences of my doing as practical goal, or find them occurring as unintended consequences. "Not the body senses, but I sense": sensation is the ego's affection localized on the body. The body is the place where physical contact is at once affection of the ego, and where kinaesthesis is at once physical movement. Action extends mediately through tools (stick, cart).

(293) I am, as ego that has an objective spatio-temporally real world from experience, a world in which a quite distinguished object, my body, is bodily contained, in which all my kinaestheses as functioning for experience and mediately through them all my experiences themselves, the manifold sensuous appearances, are localized. Again all affections by external things and by the bodily body itself are related to my body, as "on" which I am affected, and all my active reactions, all my actions have the form of events that have their primal site in the body, which I move kinaesthetically, push with, and from there act on into the rest of the world. All action is two-sided, and the original two-sidedness is that given in the body in full bodily experience, that of my ruling in the body, my moving it. The body is the "primal site" (Urstätte) of all affection and action; all action is two-sided, inner and outer, with the body as the original two-sidedness.

(293–294) It is not only the spatial-bodily world (primordially) and I with my changing experiences and whatever else belongs to me, the appearances as such in which I experience world, the kinaestheses and capacities belonging to them as those I exercise and can exercise; there are not only spatial-bodily horizons, horizons of natural objects existing "in themselves," through experiencing constitution and apperception, with affections running from them to me as ego-centre, determining me to actions in this world; but in this world a body is also constituted as body, as my body, "my" in the emphatic sense. This contrast is not to anticipate that a world could be constituted for me without a body being constituted in it as my body, but only that this stands at first as a possibility to be considered. A body being constituted as my body implies that first everything belonging to the ego as world-constituting is in a wide sense localized in this body, and the ego itself as affected, experiencing, kinaesthetically bringing experiences (the subjective objects in their how) into course, observing, in every way practical, is localized in this body; its ruling is constituted as ruling in it, it itself is constituted as bodily existing, as living bodily. And not only partly. For everything that makes up ego-being and ego-life is an inseparable unity; everything found by phenomenological disclosure has, with the constitution of the bodily ego, mediately gained its relation of localization to the body. The localization of the ego in the body is total: everything egoic, mediately, is localized. Husserl leaves open the "possibility to be considered" of a world without my body, the "non-worldly ego" of the title.

(294–295) The world is pre-given as a world in which I am bodily and am as primordial human, that is, ruling in this body, sensing in it, suffering pleasure and pain, actively moving in it, acting through it on the environment. Only one body can be constituted in my world. I am unique as ego; I cannot be there twice; I cannot have a double in my primordial world. Ruling in and by means of the body, this subjective ruling with everything subjective is primordially experienced, and in the uniqueness essentially belonging to the ego. A body cannot be there that experiences merely visually, and another tactually, a third hearing, and all this as my experiencing. For to the constitution of spatial corporeality in general (and first non-bodily) belongs that all these sensibilities are interwoven, and must be, so that in all the one and same thing or the one and same world can be experienced, to which everything experienced belongs. In the restriction of primordiality, in which the world is abstractively stripped of its sense of intersubjective world for all, the possibility must however remain open that a world could be constituted without my, the constituting one's, being in the world. That implies, of course, that by abstraction I have so to speak blinded myself to the layers of my transcendental being and the modifications in them in which empathy arises and in which empathy as constitutive function has always already performed its achievement. Two results close the text. The uniqueness of my body follows from the uniqueness of the ego and from the interweaving of senses required for one world: no distribution of senses over several bodies. And the "non-worldly ego" is possible only as an abstraction: in primordiality one cannot exclude a world without my body, but only because the abstraction blinds me to the layers in which empathy has always already worked. The suggestion is that embodiment in the world is itself bound up with intersubjectivity: my body as a thing among things is, in full concretion, my body as seen by others.

### Appendix XVIII. The Way the Lived Body Constitutes Itself as Physical Body and Lived Body, and the Ways Its Constitution and the Constitution of External Things Are Intertwined (Probably September 1931)

(295) The body is constituted optically through perspectives and kinaestheses of the first functional level and through touching kinaestheses such that it is connected with kinaestheses and sensation-data in a double way: once through the constitutive function of presentation, and secondly through an association, lying in quite another direction, of what is presented with kinaestheses and sensation-data. The hand appears optically in perspectival fashion, haptically perhaps as touched by the other hand, but at the same time so that the kinaestheses we call its own appear localized in it. The thesis of the appendix: kinaestheses and sensations relate to the body twice, as presenting it (like any thing) and as localized in it (unlike any thing).

(295–296) So the body as a whole is constituted through double kinaesthetic functions, or in a double way by the total system of kinaestheses. On one side the kinaesthetic systems function for the manifold of presentations in which the body presents itself as a body similarly to an external body (both at the level before "walking"); on the other, the way the kinaestheses constantly divide in functioning and at the same time combine brings it about that not only the objectifying kinaesthesis for a member G1 (the touched hand) is connected with its manifold presentations, but at the same time a counter-member presents itself (the touching hand, eo ipso apprehended as touchable by the touched counter-hand), so that always and necessarily counter-kinaestheses and counter-manifolds are there together. A member, say a hand, is objectified only so that other members are there as objectifying, which are themselves objectified by means of those they objectify. The body is so constituted that its members are experienced as spatial bodies, presenting themselves like external things in perceptual appearances. But in every member its kinaesthesis is given "localized" in a certain way together with its spatial position (in orientation). The body's self-constitution is reciprocal: every objectified member is objectified by another that is in turn objectifiable by it. Kinaesthesis is localized in the member together with its spatial position.

(296) In the current perceptual appearance the member has its presenting data, but it itself, as presented and appearing, has haptic data at every point of its surface and so as a whole. The haptic total appearance of the body as total body-phantom, unity of all its members (determined by the unitary body dividing into parts to each of which particular kinaestheses belong), has the unitary field of tactile sensation localized on its surface. This field is so localized, but not truly experienced as spatially extended. The tactile field is localized on the body's surface but is not itself a spatial extension: localization without extension, the "aesthesiological" layer of Ideas II § 36.

(296) Precisely in this ontological structure belonging to the body as body, in this layering of spatial surface-phantom and localized sensation-field and localized, hence distributed, kinaestheses, is grounded the privilege of the body's spatial experience. The kinaesthetic system is one with the tactile field, which is articulated so that particular kinaestheses, simple and complex, belong to its parts. To the piece "fingertip A" of the tactile field belongs a particular kinaesthesis and group of combined kinaestheses; their achievement is that this piece A can be brought to "coincidence" with every piece of the rest of the tactile surface, "laid on" it. The field is field and invariant as to its form (its immanent form of coexistence, two-dimensional, surface-like), while its filling, its constantly emerging data, change. The tactile field has an immanent two-dimensional form, and the kinaestheses make every part able to coincide with every other: the fingertip can touch any part of the body.

(296) The unitary field of tactile sensation divides into partial fields through the kinaestheses related to them, which take them into touching function. Partial fields are defined by kinaesthetic function.

(296–297) Separated parts of the tactile field, lying discontinuously apart in its extension, can enter a kind of continuity in presenting function, presenting adjoining pieces of an objective extension or, functioning together in a unitary touching process, constituting one and the same surface. If we touch with several fingers at once, without playing their special kinaestheses against each other, touching in one kinaesthesis, it is as if we had one finger and touched back and forth with it. Discontinuous pieces of the sensation field present a continuous object surface: the objective continuity is not the field's continuity.

#### "Double Sensation": the Peculiarity of the Tactile Field That Distant Parts of It Can "Touch," "Coincide" with One Another, in Kinaesthetic Situations, Freely Producible

(297) In every touching, with whatever kinaesthetic apparatus, and the corresponding course of kinaesthetically motivated presentations in which a bodily member is experienced tactually, to every experienced surface moment of this member is coordinated, as constantly running along, an emerging moment in the tactile field, or to the course of the gradually and continuously synthesized surface presentation a course of emergences in the tactile field, shifting place in its continuous mutual externality. At the same time to this course belongs a course of tactile data "coinciding" with it, those of the functioning touching organ (the touching fingertips of the other hand). The tactile field has (unlike the visual field) the basic peculiarity that all its data are mutually external, yet two data and their places can coincide despite their externality; they fuse in a way but do not really mix. The localities remain separate, and to pass continuously from one to the other one must take the paths prefigured in the local field. Locally different data "touch," "coincide," without covering each other. That is a phenomenologically quite unique occurrence, not to be confused with the covering of visual shadows, which as shadows are no longer sensation-data. Haptic data, mutually external without contact, come into contact through the kinaesthetic processes in which the body constitutes itself originally-haptically as touching itself from member to member. The contact of data is objectified as spatial contact of the bodily members and at the same time as kinaesthetic self-touching, producing this contact as continuous touching. Double sensation is analysed as an immanent structure of the tactile field: two separate locations in it coincide when one part touches another. This is unique to touch; visual overlapping concerns appearances, not data. The coincidence is objectified twice: as spatial contact of members and as kinaesthetic self-touching. Merleau-Ponty's "reversibility" of the touching and touched hand draws on this analysis, which he knew from the Ideas II manuscripts.

#### Localization of the Tactile Field on the Surface of the Bodily Body

(297) As I touch the right hand with an organ and have it given not merely as a body like an external body but necessarily in associative unity with the extensive mutual externality of the emerging data of the tactile field, the presented surface receives the layer of these data localized in it. A piece of the tactile field, of its extension, belongs to this surface, belongs to the hand as its sensation field, point for point, and remains the same through change of haptic qualification (whether I press more or less). Likewise for every member of my body treated by touch. But one must constantly remember that what we already find so objectified is first constituted as objectified by the sense-giving of experience. As concrete experience by which I always experience the familiar body as body, it gives it this sense through the well-practised capacity of mutual touching, whereby the whole sensation field is distributed in localization over the "members" of the bodily body in their phantom constitution and thereby over the whole body. Localization is constituted by association: the touched surface acquires the data as its layer, point for point. The distribution of the sensation field over the members is an achievement of practised mutual touching.

(298) In this localization the sensation-data are thought as localizing themselves, laying themselves on the touched surfaces by association. But in touching we have double data, each with its field extension and position in "coincidence." Why are both not localized on the surface of the touched member; why are the data functioning in touching not localized on the touched body surface? The answer: the data laid on in the second layer with touching are transient, not belonging to what is touched once and for all as the first do. One may touch now with this organ, now with that. With whichever, the touched has its piece of the field as belonging to it, only the qualifying filling is not exactly identical. But familiar for every manner of touching with this or that organ (perhaps mediately with gloved or ungloved hand or even with a stick) is how the same piece of field would vary with similar data. Of the two coinciding data, only the touched member's is localized on it, since it belongs to that member constantly, while the touching data change with the touching organ.

(298–299) Every organ is constituted tactually on one side through being touched, and through the kinaestheses functioning in it, but on the other side is itself constituted as actually or possibly touching, so that always and necessarily in the most original tactile experience, which yields the body as body and as lived body, we find a functional togetherness of touching and touched organ, with the always possible reversal that the touched can become the touching. As soon as this reversal occurs, the function of the pairs of sensations, always coinciding, reverses too; what was localized on the surface of the touched organ functions now in touching for the counter-organ, and what functioned in it is now localized on its partner. Thus, as soon as the capacity is formed by which the body is constituted as spatial phantom in the unity of touching experience, it is constituted with the whole haptic sensation field localized on its surface and with a relation of the total kinaesthesis of possible touching in fixed distribution over the parts of the haptic field, which are localized in bodily parts as members. Thereby the partial kinaestheses of possible touching are distributed over the bodily members; each is constituted not only as touchable but as touching, as a member with kinaestheses that can be set going freely. As soon as it functions so, sensation-data function in presenting apprehension as touching; but the same data, when the member is touched or thought possibly touched, count as localized in it. Reversibility of touching and touched reverses the function of the data: the same data are presenting (touching) or localized (touched) according to the member's role. The body is thus constituted as both touchable and touching in every member.

(299) Once this full constitution is reached, it becomes intelligible that the body, without being looked at or touched (in the dark, where it is not seen at all), while functioning exclusively for haptic constitution of external things, is constantly quite vividly conscious and can be made conscious in its haptic intuitiveness: the constantly filled tactile field, traversable in itself, and the kinaestheses firmly distributed over it constantly indicate (appresent) its tactile "look." The body is always co-present through its filled tactile field, which appresents its tactile shape even when it is not perceived.

(299) In all sensuous experience, first visual, some hapsis is involved, and my body as originally haptically constituted is involved, in space as a body and yet not seriously a body like external bodies, at rest and in motion, in change and unchange, and yet not seriously as other bodies rest and move and change, not receiving its essence so from the most original experience of spatial existence in rest and motion. The body too can be "pushed" and "push." But its pushing and being pushed are double in sense, double-layered. So pushing as body it can only push itself, and so it can only be pushed by itself, as when the hand pushes, strikes, shoves the foot. Now it is peculiar: a thing can be pushed away by it, whether rigid or with mobile parts; it can, while its parts move relative to the whole, be pushed away with these movements. But the body, pushing itself, cannot push itself away as a whole; it can push itself bodily only as a thing pushes itself, by one of its parts pushing another. Husserl's note: pushing is an example of bodily acting. To the uniqueness of the body as that in which I rule belongs that it is the only thing in and through which I act and through which external objects can take on, in their being as persisting and changing, the second layer of sense. This layer is inessential to them as external bodies; they can be and move without being pushed by me; their being is not in itself two-layered. I cannot push myself away, primordially, as if my bodily body could still be without me and I could remain the pusher without body. The body cannot push itself away as a whole: self-action is always action of part on part. The note draws the consequence: external things are one-layered (their being does not depend on my acting on them), my body two-layered; the "I cannot push myself away" excludes any separability of ego and body in primordiality.

(299) All these are evidently essential properties of bodily corporeality as against external corporeality. Connected with this is the remarkable fact that every connection of an external body with a bodily member gives it the properties of an extension of this member, though not an extension by a new "member," a new organ, since it cannot receive a new kinaesthesis. The grasping member extends bodily, and the kinaesthesis belongs to it in this extension; so it now handles as extended organ. The extending body thus gains a share in the sense "organ." Thereby something like mediate touching (with a stick) and feeling, pushing becomes possible. The tool as extended organ: it shares in the sense "organ" without becoming a new member, since it gets no kinaesthesis of its own.

(299) The primordially constituted world is therefore not homogeneous, since it consists of the unique body and the external world. Homogeneity holds only as to corporeality and includes the body in this respect, which is constituted in one layer as res extensa too. But what constitutes it as organ of the ego and inseparable from it is not an addition the bodily body happens to have and other bodies happen to lack. For only through the body's functioning as organ can external bodies constitute themselves as spatially existing with bodily properties, and the body itself as a body, by being a unified manifold of organs that can function reciprocally. Husserl's note refers to p. 309. Bodilyness is not an accidental addition but the condition of all body-constitution: things are bodies only through the organ-body, and the body is a body only as a manifold of mutually functioning organs.

(300) Going back to the original constitution of external things and of the body as thing and as organ, one recognizes that things are not experienceable without a body, but that necessarily, together and correlatively, bodily things and the body, double-layered and in one layer as a body, must be constituted together. Herein lies the first stage of the grounding of the proposition that a world of mere nature, a world without animal beings, is unthinkable. The co-constitution of things and lived body grounds the thesis of No. 1 § 10: nature without animals is unthinkable. Husserl presents this as the "first stage" of the proof.

(300) The members of the body can be experienced optically as moved like other things without being experienced as touched, and conversely. But they are also experienced as moved while neither touched nor touching, namely in the kinaesthetic "I move." They are then experienced as spatially moved by me, and yet not in an externally practical way through "pushing," which presupposes contact (we also say "from within," or kinaesthetically moved). Three modes in which a member's motion is experienced: seen, touched, and kinaesthetically "from within."

(300) And in combination: my hand is experienced haptically as spatially moved through contact (the other hand touches it); I see it move and experience it, "perceive" it as moved, but without its being touched; or it is that too, and then I perceive it as moved, but not as kinaesthetically and thereby locally moved by the hand touching it or other organs. The combinations distinguish being moved by another member from moving oneself. The heading names the practical self-relation of the body.

#### The Lived Body Constituted as Practical in Relation to Itself, Moving One Organ with Another Practically as Its External Body

(300–301) Again: the hand can be locally unmoved but experienced as moved by the other hand, in an external practice of that hand. The body is experienced as articulated; every member moves itself from me in a way in which it is not moved externally-practically by me through contact (in a push), namely by my moving another member "inwardly," with which I move the first member by contact. So every member can be moved externally-practically by me by my moving another member "from within" that touches it. Herein lies that one member can touch the other, a spatial touching. But at the same time I move the one member from within "against the other," and with external contact the one is at once inwardly touching the other. Every touching kinaesthetic doing, every hapsis of a thing, is at the same time experienced as inner movement of a member that touches the thing, in a double sense, since together externally, spatially-bodily, it is a contact of the bodily thing member with the external thing as body. Where bodily organs are experienced haptically, we have mutual contact of a touching and a touched organ in this double sense of contact. Each member can be moved "from within" or "from without" (by another member moved from within). Every contact is double: inner kinaesthetic touching and external spatial contact.

(301) The body taken as a whole is experienced or experienceable haptically through a totally performed or performable contact, and then of course, since it is a nexus of organs, through touching its organs by touching organs. But no such organ can itself touch everything. Indeed the body as unity of contact does not refer to a particular touching organ; each has its greater or smaller range of touching the body, and different organs touching the body can touch the same part of it. Conversely, the way the different manifolds of presentation come to synthesis makes identity of the presented object constituted in them, and through mutual extension and complementation the one total body is constituted in relation to all these possible combinations. The whole body is constituted by the synthesis of partial touchings by various organs, each with limited reach.

(301) In this haptic constitution a special unitary total kinaesthesis is constituted by successive synthesis, realized from the total system of kinaesthetic capacities: the surface relief, built from the partial pieces of the surface shape (the relief) of the body. The body's surface shape is constituted kinaesthetically as a relief.

(301) This kinaesthetically constituted surface form is form of a content co-constituted at all "form-points" (to each of which a momentary kinaesthetic situation corresponds): the qualifying matter of this form, as with external things touched and felt by me, the qualification being nothing but the current quality of contact sensation, entering a successive fusion in temporal sequence but apperceived as "consequent" of the kinaestheses. Form and matter of the felt surface: the shape is kinaesthetic, the qualification is the contact sensation, apprehended as the "consequent" (Nachsatz) of the kinaesthetic "if."

(301) But with the body we have the difference between the perceptual quality of the bodily body, and in this sense the quale of the touched point, line or surface piece, and the perceptual givenness of the contact sensation felt at every touched place of the body when touching. And in continuous progress we have the haptic sensation field, appearing localized on the body constituted as spatial-bodily surface, coinciding with this surface, however it may deform as body surface and however the haptic sensations may vary. With the body the touched quality (as property) and the felt sensation (as localized) are distinguished, though they coincide.

(302) In the change of organs touching my body it turns out that the same contact data apprehended by the touching organ in the apperceptive function of its and the other kinaestheses as matter of the touched body surface, and so attributed to the body as body, belong, when conversely the touching becomes the touched organ, to the localized sensation sphere. For example, when I touch my forehead with my hand, moving it over the forehead, and then, moving forehead or head suitably under contact, touch the touching hand or fingertips, and experience exactly the same series of sensations once as surface determinations of the forehead (or of the hand) and the other time as contact sensations localized on the forehead surface (or on the fingertips). The forehead example: identical sensation series are once apprehended as properties of the touched surface, once as sensations localized in it, depending on direction of attention and function.

(302) Finally I must mention explicitly the peculiarity that the total body can never function as touching such that its whole haptic sensation sphere could function as touching-feeling in relation to itself. The constitution of the body as res extensa is possible only through its articulation into organs and through the contact of one organ, one bodily part, by another. Here we have the remarkable double sensation in self-touching, whose peculiarity is that two data of the same field can coincide in their own way of "touching," while separated in the continuity of the field's locality. This coincidence of two tactile data in touching is a phenomenon of its own in the tactile sensation sphere. Careful consideration is also required for the distribution of kinaestheses over the organs, the connection of organs into one organ by connection of kinaestheses. The total body cannot touch itself as a whole; its constitution as res extensa requires articulation into organs. Double sensation is the specific phenomenon of this articulation.

(302) We must come to terms constitutively with all this and also consider that every external body is given haptically in perception only through contact and, as soon as contact is released, can be there only in the haptic horizon. This release can take the form of release from me, without pressure or push, and with push, shove; in the latter case, it is said, the external body moves as a result. Haptic presence of external things depends on contact; released, they remain only in the horizon.

(302–303) To haptic experience of motion belongs that I can set in play a suitable kinaesthesis in the familiar form of coming again to the same body, establish renewed contact, or, as in shoving, stay with the body "overcoming its resistance" and under a kinaesthesis of going along, which, in case the external body was already moving, differs from the one originally required. Haptic motion is constituted through re-establishing or maintaining contact by going along.

(303) As for my body, we have ample motion in it, originally haptically experienced and experienceable motion. But as unitary total body it is not likewise experienceable as moved. A motion (or rest) is called "originally haptically experienced" when it is haptically constituted by continuous touching contact, under "going along" modification of the kinaesthesis during the body's change of place, a non-going-along kinaesthesis, characterizing rest, being one that can be repeated again and again with arbitrary holding still at every moment without breaking contact. Husserl's note: we must distinguish two kinds of motion and rest: rest that is absolute motionlessness, and rest that is relative in that motions occur in the body, as rotation or in single mobile parts, while it "stays in its place" as a whole, like a fixed windmill whose sails turn. With such objects (small ones) we can stay haptically and return repeatedly to the same parts in their persisting existence; only in particulars must the kinaestheses function along, without our needing a kinaesthesis of going along to stay with the object at all. Haptic rest and motion are defined by the kinaesthesis needed to keep contact. The windmill exemplifies relative rest: the whole stays, parts move. My body as a whole resembles the windmill.

(303–304) Such experience of motion I have of every member moved, for example in perceptual function, also of a member moved by a push from outside (not from me), but not of my total body. Here this experience is unthinkable. So, like originally experienceable motion, its counterpart is lacking, originally experienceable rest in the normal sense of a rest that can pass into motion or has passed into it. Instead of this rest we have here a privation of motion (speaking within original experience of motion), which has in common with rest that haptic experiences of my body can always and continuously remain in play, without possibility that I could lose my body from the reach of my hapsis. I need not touch myself; but I am constantly in the reach of my possible touching, in another way than things that are lastingly there, like the things on this desk while I do not touch them; they need not be constantly there in the reach of my being able to find them again by touch. My body is always and necessarily there, whatever else is temporarily there and then not, in the living present field of my immediate grasps. The body is always there; even when we exclude visual and other sensuous experience abstractively or really, it is in a certain way perceptually there. The constantly perceptually conscious sensation field in its constant attentively-perceptual traversability constantly appresents the capacity of touching, of my own body and together of external things. For the kinaesthetic system in some momentarily actual kinaesthetic situation is also conscious originaliter. Further, in my haptic sensation field I always have, as ego that has a world, emergences which in my kinaesthetic situation, held still or subjectively changed, even when I touch nothing (sitting on a chair), already have the perceptual sense of contacts with bodies that can be touched at will from there. Husserl's note: passive being touched, by a garment or the chair, is contact in a kinaesthetic situation and in a still living horizon of experiential validity, in which the current kinaesthetic position is associatively conscious; this passivity has an associated capacity, and I can at any time pass into the active attitude, and contact thereby already has touching function. At the same time contact and kinaesthetic situation appresent my body as haptically present, as present to the other senses too, even when these senses are silent, so to speak. The whole body's rest is a "privation of motion," not rest proper. The body is "always and necessarily there," in a way no thing is, by the constantly filled tactile field and the constant consciousness of kinaesthetic situation. Passive contacts (clothes, chair) carry the sense of touchable bodies and appresent my body, a phenomenology of the background body-schema.

(304) A kinaesthesis that would first have to make my body experienceable to me is unthinkable. This appresentation of my body together with the at least haptic presentation of external contacts is a moment of original world-experience and belongs to it at every moment in which it is world-experience in whatever form. The body is not made experienceable by any kinaesthesis; it is always already appresented in every world-experience.

(304) This investigation of the being-involved of the body in all, and first original, external experience (to which belongs that the body must always be haptically perceived, but also that things must be haptically perceived together with the body) needs some supplementation. The paragraph announces supplements about activity and passivity in touch.

(304) Within our present preference for haptic experience, the following must be said: original constitutively perceiving experience is that in active touching. Within this kinaesthetic activity I can "stand still," then go on. But I can also pass into a quite different activity, active perception of other things, discontinuing my previous activity while contact remains. Contact can persist when activity ceases.

(304–305) Here one must keep in mind that the manner of "not doing" in holding still and standing still in a kinaesthetic constellation is quite different from real non-activity. For I am still in activity, still in a mode of being occupied with something as ego, still directed striving, though in the mode "lingering." But if I let my matter go, turn to another, if my occupation with the one has ceased altogether, then touching it and being touched by it is so in an essentially different sense, no longer being directed at it, being in active striving (in a mode of will). The equivocation has good grounds, however. For active contact with its horizon of capacity as active horizon of possibilities of doing has passed into the mode of passive apperception, mere association, as the sensation-datum (contact sensation) is "conscious" in originally associative temporalization, far from all real activity. If I become attentive, if this thing touched-touching in the background "affects" me, this affection is already an egoic mode that breaks through pure association (which is the sphere of "egolessness," the non-wakefulness of the ego, for what is and goes on in the "passive background" or underground of active, or better, affective-active ego-life). Holding still within an activity differs from having let the thing go. Passive contact belongs to the sphere of association, "egolessness," the non-waking ego; affection breaks through it into egoic life. Husserl's term "egolessness" (Ichlosigkeit) for the associative underground anticipates the late analyses of the pre-egoic.

(305) It is also clear that as soon as I turn, affected by a contact, and so pass into the mode of activity, inactive contact has become active. When I have become attentive to the chair on which I sit, that is not a continuing "touching," but still a mode of active tactile perception, a touching while I do not stir in place. Attention converts passive contact into active touching even without movement.

(305) With this the double sense of perception is connected: active perception, which alone originally constitutes sense of being, and passive perception, "background" perception. Two concepts of perception, the active one constituting being, the passive one in the background.

(305) As for the body, it is constantly there, since it is always already constituted, and constantly perceived in one sense or another, perceptually there. But it is constituted as body and with the haptic sensation layer quite originally belonging to it and localized in the surface (the aesthesiological layer) and with the kinaesthetic capacities belonging to its members as organs and combinable with one another, or with the actual kinaesthetic constellations of the single moments. These layers appresent one another, similarly as for an external body the optical is appresented with the haptic and conversely. So the body is constantly perceived, whether merely aesthesiologically, without being actualized as perceived through mutual touching, or also by touching. For body-perception the capacity of touching oneself is the founding one; the aesthesiological is bodily only by being constituted as localized on the surface, whose sense of being is therefore founding. But the haptic sense of being is also founding for the optical, which can be realized alone while merely appresenting the haptic. Finally every objectifying presentation, as an occurrence in the immanent, primal-phenomenal sphere, is presentation by means of appresentation, a core of what is properly-originally given of the object owing this sense of original presence of the object only to appresentation. The unity of presented and appresented is then the perceptual being-presented of the object itself. As regards the spatio-temporal world, original objectivation is nothing isolated; always and necessarily object in presenting givenness and body in presenting givenness are together and inseparably connected. A general thesis: every presentation is presentation by means of appresentation; the "original" core has its sense of presence only through appresentation. Body and object are always co-presented. The founding order: self-touching founds the aesthesiological layer; the haptic founds the optical.

(306–307) With every immediately haptically perceived object the touching body is eo ipso appresented from the contact, so also mediately from an optical mode of appearance, which as far appearance refers to near appearances and from there by appresentation to possible contact "by the body." The reference of far appearances in the far-thingly optical unity "carriage turning in the distance" and "presenting itself continuously otherwise according to my standpoint and motion" to the near thing in the corresponding mode ("if I stood immediately before it") is on one side association and apperception by analogy, going from the continuous change of far appearances to the corresponding one of near appearances. Here in factual perception the actually running appearance appresents the whole system of possible appearances and, expectantly, a next further course according to the kinaesthesis now set going. Appresentation runs from the current visual data and the kinaesthetic constellations occurring immanently with them, since the manifoldly founded system of capacity is already instituted, of being able, in "if" and "so," by means of kinaestheses to be set in play, to produce as consequents the corresponding continuous modifications and so the concordant synthesis through all of them. So the current total sensation-datum is not experienced as itself but functions as "presentation of," as appearance (perspectivization of the first, lowest level) of the originally first, "optimal" near thing; and functioning so, this synthetic unity presenting itself in it from one side is experienced "through" it. This side appresents its co-sides. In kinaesthetic traversal of the side-modifications the stock of primary originality of presentation of the current side and that of appresentation always differ, but in the traversing synthesis the same is perceived, the same as it looks from different sides, the same surface thing. But in the new modification of distance the near thing changes continuously into the far thing, or conversely the far thing into the near thing again. The latter is the "primal mode" over against the former as "intentional modification." What does that mean here? The far thing is apperceived by virtue of similarity as an analogue of the near thing, but it is not a near thing (not a diminished horse), that is, it is not seen as one. A horse in its typology of givenness has in the type of nearness (especially the most perfect, haptic immediate graspability) the same type of "size" and a typology of possible or spontaneous (receding motion) distancing in various directions. That within the sphere of living perception. Every perceived thing appresents the touching body. The far thing is an intentional modification of the near thing, apperceived by analogy but not as a small near thing (not a "diminished horse"). Near is the primal mode; far is its modification. The structure is the same as the relation of empathy to self-perception.

(307) The immediate apperception of the far thing by analogy is of the same kind as an immediate apperception of a near thing, which, if we presuppose that it is the earlier in itself, so that its apperception as near thing already guides, also transfers its whole appresentation. The near thing is characterized by appresenting immediate touchability and tangibility, or by the actually appearing side appresenting the totality of sides; then also by the properly appearing side appresenting touchability, and, if contact already occurs, this functions as haptic presentation and as co-belonging, so that in unity the visual thing is the same as the compresent haptic, both as higher-level presentations of the same thing. Through these appresentations in their connection the "small" far thing must also appresent the haptically associated "thing," hence be apprehended as near thing, as long as it is really optically similarly experienced. That concerns the things of relative nearness, which can be converted by one or a few steps into "things of graspable nearness." The far thing inherits the near thing's appresentations, including touchability, as long as it looks similar.

(307) The child that has already built up the constitution of graspable nearness grasps for the farther thing that has appearingly receded perhaps from graspable nearness, showing appearance-changes continuous with and similar to those occurring within that narrowest nearness. But grasping for it, it is disappointed. Yet it "learns" in the course of experience (as constitutive building) that through a certain perspectival enlargement in running there or being carried there graspability is restored. It learns the typology of things in nearness, in touchability and graspability, and with it the typology of near-perspective with its near-size and that of far-perspectivization, and so learns to understand rest and motion appearing optically in the manner of distance as convertible by active perspectivization of approach (or by being carried, driven there) into a corresponding individual type of "the same" thing as near thing with its essentially peculiar haptic and other layers of sense. The infant reaching for the moon: the genetic constitution of distance through disappointment and learning that approach restores graspability.

(307–308) This dangerous talk of the child's learning must be understood rightly. The experiencing gaze is guided by the sense of unity and being "near thing," directed at it, and holds it fast in continuous perspectivization, even when touchability, graspability, practical manipulability are crossed out and the receding thing as actual full thing has become illusion. It has not become nothing; it is still and to a certain extent confirmable as "phantom," identifiable and confirmable in the change of appearances and in looking away and back. But in walking (or being carried around) the higher-level constitution takes place, the formation of experience of approach effected by new kinaesthetic functions and of reconversion into a full near thing; the new connections of if and so form, the distinction of the same thing in the type of near-perspective, in the way corresponding to the acquired individual type horse, carriage, in the modes belonging to this type: the same thing above right, below left, so far, very far, nearer, related to the "antecedents" of new style becoming familiar. What presents itself perspectivally here is the thing in the present modes, optical thing of graspable nearness and optical thing in this or that far orientation. This has, in its sense-giving experience of higher level, the sense of an intentional unity that passes, by setting in play the distancing kinaesthesis, into the intentional unity near thing as optically so characterized, but also as haptically immediately graspable and practically immediately manipulable. Thus the unity of the thing is constituted as unity of its manifold appearances of higher level, and these appearances are the distance-things in an extended sense connected by continuous synthetic transition of identification, which are themselves synthetic unities, relative "things" of a lower level. "Learning" is constitutive building, not empirical acquisition: the receding thing is first a crossed-out near thing, a mere phantom; walking constitutes the higher unity of near and far things. Each distance-appearance is itself a relative "thing" of a lower level: the thing is a unity of unities.

(308) But this universe of constituting appearances, of ontic experiences (modes of the thing in the how of orientation), are not appearances of fully homogeneous structure. They stand in a system of capacity in which they all refer to one another, but so that all distance refers to nearness in a privileged way as what properly gives the thing itself; or that all distancing is distancing from nearness, a de-nearing (Entnahung), which remains appearance of the thing itself only by having the sense of the possible returning approach, from me by walking, or from the thing itself by approaching motion. So the near thing refers to possibilities and capacities of distancing, but thereby always refers back to itself as the primal state, the primal mode that modifies itself intentionally. Only so does the thing have in all modes of appearance a haptic layer of sense, which properly and originally belongs only to the primal mode of nearness, and in connection with the haptic the practical layer as that of the subjective causality of intervention in the environment, on the other side the layer of causality of self-left nature and in general the natural causality proper to things themselves. All distancing is "de-nearing," the coinage the Earth-ark text will use. Nearness is the primal mode, and the haptic, practical and causal layers belong to it properly and to far modes only by reference. Heidegger's "de-severance" (Ent-fernung, Being and Time § 23) inverts the same structure; Husserl's term goes the other way.

(309) Finally the following must be emphasized: since all sense of being of external things is related back, as to all ontic, oriented modes of givenness, to the near sphere of touchability and graspability, of practical immediate capacity of pushing, shoving, all external things (always in primordiality, within my own original experience) are eo ipso related back to my touching body. It is appresented in every conceivable perception of things and world. But while appresentation of every level under the title thing and external world belongs to a closed system of apperception and appresentation in which unity as spatial-thingly, spatio-temporal external world is constituted, closed therefore, my body is appresented through this whole unitarily constituted world, which is my primordial world of experience, as centre of all possible near things, as body functioning in all experiencing and in all thingly action and again experience of action. But it is not merely the nearest thing around which all other things group themselves appearingly by near and far, for it is body, and therein lies what separates it radically from all external things. Not as if, beyond being a thing, and in primordial experiential givenness a thing in the contingent and yet never to be changed modality of near thing, it had also taken on and had a remarkable new layer of sense of the specifically bodily, localized in it, from its particular constitution. Strictly, the near mode of appearance has, if we let the proper sense of this expression count, no sense for the body, since nearness is nothing without distance, as distance nothing without nearness. This correlation belongs to the external thing, the originally proper thing, as long as we have not clarified a new constitutive level that yields the fuller sense of world as world of experience, or of experience as experience of a world, a world comprising external things and body together as mundane realities and giving the body the experiential sense of body and yet of a body as good as others, its external bodies, a homogenization of the world into a universe of beings, which equates all beings at least in that all are homogeneous as spatial bodies, only that the body has a layer of determination which no other body can have by its sense. So in primordiality. Husserl's note refers to p. 297. The body is not the "nearest thing," since "near" has sense only with "far," which the body lacks. Nor is it a thing with an added layer. The homogenized world, in which the body is a body like others plus an extra layer, is a later constitutive level; Husserl leaves open here whether it is primordial or requires others.

(309) Once intersubjectivity and the intersubjective world are constituted, this homogenized world extends into a world of bodies with essential properties all bodies can have, and humans (animals) distinguished by "souls," almost as if it were only a particular class of things, a particular type with particular class properties, like wood and iron. But one will have to follow the constitutive sense-giving at this level too and thereby learn to see that the again originally sense-giving world of experience, the world of my and our streaming perception, is constituted from our near world. The way this near world comes to its constitutive sense leads back to my primordial constitution and the near-far world constituted in it, my primordial external world, which appresents my body and me "in" it as ruling; as conversely this body, as soon as it is constituted for me as that in which I rule and inseparable from it, constantly appresents its near-far world as the one in which I rule mediately through my body and my original ruling in it. The intersubjective world treats humans "almost" as a class of things with souls, like wood and iron with their properties: the naturalistic apperception. Its constitutive sense leads back to the near world and finally to the primordial body-world correlation, where body and near-far world appresent each other.

(310–311) Haptically I come up, touching, against a surface thing analogous to my bodily surface thing, so as near thing in originality. I see it. My body I have only partially visible, limited by my kinaesthesis of perceptual seeing. The limitation is an inhibition. In stretching out the hand to touch I am also inhibited and so have limited distance in the haptic field. (Husserl's note: is a stretching reaching a little further than I see not really thinkable? The limit of force is not so determined that it must necessarily be seen.) That is overcome constitutively by the kinaesthesis of walking. For the body that is impossible. I touch my back or my head. I can also touch another thing with a quite similar kinaesthesis, say the back of the chair or a thing similar to the head; it indicates to me the visual image when I turn round and touch the thing before me: the normal position, the normal central field of perception, the near world before me, the sphere in which things are at once touchable and graspable but also visible, and the body in the normal position (note: note well!). In it the body also has its domain of visibility together with graspability. The perceptual world before me is totally a front side and has its correlate in the "front side of my body" as normal. The things before me appresent their other sides as possible, kinaesthetically motivated front sides. My body is optically eternally a front-side correlate in actual perception. The visible members are constitutively similar to near visual and grasp things as to surfaceness. If I had a correspondingly long neck, I could see myself on all sides. The analogy makes me "represent" my body too as visible. One could say I represent my unseen side appresentingly (if I take others into account) as others see me externally from their place. But supposing so, how do I represent my tactile back as visual with the help of others? By a synthesis of my tactile back, belonging to me originally, with the touching seen by the other but understood by him as original touching belonging to me, one would have to say. That is hard to understand. Is it really so that I represent my body externally and another along with it, or that I represent it without others as if I saw myself from a there, which would again amount to representing myself as if I were another? But in any case I have no other in play representationally. The "normal position" is the frontal field, where seeing and grasping coincide; the perceptual world is "totally a front side," correlated with the front of my body. My visual self-representation is an analogical extension. Husserl tests the hypothesis that it derives from others' view of me and finds it hard to understand; the alternative, seeing myself from a "there," would be representing myself as another. He concludes that no other is in play representationally, leaving open whether one is implicit.

(311) Analogical apperception of my body in its totality as visible, like a thing before me. The appresentation from the tactile field also contributes. Of course, if I follow the analogizing clarifyingly, I come upon the inhibition and invisibility. The question is what nevertheless creates validity, real appresentation. Restated: the analogical visual self-apperception is valid despite inhibition; what grounds its validity?

(311) In walking this analogical apperception continues to work. No perspectivization of my visual body-phenomenon (as normal front-side phenomenon with back-side appresentation). On the other hand the whole external perceptual field perspectivizes optically and "extends" haptically; ever new things are touched and immediately touchable. I, my body, am immediately with ever new things. A near thing can, while I walk, also remain constantly the same near phenomenon; then it moves as a thing. But my walking can also stop; then it perspectivizes or loses its contact. If I go on walking, I have meanwhile the same phenomenon of my body as of a thing so moved. But is that in the least sufficient? In the sense of this phenomenon of motion lies not mere remaining unchanged but its perspectivizing in dependence on my walking otherwise or not walking. The carried thing and my body show the same phenomenon during walking, but motion's sense includes the possibility of perspectivizing, which my body lacks. The analogy is insufficient.

(311–312) My body can come everywhere. It is a constant near object, or analogue of a near object, which however breaks the analogy by having no capacity of haptic and visual distancing with corresponding modalities of perspectivization, but can come into contact with every other object that appears visually perspectivally or is emptily appresented haptically as there. A thing can move toward another until external contact; it can come nearer and farther externally; their relative positions change, and external approach finally leads, as constant possibility, to contact. Every resting thing has its place; every other can come to its place and take it if it makes room. Every phenomenon of approach and contact appresents a possible near phenomenon. My being with and touching a thing is phenomenally analogous to that of two things. Already in my near field, every thing in it I do not touch I can touch, and that looks just as when one thing comes into contact with another in this sphere. Through walking I come to every place. External space, the external world, is already constituted from nearness precisely by my being able to realize every distance as nearness, as where I am touching. But this being-with is also my spatial being-with; I am spatially in the near sphere like another near thing, though body, and thereby unique. My walking brings my spatial being to every spatial place; I thereby perform change of place of my body in space, just as another thing changes its place, moves. Other things of course have motion-appearances my body does not permit. Others must refer back optically through perspectivization to the near things appearing from afar as ones I could realize by going there. My body is always in the "there," needs no apparent leading back to the there. I have my unique being precisely by constituting my spatiality through myself and my various kinaesthetic co-organs, while other things are constituted through me and my organs. In the different mode of constitution different things are constituted, but qua spatial object in the system of places of space the same; my body is body in space, my walking change of place, motion. The answer: my body is spatial through contact. My touching a thing is "phenomenally analogous" to two things touching. Hence my body, which can touch every place, occupies places as things do. Its uniqueness lies in constituting its spatiality through itself, but as a spatial object it is one among others. The text thus reaches, by the route of contact rather than of carrying (Appendix XVII), the same result.

(312) If I move bodily in space under continuous contact with an external thing, what possibilities are there? If I touch the thing constantly at the same place of its surface, and in change at different places, or touch these while walking and find the old places again repeatedly at will, I have realized it constantly as near thing during my walking, and it moves itself together with my motion. If I can return to the same places only by walking, and again and again in such returning, then the thing rests, but I, my body, am on it and move on it (Husserl's note: that won't do!). If I do not walk myself, only moving members, under constant contact with an external object that is unmoved, I rest standing on it if it remains in contact with me in all kinaesthetic movements I perform bodily while holding the walking-kinaesthesis still; or my body as a whole rests not standing but otherwise (sitting, lying on) if the kinaesthesis of rising, which initiates walking, is still open. The attempt to define motion "on" a resting thing through contact and return, which Husserl immediately rejects ("that won't do!"), and definitions of standing, sitting, lying as forms of rest on a thing.

(312) Of single members it is said that they rest on a thing when all its own kinaesthetic movements do not disturb contact in its persistence, as my hand with the arm resting on the table. If an object in my field is at rest or in motion, I can stand on it so that I now rest on it. Standing is a walking movement from a stand in which one rests on one thing to another in which one rests on another (perhaps on both at once). Standing is a "stand" on a thing; walking is passage from one stand to another, the analysis of locomotion as successive resting.

(313) Nothing else is considered here than occurrences for spatial things as phantoms, not occurrences of resistance and all phenomena of specifically bodily and merely bodily causality (the thing breaking when one stands on it). By standing on a moved thing (which keeps its previous motion and its wholeness), I take part in its motion; I am moved, and if I move myself, this motion as bodily motion becomes a relative one, a moved motion itself. The analysis is restricted to phantoms; causality is excluded. Standing on a moving thing makes me moved, and my own movement becomes relative.

(313) The question is what constitutive significance my being moved has for the constitution of space, whether for being moved to gain sense for me, or my motion in space to gain the sense of a motion I can perform kinaesthetically walking or also by climbing onto something moved and perhaps causally by being thrown, a fundamentally new constitution is needed, which is also needed for a homogeneous world of bodies to be constituted, into which I am ordered as body, equal in this to other bodies. The question of Appendix XVII is posed: is being moved a new constitutive level needed for homogeneous space?

(313) If my body is constituted as body in the world of bodies, another body can be apperceived as similar to the body of my lived body, similar in its individual typology of bodily comportment, which is the bodily side of my body for its concrete-bodily, "psychophysical" comportment. The appendix ends where No. 18 begins: once my body is a body among bodies, pairing with a similar body becomes possible, and its comportment can be apprehended as the bodily side of a psychophysical comportment.

### No. 18. How Does the Mere Bodily Similarity of an External Body with My Lived Body Ground a Modification That Transcends Primordiality? The Thoroughgoing Two-Sidedness of the Constituted World. Nature and Spirit. The World Humanized in the Widest Sense (1, 2, and 3 September 1931)

(314) The analogizing of an external body with my bodily body, of its articulations and its bodily comportment in these members with those of my body, in which this comportment is two-sided and the "expression" of an egoic ruling. The opening note states the datum: analogy between external body and my body, whose comportment is two-sided.

(314) The body there has a typical behaviour like my bodily body, as if I were there and, being bodily there and ruling, set these movements in play as ego in egoic ruling. I do not experience the other as a repetition of my ego existing there, or a similarity-variant of my ego such as I can perform on myself by re-imagining myself. Such variants always yield only myself in my own various thinkabilities. For I cannot be repeated, not even as a possibility of myself, and yet I experience him as similar to me, as if I were there. But first I must have the other, as actual or possible experience and together with me, to be able to pair and compare my bodily being and his. The other is not a repetition or variant of me: self-variation yields only my own possibilities. Yet pairing of the two bodily beings presupposes that the other is already had. The circle is stated plainly.

(314–315) But the question is how I "come to the other," how mere bodily similarity grounds a novel modification in which the appresentation by which the sense "other" arises becomes meaningful. The body there, as it is for me primordial body there, does not receive a new layer of determination as primordial, as a body of the primordial world can show new properties in the course of experience, which are then prefigured as possibilities in the form of corporeality and primordial objectivity. Here this world is to be transcended, so that this body, by virtue of its similarity with my bodily body, becomes the starting point for a modification in which a primordiality is presentified and yet confirmable that is not the actually original one, mine. The other body does not acquire a new primordial property; it becomes the starting point of a modification transcending primordiality. The heading that follows takes up assimilation in general.

#### Assimilation in General and Constitution Through Appearances

(315) Two bodies appearing in different aspects and changes of aspect, bodies in plurality from the outset in "pairing," coinciding in the most general, one near, the other far, but general constitutive kinship, back-reference to the near sphere. One appears large, the other small; the circular shape of one in one perspective, of the other in another, likewise colour perspective, so equality and yet different look of the equal. But they are recognized as equal without further ado. Pairing of things across different aspects: equal things look different yet are recognized as equal, through their common reference to the near sphere.

(315) How does association work? It is not merely images that "associate" in pairing; they awaken, under their kinaesthetic situations, constitutive courses toward the optima, not all, but the style is familiar, and with the sense the paths to the optimum are prefigured. That is an important problem. Association pairs not images but constitutive systems: each appearance awakens its paths to the optimum.

(315) How then the analogy of the body there with my body, the pairing? The modes of appearance are at first very different, the whole constitutive systems very different. Optical assimilation: the body there experienced optically and only optically. Haptic assimilation: another body beside me grasped, touched. My body constantly, but very differently experienced. First bodily. The difficulty: the constitutive systems of my body and the other body are very different.

(315) First, my body is constituted (in part) appearingly, immediately like another thing, through self-touching, through seeing, visual perspectivization, though limited. The first mode of my body's givenness: like a thing, but limited.

(315) Second, indirectly from the egoic side, through contact, through kinaestheses functioning for the perception of other things, appresenting the capacities of appearing self-experience. The second mode: indirectly, through the kinaestheses functioning for things, which appresent my possible self-appearance.

(316) Third, the body at the same time experienced as site of ego-subjectivity, ego, ego-ruling, kinaestheses and kinaesthetically running appearances localized in it in various ways. Everything subjective related to it, centred in it: the human ego. The third mode: the body as site of subjectivity.

(316) If the pairing with the body there comes about... The sentence breaks off, and Husserl resumes the next day (2 September 1931).

(316) How does an appearance awaken its system? How does one appearing objective thing associate with another through the mediation of the appearances on both sides? The question restated for objects generally: association between objects is mediated by their appearances.

(316) How does a layer of constitution (layer of experience) of one "transfer" to the other associated with it by virtue of the founding layer? Can, where the validity of the transfer, confirmation, is excluded, the impossibility of the anticipated experience give rise to a modified experience, an appresentation that is confirmable not by presentation but always only by such modification? The decisive question: can impossibility of confirmation by presentation turn the transfer into a modified experience, confirmable only by further modifications? This is the answer Husserl is working toward: empathy is a mode of experience whose confirmation is always itself modified.

(316–317) Constitution of the primordial world of experience through association and through the activity of the ego, which is affected by associatively constituted unities of emergence (primal sphere of temporalization of pre-objects), reacts through turning toward, active grasp (active proper intention, being directed at something), the activation, the striving toward, running in the form of kinaesthetic striving processes related to optima. Formation of a mastery through practice over the continuous modification of the datum (the pre-object) in the modifications of kinaesthetic striving courses and of all emerging data of a field together. Transfer and confirmation: every content at once apperceived in the turning toward, which already has a practised and prefigured kinaesthetic form. The kinaesthetically formed intention terminates in an optimum of modifications. Constitution of an optimal central sphere to which all modifications lead back kinaesthetically-materially. Husserl's note refers to the analysis of this primal constitution in his manuscripts marked "Ha" (probably Hapsis), now D 12 in the Husserl Archive, titled "Constitution of nature, sensuous world, lived body," from which No. 16 and Appendix XVIII come. Primordial constitution: association yields pre-objects; affection, turning toward, kinaesthetic striving toward optima; practice yields mastery. Intention is kinaesthetically formed and terminates in an optimum. The vocabulary of "pre-objects" (Vorgegenstände) and of kinaesthesis as striving anticipates Appendix XIX.

(317) So similarly at ever new levels. Pairing, pluralization at the lowest level of pure passivity. Pairing at every level: the constituted "things" pair, or are experienced unitarily, temporalized unitarily as coexistent pluralities in a coexistent all-unity, yielding in thingly time of succession an open totality, which in every phase presents itself through a finite perceptual field. But the self-constituting world is spatio-temporal, in every phase of succession of the form of open infinite spatial world (all-unity of the coexistent). Pairing is a universal form of passive synthesis at every level: plurality is constituted as pairing.

(317) The world as world of my primordially reduced experience constituted as associative-active (from egoic intentionality of striving directedness) plurality, all-unity of persisting single things. These themselves unities in plurality. Temporalization is unification and pluralization in one. Temporalization unifies and pluralizes: every unity is constituted in a plurality.

(317) Constitution has two levels. The two levels of world-constitution follow.

(317) First, the experiencing constitution of world-nature, taken universally, the unitary constitution of the world as world of spatio-temporally persisting "things," realities. All experience in the ordinary sense is ordered into this universal achievement, achievement of the ego and egoic activity on the ground of primal passivity. All particular experience is thing-experience or a component of it, a moment co-functioning for thing-constitution and, as particular experience, co-functioning in the inseparable connections of pluralizing world-temporalization. The first level is nature: all experience is ultimately thing-experience.

(317) Second, the humanized real-practical world, in which humans are mundanized and everything worldly has human sense. The second level is the humanized world.

(317) If every activity is called practical, every active result a practical formation, then experience is a practice. But practical in the ordinary sense is only that activity which "intervenes" in the world, modifies its changes by egoic activity (change and unchange taken together!). Experience is practice in a wide sense; practice proper intervenes in the world's changes.

(318) The activity of the experiencing constitution of the world as spatio-temporal real nature is constantly going on, so far as world is experienced, and the world is constantly valid in the constituted sense; only, constituted as nature, it constantly modifies the particular determinacy of its sense of being, since continuing experience also means constantly new achievement in the universal endless unitary achievement.
