Since the world is constituted as nature, universe of realities in change (so in one: change and unchange), more exactly in change of place and qualitative change of persisting realities, change is always constituted. The activity of this constitution is real experience. Experiencing the world is a constant activity whose correlate is a world of changing realities.

(318–319) Practical intervention in the world is an active achievement that modifies real change, existing change constituted by experiential achievement and, in the case of immediate practice, actually experienced change; linguistically we can also say changes it, say rest into motion, an ongoing motion into a differently formed one, uniform into non-uniform. But however much change of changes is a real occurrence and stands under rules of real causality, it is something new that an activity builds itself on the already experiencing one, which modifies changes of whatever experiential form egoically, so that the change is not merely real change, though always also that, but takes on a second, egoic layer in which in every phase and as a whole it has the character of something striven for and effected by the ego. Motion, change (or rest, unchange) now receives a practical sense, constituted not by mere natural experience but by real practical activity on the basis of experiencing (natural) constitution. Husserl's note: since a real object is an individual-typical invariant of its spatio-temporal changes, the ego intervening in the world changes the real object itself, and doing so according to purposes, purposively, it not only changes it purposively, but the reshaped objects now have for it lastingly (until further notice) a spiritual meaning referring back to man. Practical intervention gives change a second, egoic layer: the change is effected. Reshaped objects acquire lasting "spiritual" meaning referring to their maker: the origin of cultural objects in practice.

(319) A nature-practical event is constituted originally in two separable levels. The two levels of practical action follow.

(319) First, in "I push" I perform bodily an expenditure of force that moves the bodily member beyond mere touching experience, so not merely "touching," while I still touch. "As a consequence" something new occurs that does not occur in experiencing behaviour of the member: a modification of the state of change of what is touched. In egoically free repetition I experience that my "pushing" has such consequences, and I learn them by practice. Pushing is touching plus force; its consequence (the thing moves) is learned by repetition.

(319) Second, my practical intention directs itself at such consequences, or each time at a certain consequence, and now the practical extends intentionally to it: my pushing movement of the hand unites with the resulting external process into a unity: by my pushing in the hand I push the thing, and unitarily: I push the thing, which already implies the hand-pushing and the continuing aiming at the resulting event. Husserl's note: both interpenetrate peculiarly in the body. The movement of the hand (in the experiencing intention of touching or the practical one of shoving an object) I see, directed at it in the seeing intention, experiencing it visually actively by moving my eyes, which is not itself actively experienced. But seeing the touching hand in its movement is not merely seeing as of a stone rolling off. I continuously "experience" at the same time the activity of touching of the hand, of feeling the thing it touches, or the shoving of the thing whose size and type already tell me (its apperception) that I must strain and brace myself to move it further. The unitary experience of the organ has a side of natural experience and a second, "spiritual" side, and I can activate one, perform it in proper perception spiritually, and again the other. Problems of the constitution of this unity, body, organ. The second level: the intention extends through the hand to the thing, so "I push the thing" is one action. The note: seeing my hand push is not seeing a stone roll; I experience the effort, and the thing's apperceived type tells me how much force is needed. The organ has a natural and a "spiritual" side, each activatable by attention.

(319) Pushing is an exemplary and generalizable expression for shoving, lifting, and first, per the first level, for practically endowing a bodily member with force, with practical force (which, moving in function, moves itself from my exertion of force). The heading "Resistance" follows.

#### Resistance

(319–320) This has its correlate in the resistance of the real, the body. In the world of experience different bodies behave very differently in that, to gain from me practically the same change of their same modes of change, they require very different expenditures of force. Equal bodies, experienced in equal mode of change, have equal resistance; they require the same expenditures. More exactly, since different members, and the same functioning differently, can push the same thing, we must add: with equal practice of pushing the results with equal things are equal; they resist equally. And again that does not suffice: equal things can be in different causal states, in different connections (circumstances) of causal conditioning. Things in equal causal situation, we must evidently add, but also the relation of resisting me as practical ego, and of practical pushing, to the "inert resistance" of things as things in mere causal behaviour toward one another, or to pushing and being pushed in the equivocal "mechanical" sense, spoken of as a causal process belonging to things as nature themselves, whether I intervene practically and so in their modes of change or, since these are causal, in the causality of things, or not. Whether I do or not, the world of experience is real-causal in that the changes in whose course realities persist do not have a regulation concerning isolated realities under the title of this persistence. The realities, these unities of persistence, are dependent on one another in this or in the regular course of their changes. But they are also dependent on the possible and actual practice of the ego as embodied, as intervening in the real world through bodily exertion of force, through subjective pushing. Resistance is the practical correlate of force, and it is lawful: equal things in equal causal situations resist equally. The equivocation of "pushing" (practical and mechanical) is the crux: subjective resistance and physical inertia are correlated. The real world depends both on internal causality and on embodied practice.

(320–321) According to the sense of the world of experience as such (before all theory), my body, or I as human, separates from the external world, and within the latter self-left mere nature outside my body, in which real changes run in real-causal dependence, separates from the external world practically determined from me. The former, the world experienced under my practical passivity (I can remain passive), has a familiar habitual style, since already in the lower layer of bodily nature the same happens under the same circumstances. Of course the unusual, the accidental also occurs, in the primordially reduced world already because the practice of others has fallen to the primordial reduction. Husserl's notes: practical passivity fully taken cannot exist if the world is to be a world of possible experience; even the activity perceiving nature is practical with respect to my body, impractical with respect to the rest of nature, in the most original experience, according to which my organ movements in "mere" perceptual function are not experienced as included in the causality of external nature; that is a lower constitutive level. And a long note judges the preceding text "probably not cleanly carried out": starting from the constituted world, it is through and through and essentially at once nature-world and spirit-world, in all concretions, though differently. It is transcendentally constituted at all levels as "ego-alien" nature, non-ego, which yet as constituted from the ego has its spirituality, its ego-relatedness, its egoic sense, only this is not represented in constituted nature, in its ontic sense. All its constitutive (that fits) features, which make up the content, the proper sense of nature, are unspiritual, contain nothing of the ego-relatedness through which they are constituted. At the lowest levels the constituting ego is so to speak anonymous in the world, and its spiritual side is not itself objectively constituted. But finally we have man, already at the primordial level, in the world and the world humanized, spiritualized, but as carrying sense from the human ego. In this level of completion the world of experience, taken according to its experiential sense, is in its universal lower layer nature, all-nature; in the upper layer it is endowed with sense referring to humans as ego-subjects. Humans themselves are bodily mere nature in the nexus of all-nature. All bodily changes, all changes of members of human (and then animal) bodies are, if one abstracts from the psyche embodied in human existence, from everything "ego" and egoic, mechanical, natural motions and other natural changes founded on them. The idea of a "self-left" nature, not moved by ego-subjects, can be constructed as a fictive possibility: the world as nature as it would run if all ego-subjects refrained from every intervention, even that in their perceiving movements. By the constituted sense of nature it would run "of itself" in mechanical motions and natural changes. Natural science operates with this possibility in presupposing theoretically that nature is completely determined in purely natural attitude, and in applied physics that all events can be foreseen and calculated by natural-causal analysis and pure natural laws. But the right of this idealization lies at bottom in the fact that concrete nature, as given in experience, shows, for constitutive reasons, an empirical-typical separation of humanly-practically effected processes and processes running of themselves. That is an empirical style which relativizes itself in the extension of scientific experience, itself a further constitution of the world: the deeper causal analysis penetrates, the further this type recedes; the perceiving hand movement pushes air particles aside, changes the distribution of heat in the surrounding space, which leads to idealizing approximation. The main text separates self-left nature from practically determined nature. The long note corrects it: the world is through and through both nature and spirit; nature's constituted features are "unspiritual" because the constituting ego is "anonymous" in them. Self-left nature, the presupposition of determinism, is a fictive construction whose right lies in the empirical distinction between effected and spontaneous processes, a distinction that recedes as causal analysis deepens, so that determinism is an idealizing approximation. This is the Krisis analysis of Galilean idealization in compact form.

(321) But I intervene in this style. Now my body too, and in it the pushing hand, is a body, and the thingly pushing of this body is constituted as a thingly causal process of motion that has causal consequences like that of another body. (Husserl's note: in the progress of experience, of constitutive achievement lying in the essence of experience in general, all-nature constitutes itself in progressively stricter sense of being, so that even merely perceiving movement, which originally does nothing to external things, is joined with them into the unity of a real-causal whole of nature.) So abstracting from "I push" we have the constantly valid and unbroken causality of all-nature including my bodily body. But the universal unity of this real causality as a universal style of change to which all real changes of things in the world are bound, however much it must be preserved as style, still has an ambiguity, leaves possibilities open: I can intervene by pushing at any time, and within this general style something happens that would not have happened before; my hand gains physically a kinetic energy it would not have had without my egoic doing. The compatibility of freedom and causality: nature's causal style is "ambiguous," leaving open possibilities that my intervention realizes without breaking the style. My hand acquires kinetic energy it would not otherwise have had, yet the process is physical. Husserl's position is a phenomenological compatibilism.

(322) The world as co-constituted by egoic practice: the resistance of a thing, subjectively seen, is the property belonging to it of always requiring the same subjective pushing force in the same state of change to enter a certain new state of change, more exactly a state of motion. Resistance defined subjectively: the force a thing requires to change its motion, the practical origin of the concept of mass.

(322–323) The same change of states of motion can occur without my intervention through some external causality, a causality of the type of mechanically pushing motions. Insofar subjective force has an equivalent in physical impact force, and the object's subjectively related resistance its equivalent in the "inert resistance" it has in extra-practical existence. However, that changes nothing in the fact that the world itself, on the basis of practical constitution, has a practical sense, beginning with the resistance belonging to what exists, or better the property of a certain resistibility belonging to it. For from there it has a coating of sense in that mechanical modes of motion as occurring in nature from impractical experience can also have practical sense from practical experience. That in the impractically constituted nature. It is and remains nature; it does not change its style, stemming from original experiential constitution, nor its sense as nature, but it receives and always has (since practice always comes into question) a layer of practical sense. And that goes further. Practical motions can function as themselves or for the sake of certain lasting purposes, certain end results, and so persisting objects, persisting in the real sense, take on a further layer of practical meaning as purposive objects, persisting because they embody in themselves, at any time understandably, a lasting purpose valid lastingly to the ego. The persistence lies in the habituality of the purposive will, and there are, as is known, mediacies and many types. Subjective force and physical force are equivalent; nature nonetheless carries a practical "coating of sense." Purposive objects persist as embodying a lasting purpose, grounded in the habituality of will: the constitution of cultural objects (tools, works) from practice.

(323) Supplementing (3 September 1931): the body is constituted in the world as physical body, moving and resting physically in it, so interwoven in this respect in general physical causality. But it is constituted as body, as that in which my ego "embodies" itself, in which I have my organ, my total organ with its various special organs. These are constituted two-sidedly. As perceptual organs they function in physical rest and motion as egoic, capable, moving kinaesthetically from me. In coincidence with the layer of sense of corporeality they have in this respect an egoic layer of sense, a layer of being at my disposal, functioning for me, here of perceiving things, external things, but also the bodily (an organ functioning for perception of others). The organ's egoic layer: being at my disposal.

(323–325) I perform these two-sided, egoic-physical movements on the basis of affections. The perceived things are experienced (so the world is constituted for me at all times) as able to affect me and as actually affecting me in perception. Husserl's note: affection belongs to the constituted world itself; it presupposes subjectively, for every subject that has world in wakefulness, that it is constituted for itself as human and has the habitualities by which objects are there for it with the sense of lastingly accessible acquisitions. Herein the world of experience has an egoic sense belonging to its constitutive essence, which puts the current real in its real sense in "relation," or rather in connection, with my ego constituted as embodied ego. The relation is not a subsequent connection or subsequent result of relating, as if the real things first were and then were related. Rather correlatively and always together, bodies are there as perceived and perceivable, as existing in actual and possible affection, and the two-sidedness they thereby have is correlative to the two-sidedness of the bodily and at once egoically functioning body; and as egoic relatedness the affection and then the egoic reaction of perceiving, doing egoic behaviour are constituted two-sidedly, since to both there belongs on the bodily side a parallel style of bodily connections, causalities, "to be investigated more closely" (note: in higher developed experiential constitution, to every spiritual affection and action corresponds a causal-real process running parallel between my bodily body and external bodies). As to affection, the ego is affected, but together the bodily body is experienced as localization of the affects issuing from the affecting object and occurring experientially in the ego, so to speak. The body is the place of sensibilities, or as I also say, of sensings (Empfindnisse); they appear on and in it more or less differentiated, locally distributed, localized, partly quasi-extended. So far as the ego is constituted embodied as human ego, as human together with the bodily body, the whole realm of the subjective, first the modes of appearance of things in their constituting change, is localized, related to the spatial place of the bodily body and its bodily, spatially extensive unity and its changes of place, a relation again constantly there, a correlation always pre-given in world-experience. The world's affectivity is part of its constitutive essence: things are "able to affect me." The relation of world to embodied ego is not added to pre-existing things. Two-sidedness runs through world, body, affection and reaction, each with a parallel physical causality (the psychophysical parallel "to be investigated"). "Empfindnisse" (sensings) is the term of Ideas II § 36.

(325) If we consider the world as world of experience, mine, of the primordial ego, of the primordial human, this considered as body and as ego that "animates" the body (exclusively according to the sense of being it has from experience), we must say it has in inseparable correlation bodily sense and "spiritual" sense; it is in a certain way everywhere, through and through "psychophysical" world, already when we merely look at experience as experience of nature. The world is for me only what it is as unity of actual and possible experience, of the manifold synthetic courses, actual and capable. As such it is in constant living "relation" to me, to my life, to which these appearances, syntheses, kinaesthetic activities and capacities belong. And these are co-localized in my body, have their particular relations to my perceptual organs, to the perceptually functioning bodily movements, these being always constituted two-sidedly, as bodily and as my activities in their intentional directedness at the realizing formation of the unities. But my body, as that "in" which I "live," rule perceptually and otherwise, my body persisting as body but also with the fixed sense belonging to it from the process of ruling doing running in a fixed style of capacity and always horizonally prefigured for it, is psychophysical in a distinguished and unique sense. And likewise I, as continuously one with the bodily body in egoic ruling, am human, unite under the title ego everything egoic of capacity, of life in activity and affectivity, but also embodied, shown localized in the continuously experienced bodily being of the body. Everything else bodily, the "external world," is as unity of kinaesthetically capable manifold constantly "related" to me as ego, so as I am concretely constituted, to me as human, to my functioning body, my functioning organs. The world is "through and through psychophysical": every natural object is a unity of possible experiences localized in my body. The body is psychophysical in a "distinguished and unique" sense, as the place of ruling.

(325) In the direction toward bodies we experience, grasping, only bodily things; in the correlative direction, passing over to the manifolds, these are given in a certain way together with the body and so in a certain way localized in it. But they are inseparable from the ego and the totality of its constituting life, which in this totality is one with the unique body, firmly and inseparably related to it. In a certain way the ego as experiencing and able to experience is related to everything worldly and belongs to all in one, is present everywhere, but precisely thereby by no means belonging in a particular way to "localization," except for its body, in whose perceiving ruling all kinaesthetic activities are immediately one with the bodily movements of members and thereby united with the manifolds of appearance in which bodies are perceived and perceivable; manifolds which, belonging to the body and this bodily mode of functioning, make me present everywhere as experiencing ego. The ego is "present everywhere" as experiencer, yet localized only in its body. Ubiquity through experience and localization through embodiment are two sides of the same structure.

(326–327) If I am thus localized in this particular and unique way with my bodily body, it is for me, like the real external world and things of the total world in general, a unity of actual and possible "external" experience, of actual and possible constitutive manifolds of appearance, constituted together, ordered into the total system of appearance of the bodily world. The spatial-bodily experience of the body, with its manifolds of courses of appearance and kinaestheses, is like all such experience related back again to the body as functioning, so that the perception of the bodily and of the whole body already presupposes the body, hence psychophysical experience. In the perception of my body the perceived is related back to the already perceivingly functioning body; the bodily body is already presupposed as experiencing body, in which ruling I am to experience it as bodily object and do. If I perceive my hand, it is perceived, consciously, through the seeing of the eyes, through kinaesthetically functioning eye movements and other bodily movements (bending forward), or through touching with another member (the other hand, the lips). The actual experience of these members presupposed as functioning again presupposes experientially existing bodiliness. So the body presents the difficulty that its experience always presupposes it. In actual experience, in which we are occupied experiencing with the world, this makes no difficulty. The world is always already there for us, certainty of being as acquisition of previous actual experience and of a horizon of capable experience constantly projected with it. And every return to earlier experience shows the same picture. World was always already certain to us, and now progressing perceptually means occupying oneself with the already certain world, certain with unknown horizons, realizing the contentually undetermined capacities and so the undetermined in the existent, in single things, in what is anticipated indeterminately as co-existing; it means actively engaging with the pre-given world, being occupied with it in a certain way, bringing what is pre-meant, undetermined in it, to knowledge in the "itself," in perceptual realization of pre-meant being. The circularity of body-perception: perceiving my body presupposes the functioning body. Husserl dissolves the difficulty for natural experience by the "always already": body and world are always already pre-given; perception only determines what is pre-meant.

(327) The pre-givenness of the world includes the pre-given system of realizing paths from what is already realized and currently actively realizing itself. To this belongs that for me, the perceptually striving one, my body is always already and in unbroken continuity experienced as system of perceptual organs (in a widest sense of organs functioning in perceiving doing in any way), and with the horizon: known-unknown world as to be known on the "path" of the kinaesthetic function of the members and so of their bodily movement to be set in play from me. That is merely explication of the pre-given world in its pre-given sense of being. The body as system of organs is part of the world's pre-givenness, the "path" of all knowledge. This is mundane explication, not yet transcendental.

(327) Over against this the task of constitutive explication, in transcendental reduction: to understand how this familiar system of paths and the world itself constitutes itself as accessible to me, the human, the human already pre-given for me, in bodily perceiving, how this always already pre-given being as human in the pre-given world, and the world as world of human-bodily accessibility, is to become intelligible, since it constantly has sense and only the sense it gains as certain to me and becoming certain in the course of my conscious life. This task is evidently peculiar and quite different from asking about the paths on which I, already pre-given for myself and valid to me as actuality, as human finding myself in it, must go to bring it to determinate knowledge, originally through perception, through an action ordered into the world itself, belonging to my human existence. The new task is the transcendental one, of transcendental world-constitution. The distinction of tasks: mundane (which paths must I, the human, take to know the world?) and transcendental (how is the human-in-the-world with his paths constituted?). The circularity of body-perception is a mundane non-problem and a transcendental problem.

(328) Since the body for me, as experienceable from experience, is a body among bodies, persisting as natural object in its changes and first its motions (rests), the ruling of perception in the body, which gives bodily movements the sense of egoically done ones, as a practice of the ego in the world, and indeed as a primal practice which co-functions for all other practice and has already functioned in advance, belonging to it, offers itself to us as exercisable only on the bodily body as the "primally practical object." As perceptual body it has a practical, functional sense, characterizing it as starting point of all practice and first as primal site of an always founding practice, all further practice intervening immediately in the bodily world being at home in it with new primally practical layers. The body is the "primally practical object" (urpraktisches Objekt): perception is a primal practice exercised on the body, founding all practice. The text ends by grounding the "humanized world" of its title in the practical character of the body itself.

(328) Man moves what he moves by moving himself: herein lies that the human ego (the "soul") moves the bodily body practically, psychophysically, and whatever it may do, wherever it may direct its aim in the pre-given world, the world appears to it perceptually; actual and potential perceiving, potential as capacity become inactual but itself to be actualized, to bring appearances actively into the course of self-realization of the appearing, accompanies all practical life in the ordinary sense, and actual perceiving also enters into it, into its paths and realizable goals. Striking here is the privilege of moving over all other modes of change. All activity of change of other kinds is mediated by the activity of motion, and primally-practically of bodily motion. The closing paragraph states the privilege of motion: all practical change is mediated by bodily motion, and all practice is accompanied by perception. Aristotle's thesis that the soul moves the body and through it other things (De anima III.10) is restated phenomenologically.

### Appendix XIX. Kinaesthesis as Desiring Striving-Toward and as Path of the Will (Probably September 1931)

(329) Interest turns to something affecting. From the ego the ray of direction (attending) goes to it; the direction or self-directing of the ego is a striving self-directing, a being-with in attending, being interested, occupied, doing. The ego performs an act. But that is not yet a proper doing, an acting, as being directed attentively at something is not yet being with something experiencing, perceiving or even immediately perceiving, and even as with an object. Proper doing, acting (and the word still has an unusually wide sense here), is directed at something already constituted as "goal," as attainable goal. For one must distinguish between the goal of mere desiring (pole of desire), which relates to a missed good, perhaps one already experienced as good but fading in its goodness, and the practical goal, conscious as goal of a realizing path, one I can realize capably. Attention is already striving, but not yet action. Action requires a goal constituted as attainable by a path. Desire's pole is a missed good; the practical goal is a reachable end.

(329) In the primal sphere of the living perceptual present: here what is earlier in itself is missing, becoming conscious of a lack, and desiring. Am I to say: instinctively, lively desiring or desiring intensifying passes into a first "willing"; it does not remain with the passivity of striving; "active striving" = willing arises from it (though both are "acts")? Am I to say this original willing has the form of a discharging total kinaesthesis, discharging in kinaestheses still unmastered, so that their partial kinaestheses run undifferentiated into one another, while field-data change and occasionally, even repeatedly, the missed thing comes "nearer" or returns? The "nearer" means: a change of data in ascending similarity lets the missed thing always show through in the form of similarity; it already pleases and assimilates the missed, reminds of it and has it in similarity as if within itself, but not yet enough. Every return is return through assimilation in intensification, and once it is there, with satisfaction the desiring willing, the striving, moving willing ceases. Desire is fulfilled; the active striving-toward, which is original kinaesthesis, comes to kinaesthetic rest. A genetic hypothesis about the origin of kinaesthesis from drive: lack, desire, discharge in undifferentiated total kinaesthesis, increasing similarity of data to the missed object, satisfaction and rest. The infant's random movements become, through the experience of approaching the desired, the first will. Kinaesthesis is originally desiring striving, the thesis of the title.

(329–330) Yet one may say this concerns from the outset, according to stimuli, the kinaesthetic manifolds originally assigned singly, those belonging to seeing or, with contact of the hand, those belonging to the hand. But already through the preference of positions of rest, for example positions of least exertion of force into which movement sinks back of itself, it is given that the interesting diminishes or is lost, must be sought again. The kinaestheses themselves are not modes of will, but they constitute themselves as paths of the will toward goals; in active striving toward something they become practised paths, capably traversable, transferring themselves associatively as paths through change of contents. The final correction: kinaestheses are not themselves acts of will but become "paths of the will" through practice, transferable to new contents by association. The tendency of movement to sink back to rest (least effort) explains why the desired is lost and must be sought again, the motive for forming reliable paths. The analysis grounds the "I can" in the genesis of drive and practice, a bridge from the theory of kinaesthesis to the drive-intentionality of No. 34.

### No. 19. Constitution of Unitary Time and of the Unitary Objective World Through Empathy (End of September 1931)

(331) I experience the other through appresentation, two-sidedly. I disregard corporeality and direct my gaze to the nexus of my soul and his. In nexus are my objective perceiving and his, my objective recollecting and his, my actual and possible world-experience and his, noetically, and correlatively noematically. What we perceive ontically in common coexists through coincidence, and so my time constituted in it and his. As regards my and his co-present, past and future, coincidence and identification come about mediately as to everything we can reconstruct in the same way from the common perceptual sphere. The theme: the constitution of one time through the "nexus" (Konnex) of souls. Coincidence in commonly perceived objects yields coincidence of our times, and mediately of pasts and futures.

(331) How is that to be understood? My living present, my primal-modal one, contains my empathy, and in it presentifies the other living present. The body presented in mine ("other's body") is an index for a totality of possible perceptions of my own in which it would come to givenness as one and the same according to everything it is for me and its own temporality (as originally experienceable for me). This body, experienced in my primordiality in the relation of similarity to my primordial body apperceived in the particular way "my body," is apprehended as other's body, becomes bearer of the appresentation of another primal-modal present, in which its body, primordially constituted as body, is in "coincidence" with that "body there" in my primordiality, the time of the one and the other coinciding. Correlatively the constitutions of the two bodies, first the primordialities in this respect, must coincide. The other's body is the point of coincidence of two primal-modal presents: as body in my primordiality and as body in his. Their coincidence carries the coincidence of times.

(332) We both, each purely psychically, each in his time and his living present, which is not spatio-temporal present, have appearances of things, each his own and his validities of being. Now I experience the other and naturally have self-experience. I find "in my now I experience the other" and his now; I find my now and his as being in one, my appearances and his, my appearing as valid to me and his, but both as the same. And in this same experience it has its "externally" appearing duration as its continuing objective present, and this in coincidence with my continuing appearance and with the other's continuing appearance connected with it and coinciding point for point. Two living presents, each not spatio-temporal, coincide in one "now" through the common appearing object, whose objective duration is constituted by the point-for-point coincidence of both appearance-courses.

(332) As long as I perform empathy in my living present, I have in its extension, time-point for time-point, at once my present and the other's, only the latter not perceptually intuitive for me but empathized. The empathized (presentified) is continuously experienced and continuously one with the experiencing. But one will say: it is the same with intuitive recollection. Present recollecting coincides point for point with the recollected as to both durations. But this togetherness of coincidence is not temporal simultaneity: the one is my present, the other my past. The recollected is moreover also presentified present. How does it come about that in the mode of presentification of empathy a simultaneous-temporal togetherness arises? The decisive difference between empathy and memory: both presentify a present and coincide point for point with the presentifying, but only empathy yields simultaneity. The recollected present is past; the empathized present is now. The question is how presentification can produce simultaneity.

(332–333) Here one must consider that, as long as I really perform intuitive empathy, my living streaming present, but with it also my past elapsed meanwhile and to be awakened by recollection, and that of the other come to coincidence. Further, if the other recollects an earlier past in which he was not object of my empathy, this past of memory can still become accessible to me now. Since a stretch of his and my time coincides, the coincidence continues through the whole times. We spoke with each other before; that gives a stretch of actual coincidence; in between the continuity of empathy with the other is missing, but had we come together... Coincidence propagates: once a stretch of our times coincides, both whole times are linked, through recollection on both sides and through the counterfactual "had we met."

(333) Coming together takes place bodily. But being able to come together is my and his capacity, and with times capacities coincide too. Moreover the "play" of bodily coming together is psychically a play of appearances and syntheses of appearances, of kinaestheses and the associated capacities taking place and actualized in my and the other's immanence. So in the exercise of empathy we always have an experiencing consciousness in which, purely psychically, my soul as psychically filled time and the other soul and its time come to continuous coincidence, a coincidence in validity of being. So the coexistence of my temporal-psychic being and the other's is really experienced for me, and likewise for the other when he becomes aware of me. Naturally this propagates through others' others and likewise through mediate empathies with subjects of the past with whom I now have no connection, but of whom I learn by way of others as once experienced by them, and that even in mediacies of propagation (through the generation, history). Coexistence of souls is experienced, not inferred: the coincidence of times is a coincidence in validity of being. It propagates through mediated empathy, generations, history, which grounds historical time on empathy.

(333) It follows that for me and then for everyone a coexistence of all our purely psychic coexistences exists experientially, that every single soul has the form of coexistence of its own time and is for itself and experienced as through this time, its life-time, the time of its persistence in its personal individuality, in its changing habitualities and of its persistence in this change as person of personal properties; further, that all souls have a purely psychic universe, constantly constituted for them as experience as universe of psychic coexistences, such that each has its own sphere of coexistence as primordial experience, all others from immediate and iteratively mediate empathy, actual and potential. A purely psychic universe of coexisting souls, each with its life-time, each having its own sphere primordially and the others through empathy.

(334) The form of the general coexistence in the extended sense of all souls is the universal time enclosed as experience in the souls themselves, into which the single-psychic time-forms do not fit side by side continuously, as if they were stretches in it, as if the formed content of each, the psychic content in its temporality, could continue in that of the others; rather it is a time of higher order, springing from the intentional coincidence of souls in being-for-one-another. The primal source-point of this time-constitution is, for each one's experience, his primal-modal present and each one's capacity to experience others, other egos, other concrete soul-monads, and first to "perceive" others, that is, to experience in one's own living present the alien primal-modal one and thereby the primal coexistence of one's own being and an alien being. This togetherness, which establishes a coincidence between my primal-modal present and the alien one, my primally welling now, my primally welling retentional just-past, phase for phase or together in continuity, is, as potential, at the same time the objective now of the objective, purely psychic all-time. "Objectively" now is what coexists with my primordial now, that is, is actually in coincidence or potentially to be brought to coincidence. This objective now or objective present is streaming present and source-point or welling extension for a streaming objective past and future. What constitutes itself in this streaming as objectively identical then has the objective form of the rigid objective time presenting itself in the streaming, the all-psychic time. The all-time is a time of higher order, not a line on which individual times are stretches. Its source is the coincidence of primal-modal presents in empathy. "Objective now" is defined as what coexists with my primordial now, actually or potentially. This is Husserl's most precise statement of the constitution of intersubjective time: the individual streams do not flow into each other, yet their presents coincide.

(334–336) Of course all this can be understood transcendentally as transcendental clarification of the transcendental all-time over against the transcendental times of the single monads as forms of their self-enclosed individuality. Psychic individuals are not "separate" individuals first through their bodies but in themselves; yet the word does not fit, though souls are indivisible and in this proper sense individuals. The other sense comes into consideration, uniqueness of being, and souls have it not first from a being under circumstances in a pre-given universal time, but from themselves, from their own temporal essence, in which other temporality is intentionally enclosed that is essentially another. The individuality of souls means in a certain sense unbridgeable separation, an otherness and being-outside-one-another (in the logical, not spatial sense) that can never become a continuous connection, a connection that would be continuous flowing-into-one-another of the monadically own times. On the other hand this separation does not prevent, indeed is the condition of possibility for monads being able to "coincide," in other words being able to be in community. But the same says that they coexist, and again that they are and can be in the plural. Monads in the plural, coexisting monads as possibility: the being of one leaves open the possibility of the being of another. The possibility of my monadic being lies in my actuality, and all other possibilities I gain from this actuality by free imaginative variation are only variations and possibilities of me, first only of my actuality. But I, thought as if I were otherwise, does not yet mean another. In me lies the possibility of another and so of duality: I and the other (in further consequence "we two," which already brings something new) exclusively through the possibility of empathy, as my capacity. This mode of presentification yields, when it occurs, as something peculiar the coexistence of what is experienced in this presentifying experience with me, the experiencing ego understood as psychic monad, a coexistence that establishes, as we say, the sense of common present; through which then identically common present is made possible, not only of us two but of an open plurality of ever new others in the unity of an original, that is, present coexistence. Coexistence is not an empty being-together but for me a being-there-for-me, being-there "in common" with me by my existence experiencing another in itself presentifyingly (being able to), together with experiencing itself as presentifying. The presentified as such, as having sense and validity of being in me and confirming itself in me, lies intentionally, as pole of confirmation, in me, and lies in me with its sense of being "other" unbridgeably distinct from me as existing monad in the form of monadic time. I also said "outside" my monad, but in my monad this "being outside it" is itself enclosed as intentional unity of confirmation of the concordant courses of presentification. Similarly as my own past is enclosed in my present as intentional unity of my manifold recollections in their concordant unity (though under correction), but as my past is not nothing in me and my properly living being as present, but has actuality in me as my past and constantly keeps it, has it in the streaming persistence from present to present: so the other, the co-present, is not nothing in me, but in me as my other, and I am only who I am as bearing the other and all others in me. The conclusion is among the most important passages in the volume. Monadic individuality is uniqueness of being from one's own temporal essence, not from position in a pre-given time. Separation is absolute: the streams can never flow into each other. Yet separation is the condition of coincidence and community: only separate monads can coincide. The possibility of another lies in me "exclusively" through the capacity of empathy; self-variation never yields another. The other is "outside" me, yet this outside is enclosed in me as pole of confirmation, as my past is enclosed in my present without being nothing. The final formula, "I am only who I am as bearing the other and all others in me," states the monadological thesis of mutual implication with the analogy of memory as its model, the Ineinander that No. 33 develops.

### No. 20. Constitution of Intermonadic Time. Recollection and Empathy (20/22 September 1931)

(337) Intrapsychic (or transcendental) nexus, intrapsychic time. So, first, temporalization and time within a "monad" as a "world" for itself; second, intermonadic nexus and intermonadic time as time of higher level: world of monads. The program: two levels of time, monadic and intermonadic.

(337) If we consider the nexus of souls with the question whether it still exists under abstractive exclusion of bodies, as a nexus grounded purely in the essence of soul-monads, the following must be considered. The question is whether souls have a nexus independent of bodies.

(337–338) I am bodily here and see the body there. As lived body it induces in me a psychic other. Bodily we are both separate, outside each other; our souls are separately localized. But that does not yet say that our souls cannot have a nexus proper to their essence, and even necessarily must. Closely related is the following question: we both, and humans in general, are objects in the pre-given world. The universal form of coexistence of objects, of individual real concreta, is spatio-temporality. Coexistence in the widest sense as spatio-temporal is either simultaneity, and then local mutual externality in space, or temporal succession. So every human as concretely mundane has his objective duration and in every phase of it his extension in a spatial position; every simultaneous other human another extension at another place. But naturally only by virtue of his corporeality does man have his spatiality, spatial coexistence, in necessary mutual externality, if it is to include simultaneity. On the other hand every soul has temporality not only through its body but a temporality proper to its essence as monadic form of its being as a totality of coexisting psychic "data" (which we take, formally speaking, as ultimate substrates, realities). The monad is the soul, a concrete unity, which is so to speak a world for itself, closed in its own essence. The manifold psychic experiences, and in general whatever is together in a soul and distinguishable as individual datum, are joined into an all-unity. Every such moment, say a single experience, is dependent relative to the whole, the totality in which it occurs. Husserl's note: in the all-unity = world we have a plurality of realities and pluralities of real pluralities; everything in it that can be grasped as single or as many is dependent; everything is together with everything and one together, connected, but stands in a "horizon" of what is to be connected and already connected with it in infinitum; the totality is "infinite." The monad is a "world for itself": a totality of experiences as its "realities," each dependent on the whole. The soul's temporality is its own, not borrowed from its body; souls are spatially separate only through bodies.

(338–339) This world for itself has as form of coexistence of its "realities" a time for itself, the being-time, life-time of the soul, in which all its life runs, all its acts, its associations, the persistence of once instituted habitualities, habits. Everything in it has "its time," its duration, beginning and end within the total time, which as filled with content, and necessarily filled without gaps, is the monad itself, the concrete soul itself, taken purely in and for itself, apart from its body. To the totality the word monad is to express corresponds that each such totality has its total form, its total time, into which all special times, the temporal positions and durations as those of this monad (or of the occurrences of its life), are ordered. In other words, the "life-time" proper to a monadic soul cannot be a piece of a more comprehensive time, cannot be pieced together with the life-times of other monads, cannot be grasped as if it were a mere duration within a universal time in which other and all monads last. Monadic time as form of totality of its concretely joined moments extends continuously further, in a living extension necessarily filled with continuous life-contents; it is concrete living streaming, and everything that conceivably fits into it belongs to one and the same monad, to the same ego living in its life. The time of my streaming life and that of my neighbour are therefore abysmally separated, and even this word says too little in its imagery. As soon as it became one, as this time (with the sense of time grounded in the monad as such, in its own essence), with the neighbour's, we would both be one ego with one life, one stream of experience, one capacity. Monadic time is not a segment of universal time: it cannot be pieced together with others. Two times that merged would be one ego. The separation is "abysmal" (abgrundtief), the strongest statement of monadic individuation in the volume.

(339–340) Parallel to this is that I am bodily here and the other there. As the here cannot become the there, and one human (organic) body cannot become the other, so my soul cannot become the other's; or the duality of bodily bodies goes with the duality of souls, and therein the duality of life-stream times, which are not one time and can never become one, a form to be filled with the life-moments of one and the other soul. Are souls then simultaneous and successive, temporally coexisting only through their connection with bodies, by methexis in universal natural time? Every body has its proper time too, but here it is evident that this time can only have the sense of a duration within the natural all-time, space-time. There is only one nature, and only in this universality is it truly concrete. Single natural bodies are only relatively concrete; they are what they are only as bodies within the totality of bodies, all-nature. Each is in its extensional content, but this content as filled spatio-temporal extension is not yet fully determined as body, which rather has its current extensional what as causal state. It is what it is under its "circumstances"; it has its causal form and its individual causal properties. The unity of nature is the unity all bodies have as substrates of causal properties; they "persist" in the change of their extensional states as the same bodies by virtue of persisting in their causal regulation under causal circumstances, which are themselves the surrounding bodies. In the causal interweaving of all bodies with all, nature, the universe of bodies, has its concretion in a universal causality, and thereby an individual form of concretion, that of the concrete totality, space-time. Time, understood as universal coexistence, is not an empty meaningless form into which any set of particulars could be poured. Unity of a universal time has sense only as form of unity of a totality, which is form of concretion not as empty totality but as totality of objects with a content proper to them. Are souls coexistent only by "methexis" (participation, the Platonic term) in natural time through their bodies? The contrast with bodies: a body's time is a duration within nature's all-time, since bodies are relatively concrete, constituted by universal causality. Universal time is the form of concretion of a totality with intrinsic connections, not an empty container: the Leibnizian relational view of time against the Newtonian.

(340) Certainly souls share in this form mediately through their embodiment. But that does not give them a universal form of time grounded in their own essence. If they have one, it is clear in advance from what was said of nature that it would presuppose a nexus of all souls with one another grounded in their own essence, of which it would be the form of concretion. For nature the universal nexus was established by the extensional structure of separate bodies and yet founded in their own essence by a universal causality interweaving all bodies, which were fully constituted bodies only through their being-for-themselves constituted in persisting causal properties. What would correspond for souls? In any case it is self-evident that if a universal concrete form of time existed for the totality of souls, which are on the other hand separated in their own essence, this would have to be a time of higher order, since each soul as a "world" for itself has its time. For its higher-founded form the single monads and their single life-times would be "temporal content," filling; so the contents of the various monads and these themselves, as well as their life-times, could coexist in the modes of simultaneity and succession according to the sense of a universal time. A universal psychic time would require a psychic analogue of causality, a nexus grounded in the souls' essence, and would be a time of higher order whose contents are the monads with their life-times.

(340–341) Indeed, with some deepening into the way humans, according to the sense of world-experience, as ego-subjects, concretely the human souls, coexist temporally in their living being, one can see that it is not the psychophysical connection of souls with bodies that first makes temporalization of souls and their psychic contents possible, but that in the souls themselves a nexus proper to souls and a psychic form of coexistence is grounded, a form of plurality of souls, of a possible togetherness of one and another and perhaps again another soul, and finally a form of totality making a totality, then endlessly open ("infinity"), possible. On exact consideration the situation reverses as against the supposedly self-evident: it is not that there are first several souls and the question is under what conditions they are "compatible" in existence; rather the question is how I can, being certain of one soul and deepening into its own essence (in self-giving intuition), gather from it that it is merely "one" soul and can only be so, that it must therefore refer in this essence itself to other souls, that this soul is indeed in itself and for itself, but has sense only in a plurality grounded in it itself, to be unfolded from it. The reversal is decisive: not "given many souls, how are they compatible?" but "given one soul, how does its essence refer to plurality?" The singular soul is "one" soul only within a plurality grounded in itself, the anti-Leibnizian move of Nos. 12–13 now stated as an eidetic task.

(341) It is the same already for corporeality. The body has its own essence. What is first intuitive is its extension. But this intuition does not yet give me the body; it comes to self-givenness only when I take its causality into account; it has a causal essence by which it is in advance only "one" body among bodies. The plural precedes the singular, and yet the singular of extension (which under the aspect of causal apperception is called state) precedes the plural, the many separate extensions, only these would not yet be bodies without the universal causal regulation according to which every extension is state in a special system of states in which the same body with the same causal properties is constituted synthetically. Bodies: the plural precedes the singular (a body is "one" body among bodies by its causal essence), and the singular extension precedes the plural (as state). The dialectic of one and many in nature models that of souls.

(341–342) What then is the analogue of causal achievement for the temporalization of nature, what corresponds to this achievement for making possible psychic coexistence? And what corresponds to the substantiality of the body, its peculiarity of being substrate of causal properties? What, analogically speaking, makes up the substantiality of the soul, what in its own essence refers to plurality, so that, deepening into the essence of the soul as soul, we necessarily find it as "one" soul among souls, possible and actual? The answer must evidently be: souls are not only in themselves and for themselves, or souls are human ego-subjects in the concretion of their streaming life considered purely psychically; but as such they are not merely being in themselves and for themselves, so in their monadic temporality. They are also, and essentially, in actual or potential community, in actual and potential nexus, of which commerce, intercourse in its manifold forms, is only a particular case. Instead of intercourse (Umgang) we could say "concern" (Angang): souls are not only for themselves, they concern each other (gehen einander an). But that is grounded in them themselves; each in its essence already has the essential form of a "substance," a personality, as an ego that has "relation" in its life to other such ego-subjects and their life. As soul it is concrete only through this its structure, through being only as egoic being, which other egos concern (a relation that can then reverse) and with which it can then have intercourse, communalize itself in various modes of commerce. The psychic analogue of causality is mutual concern: souls "concern each other" (Angang, a pun on Umgang). The soul's "substantiality" is its being a person related to other persons. Community is essential, not accidental; intercourse is a particular case of a prior essential nexus. The Leibnizian "commercium" of substances is reinterpreted as personal concern.

(342–343) The primal mode of concern is empathy. In self-perception, in original being-present for myself, what is originally present is the ego in my own life. To it belongs the life-moment of empathy. But through it I relate to a second ego and its life; through it it is immediately there for me as other and concerns me. It concerns me as other already in that before all intercourse with it I eo ipso not only experience its life, so far as it really becomes a there for me (in the sphere of intuitiveness and in any case of determinacy), but live with it, perceiving with, believing with, judging with, agreeing, rejecting, doubting, rejoicing with, fearing with. All modes of this "with" are modes of a primal communalization, in which, living in my (primordial, primal-original) life, I yet live with the other life co-existing for me empathically, a unity of life being established and an I-thou unity of the ego-poles through the medium of empathy. At first life-community with ego-communalization concerns only what is actually communalizing in particular; but together with it the horizons of life on both sides, and that means a synthesis of the potentialities on both sides. Empathy is "primal communalization" (Urvergemeinschaftung): to understand is already to live with, in all the modes of the "with" (co-perceiving, co-believing, co-rejoicing). An I-thou unity of the ego-poles arises through empathy, extending to the synthesis of both horizons. This is the most explicit statement in the volume that empathy is not a cognitive act preceding community but already community.

(343) In the actual performance of an empathy my primal-modal streaming present, my primal-modal "I am," whose now-being-present is being from primally presenting temporalization (the ego being present (now) in the narrowest and most proper sense), "coincides" with the other's primal-modal present, which for me is not primal-modal but appresented; and from there coincidence takes hold of the horizons on both sides. My primal-modal primal-impressional now, the absolute source-point of primal-modal originality, coincides with the empathically presentified primal-impressional source-point now, which in this coincidence is simultaneous with mine in form and content. The form of modification of the just-past repeats itself in the presentification and coincides phase for phase with the primal-modally running modification, concretely in form and content, and so the identity, constituted in the living streaming present, of what sinks streamingly into ever further fresh past repeats itself, and with it the identically persisting filled time phase for phase, and what is repeated stands phase for phase in coincidence in form and content; so a temporal simultaneity of the supra-monadic or intermonadic time of higher level is constituted. The constitution of intermonadic simultaneity: coincidence of the two primal-impressional nows, then of the retentional modifications phase for phase. The other's present is "appresented" yet simultaneous. The heading that follows takes up the objection from memory.

#### Recollection and Empathy

(343–346) One might become doubtful with regard to the similar case of the coincidence of my primal-modal present and my current present of recollection. To my living primal-modal present also belongs my recollecting now occurring and flowing off (as to it belongs the presentifying of every kind now occurring and flowing off, among them of the kind "empathy"). Here present streaming recollecting coincides phase for phase with what is recollected in it, which we address as the earlier streaming present. If coincidence were constitutive for temporal simultaneity, must not simultaneity rather than succession be experienced here? But, we shall have to answer, coincidence between recollection as present experience and the recollected experience does not by itself alone constitute the coexistence of the form of succession. First each of these experiences is dependent; each in its intuitiveness is experience in a concrete field of experience, more precisely in the field of a concrete streaming present, my primally streaming one or the total present co-presentified through the presentified experience. But even that does not suffice. On one side it is evidently essential that a present carries with it its horizon of past and a horizon of what is coming, and so every recollected present its recollective one. On the other, every recollectingly living present has a future horizon already fulfilled in past streaming on. And that this is so is itself drawn from the horizon belonging to every recollection or its re-presentifying actualization. Added to this, recollection is essentially an experience of capacity: to it belongs "I can always recollect this again, repeat the recollection," that is an ever new production of recollections in which the same present is presentified again and again, at an ever new place of my living streaming present (in the unity of continuity of one wakefulness). Only through this capable repetition under identification and through consciousness "I can do so again at will" do I gain an identical of validity and continuing validity, and this, by virtue of coincidence with ever other living presents (overflowing into one another, constituting the unity of an encompassing living present, the one within which I perform the repetitions), is characterized as coexisting with them all, but now as other, as not present. So in a certain way the ego of this presentified and not present present is addressed by me as ego, as the same I now actually present am, belonging to this primal-modally streaming present, and yet really another, not the properly existing, now really existing, but presentified ego, modification (which the word "other" should designate for the moment). But the modification gains the sense of past only from another side: I have many such modified presents available and currently actually given as "remembered," each another, each with another modified ego, each with a horizon of others and again others of the same kind to be capably fetched, and at the same time all enclosed in a total horizon that synthetically covers all single horizons. Here coexistence through coincidence is constituted again and again in actualization (which is reactualization of the actively original constitution of being). Since all coinciding is first coinciding of the primal-modal present in the primal-modal ego (I who presentify the earlier) with modified presents or egos, it is coexistence of all presentified presents with the now primal-modal one, and likewise of the presentified egos with the now present one. The universal form of these coexistents, comprising pasts and primal-modal present, is time in a sense not yet accounting for the future, or the past as belonging to the streaming primal present. But the pasts (which at this level of explication we should not yet call so) are not a togetherness, a coexistence in one plane. One must consider that they not only all coincide with the living present but also among themselves, that is, one gives itself as earlier than another. The latter as presentified present in which the other is presentified as the earlier, as in my primal-modal present any past. The earlier is itself again a possible relative primal mode (in presentification) for the one earlier relative to it. On the other hand in each any earlier one can also appear directly in presentification, in immediate coincidence. Capably I can, recollecting my living present Gm (m indicating the modification), be given as ego of this Gm, say Im, recollecting a Gm1 with its Im1, and so on. Here it is to be noted that I, the present ego, capably recollecting Gm, can capably and actively develop Gm so as to come to a recollection of second level, and so on. What I can in my present is not only present ability but ability that later enters into recollection, intentionally implied in it, and at the same time actual ability in the primally living present: to activate the implied with the sense of a recollection in the recollection, which at the same time has for me now the validity of being of actual recollection. The order essentially carried by every set of givens of memory is the order of earlier and later; every jumble of memories I can order, or at least want to order (certain that there is order), by earlier and later into a single series. To the essence of this series belongs that it is a series of recollections which, taking always only the concretely full presentified presents, are intentionally enclosed in one another, so that in the primal-modal present the next earlier is recollected, in it the next earlier still, and so in constant mediacy. The capacity to order so is one with the capacity to find in each the one earlier than it. On the other side to any two recollections belongs the possibility of seeking and finding intermediate recollections, or, more fundamentally, of going back from each to an immediately earlier one, to a recollection of the retentional just-past, and on the other side of advancing continuously from each in the protentional direction of fulfilment to continuously new recollection. So continuously I can come from each recollection to each within wakefulness. Husserl's note: how continuity is gained, indeed what it really means, must first be brought out by deeper consideration; it must be shown how I can gain for every recollection a "continuously earlier" one, and what "continuously" characterizes: the possibility of turning attentively to the "just sunk" and realizing it identifyingly as recollection; on the other hand how I let myself be carried from the recollected present in continuously streaming progress into the later present, so coming in a continuous stream of recollection from earlier to later. Finally the world of possible recollection must be recognized as a continuum with the form of continuous time, within which every recollection that occurs actualizes a stretch as revivified present. The objection: recollection also involves point-for-point coincidence, yet yields succession, not simultaneity. The answer is subtle. Coincidence alone constitutes neither simultaneity nor succession; what matters is the structure of horizons and the capacity of repetition. The recollected present has its own horizons, including a future already fulfilled; and recollection is an "I can again," which constitutes the recollected as an identical repeatable at ever new places of the living present, hence as coexisting with all of them but "as other, as not present." The recollected ego is "really another," a modification, the term "other" being used "for the moment," the closest the volume comes to identifying self-alterity in time with alterity of persons (cf. No. 28; Beilage XXXVIII). The order of earlier and later arises from the nesting of recollections in one another and from continuity of passage. The difference from empathy, implicit here, is that the empathized present is not nested in my past but coincides with my now: it is not "as not present" but appresented as present.

(346–347) The actual present and every presentified one always already has a unitarily continuous past-horizon, a horizon that harbours the capacity to awaken an open "infinity" of manifold recollections and to order them so and nest them intentionally in one another, to repeat each for itself and identify it by its "content," so that each counts from the outset as unity of repetition and as concrete-individual. Added to this the capacity to recognize that the past is a continuous unity through which the one ego, as continuously present and continuously past and ever further past, is numerically identically the same in this continuity, the same of one same life, which has its sameness in that, modifying itself continuously from the living present, in which it is originally living life, on one side it experiences ever new things in continuity streaming on, on the other it streams off and ever further into the "infinities" of "the" past. But can that mean anything other than that the living present is in itself, as first and primal phenomenon, a continuum with the twofold direction of streaming-on and streaming-off, with the centre primal impression, from the primal-impressional phase as primal source and primal mode continuously modifying itself in "streaming off," continuously "covering itself" in fading until full "darkening," non-intuitiveness and non-emergence? Thereby the dark, the "unconscious," falls out of the frame of the "primal phenomenon." Yet as such it is not nothing, but, as afterward shows, continues to stream on in its constitutive style. Darkened, it can be awakened "again," become intuitive again in the mode and frame of explicit clarity and emergence, that is, of the primal-phenomenal present, as presentified, modified present (as cogitatum of the recollection now occurring as "experience"). The living present is the primal phenomenon: a continuum of streaming-on and streaming-off around the primal impression. The "unconscious" is the darkened end of retention, outside the primal phenomenon yet not nothing, awakenable. This is the phenomenological concept of the unconscious as sedimented, darkened past (Analyses Concerning Passive Synthesis, Hua XI, the "zero of affection"), a concept Michel Henry would contest and Laplanche discuss.

(347) But does this talk of dark streaming-on and being able to become intuitive again mean anything other than that to the living present and its actually living ego belongs the capacity for this constitution, this temporalization? Or rather, of the temporalization that has long done its work and also extends further in the manner of the progress of already formed experience, can be reactivated capably again and again in the form of confirmation. But temporalization, experience-formation, constitution has various sides. The ego itself is constituted as temporal unity. It is the ontic unity already acquired as standing and abiding ego (and in further acquiring constantly further acquired): identical ego of my temporal life as the same existing ego of all my pasts, of my life that has run within the continuous unitary form of time and still streams on, which in streaming on constitutes in itself and for itself ever new past as persisting. The past is a capacity of the present ego; the ego is constituted as temporal unity, an "ontic" unity acquired and continually acquired. The "standing and abiding ego" (stehendes und bleibendes Ich) anticipates the "standing-streaming" present of the C-manuscripts.

(348) The present is "absolute actuality," most properly actuality as primally generating. As such it ontifies itself into a temporal mode, and primally temporalizing it has temporal being as ontic acquisition; primally generating, it had always already primally generated it. I, constantly in the present "possessor" of the acquired, always already conscious of myself in the present as the ego I am and have been as the same ego, having a lived life behind me and having acquired in it, all this is to be seen in primal liveliness in the primal phenomenon, because it, all this, is itself already acquisition, and to be explicated, that is, to be constituted again, constituting and at the same time in consciousness of reactivation. The living present is "absolute actuality" that "ontifies" itself into a temporal mode: the present becomes a now among nows, its own product. The ego "possesses" its past as acquisition, and every explication of it is reconstitution with consciousness of reactivation.

(348–349) More exact account must be given of the continuity of subjective time or of the self-constitution of the temporality of subjectivity, the most originally existing. We have considered recollection as awakenings, as heaps of awakened pasts ordering themselves, but have not taken account of future horizons, not only that belonging to the primal-modal present as the "coming," but that entering reproductively into every past itself. We can let ourselves be carried on by the streaming of the present. Here the being and living in the "living present" and the relativity attaching to this concept must first be clarified. In streaming primal-modal living on I am subject of affections and actions, and first those must be considered that are directed back or forward (if not at the primally welling now-persisting itself) within the living present itself. This present is a unique liveliness, a primal present, one stream, and yet a stream in which it can be said with good sense that a primal present flows over into a new primal present and ever new ones. The "still living," and yet already sunk, to which I can go back, which I can grasp actively and experience as the same through recollecting intuition, becomes intuitive and explicated as mere re-presentification of what is "still" in the streaming and still in the field for me. Carried on to new more original liveliness, the earlier may already be almost wholly sunk, indeed "wholly sunk," but I grasp it afterward, directed back, as "just still there" and as "still in the field," wholly sunk into the dark of the "unconscious," but as belonging to the stream, and then as the same that re-intuition only makes intuitive again. And conversely, from the reawakened and re-intuitive I can let myself be carried on again in reproducing and perform the activities again and so pass from present to present and recognize each continuously earlier as the just sunk, ever more unclear and yet still streaming-off. Recollection is not something new, but new only as mode; it only goes back and only clarifies a stretch of the sunk streaming. The living present is "one stream" in which primal presents flow into new ones: a relative concept. Recollection is "not something new" but a mode of clarifying the sunk stream, which remains "in the field" even when wholly darkened. This continuity of retention and recollection is the basis for the continuity of subjective time.

(349) The present goes toward the future with open arms. In its streaming on, in its intentionality, the primally streaming present acquires the future. What occurs as now in primal intuitiveness is the becoming-intuitive of fulfilment, the making-intuitive that primal-impressional present itself performs, fulfilling. But this primally streaming fulfilling acquires future really only through sinking into the retentional modes under coincidence and through the achievement of recollection. Present and future are acquired only through recollection and its capacity of again-and-again, in which the streaming process of fulfilment and in general of most original temporalization can be repeated again and again. From every recollected present I can rise continuously, living on in streaming, so living into the future, into the future that has already become past, not the undetermined, empty future, but already acquired. As acquired it is now in recollection the streaming process in which not only the originally protentional becomes primal-impressional, but this becomes retentional, and so the whole living present becomes and the sinking of the whole present into another becomes, or has become, reproductively presentified. "The present goes toward the future with open arms": protention is the present's forward openness. But the future is "acquired" only through retention and recollection, since only in recollection is the passage from protention to impression to retention reproduced as a determinate process. Even the future, as constituted time, depends on memory's "again and again." The text ends, without returning to empathy, on the self-constitution of the temporal stream, the ground on which intermonadic time is to be built.

(349–350) From every recollection, or from what it recollects, I can follow continuously in the process the becoming of future presents and reach the later recollection as the present that has become. The final sentence completes the argument: recollections are linked forward by following the becoming of futures, so the recollective series is continuous in both directions.

### Appendix XX. The Ego and Everything My Own. The Ego in Its Habitual Ownness (the Ego of Decisions). The Universe of What Is My Own as Distinct from the Other (May 1932)

(350) The stream of consciousness in its primal originality is unthinkable without a primally original ego-pole. It lies also in anonymous lived experience. The act of reflection discloses the experience unreflected in the mode just-past and its ego-pole; but both in coincidence the same pole. All presentifying experiences carry the ego-pole in themselves as presentified; if they are memories in the widest sense, coincidence: the same pole.
